# Concept lexicon, version 2

This lexicon turns what a character, place or moment stands for into the musical means that say it. Version 2 merges three new source digests into version 1 (`concepts.md`): the pooled emotion and mood taxonomies with their feature data (`src-lists.md`), Philip Tagg's listener-association taxonomy and sign typology (`src-tagg.md`), and musical topic theory (`src-topics.md`). It still rests on the music-psychology digest (`research.md`) and the device library (`devices-clean.md`), and it is written for the sampled orchestra (`format-full.md`). It is general: not tuned to any one character or listener.

**What changed from version 1.** 60 new concepts (162 in all, in the same 11 groups). Four entries renamed to cover more ground: *The sea* became **The sea and waters**, *Sky and air* became **Sky, air and flight**, *Dawn and light* became **Dawn, daylight and sun**, *Industry and machines* became **Industry, work and machines**. Entries that rested on convention or inference now carry the new evidence where the sources give any (fire, ice, sky, the sea, poverty, greed, sickness, freedom, captivity, industry, menace, madness, pride, cunning, loyalty, betrayal, guilt and others). Five rules were added (R9 to R13), an orchestra substitution table, a quick index of fantasy peoples and places, and a full coverage appendix.

**Key to citations**
- **Research digest** (as in version 1): `§1 Tempo`, `§1 Mode`, `§1 Loudness`, `§1 DynVar`, `§1 Register`, `§1 Range`, `§1 Contour`, `§1 Intervals`, `§1 Harmony`, `§1 Tonality`, `§1 Rhythm`, `§1 Artic`, `§1 Timbre`, `§1 Attack`, `§1 Ornament`, `§1 Pauses`, `§1 Change`, `§1 Texture`, `§1 Form`, `§1 Lookup` are the subsections of research §1. `§2` combination of cues, `§3` composer technique, `§4` chills and expectation, `§5` film, `§6 rN` rule N of §6, `§7.1` to `§7.5` (topics and narrative, GEMS, liking, strong experiences, contour and timbre), `§7.6 rN`, `§8.1` to `§8.5` (mechanisms, infancy, culture, context, neuroscience), `§8.6 rN`.
- **Pooled taxonomies** (`src-lists.md`; source codes as in that file): `HEV cl.N` Hevner 1936/1937, adjective cluster N, with Hevner's feature weights as reproduced in the GSTF table (e.g. "minor 20" = minor mode wins with weight 20); `SCH` Schubert 2003 (grouping unverified); `GEMS` Zentner, Grandjean & Scherer 2008, factor and item loading; `GMC` Coutinho & Scherer 2017 (GEMIAC); `COW` Cowen et al. 2020, *PNAS*; `EV` Eerola & Vuoskoski 2011, with features from Eerola, Lartillot & Toiviainen 2009; `ES` Eerola & Saari 2025; `JAM` MTG-Jamendo tags; `AS` AudioSet; `MRX` Hu & Downie clusters and the Thayer quadrants in Bischoff et al. 2009; `AMM` / `AMT` AllMusic moods / themes; `LOTR` Shore's themes as catalogued after Adams 2010 (build-note tags [W][T][R][S][A] as in the source); `SW n` Lehman, *Complete Catalogue of the Musical Themes of Star Wars* (2023), theme n; `BAR` Padovani, Ferreira & Lelis 2017 (Bardo, D&D transcripts); `HM` Hutchings & McCormack (feature correlations, Table A2); `YM` YM2413-MDB; `WIKI` the Wikipedia feature table.
- **Tagg** (`src-tagg.md`): `VVA nnnn` is a code in Tagg's verbal-visual association taxonomy (TLTT Appendix 4). Works: `TLTT` Tagg & Clarida, *Ten Little Title Tunes* (2003), with page; `MM` *Music's Meanings* (2012), chapter or page; `Kojak` (1979/2000), section; `Sth99`; `Death` (1993); `Anguish` (2004); `Tritone` (1998); `Nature` (1982); `Semio99` (1999), page; `EvTon` *Everyday Tonality II* (2014).
- **Topics** (`src-topics.md`): `Tnnn` is that file's topic number, followed by its source key: `HAT23` Hatten 2023; `HAR10` Haringer's review of Monelle 2006; `McC14` McClelland 2014; `FW23` Frolova-Walker 2023; `MCK07` McKay 2007; `WEE22` Weekley 2022; `LW23` Lavengood & Williams 2023; `LAV` Lavengood's course page; `ATK19` Atkinson 2019; `MUR06` Murphy 2006; `LEH13` Lehman 2013; `CWE14` Cweibel 2014 (after Lerner 2001); `GRAB` Grabócz (reproducing Agawu, Dickensheets, Grabócz, Floros, Mirka); `LOCKE07` Locke 2007; `SCH06` Schneider 2006; `HAL20` Halfyard 2020; `CRB` the Allanbrook summary; `WIK-x` the Wikipedia extracts; `[gen.]` a feature the topics file marks as unchecked textbook knowledge.

**Strength and kind of support**
- **[S]** strong, **[M]** moderate, **[W]** weak: strength of a research finding (research digest, and the measured studies in `src-lists.md`: Hevner's weights [M], GEMS factor structure [S], COW [M], EV [M], HM [W, one study]).
- **[T]**: Tagg's reception data. Listeners heard unlabelled title tunes and wrote what they imagined; a category "over-scored" when a tune drew at least twice the ten-tune average (TLTT 143–152). This is evidence of **connotation**, from 607 mostly Swedish listeners in the 1980s hearing Euro-American screen idioms. It is culture-bound, but players of fantasy games share most of these codes.
- **top.**: a documented musical topic. It is convention, but convention that music historians have traced in the repertoire, with its features and meaning (Ratner, Monelle, Hatten and others).
- **conv.**: scoring convention from film, TV and game practice (the device library, the leitmotif catalogues of LOTR and Star Wars, game-music course notes). Learned association, not measured response.
- *(inf.)*: inference. Neither source states it; it combines nearby findings or general practice.
- Device names in *italics* are entries in `devices-clean.md`. Everything under **Colours and devices** is convention unless it carries a research or [T] citation.

---


> **How the translator uses this file.** Read section 1 (how to combine concepts), pick the character's concepts from `concepts-index.md`, then read only those entries here (search for "#### <name>"). Every entry has the same fields: Cues, Melody, Harmony, Colours and devices, With / against, Intensity, Not, and where a near neighbour overlaps, Tell apart (R14). Coverage of the sources: `concepts-coverage.md`.

## 1. How to use it

**The method, in five steps.**
1. **Describe the subject** in two sentences. Cover what they are, how they move and hold themselves, what they want, what they hide, and how much they matter in the world.
2. **List the concepts** the subject stands for and weight each one.
   - **Carrying** (one, or two that agree): this concept owns the piece's main dials (tempo, loudness, mode, texture) from start to finish. Name its **expressive mode** too (R9).
   - **Strong** (one or two): this concept gets a layer of its own (the undercurrent, the accompaniment, a register, a solo voice) or one section or moment (the crack, the climax).
   - **Colour** (a few): an instrument choice, an interval, an ornament, a cadence, a topic fragment, or one device used once.
   - **Always weight Stature/scale too.** It is a concept like the others, and it sets how big the piece may get.
3. **Look each concept up** below. For the carrying concept, take the **Cues**. For strong and colour concepts, take mainly the **Colours and devices** and the **Intensity** level that matches their weight. For a people or a place, start from the quick index (section 3).
4. **Combine them** using the rules below. Then check the result against each concept's **Not** line.
5. **Test by commutation** (Tagg MM ch. 7). Swap one parameter at a time in your head: tempo, register, articulation, instrument, mode, room size. If one swap flips the reading, that parameter is carrying the meaning, so guard it; if no swap changes anything, the piece says too little.

**Combination rules.**

- **R1. Cues add up, so agreeing concepts reinforce each other.** Listeners combine cues roughly additively: main effects explain most of the variance, and linear models account for about 70-85% of emotion ratings (§2, lens model [S]); audio-feature models in a film-music set reached 58-85%, best for activity and worst for valence (EV [M]). Each cue only correlates with an emotion. The more cues point the same way, the more reliable the message (§6 r3). So when concepts agree (holiness, peace and wisdom all want slow, consonant, legato music), stack their shared cues and the identity sharpens. The lens model's "vicarious functioning" (one cue can stand in for another, §2) means a missing conventional colour can be covered by another cue that points the same way, which is also how the substitution table below works.

- **R2. Set the dials in order.** Set energy first with tempo and loudness, because they outweigh most other cues and arousal is easier to read than valence (§6 r1; §2 [S]). Hevner's weights agree: tempo had the largest and most consistent effect, mode came second and mattered for only about four of her eight clusters, pitch third, harmony and rhythm less, and melodic direction almost nothing (HEV ranking [M]). Tagg adds that perceived **speed is surface rate** (notes per minute) as much as beat: a 72 bpm tune with galloping semiquavers sounded faster to listeners than a 112 bpm rock track (Tagg MM ch. 8; TLTT 366–372 [T]). Set valence second, mainly with mode (§6 r2), but remember that "major is happy, minor is sad" is a common oversimplification: lively minor dances abound, and tempo, surface rate, loudness, phrase length, range and contour decide more (Tagg MM ch. 7/9; Death). Treat tension as a separate dial from energy (§7.6 r10, Thayer [M]; EV's tension-arousal axis): a march has high energy and low tension, a stalking scene the reverse. The Bardo labels for D&D table talk fall on exactly these two dials: calm, happy, agitated, suspenseful (BAR). Solemnity versus triviality is a fourth axis (§7.2, Wedin [M]), and potency (weight) a fifth. Most concepts amount to a setting of these dials plus a colour (an instrument, an idiom, a device). **The carrying concept owns the dials.** Strong and colour concepts should mostly add colour, or take over the dials only inside their own layer or moment.

- **R3. One concept carries; the others colour it or enter at moments.** The cue code has **low capacity**: it passes basic emotions reliably and complex blends poorly (§2 [S]). Listeners take expression mostly from local chunks of about six seconds, and shuffling the order of sections barely changes their ratings (§1 Form [M]). A piece whose main dials keep changing therefore reads as several short pieces, not as one character. The exception is a mercurial subject, where rapid change between topics is itself the concept (§7.6 r1; the sudden contrasts of T036 sensibility, HAT23).

- **R4. Put conflicting concepts on different layers or at different moments. Never average them.** Because cues add, mixing a calm cue set and a mad one in the same voice cancels them out toward a vague mild unease that is neither *(inf. from §2)*. Pairs like holy and corrupt, a calm surface over madness, or gentle and powerful each need separation:
  - **By layer.** Use surface versus undercurrent (a dance over a drone: *Façade* plus *Dread*), melody versus accompaniment (a sweet tune over a wrong bass), or high versus low register (a pure line on top, a corrupt pedal below). Register alone is a weak cue (§1 Register [M-W]), so give each layer its own timbre as well. Music can carry several emotions at once (§5 [W]); listeners report mixed happy-and-sad feelings (§7.2 [M]), and "bittersweet" is one of the 28 feeling categories listeners use for music (COW).
  - **By moment.** Run the surface, then a crack, then the climax or the collapse: the outsider that intrudes and is absorbed (§7.6 r5), dark-to-light or dark-light-dark trajectories (§7.6 r4), *Descent*, *Last Note Nightmare*. A crack lands hardest as a surprise against a prepared background (§4), and an ominous bed amplifies a later startle (§8.6 r13 [M]). Film horror does the same with a pastoral or galant foil before the shock (T158, McC14).
  - **By irony.** Play the surface straight while the story contradicts it (*Soundtrack Dissonance*). Listeners resolve a mismatch into a meaning (§8.4 [W-M]), so frame it in narration when the reading matters (§8.6 r19). Tagg's commutation test shows how strong this is: the same farm footage became a killer's story when the pastoral oboe was replaced with a high held dissonance and low chromatic brass stabs (Tagg Sth99).

- **R5. Scale is one concept among the others.** The subject's importance and power set the piece's ceiling (*Stature*). A great figure's piece, however restrained its surface, reaches **one grand climax** that grows out of what came before. A minor figure stays at chamber scale. Dignity governs the surface and stature governs the peak. A humble great figure gets a chamber surface and one climax that falls back quickly; a pompous minor figure gets big apparatus at the wrong size (*Mock melodrama*). The climax tools are the chill triggers: a sudden forte, new voices entering, a widened range, the theme returning, an unprepared harmony (§4 [M]). Listeners remember a piece by its peak and its end (§7.6 r19). The heroic topic tradition says the same: hold the complete heroic theme back until the climax (T084, HAT23; T085 apotheosis, GRAB).

- **R6. Density: a short piece carries one or two ideas, layered rather than chained.** Liking peaks at middling complexity (§7.3, inverted U [M]). People busy with a demanding task, like a table playing a game, prefer simpler music (§7.3; §7.6 r16). Richer orchestration lets the melody stay simple (§3; §6 r27). In a piece of about a minute, layer the strong concept *under or over* the carrying one; don't chain three concepts one after another. Apart from the climax, allow at most one change of the main dials. In variations, one changed dial still reads as the same person; three changed at once read as someone else (*Theme and variations*).

- **R7. Music supplies adjectives, not nouns.** It can give the motion of an emotion (stormy, gentle, rising, sinking) but not its object. Emotions that depend on thought content, such as jealousy, shame or treachery, cannot be stated (§7.5; §7.1 Hanslick; §7.6 r7 [W]). For concepts like these (betrayal, guilt, loyalty, greed, jealousy), write the theme's **behaviour**: an answer that never comes, a motif that keeps returning to one wound, a cadence that turns aside. Let the story, the GM's framing (§7.6 r17) and leitmotif pairing (§8.1) supply the object. Tagg's taxonomy is a useful check here: it files words by the music that evokes them, not by dictionary sense (VVA resource page), so guilt, jealousy and suspicion share one field with madness (VVA 127) because the same music calls them up.

- **R8. Write themes as gait and posture.** Music resembles how a person moves (§7.5; §7.6 r6 [W]). Tagg's kinetic anaphones make this concrete: gross-motor movement (riding, marching, walking, running, dancing, dragging) sets the groove, and fine-motor movement (flicker, ripple, tick, flutter) sets the surface figuration (Tagg MM 498 ff.). Ask how the subject walks (heavy, gliding, darting, limping, strutting) and set the rhythm and contour to match: a limp is *alla zoppa* (T054), a strut is dotted, a glide slides.

- **R9. Name the expressive mode before setting the dials (Hatten).** The topics cluster into a few modes, each a ready-made bundle of dials (HAT23):
  - **Tragic**: minor and dissonance. Slow forms: sensibility, pathetic style, ombra. Fast forms: tempesta (storm) and appassionato.
  - **Galant**: the unmarked major norm: polite, everyday, nothing at stake.
  - **Comic (buffa)**: fast, patter rhythms, gestures that swerve.
  - **Pastoral**: simplicity against complexity: drones and pedals, slow harmonic rhythm, an early turn to IV, parallel thirds and sixths, climaxes softened or undercut.
  - **Heroic**: dotted rhythms, tonic triads and fanfare arpeggios, the rising rocket, march metre, a public monumental style.
  - **Religious**: learned counterpoint, chorale or hymn, monumental texture, the sarabande, the French overture.
  - Two documented blends: the **hunt** is pastoral crossed with heroic (6/8 plus horn fifths), and the **funeral march** fuses tragic, heroic and religious.
  The carrying concept usually belongs to one mode; naming it sets tempo, mode and texture in one move. **Rank** has its own ladder (Allanbrook, via HAT23 and CRB): rhythms that regiment the body (march, minuet, sarabande) carry high, noble passions; freer, finer rhythms (gigue, pastorale, siciliano, passepied) carry common, earthy ones; bourrée, gavotte and musette sit between; the contredanse and the waltz are for mixed company. The old high, middle and low styles map the same way (T025, GRAB; HAT23).

- **R10. Choose how each concept signifies (Tagg's sign types).** Every musical sign works in one or more of these ways (Tagg MM ch. 13; Semio99 25–30), and most entries below say which they lean on:
  - **Anaphone** (resemblance). *Sonic*: a stylised outside sound (thunder as a timpani roll, a bell, a birdcall, hooves, a sigh or moan from the voice). *Tactile*: sound felt as texture (an attack-less string pad is silk and luxury, rough low brass is gravel, celesta is glass). *Kinetic*: music that moves like something (gallop, march, ripple, flicker, glide; removing the pulse altogether gives vast still space). *Composite*: several at once (the gallop, the stabbing shower figure, the telegraph rhythm of urgent news). *Social*: the texture models a group (unison is one mind; heterophony is many voices saying one thing their own way; call and response is dialogue; a figure over a backdrop is an individual against the world; dense clutter is a mob or chaos).
  - **Diataxeme** (narrative order). The *episodic determinant* is whatever makes a section new (key, scoring, metre, groove). The *episodic marker* is a short one-way process that flags a change: a one-second run-up, a drum roll, a break where the accompaniment drops out, a tail that ends open. *Diataxis* is the order of sections.
  - **Style flag**. The *style indicator* is the home idiom's constant traits; establish it, or nothing else can register as different. The *genre synecdoche* is a borrowed fragment of another style that summons that style's whole world (a drone under a simple tune for peasant life; Gregorian chant for the medieval). It works only against the home style and only for listeners who hear the source as foreign.
  - Which to use (Tagg MM ch. 14): neutral **space** by kinetic and spatial anaphones; a specific **place or period** by style flags; **time of day** by activity, tempo, register and loudness; **inner states** by the affect cues; **anticipation** by putting the threat music ahead of the act.

- **R11. Topics make new meanings when they collide (tropes).** A topic placed in a foreign context, or against another topic, yields a third meaning instead of an average (HAT23). Documented ones: heroic plus pastoral gives an inner, spiritual victory; fanfare plus learned counterpoint gives authoritative resolve; a march moved high and tinkling becomes a music box, so heroism turns into playful irony; a march in a comic context drops to comedy; a fanfare set on the wrong chord makes heroism hollow (Agawu's reading of Beethoven Op. 132, MCK07). Expressive genres are trajectories: pastoral, then threat, then pastoral regained; tragic to triumphant; tragic to transcendent (HAT23). This is the positive side of R4: separation by layer or moment makes a trope, mixing in one voice makes mush.

- **R12. Fix a home style, then mark "elsewhere" lightly, and never borrow a real people for a monster.** Fantasy players decode invented cultures through Western screen conventions (§7.5), and one snippet of a style can stand for a whole culture (Tagg MM ch. 13 genre synecdoche). But the stock exotic markers are 19th-century Western inventions that tokenise real cultures (LAV; LOCKE07), and Tagg notes they work only for outsiders (MM ch. 9). So distinguish peoples by **harmony system and invented timbre combinations** rather than by real ethnic instruments: Shore's practice as read by Rone gives hobbits diatonic major and minor, men modal music, elves chromatic mediants (T169, WIK-LOTR). Do not code orcs, demons or a dark lord with a real culture's sound (T171, T079 are the cautionary cases).

- **R13. Ration the big gestures.** A pathos theme kept for a few moments a season stays potent (T184, HAL20); the heroic theme withheld until the climax lands harder (T084, HAT23; *Withholding and the last reprise*); a shock works once (*Shock*). The more a gesture means, the less often it plays.

- **R14. Near neighbours overlap; separate them only when the brief must.** Some concepts share most of their music: listeners hear gentle nature as pastoral, shimmer as wonder, a tiptoe as both mischief and comedy, heroic brass as noble (host pairwise test, 2026-10: these four pairs went to the neighbour, while uncanny/madness, grief/nostalgia, holiness/peace and menace/dread were told apart). When a subject has both concepts, let them reinforce (R1). When the brief must mean one and *not* its neighbour (a wild forest, not a meadow), take the entry's **Tell apart** line and name the separating cue in the brief.

- **R15. Lead with the idiom a film and game audience knows.** Where 18th-century topic theory and modern film or game practice disagree, the listener hears the film idiom: nobility as grandeur at rest (brass, stately, rich), not a galant minuet (host verdicts on the Margrave and in the pairwise test). Use the period topic as colour, or when the setting itself is courtly and old.

**Orchestra substitutes.** The orchestra has no piano, guitar, harpsichord, mandolin, lute, xylophone, marimba, saxophone, accordion, bagpipes, whistle, recorder, cimbalom, theremin, synthesizer, tambourine, castanets, sleigh bells, anvil or metal plates (`format-full.md`). Where a source names one, use the stand-in below; the entries already do. Each substitute keeps the feature that carried the meaning (R1, vicarious functioning).

| Source names | Feature that carries the meaning | Use instead |
|---|---|---|
| piano arpeggios (romance, nocturne, Emmerdale) | rippling, legato broken chords | harp arpeggios, or legato clarinets in broken chords; pizzicato for a lighter ripple |
| harpsichord, lute, spinet | plucked, courtly, archaic | harp doubled by soft pizzicato strings |
| guitar, mandolin, balalaika, bouzouki, dulcimer, ukulele | plucked repeated notes, strumming | harp repeated notes; pizzicato violins; soft tremolo strings for a mandolin's tremolo |
| twangy electric guitar (Western, spy) | a reverberant single lead with bends | english horn or solo violin with short slides into notes, over open fifths |
| distorted guitar, power chords | rough, grainy, abrasive weight | trombones and tuba with cellos and basses on bare fifths ("5" chords), taiko |
| saxophone ("sexaphone"), muted trumpet | a breathy reed sliding between notes | low clarinet or english horn with slides; soft trumpets for the muted colour |
| accordion, musette | reedy thirds over a drone | clarinets in thirds over a held fifth (*Musette*) |
| bagpipes, hurdy-gurdy, drone instruments | a buzzing drone under a nasal tune | oboe or english horn over a held bassoon-and-cello fifth, a slight `wobble` for the buzz |
| tin whistle, recorder, panpipes, shakuhachi, whistling | a breathy high folk pipe | flute or piccolo; slides for the shakuhachi's bends |
| Hardanger fiddle, folk fiddle | a lone ornamented string over drones | solo violin over an open-fifth drone in the cellos |
| rhaita, zurna, shawm | a piercing double reed | oboe, high and loud, with slides; piccolo for the zurna |
| xylophone, marimba, wood blocks | dry, quick, wooden strokes | pizzicato with staccato piccolo; glockenspiel for a brighter tick; toms for wood |
| cimbalom | trembling hammered strings | fast repeated harp notes with tremolo strings and a slight `wobble` |
| sleigh bells, tambourine, shaker, sistrum | metallic jingle | triangle with glockenspiel; a soft snare pattern for a shaker |
| anvils, metal plates, chains | struck metal in a work rhythm | bells struck low, triangle, taiko; "hits" on bells and gong |
| male choir, bass choir, monks, chant | low men's voices | `men_choir` (a real men's chorus, "ah", E2-A4): unison or open fifths for chant |
| "oo", "oh", ethereal or wordless choir, humming | soft voices | `choir_oo` (a real mixed choir on "oo") or `choir_oh` (on "oh"); for humming, `choir_oo` kept pp |
| solo voice, children's choir, whisper | a single, young or breathy voice | not yet usable (the host heard the sound set's solo voice as an instrument): a flute or clarinet line for a lone voice, `choir_oo` high and soft for the young |
| wooden percussion, hand drums, bodhrán, tom-tom | dry skin or wood | toms; hard timpani strokes; pizzicato basses |
| theremin, ondes Martenot, electronic glides | a voice-like pitch that slides and wavers | solo violin or flute with `slide` and wide `wobble` (*The other-worldly glide*) |
| synthesizer, ring modulation, sequencer | inhuman regularity, metallic shimmer | celesta and high tremolo with `unhinge`; machine-like pizzicato ostinati |
| boy soprano, female voice, wordless women's choir | a pure, high, unworldly voice | choir in its high register; solo violin or flute for one voice |
| music box, toy piano, glass harmonica, vibraphone | tinkling, glassy, mechanical | celesta or glockenspiel; add `wobble` for a vibraphone or a warped box |
| sul ponticello, string harmonics, flutter-tongue | thin, glassy or rasping strings and winds | high soft tremolo, trills, `wobble` |
| contrabassoon, contrabass clarinet | a grotesque very low reed | bassoons and low clarinets doubled by basses |
| lyre, kithara, Celtic harp | plucked, bardic, ancient | harp |
| buccina, salpinx, natural trumpets | bare open-fifth brass | horns or trombones on open fifths |
| harmonica | a lone reedy voice that bends | english horn or clarinet with slides (*The cell*) |
| kazoo | a nasal comic buzz | staccato bassoon |
| brushed drums, walking bass | soft swung pulse | soft snare or ride pattern; pizzicato basses |

## 2. The lexicon

### A. The sacred and the profane

#### Holiness
- **Cues** - Slow [S]; soft to moderate, narrow dynamic range (§1 DynVar; §6 r9); firm rhythm, a chord each beat, legato; simple consonant harmony = dignified (§1 [M-S]); Hevner's sacred cluster rests on firm rhythm, slowness, low pitch, not mode (HEV cl.1 [M]). Block chords want an extreme: pp turns inward, ff hammers; mf loses both [Rumph 504-5] top. Reverberant space for awe (§7.6 r18 [W-M]). A plain slow sacred cue reads sad (catalogues tag 19 of 22 religious titles sad) unless major, rising, belled or firmer [Tagg 517-18] conv.
- **Melody** - White notes on a half-note beat in alla breve, stepwise into suspensions, entries in imitation [Dick. 124; Mirka T&M 358-59] top. Modal pastiche: b7 for the leading tone, stepwise cadence approach, cross-relations [Lehman 225] top. A whole-note chant line can ride over moving music [Rumph 505] top. Range about a ninth, half = 50-60 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Root-position I, IV, V, vi blocks (inf.); plagal amen IV - I. Church markers, any few: IV - I, falling fifths, bVII - I, I/i interchange, two-chord oscillation, weak goal; frames I - IV - I - bVII - I, dorian i - IV - i [Lehman 223-25] top. Sacred close: V pedal with 2-3/7-6 suspensions braided above, V - I delayed (Orgelpunkt, Stabat Mater Prinner) [Byros 386-91; Chapin 315-17] top. Minor amen in major: iv, iiø7 or bVI before I, sweet to cloying [Tagg 210-11] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `organ` (Tagg MM ch. 9), or pp `brass` / `clarinets`+`bassoons`+`horns` with a `tuba`+`basses` pedal (inf.); chorale homorhythm in alla breve says sacred without organ [Rumph 497-98] top. Brass chorale = divine intervention (T022). High `choir` benevolent, low malevolent [Webster 205] conv. Organum planing in `choir` or low brass [Lehman 81-82] top. Reverberant chant for monastic sanctity (T110). Holy wood: `horns` in fifths over `tremolo`, brass chorale [Winters 122-24] top. Sacred pastoral: two `oboe` lines in thirds, slow dotted 12/8, over a held I+V fifth [Monelle 198-99, 229-30] top. *Holy Pipe Organ*, *Cherubic Choir*.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by purity, peace, devotion. With the divine: diatonic fifths alternating with chromatic mediants [Lehman 225-26] top. Against corruption: chorale on top, rot beneath (bass a semitone off, `unhinge` basses), or the chorale besieged by rising chromatic basses, slides, forced `trombones` [Dick. 120] top; or one shared low pedal with choir and harp swapped for scrapes as the place turns [Summers 120-21] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one plagal cadence or Orgelpunkt. Strong: a chorale section. Whole: alla breve chorale, choir, organ and bells swelling to an enveloping peak.
- **Not** - Low minor unison chant (T159): doom. Chromatic = unholy: only chromatic *minor* chords and tritones between minor triads read as hell; chromatic mediants are the numinous [Lehman 82, 102, 225-26] top, stronger than the older Heaven-vs-Hell reading (T022, FW23). The sarabande: secular gravity [Mirka T&M 358-61] top. Full high-church weight for a penitent [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. Modal colour on a secular lead reads old, not holy [Tagg 370-72] conv. A living people's sacred material [Hayward 41-46] conv.

#### Devotion (faith as a person's act: prayer, vow, worship)
- **Cues** - Tenderness joined to solemnity: slow, legato, soft, rounded attacks (§1 Tenderness [S]); steady rhythm, as in ritual and healing songs (§8.3 [W-M]). GEMS puts spirituality in its transcendence factor [S]. Mattheson ranks supplication and repentance low, devotion middle [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. Lutheran "divine sadness": pathetic accents, sobbing rubato, chromatic harmony [Head 273-74] top. Worship can be festive: martial Kyries with trumpets and drums on feast days [Haringer 199-200] top.
- **Melody** - Singable, connected, narrow, mostly stepwise (singing style) [Day-O'Connell 240-42] top; voice-like lines spread their mood (§7.6 r22). A stepwise rise over a stepwise bass = willed hope (§7.6 r8 [W]). Plea: very slow, static rhythm, a winding line broken by leaps, a falling chromatic figure [Brill 32] conv. Adoration: rocking triple [Monelle 198-200] top. Fervour: the tune in octaves over throbbing quaver or triplet chords (stile appassionato) [Dick. 109] top. Rapture: a roving, Tristan-like yearning line [Lehman 232-33] conv. Prayer amid battle: the tune augmented to whole notes over the battle's pulse [Rumph 505; Mirka T&M 361-66] top, use (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The hymn opening I - V - vi lifts a song to solemnity (T022, HAT23); plagal cadences. Prayer fused with love: the Ave Maria chord (ii7 after I) as the second chord [Tagg 177-78] top; the same move for strength at the hour of death (*Ave verum*) [Tagg 173-75] top. Rapture: chromatic roving round a few reference chords by I - III, I - bVI, tritone and whole-step moves [Lehman 232-33] conv. Recognition: dorian counterpoint, a short outburst (IV, bvi, a chain of minor thirds), landing on bVII - I a fifth higher [Lehman 223-25] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - A voice-like solo (`cellos`, `solo_violin`, `oboe`) over `organ`; call and response between solo and `choir` (*Jubilant congregation*; Tagg MM 514). Religioso topic (T087 [gen.]). A plea on `oboe`+`english_horn` over held `strings` (inf.). A cleric: alla breve chorale as the noun, singing style as the adjective (inf., after Hatten). A `horns` obbligato makes praise lordly [Monelle 84] conv. Zealot: `violins`+`cellos` in octaves over repeated triplet `strings` or `horns` (inf.). A vow: a held horn call, answered.
- **With / against** - With obsession: the prayer figure stays fixed while the accompaniment darkens (*Bell ostinato*). With doubt: the line rises and stops short of the cadence. With heroism: a slow sonorous trumpet church call = devout heroism [Monelle 179] conv. After rapture, a plain diatonic restatement brings faith back to earth [Lehman 233] conv. Running notes against a held hymn join private prayer and public display [Rumph 505-6] conv. Oath: see **Oath and vow**.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a hushed pp hymn cadence closing the tune [Rumph 504] top. Strong: the tune recast as a chorale. Whole: a call-and-response prayer, or modal prayer alternating with chromatic rapture and ending diatonic (inf.).
- **Not** - Full organ and choir: keep that mass for the divine. High-church apparatus for a private plea [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. Fervour as calm church texture: ardent faith is a passion topic [Dick. 109] top.

#### The divine (a god's presence; transcendence)
- **Cues** - Wonder and transcendence (GEMS [S]; "transcendent/mystical" is a reliable listener category, COW). Massed, slowly built, enveloping sound, felt bass with high treble (§7.6 r18); octaves and consonance for potency (§1 [M]); loud, consonant, regular = majesty (§6 r10); time seeming to stop (§7.4 [S]). Two heavens: austere static awe or lush optimistic glory [Brill 34-35] conv. A sudden drop in loudness and texture raised sublimity [Margulis 636-38] [ev].
- **Melody** - Kind gods (celestial): stepwise thirds, lilting regular phrases, gentle tempo, G or F major [McClelland 287] top. A terrible god speaks on a monotone or triad in the bass [McClelland 285-87] top. Divine speech falls b2 - 1 (phrygian) [Lehman 227, 230] conv; a churchy 1 - 7 - 6 - 7 suspension over a pedal before arrival [Lehman 227] conv. Creation's first light: a tiny two-horn fanfare [Monelle 68-69] conv.
- **Harmony** - Modal/diatonic = earthly faith, chromatic = the numinous; one chromatic mediant against a diatonic fifth [Lehman 225-26] top. Epiphany: unfocused, clearing, coalescing on one big major triad [Lehman 227] top; e.g. I7 - bV7 thunderclap, bVImaj7, long V - I over a pedal, bII - I [Lehman 226-28] conv. A fff tritone cadence between *major* triads = ecstatic revelation [Lehman 231-32] conv. Mixed plagal iv - I glows (T157, LEH13). Klangfläche: I and V merged over a tonic pedal (about Imaj7add9), static [Horton 660] top, numeral (inf.). Wrathful god: vii°7, augmented sixths, bII, flat-key shifts [McClelland 282] top.
- **Colours and devices** - Sovereign: full `choir`, `organ`, `brass`; otherworldly: *Ethereal Choir*; polychord choir for a host [Lehman 230] conv. `harp` is the gods' instrument [Brill 29, 34-36] conv; celestial `flutes`+`harp` [McClelland 287] top. Halo: high divisi `strings` pp, attack-less [Tagg 170] top. Ascent: falling `bells`/`celesta` arpeggios under a slow `horns` tune [Brill 27] conv. Summoning: open fifth on `horns` with a slight `slide` [Brill 33, 36] conv. Offstage `trumpets` (T008). Pantheistic: pastoral breadth, chorale, tremolo halo, harp (T088) conv. A region's bed gaining the god's instruments as the party nears [Summers 75-77] conv.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by holiness, majesty, stature. Terrible god: low, phrygian, the sublime's terror (§6 r15 [W]). Wrath then grace: furies, then a celestial dance [McClelland 287] top. Transcendence by acceptance keeps dark and bright side by side [Byros 405-8] top. See also **The numinous**.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one held high chord, a harp figure, or I - bVI - I in a modal cadence [Lehman 226]. Strong: a phrygian cadence for divine speech. Whole: the inchoate-to-blaze epiphany, or a Klangfläche coda after the decisive cadence [Horton 660] conv.
- **Not** - Stillness only: the celestial lilts [McClelland 287] top (refines "the divine stands still", conv.); still no busy march. `trombones` for a kind god: oracles and damnation [McClelland 285] top. The halo alone (also disco) [Tagg 170] top. Unframed chromaticism: wonder or horror, not a god (inf.).

#### Purity
- **Cues** - Clear tonality, consonance, few tritones = pleasant (§1 Intervals [M]); low complexity = relaxed (§1 Rhythm); octaves and fifths are easiest to process (§8.2 [S]); high register leans serene (§1 Register [M-W]). Tagg files clean, innocent, pure with lightness and openness, high = light, not heavy (VVA 1158; Tagg MM ch. 8 [T]). Soft, thin, transparent.
- **Melody** - (inf.) A slow, stepwise or fifth-and-octave line in a high register, diatonic major or pure modal with no chromatic notes, legato, long notes, a range under an octave, simple 4/4 or 3/4 at about 60-72; it may sound alone.
- **Harmony** - (inf.) Open fifths and octaves, root-position I, IV, V, a plain I - IV - I or I - V - I; no sevenths, no borrowed chords; long single chords.
- **Colours and devices** - `flutes`, high `violins`, an unaccompanied line, open fifths, *Cherubic Choir*, `celesta`. An untouched thing: high `choir` or solo `flutes` (LOTR Nature's Reclamation [S], conv.).
- **With / against** - Reinforced by innocence, holiness, light. Against corruption: the pure line stays unchanged on top while the harmony darkens beneath; the Ring's seduction theme sets a pure boys' choir over minor fragments and bass-drum strokes (LOTR Seduction of the Ring, conv.).
- **Intensity** - Colour: a high unison doubling. Whole: a thin, transparent texture throughout.
- **Not** - Sugar (stacked thirds, a music box): childhood. Thick middle-register pads (inf.).

#### Ritual and ceremony
- **Cues** - Unchanging rhythm (§8.3 [W-M]); repetition builds tension (§1 Form [M]); strict metre (§8.2). Tagg's ritual field holds wedding and funeral alike (VVA 271); square periods = coordinated collective movement (Tagg MM ch. 8). Metre and tempo are a rite's undeletable markers [Rumph 498] top; pedals and repeated notes suggest processions [McClelland 283-85] top. A repeated ground = solemn persistence (T056). A state rite can be festive: a brisk major 6/8 march drew ceremonial, flags and royal at 10x [Tagg 399-406] [T].
- **Melody** - The march is a body rhythm, so any motif fits it [Dick. 101] top. Processional: slow, alla breve, dotted, half = 60, for priests, pilgrims, guilds [Monelle 119-20, 127] top. Entrée: slow, heavy, very detached [Haringer 202-3] top; priests: choral texture, rising dotted scales [Haringer 202-3] top. Funeral church call: a slow low trumpet triad [Monelle 179] top. A rite as a fixed pitch motif in free rhythm, undone by its retrograde [Summers 80-83] conv. Five march tempos: quick, filing, parade, solemn, funeral [Horton 646-48] top.
- **Harmony** - Full harmony, every part filled, strong cadences [Haringer 202] top. Modal moves and organum parallels for an archaic rite [Lehman 225] top. A drone or tonic pedal under repeated progressions (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - `bells` (church, hour, death, wedding; T101, Haines); `choir` held notes; a drone; French-overture rhythm for state rites (T024). `timpani` for civic rites, distant `trumpets` for the tower's closing call [Haringer 198] top. Mourning: muted `trumpets`, soft `timpani` [Monelle 127] top; bells, long pause, funeral march, pause, elegy [Dick. 110] top. Antique rite: `oboe`+`english_horn` over `harp` [Brill 32] conv. Trumpets and drums held back until a modulation make an event [Galand 463-65] conv. *Bell ostinato*, *Boléro Effect*, *Wedding processional*.
- **With / against** - With holiness: a chorale procession; chorale flowing into march joins sacred and state [Horton 658-59] conv. Dark rite = ombra ceremonial: funeral march or French overture plus stile antico, unison, chant [McClelland 285-87] top; "ominous priests" [Lehman 266 n23] conv; march + demonic = procession to doom [Dick. Table 1] top; or uneven 7/8, ominous chant (T159). Wedding: bells and processional under a love theme. The brisk march brings its whole bundle (flag, sport, picnic) [Tagg 412] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a bell, drum pattern or one slow low call. Strong: a processional section. Whole: a Boléro build, or a processional march with choir.
- **Not** - Free time for a procession; but an invocation or oracle is ombra declamation, slow with tempo changes [McClelland 282-87] top. One processional for all: who enters sets severity (Koch) [Haringer 201-3] top. A `gong` for every foreign dignitary [Brill 19] conv. Living peoples' ritual songs [Hayward 41-46] conv. A single light bell on a march upbeat for a sacred rite: a toy [Tagg 413-14] (inf.).

#### Judgement and justice
- **Cues** - Phrygian suits condemnation better than Ionian (§1 Mode [W]); low, slow, a firm chord each beat (§6 r9); unisons and octaves for potency (§1 [M]). Tagg files justice with wisdom and trust (VVA 1172). Controlled, impersonal, measured.
- **Melody** - The law intoned: a chorale cantus firmus in long notes, in octaves, wrapped in fixed counterpoint, austere [Chapin 311] top. The verdict: a monotone or a triad in the bass [McClelland 285-87] top. Slow 4/4 at about 50-60, minor or phrygian with b2, a range of about a sixth (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The aeolian shuttle i - bVI for the fateful (Tagg MM ch. 9; EvTon): a major bVI also means awe, myth, mourning, even a hero, so keep it slow, low, dark or use i - bvi [Lehman 100-102, 131, 171] top. bII in the cadence (phrygian close). Ombra verdict: a sudden f vii°7 with trombones after soft repeated notes [McClelland 288-89] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `trombones` (conv.), `timpani` for fate, low `choir` in unison, `organ`. Cantus in `trombones`+`horns`, counterpoint in `strings`, minor (inf.). Learned style = law, order, tradition [Chapin 323-26] top (T019, HAT23). Hold the trombones back until the verdict [McClelland 288] top. *Doom*: bell toll and *Dies irae* for the Last Judgement (T090). *The villain conducts* for impersonal precision.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by authority, order, holiness. Against mercy: judgement in low brass, mercy a high soft line; the last statement decides (*Themes in conflict*). After the verdict, strict chorale yields to warm homophony [Chapin 311] top. Music that reacts to every success acts as a judging narrator; react only where judgement is meant [Summers 104-5] conv.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a bII in the cadence or one toll. Strong: the cantus firmus with trombones entering at the verdict. Whole: *Doom*.
- **Not** - Rage or vengeance: Mattheson calls them low affects [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. Reactive stings in free play (the GM's hand) [Summers 105] (inf.). A bright major i - bVI, which reads as awe [Lehman 171].

#### The infernal and demonic
- **Cues** - Major sevenths and tritones signal danger (§1 Intervals [W, learned]); phrygian for condemnation (§6 r15 [W]); low two-note chords read negative (§1 Register); brass, percussion, repeated notes, syncopation for the cruel (§1 Rhythm [W-M]); irregular timing raises arousal unpleasantly (§8.2 [M]). Not a separate topic in the Handbook: treat it as ombra (slow creeping dread for the presence) + tempesta (fast frenzy for demons, furies, flames) + a grotesque dance, with parody as its own engine [McClelland 285-87, n8; Hatten 515] top (correction; the old "well-documented demonic topic", T089 FW23, keeps its markers but not its status). Sudden p/f contrasts; a storm plan of approach, raging centre, subsiding [McClelland n8] top. Overblown, hard-attack `trombones` [Dick. 119] top.
- **Melody** - Signature: a *rising* chromatic or altered scale low in `cellos`+`basses`+`bassoons`, something climbing from below [Dick. 118-19] top; answered by mocking glissandi or high figures outlining augmented and diminished chords [Dick. 119] top. Trombone figures in open fourths, fifths, octaves (medieval cast) [Dick. 119] top. Ombra lines: exclamatory fragments, augmented or diminished leaps [McClelland 282] top; a demon lord speaks on a monotone or triad [McClelland 285-87] top. An exposed melodic tritone leap outside any key [Murphy 162-63] top. Mephistopheles has no tune: he distorts the hero's [digest gen.] conv.
- **Harmony** - The tritone reads infernal only when exposed: a bare dyad left ringing, or a leap outside the key; buried in triads it is drive, in V7 gentle [Murphy 161-64] conv. Minor tritone i - #iv = evils beyond understanding; the major I - #IV is cosmic awe [Lehman 102, 171] top. Fiend chain: i - #iv pairs stepping down by whole steps, all minor, one major chord as the jolt [Lehman 156-58] conv. Intensified minor chromaticism with no key = gothic horror [Lehman 82] top. vii°7 chains falling, augmented sixths, bII; vii°7 over a V pedal [McClelland 282, 289-90, n8] top. Minor or a brutal major studded with vii°7 and #iv°7, plus I+ [Dick. 118-19] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `trombones` = hell and the supernatural since Monteverdi; one soft chord already says otherworldly [McClelland 285; Dick. n27] top. Low male `choir` for a demon lord [Barron 83] conv. Tremolo on diminished chords [Dick. n26] top. Sacred inversion: chant, chorale or fugato swallowed by the demonic texture [Dick. 120] top (see **Holiness**). Parody: the hero's theme in minor with tritones as a grotesque waltz, its head shrieked on `piccolo` (conv.). An act of evil: `piccolo`+`violins` sliding up, a `brass` cluster hit [Barron 88] conv. Possession: chromatic wedge bass under a root-position chorale, four tritone moves in a row, whole-tone `harp`/`celesta` wash round it [Lehman 72-73] conv. Devil's fiddle: `solo_violin` opening on the tritone (T090, T039). Hell's soundscape: `timpani` rolls, low `bells`, gong `hits`, low `tremolo` with `unhinge`, `reverse_cymbal`, `choir` wails with `slide` [Summers 128-29] conv, parts (inf.).
- **With / against** - Reinforced by cruelty, tyranny, fire. + march = infernal procession; + hunt = Wild Hunt; + fairy music = evil fey [Dick. Table 1-2, 122] top; Devil as Hunter, a dark 6/8 gallop, calls in fourths [Monelle 37, 67, 87] conv. Against holiness: high unresolved *Ethereal Choir* over the infernal pedal. Against charm: a seductive surface (a nocturne line over `harp`), tritones in the inner voices, full colour only at the pact (inf.). In an idyll, low ominous chords creep beneath [Barron 87] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one rising chromatic bass run, one i - #iv, or one f vii°7 with trombone. Strong: run plus glissando answer; an uneven-metre drum layer. Whole: every gesture at once (the Witches' Sabbath model) [Dick. 119] top, or ombra into tempesta damnation [McClelland 287-90] top.
- **Not** - The tritone everywhere: expose it, or it is energy [Murphy] / awe if major [Lehman] (corrections). A descending chromatic scale as signature: it rises [Dick. 118-19] top. *Doom* or *Madness*: the infernal is in command, its parody deliberate. An "eastern" scale for a demon's seduction [Barron 88; Brill 19-24] conv. Infernal colour in a fey realm unless invasion is the story [Barron 81] conv. The ghost code (ripping, grating) belongs to the undead [Summers 128-29] conv.

#### Corruption (the good thing going wrong)
- **Cues** - The change is the cue: major to minor (§1 Mode [S]), line dropped an octave (§1 Register [M]), darker timbre, tenser harmony (§1 [M-S]), chromatic notes (§1 Tonality [W]), a repeated semitone-wrong note (§4 [W]). Tagg's love-to-horror rule: soft attacks, long lines, round tone, consonance become sharp attacks, jerky repeats, harsh tone, dissonance (Tagg MM ch. 7/8 [T]). Monelle's five ways to make any topic dysphoric: minor triads, chromatic notes, the pianto, banality, exaggeration [Monelle 80, 92, 175-80] top. Metallic, machine-like sound invading an acoustic home cue [Barron 86-92] conv.
- **Melody** - The same tune, damaged: one interval widened (a closing fourth becomes a tritone at the harm) [Murphy n21] conv; tritones added for the abuser's variant [Halfyard & Hancock 185] conv; the theme returning with grace notes stripped amid tempest figures [Dick. 136] top; a call that sours on each return [Monelle 178] conv. A held note can survive while the chord under it changes (3 to b3 is the only moving note in a parallel swap) [Lehman 90-94] (inf.). A held third that bends as it decays, the bend widening each time [Summers 120] conv / (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Darken only the bass under a familiar tune: bVI7 and dominant colours [Webster 208] conv. Swap one chord for its parallel: bVI becomes bvi (Tarnhelm = a tainted bVI) [Lehman 102, 181] top; the hero cell i - bVI becomes i - bvi [Lehman 131] conv. Chromaticise step by step: i - v - bIII - bVII, then i - iii, then hexatonic moves, then a tritone crack [Lehman 114-17] conv. The lurch le-sol-fi-sol: bass b6 - 5 - #4 - 5 (bVI, over 5, vii°7/V, V), then reinterpret I as bVI of the key a major third up, into minor [Byros 383-85] top. For the innocent or fey: add sevenths, vii°7, I+ while the register stays high [Dick. 122] top. The minore variation toward sighs, learned style or stentorian unison [Sisman 107] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Corruption*, *Descent*, *Innocence corrupted*, `unhinge` on one part. The villain's cells hidden inside the innocent theme (SW 24 conv.). A march made high and tinkling for irony (T106). Evil overlay: phased high `tremolo` with `unhinge`, growling `trombones` with `slide`, `choir` wails, `wobble` [Summers 120-21] conv, parts (inf.). Keep a place's plodding `pizzicato` bass and sour the chords above (minor-six brass, low semitone groans) [Tagg 566] (inf.).
- **With / against** - Needs its victim, played clean first (§8.1; §7.6 r5). Holiness + corruption: the hymn kept recognisable over rot [Dick. 120] top, or one shared frame recoloured from choir and harp to scrapes [Summers 120-21] conv. Nobility + corruption: a *Façade* that turns. A pastoral broken by minor agitato and restored is a threat survived; unrestored, corruption [Sisman 110] top / (inf.). Reversed, every mechanism is a redemption [Lehman 58, 131] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one note flattened on repeat, one chord swapped, or the bend alone. Strong: the theme more chromatic each time; second half minor and low. Whole: a *Descent*, a variation set whose minore never returns, or the full overlay on the frame.
- **Not** - A new dark theme (another tune is another person); Summers' constant-frame practice supports this. Only a major-to-minor flip: gestures and chord colour do much of it [Dick. 120, 122] top. Staying major once the theme turns to tempesta or ombra: minor is their marker [Rumph 498] top. Bending past recognition: it must still be heard as the hunt or march it was [Monelle 246] top.

#### Decay (rot, ruin, entropy)
- **Cues** - High complexity with low dynamism = melancholy, depression (§1 Rhythm [W]); falling minor lines = sad (§1 Contour [W-M]); sagging and slowing (*Deflation*, conv.); thinning texture (inf.); drifting pitch. Tagg files the worn-out and putrid with dark, heavy, enclosed, sick and dead (VVA 1256, 1258 [T, by grouping]); low pitch and falling seconds and thirds lose energy (Tagg Death).
- **Melody** - (inf.) Falling seconds and thirds, phrases that sag at the end and lose notes on repeat, low-middle register, minor or aeolian, slow (about 50-60) in 4/4 with a slight ritard, a range shrinking toward a fourth, slides downward on held notes.
- **Harmony** - (inf.) A tonic that slips flat: i - bII held too long, chords losing their thirds into bare fifths, a slowly falling chromatic bass, cadences left unfinished on iv or v.
- **Colours and devices** - `unhinge` with slow drift; a warped music box (`celesta` with `wobble`, slowing; T106); dripping `celesta` (*Dungeon ambience*); a once-grand theme played thin (*Requiem for a Landmark*); the theme in fragments (*The hero's breakdown*).
- **With / against** - With age: an ancient tune worn down. With undeath: see **Undeath**. Against majesty: state the anthem whole, then let it decay over repeats. With **Decline and fading**: the slow, dignified waning rather than rot.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one sagging slide. Whole: an instrument and some pitch lost on each repeat.
- **Not** - Active menace: decay is passive. Sudden breaks (that is corruption or the reveal) (inf.).

#### Death
- **Cues** - Sadness profile [S]: slow, soft, low, narrow, legato, dark, falling seconds (§6 r4); a chord each beat (§6 r9); swan-song simplicity (§6 r28 [W-M]); a rest after an unresolved chord leaves it hanging (§6 r12). Tagg's Euro-funereal norm: soft, slow, no short accented notes, steps (widest a major third), brief repeated phrases inside a minor sixth, falling semitones b3 - 2, b6 - 5 (4 - 3 in major), slow harmonic rhythm, low accompaniment (Tagg Death; Tagg 315-17) conv; a European code, not universal. Mode decides little: 5 of 14 silent-film sad pieces were major (Handel's *Largo*) [Tagg 315-16] conv, which corrects the old minor emphasis. Tempo: Chopin's march about 52, a walking 92 is too quick [Tagg 168] top. Aftermath ("just after a killing") came only from irregular phrase lengths and harmonic rhythm [Tagg 604] [T]. A murder is scored with darkness and danger, not elegy [Tagg 539-79] [T].
- **Melody** - Funeral march: heavy slow duple, dotted figures over a repeating bass (inevitability) [Dick. 104] top; four steps a bar at 70-75 [Monelle 129-30] conv; one repeated tonic pitch per beat in `pizzicato` basses at about 58 [Tagg 563-66] conv. Death-wish incipit in minor: pickup 5 leaping a minor sixth to b3, step to 2, back to 5, then a long wavy fall (R&J drew death at 7.9x) [Tagg 434, 442-43] [T]; a later tritone or dim7 leap is the moment of dying [Tagg 442-43] conv. A companion's closing fourth widening to a tritone [Murphy n21] conv.
- **Harmony** - Rocking i - VI (Chopin) or tritone-free i - v rocking: the form without its animating spirit [Murphy 168-69] conv. Descending chromatic tetrachord 1 - 7 - b7 - 6 - b6 - 5 [Head 268-69] top. Le-sol-fi-sol close (bVI or iv6, over 5, vii°7/V, V): death, burial, sacrifice, the ghost's return in ~550 cases [Byros 383-97] top. Hexatonic pole I - bvi or i - III: catastrophe, death, "one foot in the grave"; rarest, keep it for one moment, isolated in long plain chords [Lehman 75-76, 92, 101] top. V7b9 on the beat of dying [Tagg 465-66] conv. Minor optional: Handel's Dead March is plain major; Purcell opens I then i [Monelle 127-28] top/conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Funeral march with dotted `trumpets`, repeated-note `tuba`, soft `timpani` [Dick. 104] top; muffled `trumpets` and drums [Haringer 199-200; Monelle 127] top; Gossec's drum rolls, plaintive brass, long silence, then a gong-and-tutti crash [Monelle 128-29] top. Low tolling `bells` (T101). Dies irae in low `trombones`+`tuba` [Brill 30] top. A soldier's last post: a lone trumpet (T184). Horn fifths for farewell [Sisman 102] top. *Leitmotif upon Death*, *Lament*.
- **With / against** - With grief, personal; with fate, impersonal; with holiness, consolation (plagal amen; *Ave verum*'s I - ii7 - V - I) [Tagg 173-75] top. Needs life beside it: ombra means death when it breaks into fanfare and dance [Byros 398-405] top. Funeral march, elegy, then appassionato = heroic elegy [Dick. 110] top. Then the spirit at rest: celestial `flutes` in thirds over `harp` (inf.); with the pastoral, death as sweet homecoming [Monelle 232-34] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one bell stroke. Grow by doubling the line and changing register, not by speeding up [Dick. 104] top. Plant the theme early, breaking off before the death chords; complete it only at the death [Murphy 169] conv. At the loss strip to one voice [Stephen 68] conv; then silence and a clear major close (life goes on) [Murphy 169] conv. Whole: a funeral march.
- **Not** - Weeping strings over a hymn; a generic sad piece for a known character (use their tune). Tritone-heavy harmony [Murphy 168-69] conv. Full trumpets and drums at a deathbed [Haringer 199-200] top. Fast minor compound metre (sad 0) [Tagg 288-89] [T]. The funeral march for an ordinary death: it is a 1790s genre with a heroic aura [Monelle 130] top.

#### Undeath
- **Cues** - Life's cues gone wrong (inf.): unsteady pitch, mechanical repetition, low, minor or phrygian; hissy, dynamic, unpredictable sounds as threat triggers (§8.5 [W]). Ombra is the documented sound of ghosts, oracles, the underworld: slow or moderate tempo that changes several times in a scene, strong contrasts, sudden outbursts and silences, dense octave-doubled texture, repeated notes as heartbeat or approaching footsteps [McClelland 282-85] top (T027, FW23). It freezes rather than rouses (McC14, after Huron). Tagg files ghosts among beings, bones as remains (VVA 230Z, 269).
- **Melody** - Ghosts, statues, oracles: a bass monotone or triad in ponderous dotted rhythm; fearful dotting is uneven, majestic dotting even [McClelland 282-84] top. The dead rising: a chromatic line climbing over a tonic pedal [McClelland 288; Dick. 118-19] top, a detached rising four-note motif in high `choir` [Halfyard 225] conv, or a rising sequence [Summers 77] conv. Leaping, broken lines; the Dies irae twisted into a waltz or gallop (danse macabre, T090).
- **Harmony** - Flat, remote minor keys (C, D, F minor), surprise shifts, vii°7, augmented sixths, bII [McClelland 282] top; each stage a flatter key, then climbing back [McClelland 288] top. Slide I - #i (or i - VII): the boundary state, the hero dead all along; hold the shared third high and exposed [Lehman 104-5] top. Crypt: i - bvi6 [Lehman 101] conv. Le-sol-fi-sol for a ghost's return [Byros 383-97] top.
- **Colours and devices** - Low dark scoring: `trombones`, low `strings` `tremolo`, an `oboe` pedal above [McClelland 282, 288] top; bare pp octaves [Rumph 504] top. Skeleton army: Dies irae in low `trombones`+`tuba`, then a ferocious scherzo of brass tricks (`slide`s, trills) ending on a high `trumpets` cry [Brill 30-31] top/conv. Bones: very short high `pizzicato` + `glockenspiel` an octave up + dry `toms`/`snare` [Brill 26-30] conv. Danse macabre: `harp` striking twelve, `solo_violin` on the tritone (T090). Ghostly presence: high `tremolo` with `unhinge`, sharp `violins` `slide`s, high `bells` hits [Summers 128-29] conv. Spectral host: muffled night march for winds over `pizzicato` (T005); the dead on parade, a quick march whose call sours each time [Monelle 178-79] conv; the Wild Hunt's dark 6/8 gallop [Monelle 67, 87] top. Lich's court: *Doom*, `organ`, minor fugato (T019), French-overture double dots (inf.). *Backwards* for necromancy.
- **With / against** - Undeath = death plus a dance that should not be there; Totentanz is a trope of life's dance under death's signs, ironic by design [Byros 405] top. Lich-king: the *Façade* waltz in minor with the Dies irae woven in (inf.). Ghost of grief: sensibility's sighs (T036). A beloved ghost is celestial, `flutes`+`harp` in G or F [McClelland 287] top. Laying spirits to rest: a tritone-free hexatonic swell thinning to high `strings`+`celesta` [Murphy 160-65] conv. Keep the danger bed while any undead remain [Summers 116] conv. Plant the slide motto early, unexplained [Lehman 104-5] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one retrograde phrase, one vii°7 tremolo, one slide oscillation. Strong: the ombra scene. Whole: the danse macabre or the haunting.
- **Not** - Generic horror stabs; bones in held notes. Hell's code (kettledrums, grinding metal) for a ghost [Summers 128-29] conv. Quiet slides as menace: they are hushed uncertainty [Lehman 221] conv. Tremolo always dread: it can be knocking knees, and a comic fake ghost booms halfway between ombra and tempesta [McClelland 284; Hunter 66] top.

#### Solemnity and gravity
- **Cues** - Its own axis, solemn against trivial, not sadness (§7.2 Wedin [M]). Hevner's dignified cluster won on firm rhythm, then slow tempo, then low pitch, with simple harmony; mode hardly mattered (HEV cl.1 [M]). Unhurried, regular, consonant, legato, small dynamic range (§6 r9). Tagg files "serious" with controlled emotion (VVA 1063); slowness means deliberation and age, not sadness (Tagg Death). Heavy execution: full, held, accented; grave, maestoso, sostenuto [Guymer 579-81] conv. The grave style had become simply ceremonial and grand by the late 18th c. [Chapin 306-9] top. Old styles run on uninterrupted [Sutcliffe 129-31] top. Listeners hear the concert-orchestra idiom as serious and refined [Tagg 215-16] [T].
- **Melody** - The beat's note value sets weight: half-note beats solemn, quarter beats the human stride, shorter beats lighter; the same phrase is grave in alla breve and nearly playful in 2/4 [Mirka T&M 358-61] top. Dotted and sustained notes = serious; long notes mixed with quick ones = majestic [Guymer 584] conv; heavy metre can still run quick [Guymer 581-83] conv. Sarabande: slow 3/2 or 3/4, starting on the downbeat [Mirka Intro 23] top; weight on beat 2 is one option (Allanbrook; T058), made by accent, high point, a rest on 1, a dissonance or an ornament on 2 [Irving 540-47] conv. French-overture dotted tread [Sisman 102] top. A slow sonorous trumpet line on the low triad (church call) [Monelle 179, 281] top.
- **Harmony** - Suspension chains in descending sequences; an eighth-note passacaglia bass in minor [Sutcliffe 129-31] top; a bII6 climax [Sutcliffe 131] conv. Bound style: tied notes and suspensions (T021). Instant gravity in major: minor iv6 with b6 in the bass (*Nimrod*) [Tagg 194-95] top. The Ave Maria progression I - ii7 - V - I turns away from the frivolous [Tagg 179] top. i - bIII can mean resolve and seriousness, but only in context [Lehman 100] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Low `strings`, `horns`, `organ`, low `choir`; full chords in low `strings`+`bassoons` (inf.). A slow homophonic low brass ensemble = aristocratic gravity [Monelle 125, 135] top; low `horns` with `trombones` for Bruckner's solemnity [Monelle 99] conv. Alla breve white-note counterpoint (T020). Slow processional march [Monelle 127] top. A fugal setting raises grandeur without changing the feeling [Chapin 309-10] top.
- **With / against** - The shared root of holiness, judgement, death, wisdom: add it for weight. For church-solemn use duple alla breve; the sarabande reads grave but worldly (court, mourning, a knight's vow) [Mirka T&M 358-61] top (correction). With grief: dignified mourning. With pride: pomp. Against the comic: a deadpan rite (R11).
- **Intensity** - Colour: one sarabande cadence or one iv6. Strong: a suspension chain. Whole: a slow chordal sarabande or alla breve chorale.
- **Not** - Gloom: solemnity can be major and warm. Topic switches inside a grave passage [Sutcliffe 129-31] top. Beat-2 weight as a defining rule [Mirka Intro 23 n39] top.

#### Oath and vow
- **Cues** - Music cannot state a promise (R7); it gives the act weight and constancy. Firm slow rhythm (HEV cl.1 [M]); clear V - I closure for a bond sealed (§6 r12, r34); a melody built to return unchanged, since predictability is liked (§4 [M]). A private, tender vow belongs to the singing style or the siciliana; a public, heroic resolve to the fanfare [Mirka Intro 40] top, split (inf.).
- **Melody** - Vow motif: an attention-call shape (rising triad with a repeated note) stretched to a tenth, as Wagner's Sword turns drill into myth [Monelle 171] conv, oath use (inf.). Heroic decision: a triadic, dotted brass fanfare [Mirka Intro 40] top. Private vow: stepwise, narrow, singable, in slow 3/4 or 4/4 at about 60, major or dorian, phrases closing firmly on 1 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Plain perfect cadences. Before the gods: an Orgelpunkt close, V held with 2-3 suspensions above before V - I [Byros 386-91] top, use (inf.). A chorale (T022).
- **Colours and devices** - The sarabande for a vow of knighthood (T058 RPG use), read as secular gravity [Mirka T&M 358-61] top. A king's oath as a solemn song (LOTR Elessar's Oath, conv.). A held `horns` call answered by the whole section. Knightly vow: horn-call antecedent, consequent settling into a soft `brass` chorale on an Orgelpunkt (inf.). Unison `brass` for the motif, then full orchestra (inf.).
- **With / against** - The oath is the first statement of a **Loyalty and honour** theme: write it so later returns, kept or broken, are recognised. With betrayal, the oath's cadence is the one that will turn (*The missing answer*). With devotion, a vow to a god.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one solemn cadence under the words. Whole: a slow processional to the oath and one held chord.
- **Not** - A fanfare for a private or tender vow (a public heroic resolve may have one) [Mirka Intro 40] top (correction to "a vow is never announced").

#### Evil and villainy
- **Cues** - Not named in the research; the threat composite is inference (§6 r16). Tagg's villain codes: chromatic low brass in uneven durations (Tagg Sth99); a "doomsday" drone (MM ch. 9) [T, conv.]. Streetcar's villain bundle: plodding low pulse at about 58, a long half-diminished chord on soft mid-low brass, a low semitone growl (gangsters 6.4x) [Tagg 539-79] [T]. A villain needs a theme: a menacing bed without a lead drew cruelty but zero villains [Tagg 646-58] [T]. Low, heavy instruments for evil, light balanced textures for good [Brill 25] conv. Blunt stock signs are a strength at a game table [Summers 164] conv. The villain's march is andante [Summers 160-61] conv.
- **Melody** - A simple tune returning often to the tonic while chords twist beneath (tethered chromaticism) [Lehman 207] top. Villain faction: a rising melodic-minor scale against the heroes' leaping thirds and fourths [Summers 160-74] conv. A minor bully: a staggering, bent minor line low in `bassoons`+`basses` with `slide` [Summers 74] conv. Renegade wizard: a falling two-note cell low and thick, scraping down [Halfyard 225] conv. Thrice-repeated note into a dotted figure (Imperial March, conv.). The high style is open to villains (French overture, sarabande), but give it one flaw: too loud, too long, an unsettled bass [Mirka Intro 3-7] top, flaw (inf.).
- **Harmony** - i - bvi (Tarnhelm): film's most dependable villain and dark-magic code [Lehman 8, 101] top; the aeolian i - bVI (Tagg MM ch. 9) needs a minor bvi or slow, low, dark scoring, since a major bVI reads as myth or a hero [Lehman 100-102, 131, 171] top. Chromatic but centred, keeping the tonic [Lehman 47] top; the sorcerer's i - biii6/4 - i - #iv [Lehman 212] conv. Minor tritone pairs i - #iv for single-minded contempt, the minor mirror of the hero's major progression [Murphy 157-64] conv. Harsh major with diminished chords also works [Dick. 118] top. Curse: a free-standing half-diminished chord [Tagg 192-93] top. Diatonic humans, chromatic or octatonic supernatural evil (T104, Taruskin).
- **Colours and devices** - Naked triads so the change is heard [Lehman 96, 131-33] conv. Withhold `trombones` until the boss appears, then three f repeated notes [McClelland 288] top. Malevolent magic: low `choir`, hard `pizzicato`+`toms`, tolling `bells` [Webster 201, 205] conv. Industrial percussion: `taiko`, low `bells`, low `toms` ostinato [Summers 160-61] conv. Dark lord behind the throne: minor-third oscillation, wordless low male choir (SW 17). Pedal crotchets in `basses`/`cellos`/`bassoons`/`trombones` with a chromatic run on beat 4 into each downbeat, `timpani` on 1 [Tagg 590-95] conv. Brute: `taiko`+bd, syncopated bass, brass jabs on bare fifths [Stephen 67] conv. Final boss: bombastic tutti with Latin-style `choir` fragments, heard once [Summers 167] conv. Ominous chant (T159).
- **With / against** - Give the villain a concept to carry (tyranny, cruelty, cunning, madness, the infernal). The theme arrives before the villain is seen, displaces location music, marks their territory when absent, follows them across exploring, narration and fight [Summers 74-75, 160-70] conv. Against the hero: *Themes in conflict*; the hero recolours the villain's area as mixolydian on the same root [Lehman 58] conv. A cheerful tune played against his violence = insolence [Stephen 66-67] conv. Let galant or pastoral sit beside the dark (McC14).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one i - bvi, or three motif notes in the basses. Strong: the villain's andante march. Whole: the march with choir; one continuous dissonant set piece per arc, framed by silence [Webster 203-5] conv.
- **Not** - A real culture's instruments, an exotic East, or accent as moral code (R12; [Brill 19-22; Halfyard 225] conv). Continuous dissonance for recurring villain music (allowed once per arc, correction). Major i - bVI; keyless wandering (wonder) [Lehman 47, 171] top. Tritones alone as evil [Murphy 163-64] conv. Romance textures (villains scored zero) [Tagg 165-66] [T]. Swung flat-fifth "cool" for a serious villain (parody) [Tagg 583-88] conv. Ambiguity when players must decide fast; the aural assault as a standing bed [Summers 77, 164] conv/(inf.).

#### The numinous (spirit in the land)
*New. The land itself charged with spirit, no split between sacred and secular: the haunted grove, the old stones, the sacred mountain. Not Holiness (institutional), not The divine (a god present).*
- **Cues** - Low, reverberant ambient underscore blended with natural sound, swelling to the front now and then; awe at the edge of terror with almost no "light" in it [Hayward 49, 54] conv. Sacred power striking: a high call, silence, then a growling low drone with guttural barks [Hayward 52] conv.
- **Melody** - Hardly a theme; ambience carries it. Where a line is needed, a slow melody over the act of revelation [Hayward 49, 53] conv; (inf.) long notes, a narrow modal range of a fourth or fifth, no regular metre, very slow, rising only at the swell.
- **Harmony** - Static: a long low pedal, held sonorities, no cadences [Hayward 49] (inf.). A Klangfläche-like stasis over a tonic pedal (inf., from **The divine**).
- **Colours and devices** - Soft `organ` pedal, divisi `strings` ppp with slow swells, low humming `choir` (inf.); breathy `flutes` for wind, `strings` `slide` for natural sound (inf.); the barks as short low `brass` sforzandi over `basses`+`tuba` (inf.). A holy wood: `horns` in fifths over `tremolo`, a `brass` chorale [Winters 122-23] top.
- **With / against** - With doom: deep rumbles and churning water figures [Hayward 53] conv. With the uncanny: carry the tone across scene changes [Hayward 49-50] conv. With holiness: keep them apart (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a reverberant low drone under the scene's natural sound. Strong: one swell to the front. Whole: an ambient cue with a single terror swell (soft `timpani` roll, `basses`/`tuba` rumble) [Hayward 49, 53] conv.
- **Not** - A borrowed real tradition (didjeridu, clan chant) as "spirit of the land": spiritual colonisation [Hayward 41-46] conv. A hymn or chorale (institutional) (inf.).

### B. Power and rank

#### Stature / scale (how much the subject weighs)
- **Cues** - Power cues: loud, harmonically rich, octaves (§1 Power [M-S]). Climax uses chill triggers: sudden forte, new voices, widened range, the theme's return, an unprepared chord or key (§4 [M]); enveloping sound (§7.4); peak and end are remembered (§7.6 r19). Tagg's largeness field answers to wide range, expansive gestures, large reverb; cramped figures mean small (VVA 145; MM ch. 8-9) [T]; an octave or more sounds expansive, a third cramped. Alison: the most sublime sound is loud, low, long and growing [McClelland 290-94] top. Low = big because low notes take energy and blur; the same chord two octaves up lightens [Tagg 568] (inf.). Solo to tutti reads as a cut to panorama, tutti to solo as a close-up [Tagg 387-88] [T]. Epic = the most private and a vast backdrop at once; the backdrop may be implied [Summers 63-69] conv.
- **Melody** - The grand public allegro: bold marked rhythms, powerful bass melodies and unisons, sudden remote key shifts, f/p contrasts, a crescendo on a rising line [Sisman 92-96] top. The tune in octaves; an earlier theme augmented ff in major (grandioso apotheosis, T085). Wide range, an octave and more, rising contour, broad note values (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Match harmonic distance to scale: small smooth moves for intimacy, the largest (tritones, deep bass plunges) for the vast reveal [Lehman 137-46] conv. A wall of all-major chords = the orchestra as organ, sublime [Lehman 252 n51] conv. Major mixed with dissonance (epic register, T161). A last section lifted a whole tone [Winters 120] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Stature*: tune in octaves on `horns`+`violins`, `trombones`+`tuba` below, `choir` held, `timpani` roll, cymbal or gong. Density as scale: rolls, crashes, grunting low brass, then `bells` [Brill 26-27] conv. A five-octave span from low brass to a high `choir` shout [Stephen 70] conv. Chords spread across octaves for mass [Lehman 144] conv. Rising-register crescendo, instruments joining as the line climbs [Sisman 106-8] top. `horns` a 4 on a lick = Straussian grandeur; the same lick on a lone ringing `harp` or `pizzicato` = a private figure apart [Tagg 376-78] (inf.); full brass organ chords under the lead's last up-and-out figure enlarge him to world size [Tagg 377] conv. A two-octave `harp` sweep tells listeners to be impressed [Tagg 386-87] conv. Two strands at once: a soft heartbeat or solo, and the faction's march [Summers 67-69] conv.
- **With / against** - The ceiling for every other concept. With humility or calm, a small surface and one climax that falls back. With pomposity, the full apparatus at the wrong scale (*Mock melodrama*); a pp staccato full march = small actor, tall gestures [Hunter 73-75] top. Grandeur on a mundane task as a deliberate trope [Summers 137-38] conv. Small is a concept too (Tagg VVA 146).
- **Intensity** - The scale is the intensity. Chamber: four parts, a modest swell, or one soft unison arpeggio after a big close [Sutcliffe 137-38] top. Regional: whole sections, one tutti. Great: one climax. Cosmic: sustained and enveloping, up a step. Size cues to the stakes; rarity monumentalises [Summers 23, 167] conv. Keep long chromatic awe chains for true set pieces [Lehman 66, 235] top.
- **Not** - Dignity mistaken for smallness; a fanfare from nowhere: the climax grows from what came before. A triumphant tutti while nothing happens [Summers 153] conv. Grand sweeps on a small subject ("trite", "silly") [Tagg 387] [T].

#### Power
- **Cues** - Loud, bright, harmonically rich (§1 [S]); large intervals, unisons, octaves (§1 [M]); low register = vigour. GEMS power: energetic, triumphant, fiery, strong [S]. Hevner's vigorous cluster won on low pitch and firm rhythm, barely on speed (HEV cl.8 [M]); game threat tracks loudness, large intervals, wide range (HM [W]). An unrelenting pulse under asymmetric phrases feels like control of time; drop it for the bearer's ordinary side [Stephen 75-76] conv. Power cues make music seem faster ("associative testosterone") [Tagg 483-86] [T]. Timpani mean grand or sinister; trumpet and drums meant princely rank [Sisman 108; Monelle 117-35] top. The idiom is period-bound: orchestra and brass band became the voice of old power, backbeat of new [Tagg 413] (inf.).
- **Melody** - Unison topic: a sweeping unison triad or line = authoritative control, to open, interrupt or close [Sisman 108] top. Hammer strokes: ff blocks or octaves on the beat [Byros 398; Rumph 504] top. Hero-mythical rise: two stacked fifths 1 - 5 - 9 in low unison with no third, marcato, about 52 [Tagg 624-25] conv. A rise-and-fall minor-third cell from which an action theme also grows [Halfyard 226-29] conv. Range over an octave, falling lines allowed, 4/4 moderate (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Tethered chromaticism: chords forced against the tonic's pull, sorcerous [Lehman 212] top. Escalation: i - bVI - biii - VII - bii - VI - i [Lehman 70] conv. Rough root-position blocks in one mode for effort [Lehman 143] conv. A minor ii as a point of arrival; keyless major-ninth chords [Stephen 71-74] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Brass, `timpani`, octave doublings; *Power*, *Fanfare*, *Boss Remix*. Brass beyond the horn = war, parades, pomp, danger (Tagg Kojak). Power chord: `trombones`+`tuba`+`cellos`+`basses` on a bare fifth, accented each quaver, one hit left to ring, `taiko` beneath [Tagg 652-53] conv, substitute (inf.); marcato bare fifths with a long crescendo on one chord [Stephen 66-73] conv. Close-layered `choir` for collective dominance [Stephen 74] conv. Coup d'archet: a tutti unison stroke [Sisman 93] top. Bravura: tutti octaves in `horns`, `trombones`, low strings, `violins` and `flutes` running above [Horton 649-53] conv. Ancient empire: strong brass in 7/8 on an invented scale [Halfyard & Hancock 183] conv. Land hits on the beat for the feel of synergy [Summers 191-92] conv.
- **With / against** - Power is neutral: one theme can serve an empire, its tyrant (tritone-bent) and its guardians [Halfyard & Hancock 184-87] conv. With majesty: consonant, broad, regular. With tyranny: minor, mechanical. With violence: unison + tempesta [Sisman 108] top. Old regime: brass band, square march; upstart power: `kit` backbeat under brass (inf.). Against gentleness: tune on gentle strings, power as undercurrent or one climax (R5). Finale: the agency rhythm as a `taiko`/`timpani` ostinato under the power theme [Halfyard 228-29] conv. Power as reach: the theme heard far off, filling in as the party nears [Summers 75-77] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: the melody in octaves. Strong: brass and timpani carry. Whole: *Power*. Save the first full tutti unison for one moment: three tenuto crotchets on a wide rising line, a held note, off-beat stabs [Tagg 628] [T].
- **Not** - Speed: power is weight. A brass-band march as threat (zero enmity) [Tagg 407, 430] [T].

#### Royalty and majesty
- **Cues** - Simple consonant harmony, regular smooth rhythm, loud rich sound (§1 Power [M-S]); dignified tempo; narrow range and rising lines read dignified (§1 [W]). Hevner: majestic sits with vigorous and martial (HEV cl.8 [M]). Tagg's prestige field (glory, pomp, patriotism) over-scored for the brass-band march; the brisk 6/8 march drew king 9x, royal 10x, pomp, "decorated" (VVA 108; Tagg 399-416) [T]. Royal brass low-middle and homophonic, not shrill (the court trumpet corps) [Monelle 125, 134-35] top. Trumpets signal regality (§7.5) on grand festive occasions [Haringer 197-98] top.
- **Melody** - Fanfare: a dotted brass flourish on the major triad (T008); the herald's two-trumpet call; the intrada for a great person's entry [Monelle 135, 163-64] top. French overture: slow, sharply over-dotted with rushing upbeats, half cadence, then fugal allegro (T024); double dots alone evoke it [Rumph 497] top. Sarabande for the ruler: dignity and decorum, slow 3/2 or 3/4 from the downbeat [Mirka Intro 7-37] top. Polonaise: moderate 3/4 walking pomp, cadencing on a weak beat (T065); it shares the sarabande's long-middle-note cell, only faster [Rumph 499-500] top. Triumphal entry: slow alla breve dotted march taken up by the chorus [Monelle 160-61] top. A rising realm: trumpet triplets climbing (LOTR [S]); a fading one sinks at every close (LOTR [A]). An anthem grows from fragments to a whole tune as the realm rises [Summers 95-96] conv.
- **Harmony** - Overture: rich chords, strong cadences, loud homophony against soft polyphony [Haringer 201-2] top. Square phrases on functional harmony, accented I6 or Iadd9 colours on downbeats [Tagg 409-12] conv. Walton slow march: mixolydian, major sevenths, avoiding V (SW 15); *nobilmente* with stepwise walking bass and diatonic sevenths (SW 9). Major-ninth chords planed over roots of no shared key (I, II, IV, bVII add9) for a realm beyond the ordinary [Stephen 71-74] conv. Trumpet calls harmonised in parts are court music [Monelle 168-69] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `trumpets`, `timpani`; an improvised flourish, one trumpet then the rest joining over a roll [Monelle 136] top. Close-layered `choir` [Stephen 74] conv. Chorale for an ancient seat of power [Dick. 124] top; apotheosis: fanfares through the orchestra, then the theme broadened into chorale (inf.). Fanfares also mark class [Dick. 118] top. Court stack: French overture arrival, sarabande ruler, minuet courtiers (inf.). Romantic ff octaves with double dots = an elder court [Horton 659] conv. Cities emit the anthem, louder near [Summers 96] conv. *National Anthem*, pealing bells.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by nobility, authority. The court ball: the Viennese waltz [Dick. 103] top. With tyranny: anthem over a *Power* ostinato, or turned minor.
