With decline: majesty always ending in a fall. With grief: *Requiem for a Landmark*. Hidden royalty: a simple 6/8 pastoral with `oboe` and light drone (shepherd-king) [Monelle 187-93] top. With the hunt: the lord of the forest [Monelle 64] top. One march, three meanings by placement: social sign, story's goal, or done to the protagonist [Horton 643-44] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a trumpet call or the anthem's first notes [Summers 96]. Strong: a French-overture introduction. Whole: the anthem brass alone, then tutti with choir.
- **Not** - Syncopation in the anthem; minor too, unless the realm's idiom treats minor as festive (*Rákóczi March*) [Tagg 320] conv (correction); the real needs are singability, moderate or quick tempo, festive rhythm. A virtuosic trumpet [Haringer 197-98] top. A `gong` for a dignitary [Brill 19] conv. Perky royal marches over disasters [Tagg 416-17] (inf.).

#### Nobility (of birth or of bearing)
- **Cues** - **To a film and game audience nobility is grandeur at rest** - a stately processional or anthem (moderate-slow, broad), horns and a brass chorale over rich strings, full diatonic harmony with firm V - I cadences, dotted ceremonial rhythm (French overture): weight without strain. The host heard a heroic brass sketch as nobler than a galant minuet (host pairwise test, 2026-10), and missed grandeur in the Margrave's first pieces. The minuet and galant manners below are period colour for refinement and bearing, not the core. Dignity profile: unhurried, regular, consonant, legato, small dynamic range (§6 r9); courtly writing primes status (§8.4 [M-S]). Allanbrook: dances that regiment the body (minuet, march, sarabande) carry noble passions (R9; HAT23 top.). Tagg files honour, nobility and pride together, refinement and chivalry with prestige (VVA 1165, 1083-84). Concert-hall sound, the soloist placed inside the orchestra's acoustic, drew upper class, palace, ballroom, manor [Tagg 158-66, 215-16] [T]. The richer the harmony, rhythm and melody, the more courtly [Haringer 208] top; rich harmony for the high-born, bare octaves for the low [Rumph 503-5] top. The minuet shows natural grace to spectators; galanterie wins distinction by pleasing [McKee 168; Zbikowski 147-48] top.
- **Melody** - Minuet: moderate 3/4, near-equal beats, two-bar units with a light accent on each, motto of three repeated crotchets or a crotchet and four quavers, grazioso [Lowe 603] top; most strains are sentences 2+2+4 [McKee 172-73] top (T057). Court waltz: full, balanced periodic phrases; the phrasing, not the 3/4, sets its class [Dick. 103] top. Prepared melodic ninths and suspensions approached from below and resolved by the book [Tagg 205] top. Hunting call: triads and repeated notes in 6/8 closing in two-horn horn motion; the hunt meant the landed class [Monelle 64-65, 89-98] top. A princess: a long arching line opening with a rising major sixth (SW 4).
- **Harmony** - Rich diatonic harmony, balanced cadences; the high waltz uses minor keys and modulation as sophistication [Dick. 103] top; modal-mixture supertonic chords (SW 4). Upright dotted rhythms over I - IV - V (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - `horns` (conv.); horn timbre alone can carry the noble [Monelle 76, 84, 99] top; horn calls are upmarket [Sutcliffe 122-23] top. `cellos`, a warm low voice. *The court dance* on `harp` with light strings (Tagg reads the court keyboard sound as upper-class olden times, MM ch. 9). High style: counterpoint, alla breve, sarabande, French overture (T025). Bourrée and gavotte also carry old courtly elegance [Dick. Table 1] top. A short trumpet fanfare to introduce a noble [Dick. 118] top. A canon lifts a minuet; drones pull it down [Sisman 111] top. Horn calls on `violins` or `clarinets` = the idea of nobility, not the thing [Agawu 490] conv. *Façade* for a noble hiding something.
- **With / against** - With pride: more ornament, upright dotting. With restraint: surges capped at once read as breeding [Tagg 224] [T]. With corruption: *Façade*, or a minuet as decadence. Nobility earned: give a commoner's song a fugato (Lied + learned) [Dick. 135] top, a documented route beside the inferred Ländler-to-minuet (T016). Common nobility: open fourths and fifths, no third, slow marcato brass [Tagg 624-25] conv. The mask slipping: a prim minuet falling into contredanse; a noble truly in command holds it [Hunter 87] top. A commoner's Ländler slipped into the minuet is irony, even threat [Hatten 521] top. An old-fashioned dance = a passing society [Monelle 6] top.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a dotted rhythm or a horn doubling. Strong: the theme as a minuet. Whole: a court dance or ternary ball waltz with trio [Dick. 103] top.
- **Not** - Battle drums; but ceremonial brass in an entrée or march suits nobility, since march-pasts were court ritual [Haringer 201-3; Zbikowski 147-48] top (correction). A Ländler for a court [Dick. 102-3] top. Close, dry, intimate solo sound (private or sensual) [Tagg 264] top.
- **Tell apart** - From *Heroism*: nobility stands, heroism strives. Nobility: stately tempo, cadences that arrive, no driving ostinato, no sequence of ever-higher peaks; heroism: forward drive, rising leaps to new peaks, the withheld V - I. A noble hero takes both, layered.

#### Authority and order
- **Cues** - Regular rhythm with key notes on strong beats = stable (§1 Rhythm [M]); clear V - I (§6 r12, r34); repeated notes [W-M]; low complexity. Square periods in fours = coordinated collective movement (Tagg MM ch. 8); order is a positive value (VVA 118). The march = order, procession, collective purpose (T006, HAT23). Four-bar periods over predictable circle-of-fifths harmony drew "as it always has been", normal, everyday [Tagg 514] [T]; regular march phrasing = confident inevitability [Stephen 75] conv. The Sousa march topped order and discipline, on the "old and rigid" side [Tagg 420-21] [T]. Drill speeds (steps a minute): slow 60-75, quick 108-120, double 130-140 [Monelle 119-23] top. Military themes typically open a scene [Galand 456] conv.
- **Melody** - Duple martial or hymn metre with a diatonic tune = reason, magic tamed [Webster 203, 209] top. Equal steps, unbroken metre (Koch) [Haringer 200-201] top. A unison fanfare preface before the march sets the pace [Monelle 120-22] top; many calls stay on one note [Monelle 281] top. One anacrusis per beat into a long downbeat marks time on the spot [Tagg 297] conv. Halting trochees on alternating chords for contemptuous authority [Murphy 157] conv. Leader and echo (drill call and response) [Tagg 425] conv.
- **Harmony** - I - IV - V - I = reason and the familiar [Webster 203-6] top; functional harmony = normality [Lehman 203-7] conv. Strict style: dissonance prepared, resolving down by step [Chapin 302-10] top. Symmetric cycles read as meticulous order when phrasing and scoring are regular [Lehman 156] conv. The rules as a stable repeating bass: ostinato or ground in `pizzicato`+`basses` [Summers 106] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - `kit` snare (conv. army); strict unison rhythm (*The villain conducts*); unison topic for control [Sisman 108] top; fanfare as a call to attention [Sisman 115] top. March: exalted 2/2, slow 2/4, infantry 4/4 (T006). Band-march engine: `basses` "x " on each step, mid `strings` chords " x" off-beat [Monelle 123-24] top, format (inf.). Arbeau's drum command with unison `piccolo`/`flutes` [Monelle 114] top. Learned style for law and tradition: imitation, suspensions, alla breve [Chapin 323-26] top (T019); one marker suffices, a suspension line or imitation [Rumph 497] top. Planing for massed strength [Lehman 81-82] conv. Council scenes: homophonic swelling `strings`, a soaring high voice [Summers 137-38] conv.
- **With / against** - March + learned imitation = authoritative resolve [Hatten 523-29] top (R11). With tyranny: a mechanical minor ostinato; rigid planing that never repeats = imposed military order [Lehman 144-45] conv. Against chaos or rebellion: order in the bass, the other side syncopated, or break order once. Order against fear: a diegetic dance band against a dissonant orchestra [Webster 202-3] conv. Order against freedom: a free solo over the fixed ground [Summers 106] conv. Officious or corrupt authority: the march in a comic context (HAT23); staccato `harp`+`pizzicato` on every beat with out-of-key `bassoons` blurts [Tagg 585-87] conv; empty drill as "circus" [Tagg 416] [T].
- **Intensity** - Colour: a metronomic pulse. Strong: a band-march section. Whole: a strict march or fugato.
- **Not** - Loudness: order is precision. An unmarked march (mistaken for a bourrée): add dotting or a drum riff [Rumph 499-500] top. Learned style straight where order is mocked (pedantry) [Chapin 323-26] top; thin childlike scoring turns it to parody [Hatten 523-29] top. A carefree march for thinking authority (zero for thinking) [Tagg 407, 430] [T].

#### Tyranny
- **Cues** - Threat composite (inf., §6 r16): low, minor or phrygian, loud brass and percussion, repeated notes; inevitability from an unchanging ostinato (§1 Form [M]). Violence can be steady and unstoppable or erratic; low brass on a chromatic line is the male-violence code (Tagg Sth99 [T]). Implacable topped for a two-bar low unison riff repeated unchanged, the pulse barely audible; it also drew mechanical, tiring: the regime as uncaring machine [Tagg 607-23] [T]. Andante [Summers 160-61] conv. Lust for power is high style, but arrogance is bombast: let the grandeur sound inflated [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. A fast metronomic machine surface reads as a regime's force [Tagg 483-86] (inf.). For terror and pursuit by the regime's hounds: fast minor with sforzandi and rapid repeated notes [Tagg 317-19] conv.
- **Melody** - A simple tonic-centred tune that keeps returning home [Lehman 207] top. A rising melodic-minor scale in low brass or strings [Summers 160-61] conv. Imperial March shape (SW 10). Climbing four-note minor figure in `basses` or `horns` with snare (SW 43). Satire: one empty tune repeated unchanged over a snare tattoo, passed round in limping canon [Monelle 177-78] conv. Moderate 4/4, narrow range, heavy downbeats (inf.).
- **Harmony** - i - bvi oscillation (the empire's march) [Lehman 47] conv; the aeolian i - bVI (Tagg MM ch. 9) only slow, low, dark, else it reads as awe [Lehman 171] top (correction). Emperor: i - biii6/4 - i - #iv [Lehman 212] conv. Minor tritone i - #iv for an implacable will [Murphy 157-58] conv. Minor triads in rigid blocks, tonic held by insistence [Lehman 212-13] conv. `choir` on minor chords [Summers 160-61] conv. Superweapon: minor arpeggio with major seventh, polychords (SW 5).
- **Colours and devices** - *Power*, *The villain conducts*, low brass and snare, `choir` in rhythmic unison (one will; Tagg MM 514). Industrial percussion: low `bells` hits, `taiko`, `toms` ostinato [Summers 160-61] conv. A host on the move: planed root-position minor triads never repeating a move [Lehman 144-45] conv. Brass menace: loud low clusters for open force, soft held discords for the hidden hand [Tagg 570-72] conv. Army approaching: `kit` bd, cymbal, triangle in a long minor crescendo, without alla turca tune markers (R12) [McClelland 295; Sisman 109-10] top / (inf.). The regime swallowing all: a brass blast with a military bass taking over the orchestra [Monelle 177-78] conv. Darkest powers: primal drum with low accents on 2 and 4 under bare fifths [Stephen 71] conv. Machine army: minor call-and-response brass and snare (SW 26).
- **With / against** - With royalty: a corrupted anthem. With cruelty: *Soundtrack Dissonance*. Against rebellion: *Themes in conflict*; one power theme can serve empire, tyrant (tritone-bent) and rebels [Halfyard & Hancock 184-90] conv. The oppressed: the march done to the protagonist, minor, low brass, `timpani` [Horton 643-44] top. With cheer: **Propaganda and official cheer**. With sport: the regime's games [Tagg 426] (inf.). With industry: the forge rhythm. The theme displaces location music and recurs in the faction's places [Summers 160, 170] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a heartbeat ostinato in low strings, or the scale inside a location cue. Whole: the march or the tyrant's character cue.
- **Not** - Chaos: the tyrant is controlled; irregularity belongs to the resistance. Grandeur for a puppet regime: satire works by banality (copybook snare, piccolo too high, trite horns) [Monelle 176-78] conv. Military chants as harmless [Tagg 425] conv.

#### Rebellion
- **Cues** - Offbeats and syncopation (§1 Rhythm [W-M]); rough irregular rhythm for anger (§1 [M]); rising sequences; accelerando. "Indignant/defiant" is a cross-cultural feeling dimension, with a growl-like timbre (COW [M]). Tagg's rebellious and cool went to rock tunes (VVA 109, 1097 [T]); revolt = eruptive, bursting motion (VVA 5624). "We won't take it any more" scored 5.2x for a tune that sounded *played by* a band, so listeners heard "we" [Tagg 607-21] [T]. The square brass march is the "old and rigid" pole, a backbeat the emancipatory one [Tagg 413, 420-21] (inf.). The clash of rank: the low clown bursting into the dignified piece ("disorderly style") [Mirka Intro 5-9; Hunter 65] top.
- **Melody** - The appassionato tune doubled in octaves, soaring over throbbing triplets, also means a people's cause [Dick. 109] top. A folk tune taken up by brass; a rising stepwise minor line in pseudo-Baroque trumpets (SW 47). Syncopated 4/4, minor or dorian, a range of an octave climbing in sequence (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Aeolian over a pedal, bVI - bVII - i, lifted again and again into a major key (the bVI root rising a semitone to the new third): defiant minor overcome [Stephen 74] conv. Rebel fanfare: parallel major triads over a pedal, a key that will not settle (SW 2).
- **Colours and devices** - *The crowd*; `taiko` and bd stamping; *Rock drive* (`kit` and low brass); *National Anthem* for the new flag. Write the rebels as if they play it themselves: `kit` backbeat, a low unison riff in `cellos`+`basses`+`bassoons`, one rough `trombones` line, little sweetening [Tagg 618-21] (inf.). A region's own idiom for a national rising (*Finlandia*) [Dick. Table 3] top.
- **With / against** - Against authority: order holds the accompaniment, the rebel tune syncopated against it until it takes the orchestra; the last statement wins (*Themes in conflict*); `kit` backbeat against the establishment's 6/8 march (inf.). A low dance breaking into a minuet or overture and refusing to leave (inf.); composure failing as minuet slides to contredanse [Hunter 87] top. Rebels keep the empire's power theme unbent; the tyrant bends it [Halfyard & Hancock 184-85] conv. The march as the story's goal, a new order [Horton 643-44] top; folk material raised to grandioso = the commoners' triumph (inf.). Fallen rebels: funeral march, elegy, appassionato [Dick. 110] top. With despair: they share the overdriven minor sound [Tagg 618-21] conv. With hope: *Rallying*.
- **Intensity** - Colour: offbeat accents or a trombone growl. Strong: the rebel tune in syncopation against order. Whole: the mob build.
- **Not** - Polished majesty early: the rebels earn their brass.

#### Wealth and luxury
- **Cues** - Classical music primed quality and pricier choices (§8.4 [M-S]). Richness itself is the sign: more elaboration reads more courtly [Haringer 208] top. Tactile luxury: bowed string pads with no audible attack, even envelope, plenty of reverb, thick and silky; a quicker line on top adds sheen (Tagg MM 494; Semio99) [T, conv.]; the pad is mid-register "wallpaper" that cushions, tagged lush, silk and satin, velvety [Tagg 100, 170-71] top. "Brilliant" meant diamonds and twinkling stars, a shimmering timbre [Ivanovitch 332] top. Romance textures drew leisure: pleasure craft, parks, manor rooms [Tagg 158-66, 249] [T].
- **Melody** - (inf.) Ornamented, turning lines over the pad, graceful 3/4 waltz or moderate 4/4, major, mid-high register, a wide but smooth range, appoggiaturas and turns on strong beats. Brilliance as clear, detailed high figuration, orderly lamps not fireworks [Ivanovitch 342-48] top.
- **Harmony** - Rich sevenths and ninths (inf.); the high waltz's minor keys and modulations as sophistication [Dick. 103] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *The court dance*; `harp`; full `strings` pad (T185); `glockenspiel` glints for gold and glass [Webster 206] conv; *Musette*; *Festival trumpets*. Waltz for glamour (T066). Untold riches: `celesta`, `harp`, `glockenspiel`, `bells`, triangle and soft short `trumpets` falling through an invented scale [Brill 28, 34] conv. A rich hall: high `harp` + `glockenspiel` + `pizzicato` [Ivanovitch 338-41] (inf.). New-money glamour: `harp` glissandi, `glockenspiel`/`celesta` sparkle over quick dry `toms` [Tagg 648] (inf.), standing in for dated drum-machine gloss [Tagg 645-50] [T]. Pleasure resort: a jovial contrapuntal baroque-dance theme [Summers 163] conv.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by nobility. With decay: decadence, lush sevenths slowed and sagging, the waltz as decadent revel (T066, McC14). Against poverty: the beggar's lone tune under the feast music. Shops and markets: see Commerce (other group).
- **Intensity** - Colour: a harp flourish or a pad. Whole: a full court dance or ball waltz.
- **Not** - Loud brass (power, not money). Biedermeier: it is modest middle-class comfort [Dick. 114-15] top (see **Respectability**). The high halo, which shines rather than cushions [Tagg 170] top. "Oriental" scales for treasure [Brill 34] conv. Trendy production sounds that date [Tagg 649-50] conv.

#### Poverty
- **Cues** - No research on poverty as such. The low style (gigue, contredanse, Ländler, patter) is the common people's register (T025, HAT23); Scheibe's low style: few chords used sparingly, short phrases, no development, no fire [Haringer 207-8] top. Street song: simple tune, square phrases, folk harmony (T049 [gen.]). Tagg files poverty with lower class and folk (VVA 3631, no tune data). Slums drew 5.8x for *Streetcar*'s low plodding pulse, minor-six chords and semitone groans [Tagg 539-72] [T]. The pastoral hides real rural misery; to show poverty, sour it on purpose [Monelle 200-202, 272] top / (inf.). (inf.) Thin texture, few instruments, plain narrow melody.
- **Melody** - Bare unison or octave lines are the low characters' sound against rich harmony [Rumph 503-5] top. Real peasants: siciliana rhythm twisted into constant syncopation, an untidy flood of ideas [Sutcliffe 135-36] top. Ländler tunes: diatonic, major, arpeggios [Dick. 102-3] top. A laconic rubato solo with heavy vibrato for a hot, poor South (the faded woman) [Tagg 597-98] [T], here soft `trumpets` or `clarinets` with `wobble`. Narrow, stepwise, slow or jig-quick (inf.).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) I and V only, or a bare drone fifth; minor-six chords and low semitone groans for the slum [Tagg 570-72].
- **Colours and devices** - Hurdy-gurdy beggar: `oboe` or `english_horn` nasal tune over a buzzing `bassoons`+`cellos` fifth with a little `wobble` (T013); but the hurdy-gurdy also played at court, so it means beggary only when the scene says so [Monelle 214-15] top (correction). A lone reedy voice (*The cell*), a street fiddler's `solo_violin`, `pizzicato`, *Simple Score of Sadness*, a tavern tune on one instrument. A close rough copy of pipers = poor rustics [Monelle 229-30] top. A strummed street serenade (`harp`+`pizzicato`) (inf.); stamping `toms`+`basses` for hobnails [McKee 176] top.
- **With / against** - With dignity: a plain chorale for a poor, proud people. With mischief: a street urchin (*Sneaking*). Against wealth: the drone under the feast. Low in a high frame stays unrefined and unresolved [Dick. 135-36] top. The musette serves shepherds or peasants: match the music to which [Haringer 205] top. Rank rather than money: see **The servant and the commoner**.
- **Intensity** - Colour: taking the bass away, or one drone. Whole: a single voice over a drone.
- **Not** - Pity by default: a jig on one fiddle is poor and merry. Style hongrois as "the poor" (an old class and ethnic stereotype) [Dick. 100, 134-35] top. Jazz wholesale for a slum: keep the plodding pulse, minor-six chords and groans, drop the markers [Tagg 570-72] (inf.).

#### Pride
*Also covers false pride: the braggart, the impostor, the charlatan (the proposed "Pretension and the impostor" folds in here).*
- **Cues** - Pride may blend happy and angry codes at lower activity: major, firm, bright, accented, moderate (§2 [W]); wide leaps; a strutting gait (§7.6 r6). "Proud/strong" is a listener category (COW). Tagg's honour/pride field over-scored for the galloping Western with its lone lead (VVA 116 [T]); bravado to jazz and rock (VVA 1092); steady legato dotted horns = calm confidence (Kojak [T]). Pomp, bombast, gaudiness went to the brisk major brass march (success 6.9x) [Tagg 399-416] [T]. Arrogance is bombast, not nobility (Mattheson) [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. The sarabande carries ambition [Mirka Intro 7] top.
- **Melody** - Swagger: the bourrée's two-eighth pickup lifting onto the downbeat (the cocky groom) [Zbikowski 144, 157] top; halve the tune's speed while drums keep theirs [Stephen 67] conv. Bold: short articulated notes, wide leaps, Lombard snaps [Guymer 584] conv. False pride: the seria "elevated march" as parody, long notes over busy accompaniment, dotted triadic tune over a drum bass [Hunter 84] top; nearly every note from the fanfare triad, "too military" [Monelle 150, 175-76] conv; a call spanning an eleventh overreaches [Monelle 79] conv. The impostor: abrupt switches among march, minuet and gigue, each cued by a cadence; a poor repeated idea with a loud fanfare in the wrong place [Hunter 66, 73-78] top.
- **Harmony** - Plain tonic-dominant pomp over a drum bass, full V - I cadences marking each switch [Hunter 66, 84] top. Bright major, I - IV - V with dotted cadences (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - `trumpets`, dotted processional rhythms, *horn call*, over-dotted French overture (T024). Pompous general: strutting dotted 6/8 halting on rests, brass and snare (SW 39). Braggart: heroic long notes in `trombones` or `horns` over busy repeated-note `strings`, too loud or too long, a `bassoons` echo (inf.); a charlatan wizard adds a `glockenspiel` glint on each cadence (inf.). *Showman's bookends* for the vain; *Mock melodrama* for the pompous. Stock buffa types (arrogant noble, social climber) [Hunter 83] top.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by nobility. Pompous officer: march + imitation in thin toy scoring [Hatten 523-29] top. Stiff-upper-lip redone as pomp mocks imperial arrogance [Tagg 223] top. With cowardice: the boaster up a tree; with deceit: fake supernatural pomp [Hunter 66, 84] top. Unmasking: a big close deflated by a soft falling unison arpeggio [Sutcliffe 137-38] (inf.). With a fall: the proud theme in *Deflation* or *Descent*. Ostentation over rage turns comic (§7.6 r3). Against humility: *Themes in conflict*.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a dotted strut, or one over-dotted fanfare cadence. Whole: a self-important march; for an impostor, the mock march switching idiom at each cadence (inf.).
- **Not** - Majesty: pride claims more than it has, so the apparatus is slightly too big (inf.); real majesty uses the same topics without the gap [Hunter 84] top. "Circus" display for its own sake unless mocked [Tagg 416] (inf.).

#### Humility
- **Cues** - Soft = low activation, perhaps yielding (§1 Loudness [W]); simple predictable melody (§3 [W-M]); stepwise, narrow, regular, small dynamic range. Tagg files humility under smallness with intimacy and secrets (VVA 146Q). Hevner's "yielding" sits in the soft, slow, dreamy cluster (HEV cl.3). Repentance and supplication are low affects, patience middle [Mirka Intro 3-7] top. A slow section with no pulse for a hero's humble side, against relentless pulse elsewhere [Stephen 75-76] conv.
- **Melody** - Lied ideals: near the tonic, moderate range, free-flowing, singable at once by anyone [Day-O'Connell 249-54] top; a sweet voice of small compass, more feeling than skill [Day-O'Connell 250-53] top. The air rather than the aria (T035, HAT23). Correctio: after an outburst, a soft horn-call figure in pure thirds takes it back like an apology [Beghin 559-64] conv. Major, 4/4 or 3/4, slow-moderate, range within an octave (inf.).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) Plain I - IV - V - I, a simple plagal close, no chromatic colour.
- **Colours and devices** - Solo `oboe`, `flutes` or `cellos`; a plain hymn; lullaby simplicity; no brass. Humble wisdom: a quiet solo flute (LOTR [S], conv.). One voice with plain `harp` chords (inf.). A lead that does not match its accompaniment (`harp` or `pizzicato` over held strings) makes heroism private and unofficial [Tagg 378] (inf.); a thin decaying timbre makes a fanfare laconic [Tagg 376] (inf.).
- **With / against** - With stature: a chamber surface and one restrained climax. Reinforced by devotion. Surrender: the same plain line after a defeat.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one plain cadence. Whole: a nearly unaccompanied tune.
- **Not** - Sadness: humility is content, major and gentle (inf.). Ornament: the singing style is chaste [Day-O'Connell 245-48] top. Pentatonic "simplicity": Hollywood's code for groups it talked down to ("knows its place") [Tagg 531-32] conv [T].

#### The army and the soldier
- **Cues** - A documented topic, not an emotion. Military topic: snare, trumpets and brass, dotted and triplet rhythms, fanfare-built tunes, duple or quadruple metre (T005, HAR10; LAV top.); mostly the warrior's myth, though the dysphoric march (a march done *to* someone) is already 19th-century [Horton 643-44] top and lieder prefer the doomed soldier [Monelle 176-80] top (correction). Tempo follows the arm: infantry slow and "pathetic", cavalry quick; triple allowed if proud [Haringer 200] top (correction to "moderate"). Speeds: slow 60-75 steps, quick 108-120, double 130-140, cavalry walk 72, trot about 140, charge 160 [Monelle 119-23, 161-81] top. The brisk 6/8 band march drew military, marching, uniform near 10x, but always as parade, homecoming or victory, never enmity [Tagg 399-410] [T] (correction: evidence for the army as display, not war). Square periods = coordinated movement (Tagg MM ch. 8); "martial" low and firm (HEV cl.8 [M]). The drum is warlike only; the trumpet a call to battle [Haringer 197-98] top.
- **Melody** - March (Sulzer): major, 4/4 or alla breve, even pace, dotted, two-bar incises from the downbeat or a crotchet upbeat [Mirka Intro 22-23] top; dotted notes full and emphatic [Haringer 200-201] top. Instant soldier: a triadic call on harmonics 3-8 (G3 C4 E4 G4 C5 in C, no b7) with fast triple-tongued *ritiriton* [Monelle 166-67] top. Cavalry calls lie low and dignified, infantry bugle calls higher and nervous [Monelle 139-41] top (correction). A linear, thrusting tonic arpeggio = military; circling = pastoral [Rumph 507-11] top. Trio: a cantabile tune, peace after war [Monelle 124] top. Soldier's song: a plain folk-like Kriegslied [Dick. 106] top.
- **Harmony** - Band march: a bass on each step (I and V roots), chords off-beat in the middle [Monelle 123-24] top; low strings on I and V in homorhythm [Summers 51] conv. Martial close bVII - I [Lehman 242] conv. Harmonised calls suggest civic or hunt unless war is shown [Monelle 168-69] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `trumpets`, `horns`, `timpani` [Sisman 108] top; `kit` snare and bd; fanfare fragments dropped into the texture [Monelle 121-22] top; strings imitating trumpets and drums [Haringer 199-200] top, crushed rising notes for the roll [Monelle 178] top. Allies' onslaught: bugle `trumpets` over brass bare fifths, `taiko`/`timpani` [Stephen 65] conv. Ancient soldiery: winds, brass, percussion, `harp` the only strings [Brill 29] conv. Drill chant: leader and unison male `choir` over snare at 120 or 180 steps [Tagg 425] conv. An army far off: a quiet march with Janissary colour [Hunter 75-76] top. Invent the realm's patriotic song and quote it [Summers 67-69] conv.
- **With / against** - Tyranny: the machine march (SW 26). Heroism: expand with tutti fanfares and trumpets-and-drums when the army wins [Dick. 118] top. Battle: tempest + military + heroic [Dick. 127] top (see War). Grief: the last call, a soft slow trumpet or soft solo horn answered by woodwind echoes [Monelle 109-10, 169-79] conv; a sweetheart's grief, march tempo with grinding minor and tiny bugle figures [Monelle 176] conv. With the hunt: the ranger [Monelle 82, 138-39] top. Enemy army: its anthem in minor; the soldier's heartbeat against the national strand [Summers 67-69] conv. Duty against feeling: a drum riff answered by a sigh [Hatten 523-24] top. Toy soldiers: trumpets and drums entering first at a small modulation [Galand 463-65] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a one-note call, a snare figure, a trumpet fragment. Strong: a fanfare preface. Whole: band march (two strains + trio) [Monelle 120-24] top.
- **Not** - A battle (parade is order and pride). Sighs or cooing in the march [Haringer 200] top. An unmarked march (reads bourrée) [Rumph 499-500] top. A quoted real bugle call (modern army) [Monelle 165-66] top. Assuming an old march sounds "olden" to everyone [Tagg 420] [T].

#### Chivalry and knighthood
- **Cues** - The chivalric style is a dialect: heroic, noble and specifically medieval, an idealised past; its gestures must occur together, since a horn fifth alone reads as hunt [Dick. 124-25] top (correction; this upgrades the old named-only status, Horton 645 / T093, to a checked source). Film's medieval icons: church bell, trumpet fanfare, horn call, banquet dance, minstrel, chant, galloping riders (T166, Haines conv.). Chivalry = courage, generosity, manners, lineage, fame; prowess in single combat [Monelle 142-43] top. Modern listeners hear chivalry as ceremonial display (it topped for the brisk march) [Tagg 420] [T]. The hunt's codes (nobility, risk, youth) pass to it [Lowe 625-26] top; the horn is "heavenly" [Haringer 196-97] top.
- **Melody** - Allegro 6/8 gallop from the metre itself, not a dotted march [Dick. 125] top; horn fifths, fanfare figures, repeated-note trumpet calls [Dick. 125] top. Riding theme: 6/8 or 9/8 gallop under a harmonic-series call (inf., after Monelle 4-5, 166). Knightly triplet fanfares: horns the huntsman, trumpets the double-tongued warrior, then both [Monelle 100] conv. Swashbuckler contour: tonic down to the low dominant, back, then up through the high dominant to the upper tonic (Korngold via Zelda) [Summers 148-49] top. Mystical order: a minor line rising from an opening fourth on solo horn (SW 3). Seria hero mixing pride, amoroso, sigh in one tempo [Hunter 67-68] top.
- **Harmony** - Modal progressions for archaic colour [Dick. 125] top, e.g. I - bVII - I or dorian i - IV - i (inf.). Modal cadence with `timpani`.
- **Colours and devices** - Four `horns` in horn-fifth voicing, `trumpets` answering with repeated-note calls, `cellos`+`basses` on dotted-crotchet beats, `strings` off-beat quavers (conv.). Paladin: horn-call antecedent, consequent a soft `brass` chorale with a 2-3 over a V pedal [Byros 386-91] (inf.). Imagined host with heraldry: four heavy steps over two bars, `harp` and `organ`, broad trio at about 80 [Monelle 131-33] conv. Troubadour: `pizzicato` strummed chords or `harp` (conv.). An order's dignity in mourning: the English containment style (see **Restraint and composure**) [Tagg 221-24] top / (inf.). Fallen knight: a far solo horn, unanswered (Roland) [Monelle 106-7, 152] top. A bell.
- **With / against** - With nobility: the courtly side. With heroism: the quest opening; thin the texture and go minor-modal for the quest itself (inf.). With the oath: the vow (sarabande, secular gravity, T058). Courtly love belongs to the bardic dialect, as does the harp [Dick. 126-27] top; for it, a Lied-style `cellos` or `horns` line over nocturne `harp` (inf.). Honour against love as a movement's plan [Sisman 98-99] top. Living knight on `horns`; a dead order remembered on `violins` or `clarinets` [Agawu 490] conv. With corruption: the call inverted in trombones. With the hunt: a royal chase.
- **Intensity** - Hint: two gestures (gallop + horn fifths). Strong: + repeated-note trumpet calls. Whole: all four with modal cadences, a tournament or quest opening [Dick. 125] top / ladder (inf.).
- **Not** - Dotted gallops, a moderate march or processional as its core [Dick. 125] top (correction). Drill calls quoted literally [Monelle 165-71] top. Screen "medieval" as history: it is a 19th-century construct (T166). The modern superhero sound: what reads as cinema is the pop-march setting (square phrases, syncopated ostinato), not the rising fifth; a fifth motif in late-Romantic manner reads as myth [Winters 119-28] conv, and filmic Korngold idioms suit knightly legend for game players [Summers 110-12, 148-49] top (corrections; both outweigh the old T162 line).

#### Respectability and the middle class
*New. The burgher, guild master, forester's daughter, shire folk, merchant family: proper, virtuous, comfortable, a little old-fashioned. Not Nobility (birth), not Wealth (display).*
- **Cues** - Biedermeier: old-fashioned elegance, middle-class propriety and nostalgia for the recent past in a Mozartian idiom; moderate tempo, small dynamic range, clear periodic form, like Classical style in minor hands [Dick. 114-15] top. Its sense shifts with context: respectable, prim, nostalgic, cosy [Dick. 114] top.
- **Melody** - Symmetrical four-bar phrases, a lyrical tune close to dance, poised grace [Dick. 114] top. Lied style: simple, unornamented, folk-like = middle-class simplicity [Dick. 106, n43] top. Major, 2/4 or 3/4 at a moderate walk, range about an octave, stepwise with small leaps (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Mostly diatonic, slow harmonic rhythm, strong cadences [Dick. 114] top: I - IV - V - I with a firm V - I closing each four-bar unit (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - An occasional Alberti-like bass: `violins2` in slurred broken chords, or `bassoons` [Dick. 114] top / parts conv. Tune on `clarinets` or `flutes` over light `strings`, no brass display (inf.). A respectable heroine's virtue set against the folk music around her (Weber's Agathe) [Dick. 115] top.
- **With / against** - Against the low style or an outsider group: the two themes side by side, never merging [Dick. 100-101] top. Against nobility: the court waltz's modulating balanced phrases against the burgher's diatonic song (inf.). With nostalgia: Biedermeier already looks fondly back [Dick. 114] top. With home and comfort: it suits them, but its core is class and propriety (corrections, Dickensheets 7).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one strong four-bar cadence. Strong: an Alberti-like accompaniment under the character's tune. Whole: a Biedermeier dance-song in rounded binary (inf.).
- **Not** - Salon glamour or luxury [Dick. 114-15] top. A salon idiom at all: Biedermeier is not one [Dick. 114-15] top. A fixed meaning [Dick. 114] top.

#### The servant and the commoner (rank below the gentry)
*New. Maids, valets, peasants, tradesfolk as comic opera sets their rank. Not Poverty (lack of money), not Nobility.*
- **Cues** - Buffa casts mix ranks, each with its own music: buffo nobile, mezzo carattere, caricato [Hunter 65-66] top. Rank caps intensity: the low-born may be frantic (patter, stamping, sudden swings), the noble stays measured [Mirka Intro 7-8] top. Comic dances show common people's customs and love intrigues: lively, mischievous, agile [McKee 169] top. The worldly-wise maid is carefree galant [Hunter 84-85] top.
- **Melody** - The maid: a gavotte demoted from 2/2 to 2/4, graceful scalar sixteenth pairs, half-bar pickup, slipping into patter when she shows her hand [Hunter 84-85] top. Patter: very fast repeated notes in a tiny range, for basso buffo and lower-class women [Hunter 79-82] top. Common dances: contredanse (fast 2/4 or 6/8, triplet lilt, 4+4 periods), Ländler (arpeggiated quavers), the bourrée's swaggering two-quaver pickup [McKee 169-80; Zbikowski 144] top.
- **Harmony** - Plain galant or folk harmony: I and V, few chords, short undeveloped phrases [McKee 180; Haringer 207-8] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `flutes`+`violins` on the tune, light `pizzicato` bass, patter in staccato `bassoons` or `clarinets` (inf.). Stock types with stock music: wily servant, worldly maid, bumpkin, ingénue lovers [Hunter 83] top. Bare octaves for low characters against rich harmony for their betters [Rumph 503-5] top.
- **With / against** - Against the noble: the clash of rank, the clown bursting in [Mirka Intro 9] top. A servant in command may use the minuet legitimately; one posing as composed cracks into contredanse [Hunter 87] top. Ambiguous rank (a princess fallen low): the court dance in its servant metre [Hunter 84-85] top. With poverty: see **Poverty**.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a 2/4 half-bar pickup or one bar of patter. Whole: a servant's buffa song ending in patter (inf.).
- **Not** - Patter for a noble or seria character [Hunter 79-82] top. Coarseness by default: Sulzer warns against making simple folk coarse [Haringer 207-8] top.

#### Propaganda and official cheer
*New. The regime's herald, the recruiting crier, the cheerful public face of power over hidden suffering. Not Tyranny (open threat), not Festival (real joy).*
- **Cues** - The "fun-and-power" bundle: one music lends power the friendly face of military, flag, newsreel, sport and picnic [Tagg 412] conv. Carefree music shuts out passion and thought, so it asks nothing [Tagg 430] (inf.). The march scored zero for enmity and villains; menace comes from context [Tagg 407, 430] [T].
- **Melody** - Brisk major 6/8 or 2/4 march: square two- and four-bar phrases, perky dotted figures, one anacrusis per beat into long downbeats [Tagg 297, 409-12] conv.
- **Harmony** - Functional I - IV - V - I with small chromatic passing notes and accented sixths, sevenths or ninths on downbeats; no conflict, nothing modal [Tagg 409-12] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Brass band, `kit` snare and bd, a `bells` stroke on an upbeat, a drill chant (leader and unison male `choir`) [Tagg 413, 425] conv.
- **With / against** - By moment: play the cheer straight, then cut to slow sombre underscore when the truth lands, as newsreels did in 1944 [Tagg 416-17] conv. With sport: the regime's games; sports and war marches are interchangeable [Tagg 426] (inf.). With tyranny: the jolly face over its machine.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a perky march fragment on a herald's trumpet. Whole: a full brass march over a scene of loss; use it once, or the table feels manipulated (inf.).
- **Not** - Sincerely over real suffering: the jolly newsreel march over disaster eroded trust in all music scored that way [Tagg 416-17] (inf.). Making the march itself menacing [Tagg 407, 430] [T].

### C. Mind and temperament

#### Wisdom
- **Cues** - The solemnity axis (§7.2 [M]); consonant simple harmony = serious, dignified (§1 Harmony); slow; moderate originality (§7.3 [W-M]; §7.6 r14). Learned style: imitation, fugue and canon, suspension chains, alla breve half-note pulse, long notes, unsingable subjects; it means authority, tradition, church, intellect, and pedantry in a comic frame (T019, HAT23; MCK07 top.). Tagg files wisdom with trust and justice (VVA 117); slowness = age and deliberation (Tagg Death). A fugal setting raises dignity without changing the feeling [Chapin 309-10] top.
- **Melody** - Fugal subjects and imitation in a grave, sober manner [Chapin 306-9] top; learned style is recognised from one syncopated suspension line or from imitation [Rumph 493-98] top. Priests of wisdom: choral texture and rising dotted scales [Haringer 202-3] top. A mentor's destiny theme: a rise-and-fall minor third, minor, on `horns` and `strings`, with a derived agency motif for his interventions [Halfyard 228] conv. Fighting mentor: a balanced minor phrase pair climbing a minor third into a new key (SW 29). Slow 4/4 or 2/2, modal or major, stepwise, range about a ninth (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Simple triads with a lydian #4, gentle homophony (SW 12). Crotch's sublime: learned, unpredictable modulation over a grave melody [McClelland 290-94] top. Suspension chains, plagal and modal cadences (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - A sage's slow modal tune on `english_horn` (*Spellcraft*); `cellos`, low `bassoons`, a warm low voice; a canon or fugato. Humble wisdom: quiet solo `flutes` (LOTR). A playful sage gets a "giggle" (SW 12). Spell-casting as conducting: `brass` hits on the gestures, `strings` sweeping with the arm [Halfyard 219, 223] conv. Absent-minded sage: a phrase repeated too often and fading, then a sudden tutti recovery [Sisman 104, 108] top.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by age, magic, mystery. Against madness: a wise surface with one voice unhinging. Lich's intellect: learned style in minor (T019). Learned + fanfare = authority [Hatten 515-29] top. An idea spreading through a council: a short strong phrase taken up by one voice, then many, each varying it (Forkel's fugue myth) [Chapin 323-26] top / (inf.). The mentor's death hands his agency motif to the pupil [Stephen 62; Halfyard 228] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a canonic answer to the motif. Strong: a fugato. Whole: a slow modal chorale or fugato.
- **Not** - Pomposity: wisdom is unhurried. Learned style tipping into pedantry; leaping exaggerated answers parody it [Chapin 323-26; Hatten 515-29] top. A canon slipped smoothly into a plain period: no one hears it [Lowe 603-10] top.

#### Cunning and deceit
- **Cues** - Music cannot say "treacherous" (§7.6 r7 [W]): write behaviour. A surface too smooth; semitone wrong notes approached by leap and repeated = quirky, impertinent (§4 [W]); harmony that avoids resolving. Tagg's crime and intrigue codes: minor, chromaticism, prominent tritones (a melodic b5, or inside half-diminished and minor-six chords) for the hidden and suspicious (Tagg Tritone; TLTT 579-88 [T]). Imbroglio (plotting, scurrying): quick rhythms, syncopation, soft (p), detached, bass held back at first, motive tossed among winds, timpani-backed outbursts [Sisman 90-91] top. The counterfeit uses the right vocabulary, too polished, adapted to each listener [Day-O'Connell 250-53] top.
- **Melody** - A narrow line trapped inside a minor third (SW 33). Rogue: brilliant runs with displaced accents and sudden pauses, light and detached (inf.); the accompaniment stopping dead, leaving the tune scrambling [Guymer 589-90] conv. Feigned hesitation before a cadence that then lands harder (dubitatio) [Beghin 552-70] conv. The impostor's switches of idiom at each cadence (see **Pride**) [Hunter 66] top. Chromatic lower neighbours; moderate 2/4 or 6/8, minor, low-middle register (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Conversion: slip from one idiom to another through a hidden pivot so the seam is unheard, insinuating [Lehman 218-19] conv. Serious schemer: plain unaltered minor triads on odd roots (ii, iii, bv) rather than jazz extensions [Tagg 585-88] conv. Unresolved half-diminished and minor-six chords (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Low `clarinets` (sly), `pizzicato`. *Façade*, *Creepy jazz*, *Sneaking*, *Seduction*, *Foreshadowing and the lie*. Fawning traitor: a slow chromatic crawl in low reeds, `tuba`, low `strings`, bd, clustered high strings (LOTR Gríma [W]). A con: a merry gigue, frantic music for the dupe, then fake supernatural pomp [Hunter 66] top. Mockery in the target's own idiom [Hunter 86-87] top. Flatterer: the honest tune given brilliant runs and shifting style (inf.). Schemer as stealthy hunter: a horn colour [Monelle 76] conv.
- **With / against** - Two layers: a pleasant surface plus a give-away (a drone under the *Façade*, one bar of the real motif in the basses). The liar's music converts, the honest music switches cleanly [Lehman 218-19] (inf.). Lovable trickster: *Sneaking* with wrong notes in major; one cheeky chromatic note in a noble fanfare [Monelle 97] conv. A posing servant: a prim minuet cracking into contredanse [Hunter 87] top. Intrigue moved to a green world: a `flutes` countermelody or rustic winds [Sisman 90-91] top. With crime: see Crime and the underworld.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one chromatic slip or one flat fifth. Strong: imbroglio. Whole: *Creepy jazz* or a *Façade* with an undercurrent.
- **Not** - Menace on the surface: a good liar sounds pleasant. Irony the delivery does not signal: mark the double meaning audibly [Beghin 552-64] conv. Swung flat-fifth jazz for a dangerous schemer (now petty larceny); keep it for the lovable rogue [Tagg 583-88] conv.

#### Secrecy
- **Cues** - Soft, sparse, short notes broken by rests; low level plus staccato is also the strongest fear cue (§1 Artic [M]), so keep the tempo moderate. Tagg files secrets and private under smallness (VVA 146P), hidden and in shadow under darkness (VVA 1251), over-scored by the slow minor *Streetcar* [T]. Whispering as an anaphone (VVA 5964). Imbroglio dynamics: soft (p), detached, bass held back [Sisman 90-91] top. Stealth ladder: undetected rubato and sparse; suspicion adds instruments, a pulsing rhythm, volume; discovered escalates further; step down when guards give up [Summers 122-23] conv.
- **Melody** - Spy riff: a chromatic inner voice circling the fifth, 5 - b6 - 6 - b6, under a held minor chord (spy, thriller) [Tagg 599] [T]. A low riff rising to the 9th, touching b3, dropping to #6 (cloak and dagger, 23%) [Tagg 625-26] [T]; a strong-beat augmented fifth over the minor third (mid-60s cliché) [Tagg 626-27] conv. Cipher motif: a name spelled in pitches, meaningful only to insiders [Dick. 131-32] top. The tiptoe march: a full march pp, very staccato [Hunter 73-75] top. Moderate 4/4, minor, narrow low range (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Bed im6(add9); closing i(maj7) or i(maj9), "the number one spy chord" [Tagg 577-79, 626-27] conv. bVI7 - V7 under a melodic b5 - 4 [Tagg 629-30] conv. A harmonic motto heard early whose meaning comes after the reveal [Lehman 104-5] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Sneaking*, a held `tremolo` note, low `clarinets`. *Musical Spoiler*: the hidden motif in an inner voice. Hold the im6(add9) on soft `organ` or divisi low `strings`+`clarinets` [Tagg 627] (inf.). Spy lead: `harp` in minor, left ringing, over soft low strings [Tagg 367] conv; very soft `kit` snare tattoos under a lead [Tagg 377-78] conv. Ladder scoring: held `strings` + solo `clarinets`; then `pizzicato` ostinato + soft `timpani` heartbeat; then full ostinato and clear `brass` tune (inf.). Rogue sneaking: `piccolo`+`flutes` tune, `pizzicato`, `clarinets` tattoos, f tutti stabs on discovery (inf.). Hidden reference: a horn-type theme first on the "wrong" instrument, the horn kept for the revelation [Monelle 3, 100] top. Guarded inner power: a seven-note `celesta` cell (SW 46). An earlier theme quoted as code [Dick. 131-32] top / (inf.).
- **With / against** - With mystery: investigation (see **The investigator**). With menace: the assassin. With mischief: the burglar. *Dread* under *Façade* for a spy. Hidden royalty in pastoral dress [Monelle 190-93] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a motif buried in a low inner voice. Strong: the suspicion rung of the ladder. Whole: *Sneaking*; climb by denser rhythm, faster tempo and harmonic rhythm, louder, clearer lines, more instruments [Summers 122] conv.
- **Not** - Loud full textures. An unreliable stealth score: players read it as a barometer [Summers 123, 134] conv. Pitched-percussion shimmer and consonant harp ("can't be dangerous") [Tagg 300] (inf.). The spy signs swung: keep them straight, unswung, even in an odd metre [Tagg 583-88] conv.

#### Mystery
- **Cues** - Missing or competing expectations breed apprehension (§4; §6 r21 [W-M]), at a low dose curiosity (inf.). Avoid V - I; questioning upward leaps (§1 Contour [W]); an unfamiliar idiom adds uncertainty (§8.3 [M]). "Eerie/mysterious" is a reliable listener category (COW). Harmonic soft focus marks a story behind the scene: unbroken parallel motion keeping a mild diatonic dissonance, shuttling IV and V without arriving (drew questions, searching, complex plot) [Tagg 560-61] [T]. Unprepared changes (a solo replacing massed strings, a sudden metre change) send listeners hunting for a reason [Tagg 555-62] [T]. Otherworldly pianissimos where fantasia meets ombra [Head 259-63] conv. A fragment can be left ambiguous and named by later context [Agawu 475-79] conv.
- **Melody** - One held common tone anchoring wandering chords; a terse motif revolving round one note [Lehman 150-55] conv. Fog: a legato chromatic low `flutes` line with raised-fourth leaps over a low tonic pedal and a tight minor cluster [Tagg 588-90] conv. Foreboding: two `flutes` in thirds sliding down chromatically over a static `harp` octave [Brill 31] conv. Disembodied fragments loosely linked, as if improvised [Head 259-63] top. Clue chime: three notes in a timbre heard nowhere else [Summers 118] conv. An unaccompanied horn call on the flat 7th harmonic [Monelle 102-3] conv. Questions followed by real pauses [Beghin 552-70] conv.
- **Harmony** - Whole-tone and octatonic (Tagg MM ch. 9). Dark secret: i - bvi [Lehman 101] top. Ancient mystery: i - v - bIII - bVII on long durations, no dominant; riddle: i - VII - bVI - IIImaj7 round one held tone; enigmatic object: I - iii - bIII over a shared pedal; gentle mystery: I - v oscillation [Lehman 114, 134-55] conv. A long minor-six (half-diminished) chord on soft mid-low brass, never resolving (misterioso; shady 7.3x) [Tagg 566-72] [T]. The fantasia losing its way through vii°7 and enharmonic turns [Head 259-63] top. Minor arpeggios with a clashing added note, stepping (SW 34); two minor triads a tritone apart rocking (SW 48).
- **Colours and devices** - *Mystery and investigation*. A hypnotic `flutes`, `celesta`, `harp` ostinato (Tagg MM ch. 7). Tag the mystery cell with one timbre every time [Lehman 148] conv. A crisp dry clue figure in high `harp`+`pizzicato`+`celesta` [Webster 201] conv. Investigation bed: low `clarinets`+`harp`, soft `trumpets`, `pizzicato` basses, `celesta` with slight `wobble` [Summers 118] (inf.). Revealed truth: falling minor arpeggio triplets on `harp`/`celesta` (SW 23). The Romantic horn of the forest's mystery [Monelle 101-7] top. *The half-remembered tune*.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by secrecy, the arcane, wisdom. Against dread: keep the energy light. A floating theme heard over unrelated situations sets players hunting for the link [Murphy 151-65] conv; one shared cell under mystery, friendship and heroic themes binds them [Lehman 148-49] conv. Ombra introduction dispelled by an Allegro [McClelland 295] top. Mystery chords without ethnic markers [Brill 23] top.
- **Intensity** - Colour: an unresolved sus chord. Strong: the riddle chain. Whole: an investigation loop on a hypnotic ostinato; it stops when all is found or the party leaves [Summers 118] conv.
- **Not** - Dread. Ending a loop on the tonic: stop on its least stable chord [Lehman 263 n42] conv. A decorative clue chime: use it only to help [Summers 118-19] conv.

#### Madness
- **Cues** - Not studied by name; the composite is inference (§6 r13, r17): minor seconds, tritones, leaps beyond the octave (§1 [M]); high dissonant dyads; irregular rhythm, wide range (§6 r8); large rapid dynamic swings; high complexity with high dynamism = anxiety (§1 [W]); deliberate wrong notes (§4). Tagg: semitone glissandi = moaning, weird, drunk, mad (TLTT 573-77); a quiet, high, held violin dissonance = mental tension, against low brass stabs for physical violence (Sth99; MM ch. 8 [T, conv.]); *Streetcar* over-scored on madness and suspicion (VVA 127). Register decides a wobble's meaning: high = weird or mad, middle = sad, low = sick [Tagg 576-77] conv. Mad scenes belong to tempesta (fast frenzy) [McClelland 285-87] top; sensibility at its extreme breaks syntax and closure [Head 271-72] top; patter = panic and harping on one subject [Hunter 79-82] top.
- **Melody** - The madwoman's song: a plaintive minor song, then the same "with more expression", words cut by rests, a sigh on a tritone appoggiatura, a fermata, bII winding round the tonic, until it dissolves into recitative [Day-O'Connell 251-53; Head 271-72] top. A slow portamento wobble between two pitches with quarter-tones (madness, hallucination); a short chromatic figure repeated endlessly ("something weird") [Tagg 575-76] conv. A whining voice-like line high on `solo_violin` with slides [Tagg 588-89] conv. The frantic aria changing manner every few bars [Hunter 65-66] top.
- **Harmony** - Unprepared nonfunctional chromaticism plus an odd sliding timbre = "chromatic psychosis" [Lehman 71] top; chromaticism and unreasoned progressions [Webster 200, 208] top. The minor-major seventh (Hitchcock chord) for irrationality [Lehman 61, 132] conv. An ambiguous V7b5 under the whine [Tagg 588-89] conv. Restless modulation that finds clear cadences only as the mind opens [Lehman 35-36] conv. bII circling i; raging vii°7 over a V pedal [Head 271-72; McClelland 282] top. Semitones, discords, brisk and languishing in turn can play listeners to distraction [Head 266-67] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Madness* ("Psycho" strings: sharp repeated down-bows, high dissonances spreading down, Tagg MM 510); `unhinge` on one part; `solo_violin`; *The circus* (creepy); slides on one solo line; *The other-worldly glide* (`choir` or `solo_violin` sliding) for film's odd gliding timbre (inf.). The song first on high `choir` or `flutes`, then `solo_violin` breaking into unmeasured recitative over `tremolo` (inf.). A split creature: fast `harp` repeats with `wobble` over `bassoons`+`basses` (T172). Tarantella's relentless fast 6/8 = compulsion and hysteria (T069).
- **With / against** - Madness is one voice breaking; a dream is the whole world gone soft. Calm surface over madness: keep it intact and unhinge one inner voice, or crack it once. With grief: a lament that breaks. With obsession: the fixed figure while others unhinge. Recovery: let the harmony find clear cadences [Lehman 35-36] conv. With genius: same idiom, regular phrasing (see **The brilliant mind**).
- **Intensity** - Colour: one slide, one displaced note, one rest cutting a phrase. Strong: an `unhinge` window or a held high dissonance. Whole: *Madness*, a tarantella, or the song dissolving into recitative. Ration it (§3; §7.6 r12).
- **Not** - Noise: madness needs a recognisable tune to break. Madness devices for the merely strange or magical (difference read as threat) [Binns 139] conv. The old gendered code (feverish chromatic sequences as "hysterical femininity") [Lehman 35-37] conv. A low slow wobble (drunk, seasick) or a fast two-pitch wobble (comic double take) [Tagg 575-76] conv.

#### Obsession
- **Cues** - Repetition, condensation, sequence raise tension (§1 Form [M]); small pitch variation (§1 Range); a narrow figure that keeps returning. Tagg's idées fixes sit in the madness field (VVA 1272); a range of a third or less sounds constrained (Tagg MM ch. 9). Michaelis' sublime of extreme uniformity: one note or chord repeated, long weighty notes, long pauses [McClelland 290-94] top. Exact repetition in place of an answer was heard as unreasoning [Sutcliffe 124-29] top.
- **Melody** - A short cell inside a third, repeated exactly; patter on words already heard, very fast in a tiny range [Hunter 79-82] top. A one-bar gesture repeated with written-out acceleration and crescendo gives vertigo [McKee 180-87] top. The idée fixe keeps its pitches but takes on each scene's topic (a ballroom waltz) [Dick. 103] top. Moderate fixed tempo, minor or chromatic neighbour notes (inf.).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) A ground bass or pedal that never moves, or one chord repeated; the harmony may darken around the fixed figure while the figure stays.
- **Colours and devices** - An ostinato unchanged while everything grows (*Bell ostinato*, *Boléro Effect*); a ticking figure (*Tick Tock Tune*); the motif cutting into other music; a ground that will not stop (T056). The absent-minded phrase inverted: repeated too often and never recovering [Sisman 104] (inf.).
- **With / against** - With love: a fixation, the beloved's tune intruding into every scene in that scene's dress [Dick. 103] top. With madness: the fixed figure while others unhinge. With devotion: a zealot.
- **Intensity** - Colour: the motif returning once too often. Whole: a Boléro.
- **Not** - Development: obsession keeps its pitches and refuses to develop, though it may change its dress [Dick. 103] top (correction).

#### Courage
- **Cues** - Confidence is steadiness first, loudness second (*Rallying*). A pulse entering (§8.1 [W-M]); rising lines, octave doubling; the mode turning major. Tagg files courage with strength and pride (VVA 1161), over-scored by the galloping Western and the sports tune [T]; the octave whoop on unison horns is his call to action (Kojak [T]). Inspiring, empowering are listed moods (GEMS; JAM). Sublime music inspires devotion and courage (Beattie) [McClelland 290-94] top (T026). The trumpet is heart-shaking, the march lifts the marchers [Haringer 197-200] top. A call bent away from the parade ground speaks of courage and decision; the hunt means overcoming danger [Monelle 95, 166] top.
- **Melody** - Heroic decision: a fanfare [Mirka Intro 40] top; a true march is brave and rousing [Haringer 200-201] top. The hero's call: one horn line in 6/8 (Siegfried), not a duet [Monelle 95-96] conv. Pastoral to martial in one theme: the circling arpeggio made linear and thrusting, dotted, 6/8 to 2/4 or 4/4 [Rumph 507-11] top / (inf.). Mannheim rocket: a fast rising broken chord over a crescendo (T050).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) Minor to major on the same tonic as the resolve forms; firm I - IV - V - I once it holds; a rising bass.
- **Colours and devices** - *Rallying*, *horn call* with octave whoop, `timpani` on the beats, *Fanfare*, *Theme Music Power-Up*. A planned assault: bass alone, then layers, then drums and full brass, hitting hard [Summers 136] conv. A warrior woman scored without gender codes (TLTT 666-77).
- **With / against** - Courage exists against fear: set out the fear, then the steadied form. With cowardice: the brave version comes late and small (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Colour: a horn doubling one phrase. Whole: a rally build.
- **Not** - Starting loud: the change is the point. Janissary tune markers (a period caricature, R12): borrow only the steady downbeat stroke [Mayes 221-23] top / (inf.). A brave heroine with no driving theme comes across weaker [Tagg 676-77] conv.

#### Heroism
- **Cues** - GEMS power (heroic, triumphant, strong); "triumphant/heroic" is cross-cultural (COW [M]); large rising intervals (§1 [M]); firm regular rhythm. Mode: film heroes are often minor or modal; what marks them is diatonic or modal clarity and the heroic cadence, against the villain's centred chromaticism [Lehman 47, 131, 181, 200] conv (correction to "major key"). Tagg's Kojak horn code: unison horns, forte, high; an octave whoop; triplet fanfares spanning a minor seventh; steady legato dotting for calm confidence [T]; the whoop now means heroism to listeners with no thought of hunting [Tagg 271-75] top. Hatten's heroic mode: victorious (major marches, fanfares), tragic fast (tempesta rockets, vii°7), tragic slow (funeral march, lament bass) [Hatten 523] top. A hero needs a lead line: figure against ground [Tagg 657-58; TLTT 669] [T]. Music sides with the warrior's myth [Monelle 158-59, 181] top.
- **Melody** - Heroic style: triadic fanfare arpeggios, dotted rhythms, march metre, the rocket (T084). Short-short-short-long stamps a motif heroic [Dick. 135] top. Williams template: triplet upbeat into a rising fifth, then a falling figure, regular four-bar phrases [Stephen 69-70] conv. Swashbuckler contour: tonic, down to low dominant, back, up through the high dominant to the upper tonic [Summers 148-49] top. Mythic hero: a falling fifth in dotted compound duple, the "Bruckner rhythm" [Winters 119-22] conv; rising fifths in heavy unison, no third [Tagg 624-25] conv. Heroic consequent: up and up again, each arrival a new peak, mostly leaps, off-beat snaps, short motto repeated at once, heading to a long note [Tagg 375-85] conv / (inf.). Dorian for a folk hero [Lehman 181] conv.
- **Harmony** - Heroic cadences bVI - IV - I and bIII - IV - I; minor hero bVI - bVII - i, Picardy bVI - bVII - I for the victory [Lehman 199-200] top. Minor hero cell i - bVI [Lehman 131] conv. Save the true V - I for the decisive moment; bII - V - I as a solo jumps to tutti [Lehman 23, 181, 199] conv. Underdog: bVI - bVII - i lifted to major as the hero acts [Stephen 74] conv. Superhuman extremity: major I - #IV loud and sustained [Murphy 155-57] conv. Root-position I and V "powerful major" [Dick. 118] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Horn call*, *Fanfare*, *Triumphant Reprise*; `horns`+`violins` in octaves; fanfares spread through the orchestra, `trumpets`+`timpani` [Dick. 118] top. Timbre sets the mode of presence, not the topic: real `horns` for heroism happening now; the same shape on strings or clarinets for heroism remembered or lost [Rumph 497-98; Agawu 490] top/conv; a non-sustaining lead (`harp` left ringing, `pizzicato`) makes it private, `horns` a 4 with brass organ chords public and landscape-sized [Tagg 376-78] [T] (corrections; the figure must survive the swap [Monelle 98, 172-73] top). Steigerung waves, last section a tone higher [Winters 120-22] conv.
- **With / against** - Reinforced by courage, loyalty, freedom. Heroic + pastoral = inner victory (R11). Heroic + lament = noble sacrifice [Caplin 446-47] top; rocket answered by sigh [Hatten 523-24] top. Can be dropped into any style for a victorious turn [Dick. 118] top. Over the villain: his area recoloured mixolydian [Lehman 58] conv. With grief: *Sad Battle Music*.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a rising horn fourth or fifth, or the four-note rhythm. Strong: the motif as a full fanfare. Whole: the theme growing to its climax. A campaign theme is stated in full at the opening and reprised at the finale; withhold its full tutti apotheosis, not the theme [Summers 16-19, 170] conv (correction to R13); within one arc, bring it in as the hero gains the upper hand [Brill 35] conv.
- **Not** - The full apotheosis in every scene. Quoting a famous heroic theme: write in the style [Summers 148-49] top. Gendered defaults [Halfyard & Hancock 178] [T]; [Tagg 676-77] (inf.). Rustic plucked repeated-note leads (hillbilly) or horns a 4 for a laconic wanderer [Tagg 376] (inf.). A literal army call [Monelle 165-66] top. The romance stack (strength about zero) [Tagg 158-66] [T].
- **Tell apart** - From *Nobility*: see there (heroism strives, nobility stands). Listeners hear heroic brass as noble too (host pairwise test, 2026-10), so a heroic piece needs drive and struggle, not just brass.

#### Cowardice
- **Cues** - The fear profile played for comedy: quiet staccato, fast, sudden swings, high register (§6 r7); phrases that run away from their peaks (inf.). Tagg files fear with weakness and failure (VVA 126: weak, hesitant, petty) [T, by grouping], so small, hesitant, falling music. Comic colours in Tagg's substitutions: piccolo (cheeky), trombone slides (Kojak). Tremolo as knocking knees and chattering teeth [McClelland 284] top.
- **Melody** - The tiptoe march: triadic tune, fanfare tag and wind tattoos, but pp and very staccato, heroism shrunk to comedy [Hunter 73-75] top. The boaster who fled up a tree: the seria elevated march parodied, long notes over busy accompaniment, dotted, over a drum bass [Hunter 84] top. The terrified dupe: clumsy, frantic, loud off-beat ornaments [Hunter 66] top. A bumbling fool: a repeated note in short-short-long (SW 28). Fast 2/4, high, scurrying runs after one brave phrase (inf.).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) Bright major I - V that loses its nerve: a fanfare cadence breaking off on V, a sudden flat chord (bVI) at the scare, diminished chords for the flinch.
- **Colours and devices** - Staccato `bassoons` and `clarinets`; `pizzicato` tiptoe (*Sneaking*); a fanfare that breaks off; *Deflation* (a `trombones` `slide`); *Mickey Mousing* for flinches; `pizzicato` oom-pah and odd wind pairings (SW 28). Braggart's march in `trombones` over repeated-note `strings` answered by a `bassoons` echo; the tiptoe version on `piccolo`+`pizzicato` (inf.). Trembling `tremolo` for shaking knees.
- **With / against** - With pride: the braggart, a *Showman's* pickup then deflation (see **Pride**). With luck: the lucky fool. With sympathy: a *Rallying* that half succeeds. With courage: the brave version late and small.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a scurrying run after one brave phrase. Whole: a tiptoe piece.
- **Not** - Real terror: the joke is the gap between danger and reaction.

#### Luck and fortune
- **Cues** - No research or topic addresses luck. (inf.) Pleasant surprise: an unexpected harmony that turns bright, a deceptive cadence landing on a major chord. Partial support: the comic mode's swerves (HAT23) and the chromatically modulating cadence that lands in a distant key, surprise and fulfilment at once (T156, LEH13 top.). "Surprise" was too unreliable to keep in a film-music rating study (EV, alpha .66), so let the story frame luck (R7).
- **Melody** - A rising fourth for a windfall (Mario's coin), an ascending sequence for a stroke of power-up luck [Summers 193] conv. (inf.) Quick, light, high, major, a short figure ending upward; for the wheel of fortune a spinning 3/4 at a quick waltz.
- **Harmony** - Deceptive V - bVI or V - IV swerving bright; the chromatic modulating cadence (T156); a key change up a semitone (*Happy Battle Music*).
- **Colours and devices** - `glockenspiel` and triangle sparkle (conv.), a `harp` run, a comic *Sting*. A short stinger on `glockenspiel` or `celesta` laid over the bed and tuned to its current harmony, so it fits at any moment [Summers 23, 193] conv, parts (inf.). A spinning waltz for the wheel (inf.).
- **With / against** - With cowardice or mischief: the lucky fool. Against fate: chance against the inevitable ostinato.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one sparkle on the rescue chord. Strong: a running gag of near-miss cadences that swerve bright.
- **Not** - Fate: inevitability, not chance. Treating the surprise itself as the meaning (R7).

#### Determination and resolve
- **Cues** - Tagg's determination field (deliberate, definite goal, confident look; destiny beside it) over-scored for the slow minor jazz tune with heavy anacrusis and accelerando urgency (VVA 104, 1040 [T]); steady legato dotted figures = calm confidence (Kojak [T]). Steadiness first (*Rallying*). Gutsy, earnest (AMM). The processional family is serious, heroic and resolute; the overture has "steady fire" [Haringer 201-3] top. Repeated ii - V - I spirals round the circle of fifths gave the strongest sense of direction of ten tunes ("definite goal") [Tagg 215, 269] [T].
- **Melody** - Heavy anacrusis spelled out: bass pedal crotchets with a rising chromatic run on beat 4 into each downbeat, so every downbeat is a goal (approaching, decided) [Tagg 590-95] [T]; lightened (oboe on top, major) it is purposeful travel (conv.). A defiantly resolute lyrical minor theme [Summers 163] conv. Stern resolve: a rising stepwise minor line in pseudo-Baroque trumpets (SW 47); a stalwart's simple tune of ambiguous mode (SW 49). Fanfare for a heroic decision [Mirka Intro 40] top; an even, dotted, emphatic march [Mirka Intro 22-23; Haringer 200-201] top. Heavy metre at a quick tempo [Guymer 581-83; Mirka T&M 358-61] conv. Regular sequences make a dreamy line purposeful [Tagg 268-69] top.
- **Harmony** - One key held relentlessly = resolve or inescapability [Lehman 41] conv. Restating a semitone or tone higher = strain and effort [Lehman 54, 62-64] top. A iiø7 launching a secure circle of fifths is not woeful: direction outranks chord type [Tagg 191] top.
- **Colours and devices** - Fanfare + learned counterpoint = authoritative resolve (R11; HAT23). Resolve in motion: hunting fanfare + learned stretto + brilliant style + gigue [Hatten 523-29] top. Military percussion (`kit` snare, `toms`) under the minor theme [Summers 163] conv. An unvarying walking or marching bass.
- **With / against** - Courage after the decision. With adversity: the line climbing against a falling bass. With obsession: it narrows. With grief: a vow of vengeance. Resolve can be the enemy's: the approach to their stronghold [Summers 163] conv. A decision made in dread: a wide falling blues-like phrase after it [Tagg 595-97] conv.
- **Intensity** - Colour: a heavy upbeat, or one step up into the last phrase. Whole: a stepwise minor march rising by sequence, or the theme held in one key then lifted once at the decision (inf.).
- **Not** - Triumph: resolve has not won yet.

#### Restraint and composure
- **Cues** - Tagg's balance-and-control field (controlled, balanced, cool, unruffled) (VVA 106); long breaths read controlled, short motifs stressed (MM ch. 8); dignity profile with small dynamic range (§6 r9); austere, restrained, reserved (AMM); low loudness variance (EV [M]). Singing-style delivery: steady tone, sparing vibrato, swell-and-fade on long notes, rubato in the tune over a strict bass, legato, chaste ornament [Day-O'Connell 245-48] top. Rank caps feeling at "medium" (Scheibe) [Mirka Intro 7-8] top; the learned ethos is poise against sensibility [Chapin 301] top; Sulzer's pastoral: studied nonchalance [Haringer 207-8] top. Restraint can be one surge that recedes at once: the only tune to score controlled emotion (4.9‰ vs 0.5) had one mf-f-mf swell and sighs throughout [Tagg 169, 224, 270] [T].
- **Melody** - Restrained leaps, room to breathe, no impetuous rhythm, narrow range [Day-O'Connell 242-45] top. English containment: chains of on-beat sighing sevenths and sixths in baritone register, many leaps, each surge capped as it rises (*Nimrod*) [Tagg 221-24] top. Understated simple triadic figures, no busy runs [Tagg 375] conv. Moderate 3/4 or 4/4, about 60-72 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Mid-register legato 7sus4, add6, ii7, vi7, maj7, sus9; one minor-subdominant crisis chord (iv6 = iiø7) near the end [Tagg 221-24] top.
- **Colours and devices** - English string layout: conjunct legato bass, tenor-range parallel sixths, `cellos` a fifth below the tune, `basses` a tenth below the violas [Tagg 195] top; or soft low `organ`, or `cellos` and `horns` on the tune over strings [Tagg 223] top / conv. Models: Elgar, Parry, Coates [Tagg 221-24] top. A non-sustaining lead (`harp`, `pizzicato`) turns even a fanfare laconic [Tagg 376] conv. Guarded inner life: a short `celesta` cell (SW 46). Austere hermit: a glum minor sarabande, a falling six-note horn scale (SW 55).
- **With / against** - Restraint is a lid: layer it over the concept it holds down (grief, rage, love) and let that leak once (R4 by moment). With cunning: the poker face. With nobility: the stoic lord ("upper-class, controlled emotions") [Tagg 224] [T]. Composure failing: a prim minuet sliding into contredanse [Hunter 87] top. Restrained pathos: noble simplicity without introduction, like a soliloquy [Head 271-72] top.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one phrase stopping before its peak. Strong: one swell that recedes. Whole: one dynamic and texture held to the last bar.
- **Not** - Coldness: feeling held in, not absent. Wide unbridled crescendos; overdone pomp (parody) [Tagg 223] top.

#### Passion and turmoil
- **Cues** - Hevner's exciting cluster (passionate, impetuous, restless) won on fast tempo and complex harmony, with low register and falling lines (HEV cl.7 [M]). Appassionato/agitato: the fast tragic mode for an inner storm, agitated repeated chords or broken octaves, surging dynamics, rising sequences (T033, HAT23; GRAB top.); its core is an octave-doubled opera-derived tune over throbbing quaver or triplet chords, feeling held back with effort, mainly love or desire [Dick. 109] top. Passion can be slow: the andante romance drew passion at twice average from sighs, swells and ninths, and the slow legato madd9 R&J line drew ecstatic/passion 5.1x [Tagg 158-66, 434] [T]. Trembling is a general sign of violent emotion; unmeasured tremolando is inner and personal, measured reiteration external and mechanical [Tagg 471-72] conv / (inf.). Chambers: semitones, skilful discords, motion brisk then languishing [Head 266-67] top.
- **Melody** - The tune in octaves or one soaring line [Dick. 109] top; the aria's leaps, sighs and portamenti (T035). The anapest as passionate pianto (Mozart 40) [Sisman 113-15] conv. Mattheson's gigue: volatile ardour [Mirka Intro 7] top. Driving rhythm and impassioned declamation (Sturm und Drang in Ratner's sense) [Head 264] top.
- **Harmony** - Feverish rising sequences and restless key changes settling only when the passion resolves [Lehman 35-37] conv. Warm passion: unprepared dominant ninths round the circle of fifths, each keeping the last chord's third as its seventh; a ninth leapt to from below is an ecstatic outburst [Tagg 204-8] top. Minor agitato with syncopation, dissonance, imitation, suspensions [Sisman 110] top.
- **Colours and devices** - `tremolo` strings under a surging `cellos` or `violins` line; `violins`+`cellos` in octaves over repeated triplet `strings` or `horns` (conv.); one thin `tremolo` line close-up restating a known tune [Tagg 471-72] (inf.); swells; *Opera Means Drama*. One mf-f-mf swell with winds joining in the penultimate phrase [Tagg 169-70] top. Display figuration for drive while characters clash [Dick. 135-36] top.
- **With / against** - Passion surges and sighs; when it turns to frenzy (rage aria, mad scene, vengeance, flight) choose tempesta, which covers personal frenzy too; the documented split is ombra (creeping horror) against tempesta (fast terror), not personal against world [McClelland 280-87] top (correction to "passion is personal, tempesta is the world", T028/T033). "Drang" (restless yearning) belongs to sensibility [McClelland 281] top. With love: stormy love. With rivalry: the duel's inner side. With restraint: the lid that lifts. Alternate nice ninths with worried chords (iv6, vii°7, iadd9, b9), or it stifles [Tagg 207-8] top. Lament + appassionato = lugubrious (conv.). Agitato plus pianto as an indirect opening [Sisman 108-9] top.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one surging swell. Whole: an appassionato.
- **Not** - Rage: passion surges, rage hits. Minor as compulsory [Dick. 109] top. An isolated ff half-diminished chord (panic) [Tagg 192] top. The 1940s "hysteria" code for women [Lehman 247 n51] conv.

#### Reflection and contemplation
- **Cues** - Tagg's reflection field (reflective, contemplative, introvert, lyrical) over-scored for slow legato tunes: oboe pastoral, pentatonic strings, the madd9 love theme (VVA 103 [T]); soft, slow, grooveless irregular periodicity = meditative rhapsody (MM ch. 8); thinking, pondering, remembering (VVA 593). With no pulse and no goal-directed structure, time stops, which suits planning [Summers 102] conv; slow percussion-free overlapping ostinati invite careful thought [Summers 134-35] conv. *Sayonara*'s still texture (parallel non-triadic chords, wide spacing, static bass) led reflection [Tagg 521-37] [T]. The madd9 needs a slow reflective frame to read as contemplation [Tagg 458-64] [T]. Pulse-free parlando reads as timeless expanse more than funeral [Tagg 317] (inf.).
- **Melody** - Lied style: simple, unornamented, folk-like private lyricism [Dick. 106] top. Reichardt's sentimental tune: introverted, gentle, pensive [Head 268] top. A solo recitative suspends time; opening on I6 is its hallmark [Head 259-63] conv. Free improvisation on small fragments of a known tune, rubato [Summers 102] conv. The fantasia's wandering line for thought (T037), which can also be virtuosic, a display of free thought [Dick. Table 2] top.
- **Harmony** - The contrapuntal Prinner for crystalline stillness before a return [Chapin 315-18] conv. A nocturne episode on bI inside a fast piece as a pause for thought [Horton 653-56] conv. Held harmony, slow change (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - A homeland tune set pensive on `clarinets` or `flutes` (LOTR [W]). A solo line with long rests over held `strings`; a solo instrument "speaking" over held chords [Sisman 106-7] top. Solo `harp`, `celesta`, `clarinets` or `flutes` ad lib over a soft repeating `harp` figure (inf.). Legato rising minor triplet arpeggios over a tonic pedal, tune on the fifth (*Moonlight* texture) on `harp` or divisi violas (reflective 41‰, nostalgic) [Tagg 515-18] [T]. Let the theme run long over a still vista [Winters 117] conv; symphonic contemplation as worship [Winters 123-24] top.
- **With / against** - With memory: looking back. With wisdom: a sage's study. With grief: quiet mourning. With the journey: the night camp. Against urgency: the same puzzle with a timer gets drums and short fragments [Summers 134-35] conv.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one unaccompanied phrase. Whole: a slow free solo over held harmony, or the theme complete and unhurried over a vista [Winters 117] conv.
- **Not** - A beat-driven loop; drums under planning tell the table to hurry [Summers 134-35] conv. A brisk carefree march (zero for thinking) [Tagg 407, 430] [T]. The allemande as "serious thought": its affect is contented, and it was a historical dance by the 1780s; use it for calm or the remembered past [Mirka Intro 7; McKee 165-66] top (correction; its tempo and running-semiquaver traits remain [gen.]).

#### Suspicion and jealousy
- **Cues** - Jealousy cannot be stated (R7; §7.5). Tagg files guilt, jealousy, paranoia and suspicion with madness and the absurd (VVA 1274), over-scored by *Streetcar* (slow minor jazz, flat fifths, half-diminished chords, semitone slides) [T]. "Paranoid" is high energy, high stress (MRX). The madd9 in a crime style frames adversity as suspicion and threat; in a romance, as sadness [Tagg 461] [T]. The jealous princess is a stock seria type: give her composed high style with a crack in it (inf., after Hunter 68 top).
- **Melody** - A high violin line opening on b5 and falling b5 - 4 - b3 in syncopated triplets, extended b5 - 4 - b3 - 7 - #9: suspicion 8.1x, jealousy, ice-cold nerves [Tagg 580-83] [T]; no theme in about 20,000 catalogued opens on b5, so the opening is the marked sign [Tagg 583] conv. A narrow figure glancing back by semitone neighbour (inf.). Slow-moderate, minor, high register over low (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Half-diminished and minor-six chords left unresolved; a chromatic inner voice that will not resolve under the love theme (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - A motif that watches: semitone glances, a flat fifth (Tagg Tritone), low `clarinets`. High `violins` for the b5 line over a low bed. *Musical Spoiler* turned against the listener: a fragment of the rival's theme in an inner voice.
- **With / against** - With love: jealousy, the love theme invaded by a chromatic inner voice. With cunning: mutual distrust, two motifs that never align. With madness: paranoia. With the investigator: the noir lone wolf shares the b5 line.
- **Intensity** - Colour: one glance of a rival's motif. Whole: a love theme slowly invaded.
- **Not** - Anger: suspicion watches, it has not struck. Open agitation for a high-born jealousy (inf.).

#### Bitterness and cynicism
- **Cues** - Tagg files "bitter" under asperity (rough, hard, sharp; VVA 1417), irony under humour (VVA 107P); bitter, cynical, sarcastic sit low energy, high stress (MRX). Irony is a layering effect, not a sound (R4): a topic that resists its context and keeps its character [Hatten 514-15] top, and it must be audible in the delivery [Beghin 563-64] conv. The madd9's core charge: a lone adult facing adversity, "it just isn't fair" [Tagg 456-64] conv.
