- **Melody** - Minor-add-nine line: 5 up to b3, then straight to 2 [Tagg 453-54] conv; a semitone rocking between 9 and b3 over a minor chord (bitter 7x, weakly) [Tagg 521-37, 573-77] [T]. A repeated-note figure that keeps interrupting and grows into ironic comment [Hatten 527-33] top. Bugle calls mocked or soured [Monelle 178-80] conv. Low-middle, slow-moderate, minor (inf.).
- **Harmony** - iadd9 held as a chord in its own right: sad, wistful, bitter-sweet [Tagg 577-79] conv. A Picardy third undercut by a chromatic lament beneath: a major ending that does not believe itself [Hatten 527-33] top. A fanfare on the wrong chord for heroism no longer believed (R11; MCK07).
- **Colours and devices** - A sweet tune with one sour note per cadence; *Soundtrack Dissonance* in miniature; dry `pizzicato`; a wry `bassoons`. A sincere `oboe` or `strings` line with `bassoons` muttering staccato repeats under it [Hunter 79-82] top / (inf.). A recognisable aria type mocked in its own idiom [Hunter 86-87] top; a mock fugue for pedantry [Chapin 323-26] top. A terse modal lead keeps drama cool and distanced [Tagg 372] (inf.).
- **With / against** - With grief: grief hardened, the lament with harsher attacks. With humour: wry comedy. With toughness: the jaded mercenary. With injustice: see Injustice (other group).
- **Intensity** - Colour: one sour cadence. Whole: a pleasant tune undercut every phrase.
- **Not** - Rage: bitterness is cold and low. Unmarked irony [Beghin 563-64] conv.

#### Toughness and grit
- **Cues** - Tagg's asperity field (rough, hard, hard-bitten, dry) over-scored for distorted rock (VVA 141 [T]); rough grainy timbre = abrasion and power (MM ch. 13); tough themes use brass, percussion, repeated notes, syncopation (§1 Rhythm [W-M]). Toughness comes from the style (heavy brass, chromatic and key-extrinsic notes), not the chord: the same madd9 was hard only in the crime tunes [Tagg 458-62] [T]. Low tessitura and riffing, with continuous subdivision only in a quiet top layer, gives hard and mechanical [Tagg 622-23] [T].
- **Melody** - A two-bar low unison riff (`cellos`, `basses`, `bassoons`, `trombones`) opening with rising fifths, later a tutti of tenuto crotchets cut by accented off-beat stabs: hard-boiled 6.6x, tough guys 6.5x [Tagg 607-28] [T]. Moderate 4/4, minor or dorian, narrow low range, short dry notes (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Bare fifths, power-chord style (see **Power**); chromatic, key-extrinsic notes [Tagg 458-62] [T].
- **Colours and devices** - Low `trombones` and `tuba`, `cellos`+`basses` on bare fifths, hard `timpani` or `taiko`, marcato strings; dry short notes, little reverb. Rock-orchestra swagger: `brass` rips, sforzando low brass, snare and bd, low `tremolo` (inf., after Summers 91, 210). Morricone colours: a `kit` snare crack, a struck high bell, fast `piccolo` runs, low `flutes` [Tagg 370] conv. Hard men need no horse rhythm [Tagg 303-5] conv. Quiet top layer: soft ride or high `pizzicato`.
- **With / against** - With loyalty: the gruff friend. With poverty: the street. With humour: the brawler. Toughness over tenderness: the soft heart behind the hard face (R4 by layer).
- **Intensity** - Colour: one marcato low-brass hit. Whole: a hard dry ostinato.
- **Not** - Cruelty: toughness endures, it does not enjoy harm. Busy high fast figuration (comic bustle) [Tagg 494-98] [T].

#### Doubt and hesitation
*New (merges the two "Doubt and hesitation" proposals and "Indecision and moral ambiguity"). A mind that cannot commit: the fork in the road, a decision not yet made, unease with no visible threat, a character's wavering between good and evil. Suspense waits for an event; doubt is undecided inside a person.*
- **Cues** - Psychological unease, not physical threat: no semitone clashes, soft attacks [Tagg 190] top. Soft, slurred, portato delivery for inward feeling [Guymer 579-81] conv. Soft to moderate, unresolved, no committed pulse (inf., after Summers 120-21). Pauses placed after the cadential six-four; the topic decides their colour (ominous in a minor siciliano, comic in brilliant style) [Margulis 636-38] [ev].
- **Melody** - A confident theme broken into one-bar cells, each ending on a half cadence (the fanfare turned private and uncertain) [Agawu 479-80] conv. Phrases that break off before their goal, questions followed by real pauses [Beghin 552-70] conv; sensibility's sighs answering a bold figure [Hatten 523-24] top. A line that rises and stalls or turns aside at the cadence (inf.). Moral wavering: slow pitch bends on held notes that sag and return (`slide` and back, slow `wobble`) [Summers 121] conv. Moderate-slow, mid register (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The half-diminished iiø7 (= iv6) as a pivot: one step from almost any key, it hints at a modulation that may not come; Romantic "where now?" worry (K364's slow movement over a falling bass; the Tristan chord) [Tagg 187-90] top; woe in 1600-1750, indecision after [Tagg 190, 203] top; a pivot aiming at a key that never arrives [Tagg 194] top. Half cadences for full closes; the lament placed at a theme's start [Caplin 419-21] top / (inf.). A held major third over a low pedal that bends as it decays, neither good's consonance nor evil's abrasion; a third wavering major/minor [Summers 120-21] conv / (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Solo `clarinets`, `oboe` or `cellos` with the accompaniment dropping away under its questions (inf.); mid `strings` holding the iiø7 on the beat (inf.). `strings` or `solo_violin` portamento, a small `unhinge` drift on one part (inf.). Dubitatio: doubt before a cadence that then lands harder [Beghin 552-70] conv.
- **With / against** - With heroism: the hero's march in half-cadence cells = private doubt (inf., after Agawu). With determination: doubt first, then the full statement (R13); or the pivot turned into a secure circle of fifths [Tagg 191] top. With love: one minor iv6 before the happy moment (*Otello*) [Tagg 194-95] top. With longing: the pivot that never lands [Tagg 187-90] top. With guilt: the cells over a lament bass (inf.). Between **Holiness** and **Corruption** on one shared frame: the bends drifting toward either overlay track a character's moral drift [Summers 120-21] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one iiø7 at 60-80% of the phrase before the normal cadence, or one bending note [Tagg 187-92] top. Strong: a theme heard only in half-cadence fragments, or a pivot that truly modulates. Whole: the theme passed through keys by half-diminished pivots, ending on a full cadence (resolved) or on V (not) [Tagg 187-92] top / (inf.).
- **Not** - Fear or madness: no tremolo, no unhinged voice breaking; keep bends slow and returning (inf.). An isolated loud fast half-diminished (panic) or a semitone grind (horror) [Tagg 190, 192] top. Pivots in predictable slots that always resolve home: lip service [Tagg 204] [T]. Dreams (everything soft and drifting) (inf.).

#### Duality and the divided self
*New (merges two proposals). Two natures in one: the paladin with a dark past, the lycanthrope, the noble with a common side, the tiefling torn between blood and choice. Not Madness (a mind breaking), not Cunning (a hidden surface), not Corruption (a one-way change).*
- **Cues** - Agitated gestures and disruptive octaves at the seam between the two sides announce the conflict [Dick. 133] top. Isolate the pair: long chords, plain texture, oscillation, so it is heard as an absolute progression [Lehman 75-76] top.
- **Melody** - One cell, two topics: give both sides the same intervals and contour and change only the topic; then it is one person's other side (Brahms) [Dick. 134] top. The second side may keep its topic's grace notes and limping rhythms while the first stays plain [Dick. 134, 136] top. Or keep the tune and flip only its third (3 - b3) on a held root (inf., after Lehman 90-94). A third facet from the same cell: the heroic short-short-short-long [Dick. 135] top.
- **Harmony** - Parallel oscillation I - i = duality, a split personality (a general's mask slipping over D - Dm) [Lehman 100] conv; mode oscillation repeats an affect change [Lehman 94] conv. Ambivalence rather than split: the slide I - #i [Lehman 104] top. A cell returning with modes swapped is still recognised, so both faces can share one leitharmony [Lehman 149] conv. The other side in a remote related key (biii from a minor tonic) [Dick. 134] top.
- **Colours and devices** - Each side its own timbre: lyric side on `cellos`, the other on `solo_violin`; or major face on `horns`, minor face on low `clarinets` and `trombones` (inf.). A narrator's frame (bare octaves + `harp`) round a self-told story [Dick. 133-36] top.
- **With / against** - Integration: elevate one side by fugato [Dick. 135] top. Unresolved: one side returns at peace, the other distorted, ornaments stripped, among tempest figures [Dick. 136] top. With corruption: let the oscillation stop on i; with redemption, on I (inf.). With cunning: the honest and the hidden face (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: the motif heard once in a second topic, or one parallel-minor chord under its last note. Strong: the two topics, or I and i, alternating at the cadence. Whole: a ballad where both sides converse and only one resolves (inf.).
- **Not** - Two different tunes: the table hears two people [Dick. 134] top. A one-way swap (that is corruption; duality needs the return) (inf.). An ethnic topic as the "dark side": invent the second idiom (R12) (inf.).

#### The brilliant mind (genius, invention, intense thought)
*New. The inventor, the artificer, the savant, the wizard at research. Not Wisdom (age, tradition), not Madness.*
- **Cues** - Symmetric cycles read as meticulous order when phrase rhythm, dynamics and scoring are regular [Lehman 156] conv. Since about 2000, unprepared nonfunctional chromaticism, once film's madness code, also stands for genius and intense thought (*A Beautiful Mind*, *The Martian*) [Lehman 71-72, 251 n40] conv.
- **Melody** - One note held in the tune while the chords around it shift by slide and third: a thought turning [Lehman 153-55] conv. A short phrase or arpeggio repeated as a module, nonfunctional beneath [Lehman 133] conv. Moderate-quick, steady pulse, mid-high register, regular phrase lengths (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Nonfunctional chromatic chains under the held note [Lehman 71-72] conv. Calculation against the clock: a pulsing ostinato under a minor-third cycle [Lehman 162] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - `celesta`, `harp` and `pizzicato` on the module, `strings` holding the shared note (inf.).
- **With / against** - With madness: the same idiom; regular phrasing keeps it genius, irregular phrasing and an odd sliding timbre tip it over [Lehman 71, 156] (inf.). With the arcane: a wizard at research (inf.). With wisdom: the learned style for tradition, this for invention (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one slide under a held note. Whole: a ticking module under a slowly changing chromatic chain (inf.).
- **Not** - A gliding, wavering voice (choir or violin slides), which marks psychosis [Lehman 71] (inf.).

#### The investigator (detective, sleuth, inquisitor)
*New. The one who works out what others hide: the rogue on a case, a watch inquisitor, a hired finder. Not Mystery (the unknown itself), not Secrecy (hiding). Detective was among Tagg's most frequent responses (4.3x *Sportsnight*, 2.2x *Streetcar*, over 15% *Owed to 'g'*) [Tagg 477-82, 539-50, 607-17] [T].*
- **Cues** - A distinct lead line is essential: with no melody, listeners imagined no individual [Tagg 657-58] [T]. The milieu is urban and nocturnal: minor, chromatic, tritones [Tagg 580-83] conv. Tempo: about 116-126 for the brisk procedural, about 58 for brooding noir [Tagg 476, 541, 633] [T].
- **Melody** - Noir lone wolf: a high violin line opening on b5 and falling b5 - 4 - b3 in syncopated triplets (private eye, ice-cold nerves) [Tagg 580-83] [T]. Tough modern detective: a two-bar low unison riff rising to the 9th, through b3, down to #6 [Tagg 625-26] [T]. A lead leaning on the 9th [Tagg 626-27] conv.
- **Harmony** - im6(add9) bed, the minor-add-nine family [Tagg 626-27] conv. Hero-detective: bVI7 - V7 under a melodic b5 - 4 [Tagg 629-30] conv; plain bVI7 - V7 for a low-rent gumshoe (inf.). Ending on i(maj9).
- **Colours and devices** - Noir: high `violins` over soft mid-low brass minor-six chords and a `pizzicato` pulse. Procedural: `trumpets` and staccato `piccolo` over `pizzicato` for a cheery sleuth. Genteel cosy mystery: quasi-classical filigree in `harp` with `pizzicato` [Tagg 476, 495, 580-83] conv / (inf.).
- **With / against** - With **Mystery**: the case. With the city: the beat. With crime: the opponent. With loneliness: one high line over a low bed [Tagg 580-83] conv. With **Secrecy**: the spy riff and chords are close kin. Give a woman investigator the same driving theme (*Black Widow*'s heroine lacked one) [Tagg 676-77] (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one b5 in a melody, or a held im6(add9). Strong: the low riff or the b5 line as the character's motif. Whole: a noir cue with plodding pulse, minor-six brass, b5 violins, a chase episode, ending on i(maj9).
- **Not** - Major, high, busy figures for a hard-boiled detective (comic walking and briefcases); flattened thirds put the furrow in the brow [Tagg 494-95] (inf.). Swung flat-fifth jazz played straight (parody) [Tagg 583-88] conv.

### D. Bonds and morality

#### Love
- **Cues** - Moderate-slow, legato, steady mf with one swell; higher and slower than other songs (§8.3 [W-M]); the GEMS tenderness factor (§7.2). Tagg's romance stack: mid-register string pad in long notes, low legato arpeggio waves, a walking tune on top, hall reverb, no percussion or staccato: love 4x, romance 5x the average (VVA 111/1112; TLTT 168–69, 249–52 [T]). It needs a private space: a public, competitive bustle drew no love (TLTT 498–501 [T]).
- **Melody** - Open with an on-beat legato fall of a major sixth, then a stepwise rise (the sigh; TLTT 217–18 top./[T]); later leap above the octave to degree 6 and reach the goal stepwise from above (TLTT 225–27 top.). Wide range, leaps up as readily as down (TLTT 217, 229–30 top.). ♩≈92, 4/4, poco rubato, 4+4+4+4 bars, the second phrase a step up in ii (TLTT 168–69, 180 top.). Courtship: the gavotte, 2/2 with a half-bar pickup of two repeated notes (Mirka-b 366–73 top.); languid love: the siciliana (Mirka 40 top.); young love: a plain major folk duet (Monelle 259–61 conv.). Lovers' themes share intervals (Summers 170–74 conv.).
- **Harmony** - I – ii7 – I6, then iiø7 – V7 – i of ii, dominant ninths round to bVI → V6/4 on the swell, an inner voice sliding down by semitones into the climax (TLTT 180, 209–14 top.); iiø7 in regular slots keeps worry muted ([T]). Korngold's "oases": remote major VI, bII, II, each lusher, then home (Lehman 24–26 conv.). First kiss: 4–5–b6–b7–1 over bVII – v – bII – bvii – I (Lehman 70 conv.). All-diatonic = innocent, old-world love (TLTT 432–33 [T]).
- **Colours and devices** - `harp` waves (six a beat, a tenth every two beats) or divided `cellos` in 3+3 for a darker swell; `violins` octave-doubled only at peaks (TLTT 168–72 top.; parts conv.). Nocturne: tune over tenor-register arpeggios, a second line a sixth below (Dick. 108 top.). Waltz for the couple in a crowd (T066; McKee 176–79 top.). Night garden: `horns` with `cellos` (Monelle 104 conv.).
- **With / against** - A duet: an answering voice, or antecedent and consequent split between two (VVA 222 [T]; Horton 653–56 conv.). Desire: the appassionato, octave tune over throbbing triplets (Dick. 109 top.). With doom see **Tragic love**; keep the love tutti distinct from a vista's (TLTT 394 inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one sighing sixth or one remote-major oasis (inf.). Colour: a woodwind fragment, completed in lush `strings` when love is returned (Brill 35 conv.). Whole: the full stack; strip pad and waves and it goes plain (TLTT 205 top.).
- **Not** - Percussion, staccato, fast surface (Virginian −46 on love, TLTT 288–89 [T]). Wall-to-wall sweetness (TLTT 207–08 top.). Slow, conjunct, static, droned = parental love ([T]). Corrected: only an unanswered new solo line is longing; a close solo restating a known love theme strengthens love with a tinge of separation (TLTT 471–73 [T]). Exotic flat-2 or "fallen woman" chromatic codes as verdicts (Brill 35; H&H 177 conv.).

#### Tenderness
- **Cues** - Slow, soft, legato, gentle attacks, soft timbre (§6 r5; §1 [S]); low loudness variance, clear key, slightly less major, darker spectrum (Eerola 2009 [M]); slow (−.64), soft, regular phrases in game music (HM [W]); Hevner's dreamy-tender: slow, minor, flowing (HEV cl.3 [M]). Gentle characters are played markedly softer (Türk via Guymer 579–81 conv.). Intimacy is staging: close, dry, few voices; mix the solo up and dry (MM 500; TLTT 472 inf.).
- **Melody** - The singing style: moderate tempo, long notes, narrow range, stepwise, *messa di voce* on held notes (Dick. 105; Day-O'Connell 238–48 top.). Short phrases, conjunct, no big leaps or sweeps: tender-innocent, not romantic (TLTT 250 top.). Slurred close intervals (Quantz via Guymer 584 conv.). The siciliana: 12/8, dotted, larghetto to poco andante, almost no trills, often minor (Monelle 219–20; Haringer 204–06 top.). Lullaby: quavers rocking across the span of a romance arpeggio, much slower surface (TLTT 250 top.).
- **Harmony** - Slow, plain diatonic I – IV – V – I (inf.). Inside a sad tale, the turn from minor to a tender major, i → I or i → III, for a memory or a love (Dick. 126 top.; numerals inf.). A soft iadd9 also reads tender: R&J alone scored "soft" (14‰ vs 0; TLTT 459 [T]).
- **Colours and devices** - Soft `strings`, `cellos`, low `flutes`, `harp`, `celesta` for the childlike; `solo_violin` or `oboe` swelling over soft `pizzicato` (inf.). Lullaby on `oboe` + `english_horn` in repeated chords (Monelle 233–34 conv.); a `flutes` cantabile over `harp`, stated twice in new scoring (Summers 164–68 conv.). The musette's caressing, coaxing tune (Koch via Haringer 205 top.). *Lullaby*, *Nostalgic music box*, the Lied (T097).
- **With / against** - With love and innocence (romance stack: tender 6.6‰ vs 1.0 [T]). Against power, tenderness takes the tune and power stays in the bass (inf.). With grief, a cradle song for the dead. A gentle theme for a man is legitimate; frame it as his (TLTT 676–77 inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one soft legato answer. Whole: a lullaby.
- **Not** - Brass, drums, sharp attacks, big dynamic changes. Leaps, sweeps and modulation, which make it romance (TLTT 250). The default for a woman (Summers 164 conv.).

#### Loyalty and honour
- **Cues** - Not studied (inf.): constancy as a theme that returns unchanged (§4 [M]), a regular metre, two instruments always together. Unison and fixed doublings as a bond (MM 514). Trust sits with wisdom, honour with pride and nobility (VVA 1174, 1165). Drones heard as "the constancy of shepherds" (Monelle 210–14 top.). Chivalric honour: lineage, glory, fame, perseverance; Roland as true comrade (Monelle 142–43, 153 top.). The faithful confidant speaks the hero's own high style (Hunter 68 top.).
- **Melody** - (inf.) Plain diatonic tune in square four-bar phrases, 4/4 at a walking-hymn ♩≈80–96, mostly stepwise with one firm rising fourth, closing on 1; a small fixed tune that every set piece quotes unchanged (Mirka 20–21 inf.).
- **Harmony** - Plain perfect cadences V – I; a held I5 (open fifth) under the loyal character's tune (Monelle inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - *The sidekick's answer* (the reply always comes), *National Anthem*, *horn call*, *Funeral hymn* for oaths at graves. A fellowship theme on `horns` that grows as members join and fragments as they part (LOTR [W], conv.). See **Oath and vow** and **Friendship and fellowship**.
- **With / against** - Against betrayal the answer stops (*Un-Duet*). Honour against love is the classic heroic conflict (Sisman 98–99 top.): *Themes in conflict*.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a doubling that never leaves the tune. Whole: an anthem.
- **Not** - Blind obedience, which is order.

#### Betrayal
- **Cues** - A surprise in place of a strongly expected event feels willed (§4 [W-M]); a deceptive cadence (§6 r20); mismatched foreshadowing is remembered (§6 r33 [M]). Suspicion sits with guilt and madness (VVA 127). It works only by moment: trust heard, then the turn.
- **Melody** - (inf.) The trusted theme stated whole, then at the repeat its last phrase bends to b6 or b2 instead of stepping home. Begin a theme on the lament bass and the opening itself feels unstable (Caplin 419–21 top.; as betrayal inf.). The sycophant: sluggish low chromatic lines in reeds and strings under high clusters (LOTR Gríma [W], conv.).
- **Harmony** - Corrected (Lehman 54, 200–02 top.): use the deceptive V → vi (falling back) or turn the swerve chord minor, V → bvi; a swerve to a major bVI or bIII that stays reads as wonder. A fanfare harmonised on the Neapolitan makes the trusted heroism hollow (Agawu via MCK07; HAT23 top.). A second break that turns major into its own minor hits harder (Agawu K. 614 484 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - *The missing answer*, *Corruption* of the trusted theme, *Last Note Nightmare*, *Shock* at the reveal; tempesta breaking in through the deceptive cadence (Agawu 484 conv.).
- **With / against** - Needs a bond heard first (loyalty, love, fellowship); grief or rage follow.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one cadence falling to vi or bvi. Whole: the duet with the answer missing, then corrupted.
- **Not** - A villain theme from the start. A major-chord swerve held as an arrival (wonder, not treachery).

#### Cruelty
- **Cues** - Tough or cruel themes: brass, percussion, repeated notes, syncopation, sharp attacks (§1 [W-M]); "cruel" over-scored for the synth-and-Latin-percussion tune (VVA 1216 [T]). Mechanical regularity against chaotic images disturbs more (MM 510): indifference. Fast, glossy action turns cruelty into spectacle: *Miami Vice* led cruel (about 4.0x) with no one naming pain or guilt (TLTT 646–47 [T]).
- **Melody** - Implacable cruelty: a rising, wide-leaping tutti unison in tenuto crotchets, a held note, then accented off-beat stabs; it drew cruel, implacable and inhuman (TLTT 628 [T]). Cold cruelty: a sweet minuet or music-box tune in 3/4, played straight and in time (T057, T106).
- **Harmony** - Cold: a plain I – IV – V – I surface over a low drone that does not belong (R4; inf.). Hot: i with a stab chord (bII or vii°7) where a kind chord was due (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Soundtrack Dissonance*, *Façade*, *Creepy jazz*, *The villain conducts*, soft `trumpets` stabs; the implacable stabs in low `brass`, `timpani`, kit bd (TLTT 628). A cheerful song over violence becomes the perpetrator's insolence (Stephen 66–67 conv.). Music never showed the period's butchering hunts, so a bright `trumpets`-and-`timpani` victory flourish over slaughter is a deliberate dysphoric choice (Monelle 59–61 top.; use inf.).
- **With / against** - Cold cruelty is a sweet surface over a low drone (R4); hot cruelty takes the rage cues; brute cruelty is **Savagery and brutality**.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one stab where a kind chord should be. Whole: *Soundtrack Dissonance*.
- **Not** - Bending the music toward the horror; the indifference is the cruelty. Sources conflict on which test tune "wins" cruel: Tagg's footnote names *Owed to 'g'*, his tables give *Miami Vice* (4.0x vs 2.9x) and *Owed to 'g'* the lead on implacable; the tables are stronger (TLTT 628, 646–47 [T]).

#### Mercy and forgiveness
- **Cues** - A full V – I or a closing major chord made viewers assume reconciliation (§5 [M]; §6 r34). Resolving suspensions; dark to light (§7.6 r4); softer dynamics raise valence (§1). Consolation and reconciliation sit with thinking and remembering (VVA 5938); "compassionate" is a listener category (COW). Kind gods calm the storms angry gods raise: the celestial answers tempesta (McCl. 280–87 top.).
- **Melody** - *Correctio*: a soft horn-call figure in pure thirds answering an outburst and retracting it (Beghin 559–64 conv.; as forgiveness inf.). (inf.) A slow falling stepwise major line, 3/4 or 4/4 at ♩≈60, ending on a long 3.
- **Harmony** - Plagal IV – I; the mixed plagal iv – I for forgiveness touched by loss (T157). The storm subsiding onto a Picardy third, i → I (McCl. 289–90 top.). For comfort given, see **Consolation and comfort**.
- **Colours and devices** - *Sentimental Music Cue*, entering just after the words that turn the scene; *Cherubic Choir*. The celestial: `flutes` and `harp`, lilting thirds (McCl. 287 top.).
- **With / against** - Against judgement: stern low brass answered by a soft high line; strict learned counterpoint released into warm homophony (Chapin 311 top.). Wrath then grace: the Furies followed at once by the Blessed Spirits (McCl. 287 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a major chord where minor was expected (inf.). Whole: the sentimental cue.
- **Not** - Entering before the turn.

#### Vengeance
- **Cues** - The anger profile, aimed: loud, sharp, fast, dense (§6 r6 [S]), small pitch range, repeated notes (§1); anger in game music tracks loudness (.72) and interval size (HM [W]); revenge sits with rage and the implacable (VVA 1212–13). A grand surface on a base motive: high style for Scheibe, morally low for Mattheson (Mirka 3–7 top.).
- **Melody** - The vengeance aria is tempesta: disjunct, very wide and augmented leaps, rapid scales, repeated notes, fast 4/4 (McCl. 282–87 top.). Or the victim's own lyrical theme, unchanged (Summers 160 conv.).
- **Harmony** - D or C minor; vii°7 and augmented sixths over a V pedal (McCl. 282 top.). A chaconne ground for a vow that will not let go (T056).
- **Colours and devices** - *Power*, `taiko`, *Bell ostinato*; the victim's tune minor and low (*Corruption*). The drum as avenger (Schubart's myth of a drum skinned from a dead enemy, Haringer 197 top.): a `timpani` or `taiko` ostinato under the vow (inf.).
- **With / against** - Grief plus rage plus obsession. Confirmed: when a loved character dies just before a fight, keep their theme through the fight instead of the battle cue (Summers 160 conv.); layer it high and slow over a driving low ostinato (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: the victim's motif in low `brass`. Whole: the battle scored entirely from the victim's theme (Summers 160 conv.), or the ostinato march.
- **Not** - Chaos: vengeance has a target. The routine battle cue for that fight (Summers 160).

#### Guilt
- **Cues** - Not directly expressible (§7.5 [W]); give its motion: a theme circling back to one wound (§1), the sadness cues (§6 r4), falling minor seconds (§7.1 [W]). Guilt sits in the madness field, over-scored for slow minor jazz with flat fifths and semitone glides (VVA 1274 [T]): a mind turned on itself. Repentance is plain, low-style church affect, not grand (Mirka 3–7 top.).
- **Melody** - 1 – b3 – 5 – b6 – 5 (Parsifal's plea; TLTT 189–90 top.). Remorseful weeping: pathetic accents, sobbing rubato (Head 273–74 top.); sensibility's sighs, broken phrases, sudden pauses (T036). A horn line made only of falling semitones (Britten, Monelle 108 conv.). Slow, ♩≈50–60, minor (inf.).
- **Harmony** - That formula over a half-diminished chord (e.g. iiø7) with no semitone clash in the voicing (TLTT 190 top.). A bare fifth I5 as the only harmony, so the falling semitones grind against it (Monelle 108 conv.). Chromatic harmony under weeping (Head 273–74 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Lament*, solo `cellos`, the wronged person's motif intruding (*Musical Spoiler*; §7.6 r5); a solo `horns` turned dark and inward over `tremolo` on the fifth (T002, Monelle 108); a semitone `slide` in an inner voice.
- **With / against** - With nobility (a lord with a past), *Façade* and *Lament* at their moments. With madness, the motif that keeps returning.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one bar of the victim's motif. Whole: a lament.
- **Not** - Menace: guilt points inward. A semitone grind and sharp attacks bring in the body and the knife, physical horror rather than conscience (TLTT 190 top.).

#### Greed
- **Cues** - No research (inf.): accumulation, a figure that adds a note on each repeat, chromatic creeping upward. The miser sits with weakness, fear and pettiness, not power (VVA 1268 [T, by grouping]): small, hesitant, mean music. Treasure has no musical correlate (VVA 2671).
- **Melody** - (inf.) Narrow range (a fourth), repeated notes and chromatic neighbours, 2/4 at ♩≈100 tightening into an accelerando; the motif grows a note each time. The buffo miser: patter on repeated notes in a narrow range, speeding up (Hunter 79–83; parts inf.).
- **Harmony** - (inf.) A static i over a held root while an inner voice creeps 5 – #5 – 6 – #6; comic avarice on an oom-pah I – V.
- **Colours and devices** - `glockenspiel` glints for gold; a hoard in the invented metal texture of **Wealth and luxury** (Brill 34 conv.). *Creepy jazz* for a crime lord; `tuba` oom-pah and *Mickey Mousing* for comic avarice; a pompous crime lord on solo `tuba` with tumbling arpeggios and a lurching dance (SW 18, conv.). A treasure swindle: a merry gigue for the hunt for gold, frantic off-beat ornaments for the dupe, then "spirits" between ombra and tempesta (Hunter 66 top.). Miser's patter in `bassoons` or `tuba` over a repetitive `strings` figure (inf.).
- **With / against** - With cunning, the swindler; with wealth, the miser (a small figure in a big room); with the monster, the dragon on its hoard.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a coin-glint figure. Strong: an accumulating ostinato.
- **Not** - A sound for the noun, or power: the miser is small (R7). Pinning greed on a foreign people; the familiar adventurers can be the greedy ones (H&H 191 n. 10 conv.).

#### Temptation and seduction
- **Cues** - The tenderness cues plus chromatic slides (§6 r5). "Allured" loads on wonder (.86), "sensual" on tenderness (.98) (GEMS); erotic desire is a cross-cultural dimension (COW [M]). The erotic went to slow minor jazz, not the romance tune (VVA 1113; TLTT 251 [T]): a close, dry solo with no setting ("all figure, no ground") is the sexual register (TLTT 262–65 top.). The song is the spell: a dance is already playing and the seducer joins on one line (Giuffre 104–05 conv.).
- **Melody** - Linger on 2 and b3; or b5 on a downbeat falling chromatically to 4; a three-step chromatic fall with a melodic b6 (TLTT 573–98 [T] conv.). An ostinato with a rising chromatic pickup into each downbeat: seduction or predatory pursuit (TLTT 590–95 conv.). A serpentine stepwise line in parallel thirds over a long falling bass (Lehman 210–11 conv.). The syrinx slide: a lazy chromatic slide on `flutes` that returns to its pitch (Monelle 265–67 conv.). Waltz: one-bar gestures with written accelerando and crescendo; 1+3 or 2+3 groups against two-bar hypermeter for the illicit (McKee 176–87 top.). Sirens: fairy music's slow form, high thirds in long notes (Dick. 122 top.).
- **Harmony** - Seventh and ninth chords; minor-six and half-diminished for the femme fatale (Tagg Tritone). Erotic and evil: i ↔ iii, two minor chords disputing home (Lehman 210–11 conv.). An alluring dream: I → iv or i → V, the 5 → b6 inflection (Lehman 98–100 conv.). Charm over darkness: i ↔ bvi under a waltz (Lehman 256 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - Low `clarinets` or `english_horn` with slides; one upward clarinet scoop (TLTT 598 [T]); soft `trumpets`; brushed off-beat snare; a close dry mix (TLTT 251 conv.). Breathy unison `flutes` (Hayward 47; Barron 87 conv.). Intertwining `piccolo`/`flutes`/`clarinets` cut by `brass` with cymbal and `triangle` for serpentine allure (Brill 25 conv.). *Ethereal Choir* for a siren; a humming `choir` over minor fragments and bass drum for corrupting allure (LOTR conv.). The tango (T074 [gen.]).
- **With / against** - With menace, minor and `tremolo`; with the fey, a waltz over i ↔ bvi (inf.); with the infernal, a nocturne over `harp` with tritone inner voices (after Hatten 515, inf.); Pan's amoral Eros, chromatic flute against pentatonic horn (Monelle 187–88, 265–68).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one slide into a note. Whole: the full device, a waltz or a tango.
- **Not** - Loud or fast. The lush romance stack (below average on sensual, TLTT 251 [T]). Harem, Gypsy-dancer, castanet or "Eastern" codes (Dick. Table 3; Brill 28; Barron 88; Mayes 222–23 top.). The vamp shorthand as the only colour for strong women (TLTT 676–77 inf.).

#### Tragic love
- **Cues** - Tagg's anguish traits make love tragic: iadd9 (2 rubbing b3), the half-diminished chord, a tortuous disjunct line. R&J over-scored love, sadness, loneliness, destiny, parting, death (VVA 111, 123, 1236 [T]). Under it, a legato quaver oscillation b3 – 2 in the middle register almost throughout, which "nags and gnaws" (TLTT 453–54, 464). At the loss, strip to one voice (Stephen 68 conv.).
- **Melody** - The R&J incipit: pickup 5 up a minor sixth to b3, then 2, back to 5 on the downbeat; a smaller rise, then a long wavy stepwise fall (TLTT 442–46 conv.). It arches inside the 5 – b3 sixth and widens downward (TLTT 452 conv.); slow crotchets, legato. An opening minor sixth in compound triple with a Phrygian cadence (SW 32 conv.). The minor siciliana, 6/8, fragmentary lament phrases over a falling chromatic tetrachord (Sutcliffe 134; Hatten 523–24 top.). Over V9, a fall of a minor seventh from the ninth to the third (TLTT 206 top.). Love into madness: phrases broken by rests, a tritone sigh, a fermata (Head 271–72 top.).
- **Harmony** - iadd9 over a pad; iv as pre-dominant to V7 → i; a più calmo bridge in III round the circle of fifths (TLTT 432–49 conv.). One minor iv6 just before the kiss in a major scene, or a leapt-up V9 then iv6 for death (TLTT 194–207 top.); bVI → V6/4 with the tune falling to a b9 (TLTT 212–13 top.). Evaded home: hints of III, IV, a hanging Vsus4, at the death V onto a hollow minor, then falling minor triads by chromatic mediant (i – bvi – ...) (Stephen 67–69 conv.). bII winding round i (Head 271–72 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - `oboe`, `flutes` and `strings`; the oscillation in `violins2` or two `clarinets` (parts inf.). Corrected: the trembling return of the tune goes to a single close `tremolo` line with slight `wobble`, or `solo_violin` in high tremolo, not `harp` (TLTT 466–73 [T]). Fate pressing as a symbolic tempest (Dick. 116 top.); a `harp` "bard" frame (Dick. 127 top.); hunting `horns` fading as the lovers meet (Monelle 80 conv.).
- **With / against** - Love's duet over fate's ostinato (R4). Across forbidden frontiers: sighing sixths and slow madd9 (TLTT 469 [T]). With war, on-beat half-diminished concerto themes (TLTT 198–203 top.). A gavotte with sighs and agitation (Mirka-b 366–73 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: the b3 – 2 oscillation, or one iadd9 under the love theme. Early scenes cut the theme short of its last chords; it is completed only at the death (Murphy 169 conv.). Whole: incipit, arch, major bridge, close tremolo return (inf.).
- **Not** - Plain sadness: the love must stay audible. Worried chords only in expected slots, which is lip service: let iiø7 surprise (TLTT 198–204 top.). Plunger trombones, rock timbres or #6 and b5 on the chord turn it into urban crime (TLTT 458–62 [T]).

#### Friendship and fellowship
- **Cues** - Tagg's social anaphones: unison, heterophony (each saying the same thing their own way), polyphony as a community of distinct voices, call and response as dialogue (MM 514). Friendship sits in the love field (VVA 1110, 1118–19); "friendly" and "amiable" are listener factors (ES; MRX C2). A tune on the full ensemble suggests several people more than a solo does (TLTT 391–94 inf.). The contredanse is danced for the dancers' own pleasure and grows with their number; in the anglaise everyone meets everyone (McKee 166–68 top.).
- **Melody** - Companionship is two instruments carrying the line in parallel: a minor siciliana (solitude) turning into a major musette with paired lines over a long pedal (Sutcliffe 134 top.). Build the party as an intervallic family, every companion's motif drawing on falling minor thirds and perfect fourths (Summers 170–74 conv.), or a shared cell such as a rising stepwise run then a fourth leap, so they combine in counterpoint (H&H 180 conv.). (inf.) Moderate 2/4 or 6/8, major, mid-range, thirds.
- **Harmony** - A shared harmonic cell (I – iii – bIII, later IV – vi – bVI) under the group's mystery, friendship and heroic cues ties the adventure together, tagged by the same timbre each time (Lehman 148–49 conv.). A bond theme with a Lydian double neighbour and IV – iii motion (SW 57, conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - A company theme on `horns` that grows as members join and fragments as they part (LOTR [W][R], conv.). One `brass` action theme on a rising fifth for every companion, even the comic one (H&H 187–88 conv.). *The sidekick's answer*; *The party in counterpoint*; a round, each member entering in canon (Chapin 311–14 top.; inf.). Two-sounding-as-one: `oboe` doubling `harp` or `solo_violin` in unison for a close pair (TLTT 388 inf.).
- **With / against** - With loyalty, the doubling that never leaves. With farewell, thin the texture voice by voice. With betrayal, one voice leaves. Rivals turned friends: two scansions (half-bar and quarter upbeats) until one folds into the other (Irving 541–44 conv.; inf.). Every earlier theme gathered in one fugato reads as apotheosis or embrace (Hatten 526 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one phrase in parallel thirds. Whole: the party in counterpoint, a round or a contredanse (Sutcliffe 134; McKee 168 inf.).
- **Not** - Unison all the time, which is an army. A member shut out of the shared material by a maximally opposite theme, unless isolation is the point (H&H 180–82 conv.).

#### Family and kinship
- **Cues** - Parental love reads in lullaby-like settings: slower surface, simple, conjunct, harmonically static, no big leaps or swells (TLTT 250–52 [T]; §8.6 r8 [S-M]). Family words have their own codes (VVA 230F, 211P). A child's world and ecstatic passion exclude each other in listeners' answers (TLTT 251–52 [T]). Home song: lullabies, evening and morning songs, plaintive ballads for the family circle (Day-O'Connell 249–54 top.).
- **Melody** - A simple conjunct cradle song in 6/8 or 3/4, ♩.≈50–60, narrow range, major (inf.). A sibling bond: a long three-phrase melody over pedals (SW 20, conv.). A mother's restraint: a free-floating aeolian line on solo `oboe` (SW 30, conv.). Father and son at odds: two motifs sharing an interval but not a key (LOTR; treatment inf.).
- **Harmony** - Domestic peace: the "Ave Maria" chord, I – ii7 (IV6) – V – I, with the tune sighing down a seventh onto ii7 (*Siegfried Idyll*; TLTT 175 top.). Copland's plainness, pandiatonic and subdominant-leaning triads held still, drew family at 3.1x with farms and small towns (TLTT 536 [T] conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - `cellos` and `horns` over pedals; a domestic song with plain `harp` chords and one voice (inf.); *Lullaby*, a hearth tune.
- **With / against** - With home, the hearth. With grief, the lost parent's lullaby slowed (*Leitmotif upon Death*). With youth and age, the same tune high then low. The heroic theme can first sound for a parent defending a child (H&H 187 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a lullaby cadence under the tune, or the theme when a character speaks of those they protect (H&H 188 inf.). Whole: a cradle song.
- **Not** - Romantic sweeps and modulations for a new parental theme (Tagg). Corrected: extending an established love theme to the couple's child is a valid, documented move (H&H 188 conv.).

#### Compassion and pathos
- **Cues** - "Compassionate" is a listener category (COW); Hevner's "pathetic" is minor, low, slow (HEV cl.2 [M]). Being moved is its own cluster (GMC; GEMS .75). Sad music is rated beautiful (EV r=.59 [M]) and gives chills twice as often (§4 [W-M]). Melancholy markings are played slurred and portato, never loud (Guymer 579–81 conv.). Sensibility began as moral sympathy, not a style (Head 264–69 top.).
- **Melody** - Adagio ♩≈58, legatissimo, noble and restrained (Morricone; TLTT 196–97 top.). The pathetic aria: slow, tender, sad or loftily serious (Day-O'Connell 245–48 top.); dotted and sustained notes, slurred close steps, minor (Quantz via Guymer 584 conv.). Elegy: up a semitone, down a tritone, slowly (SW 51). A speaking pianto: a solo `oboe` recitative (Sisman 106–07 conv.).
- **Harmony** - i → iiø7 (iv6/5) as the second chord after a minor tonic = woe (Bach, Mozart 40; TLTT 184–86 top.); iiø7 over a chromatically falling bass, without which the pathos mostly goes (TLTT 187 top.). Leading-tone moves I ↔ iii and i ↔ bVI supply sentiment (Lehman 100, 152 conv.). The pathetic style: dense chords, chromatic bass (T031).
- **Colours and devices** - The *pianto* sigh (T029); a rationed pathos theme, used a handful of times a campaign (T184). The stag's death: a halali that fails to rise, repeated a step lower, a falling semitone, one `horns` holding a single note (after Monelle 36, 72, 80–81; inf.).
- **With / against** - Grief felt for another: the pitied character's motif in the melody, the pathos in the harmony. The party sings the stricken one's own song back to them (after Day-O'Connell 251–53; inf.). Pity for a monster: keep its motif's transformations but recombine them toward a tragic hero's moves (Lehman 149–53 conv.). With mercy, the forgiving cadence.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one sigh, or a crisis chord at 60–80% of the phrase that is not worked through. Whole: the chord arriving unexpectedly or modulating (TLTT 191–204 top.).
- **Not** - Melodrama; pathos is earned by restraint (R13). Regular, predictable half-diminished chords: heard as trite (TLTT 204 [T]). For comfort offered see **Consolation and comfort**.

#### Savagery and brutality
- **Cues** - Tagg's violence codes: chromatic low-brass stabs of uneven length with no metre to hold (Sth99 [T]); fast asymmetric additive rhythm with atonality, scattered rests and sforzandi = terror and barbarism, while the same asymmetry over plain metre is just busy (Kojak §6.5.1 [T]). Brutality and terrorising (7.0x) came from loud, dry staccato low octaves, a long-tailed low drum boom and power-chord fifths (TLTT 633–56 [T]). Fast minor with sforzandi and repeated notes is the 19th-century "bad" minor (TLTT 317–19 conv.). Tempesta covers Furies and monsters: fast, loud, driving, syncopated (McCl. 280–87 top.).
- **Melody** - A minor six-note figure in 5/4 (LOTR Isengard [T][S], conv.). Swagger inside the rampage: the tune at half speed while the drums keep pace (Stephen 67 conv.). Fast, ♩≈140–160, narrow range around low 1 – b2 – b3 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Power chords I5 and bII5 in low octaves (TLTT 633–56 [T]; numerals inf.). Primitivism: an octatonic bass stressing minor thirds, with tritone-related seventh chords rocking (e.g. I7 ↔ #IV7), set against dorian music for the civilised side (Lehman 43 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - `horns` + `trombones` + `tuba` marcato on bare fifths, a spiccato `cellos`/`basses` riff, `taiko` and bd on every beat (Stephen 66–67 conv.); `taiko` and `toms` in uneven metres, low brass clusters, growling `trombones`, male `choir`. A rampage: driving drums, syncopated bass, stabbing jabs, relentless repetition (Stephen 67). *Drumbeat of Chaos* for the rout.
- **With / against** - With war, the horde; with industry, the war-forge (5/4 on metal); with menace, a chieftain's steady theme over erratic drums; with comedy, a blustering ogre (§7.6 r3).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one irregular low-brass stab. Whole: an uneven-metre drum assault.
- **Not** - Any real people's drums or chant (R12). The alla turca, the West's documented construction of a "barbaric East" (Mayes 216–23 top.); the Indianist war dance, itself a stereotype (Dick. Table 3 top.); minor pentatonic over a drone for a "savage" people (TLTT 326 conv.); the generic "savage" downbeat drum with falling brass minor thirds, or on-screen tribal rattles (Brill 23, 33 conv.); "jungle" imagery (TLTT 605, 646–47 inf.).

#### Rivalry and irreconcilable enmity
- **Cues** - Two factions, houses or rivals who share the stage and never make peace; not the fight itself (**Combat and the duel**), one side's aim (**Vengeance**) or a broken bond (**Betrayal**). Juxtapose, never blend: the contrast itself speaks of the gulf between two worlds (Dick. 100–01 top.). Give each side its own instrument family (woodwinds against strings, `horns` against `solo_violin`) so they never sound like one body (inf.).
- **Melody** - Each side has its own topic and phrase type: one in balanced four-bar diatonic periods, the other limping, extrovert and irregular (Dick. 100, 114, Table 3 top.). (inf.) For instance one stepwise in 2/2 at a stately ♩≈72, the other leaping in 6/8 or with displaced accents, faster.
- **Harmony** - One side functional, closing on strong V – I cadences; the other non-functional, with sudden shifts between distant triads (I → bIII → VII) (Dick. 114, Table 3 top.; numerals inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Virtuosic figuration keeps the drive going while the rivals clash (Dick. 135–36 top.). The rival's motif cutting into the other's cadence.
- **With / against** - In a form that expects reconciliation (statement, development, return), weaken the return: bring both back, each still in its own topic, with no merged version (Schubert's Quintet; Dick. 100–01 top.). The lower-coded side "cannot be refined": development does not raise it (Dick. 135 top.). Its opposite is **Reconciliation and union of opposites**.
- **Intensity** - Hint: the rival's motif interrupting a cadence. Strong: alternating sections. Whole: both themes stated, developed in dialogue, returned unmerged (inf.).
- **Not** - A final combined statement in counterpoint, which says the feud is over (inf.). Coding one faction with a real people's stereotyped idiom, as the hongrois was coded against the Biedermeier (Dick. 100 top.; R12).

#### Reconciliation and union of opposites
- **Cues** - Two opposed forces (sacred and secular, rival houses, two peoples, war and peace) becoming one, or pointedly failing to; not **Mercy and forgiveness** (one side yields) or **Friendship and fellowship** (a bond already made). A full V – I or a closing major chord made viewers assume reconciliation (§5 [M]; §6 r34); reconciliation sits with thinking and remembering (VVA 5938). Reconciliation by reversal: two topics first heard apart, separated by caesuras, return in the opposite order and flow into each other without a break (Bruckner 7 finale; Horton 657–59 conv.). Merger of compatible topics reads as metaphor, resistance as irony: choose which the story wants (Hatten 514–15 top.).
- **Melody** - Two rival scansions, one theme on half-bar upbeats and one on quarter upbeats, until one's upbeat folds into the other's (Irving 541–44 conv.). A shared theme: one side states the antecedent, the other the consequent (Horton 653–56 conv.). Every earlier theme gathered in one fugato: the all-embracing trope (Hatten 526 top.).
- **Harmony** - A late-arriving countermelody that bridges two extreme topics (Hatten 523–24 top.). (inf.) Keep the two themes' keys apart until the union, then give the piece its first full V – I in a shared key.
- **Colours and devices** - The hunt as mediator: it reconciles peace and war, human and animal, and hinges a pastoral and a violent section (Rumph 507–11 top.): `horns` in 6/8 between the two.
- **With / against** - Its negative is the bifocal ending: opposites set side by side by rupture and left unreconciled, for a peace that costs or never comes ("Eroica" finale; Byros 405–08 top.). With triumph, a reconciliation finale (inf.). See **Rivalry and irreconcilable enmity**.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a bridging countermelody under two motifs. Strong: the motifs in alternation, then in counterpoint. Whole: Bruckner's plan, separated, reversed, merged, then a static sound field (Horton 657–60 inf.).
- **Not** - Averaging the two into a mild middle (R4): each side must keep its marked feature to be heard inside the union (Rumph 497–98 top.).

#### Injustice and unfairness
- **Cues** - Wrong suffered by a person: the exile, the wrongly condemned, the victim of a rigged world; not the act of judging (**Judgement and justice**), the hardened attitude (**Bitterness and cynicism**) or acute pain (**Anguish**). The madd9's core is a lone adult in adversity with room to reflect, "it just isn't fair": slow, a moderate surface rate, mid-register and quiet, so the crunch nags and gnaws rather than shouts (TLTT 463–64 inf.).
- **Melody** - A legato minor line touching b3 and then 2 (5 – b3 – 2), returning again and again to that semitone and sloping down inside the 5 – b3 sixth (TLTT 452–54 conv.). (inf.) 4/4 at ♩≈60–70, long notes, narrow range.
- **Harmony** - iadd9 as the home chord, struck again and again or rocked b3 – 2 in quavers over a bass stepping 1 – b7 – b6 (TLTT 456–57, 464 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - A quiet b3 – 2 oscillation in `violins2` or `clarinets` under a solo (`oboe`, `solo_violin`, low `flutes`); `harp` semiquavers on b3 – 2 under a `flutes` lead 5 – b3 – 2 – 5, after Morricone (TLTT 456 n. 17, 458 conv./inf.).
- **With / against** - With a hard world (crime, a corrupt city): chromatic notes, groaning `trombones` with slides and a heavy bass, for victims of an unfair system. With love, separated lovers (**Tragic love**) (TLTT 461–64 [T]).
- **Intensity** - Hint: the oscillation alone under other music. Strong: the 5 – b3 – 2 incipit. Whole: a slow madd9 lament with the oscillation throughout (inf.).
- **Not** - Twist-of-the-knife chromaticism or half-diminished melodrama, which are torment and woe; this is quieter and reflective (TLTT 463 inf.). Rage: injustice suffered waits (TLTT 463–64 inf.).

#### Consolation and comfort
- **Cues** - Comfort offered to someone in pain: the moment after the blow, the healer's word, a prayer answered. Pain and relief heard together; not **Peace** (nothing wrong), **Sanctuary and safety** (a place) or **Compassion and pathos** (feeling for another). Moderato or slower, soft attack, a held tonic in the bass under the second chord, no tritone or semitone harshness (TLTT 173–79 top.). The texts and scenes of its models share consolation, bliss, well-being, positive tranquillity and serious feeling (TLTT 179 top.).
- **Melody** - Legato, mostly stepwise; the tune sighs down a seventh onto the consoling chord (*Siegfried Idyll*; TLTT 173–75 top.). Sighing sixths and sevenths (an on-beat fall, strong to weak, then a step up) carry the pain-and-relief pair itself: six in sixteen bars of *An die Musik* (TLTT 217–20 top.). (inf.) 4/4 or 3/4 at ♩≈60–76.
- **Harmony** - The "Ave Maria chord": after a firm I, a second chord that keeps the old tonic as its sixth or seventh (IV6 or ii7, the same notes), then V with the tonic held as a 4–3 suspension, then I: I – ii7 – V4-3 – I (Bach-Gounod, *Ave verum*, Tallis's Canon, Anglican chant; TLTT 173–79 top.). Minor form: iv6 as the second chord after i (TLTT 180 top.). The tune built on it came first of ten on tranquillity (49‰ vs 18) and on beauty (TLTT 177 [T]).
- **Colours and devices** - Low `strings` or `horns` on the progression, `harp` optional (inf.). Sacred comfort only with `organ` or `choir`: without them no listener heard it as religious (TLTT 176 [T]).
- **With / against** - With grief, pain and comfort alternate: a worried iv6 or iiø7, then the Ave Maria chord (TLTT 207–08 top.). With death, strength in the hour of death (*Ave verum*); with love, love fused with prayer; with home, domestic peace; with sleep and healing, *Egmont*'s sweet sleep (TLTT 173–78 top.). With mercy, the forgiving cadence.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one Ave Maria chord as the second chord of the character's theme. Strong: the full I – ii7 – V4-3 – I with a sighing seventh onto ii7. Whole: a slow hymn-like chain of such phrases (inf.).
- **Not** - Diminished sevenths, tritones, semitone clashes, or any sharp-attack pulse (TLTT 179 top.). The chord as a passing chord in a busy texture: it works as the second chord over a held tonic, moderato or slower (TLTT 173–75 top.).

### E. Emotion and mood

#### Joy
- **Cues** - Fast, major, simple consonant harmony, varied rhythm, bright timbre, staccato, high register (§1; Hevner [S]); the best-recognised emotion across cultures (§8.3 [S/M]). Hevner's happy cluster: major 24, fast 20, simple harmony 16 (HEV cl.6 [M]); in game music, diatonic notes (.67), a quick note rate, a slightly narrower range (HM [W]). It carries an urge to dance (§7.2; GEMS .72). Tempo predicts happiness better than mode: The Virginian in E minor scored happy 2.4x, sad 0, because it broke every sadness trait (TLTT 288–90, 317, 330 [T]).
- **Melody** - Quick repeated notes, sudden dynamic contrasts (TLTT 317 conv.); short detached notes, wide leaps, Lombard snaps (Quantz, Türk via Guymer 579–84 conv.). The contredanse: fast 2/4 or 6/8, major, a lilt with triplets, artless, in 4+4 bar periods with sharp section breaks (McKee 169–73 top.). The gigue: fast compound metre, running triplets (T059; HAT23 top.). A lydian #4 for youthful brightness (Lehman 16, 62 top.). Ecstatic joy: a leap from far below onto the ninth of V9 (TLTT 207 top.); a hearty skip to 6 (TLTT 226 top.).
- **Harmony** - Simple I – IV – V. Giddy optimism: every chord major, rocking I ↔ II (Lehman 82–83 top.); a borrowed major bIII, bVI or II sounds brighter than a diatonic chord (Huron via Lehman 261 [ev]). Victory: lydian, a I – II loop in bright brass (SW 22).
- **Colours and devices** - Jig, reel, galop; *Festival trumpets*, *Jubilant congregation*, *The tavern*. Exultant `trumpets` flourishes (Haringer 197–98 top.); tutti outbursts with `timpani` (Sisman 90–91 top.). Terraced four-bar units: `strings` f, then winds p (McKee 169–73 top.).
- **With / against** - With grief, bittersweet (*Melancholy Major Key*). Joy in minor: minor reels, tarantellas and wedding marches abound (TLTT 310–11 conv.; *Merry in Minor Key*). A dance gains joy against a dark neighbour (Byros 405 top.); a low-style dance confirming a new key reads as landing (Agawu 481–82 conv.). Fugal treatment makes joy "distinguished" (Chapin 309–10 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a dance rhythm in the accompaniment, or one lydian II (Lehman inf.). Whole: a dance, or an all-major chain landing on the theme.
- **Not** - Loudness without a pulse. An all-major chain with no theme to land on draws a laugh (Lehman 83 conv.). Equating minor with sad.

#### Playfulness
- **Cues** - The play-song register: high, wide range, rising contours, strong regular rhythm, peaks on accents (§8.6 r9 [S-M]); quick dynamic changes (§1). Hevner's sparkling cluster: major 21, high 16, simple harmony 12 (HEV cl.5 [M]). Carefree music keeps passion and thought out: the Sousa march drew the most child responses and none for conflict (TLTT 410, 430 [T]). A general pause after a cadential six-four raised playfulness in brilliant style (Margulis 636–38 [ev]).
- **Melody** - Short beat values: the same phrase in 2/4 or with quaver beats reads gayer (Mirka-b 358–61 top.). Buffa scurrying: light-footed and dialogic, cued by a drop to p and a small upbeat figure (Hunter 62–64 top.). Passepied: light, fast 3/8 (T062; frivolity, Mirka 7 top.). (inf.) Major, ♩≈120–140, staccato, leaps of a third to a sixth, range about a tenth.
- **Harmony** - Plain major triads give the toy quality (TLTT 494–95 inf.); quick I – V alternation; a little chromatic quirk over an oom-pah (SW 7).
- **Colours and devices** - Leroy Anderson's light orchestra: `pizzicato` everywhere, perky unison `flutes` or `clarinets`, call and response with `solo_violin`, `harp` and `glockenspiel`, trills (Summers 102–03 conv.). Bright dry strokes up high (`pizzicato` with staccato `piccolo`, `glockenspiel`) sound like toys and cartoons (TLTT 495–96 [T] conv.). A toy march (Monelle 176 conv.); one light `bells` stroke on an upbeat (TLTT 413–14 inf.). A high shower of plain `harp` + `glockenspiel` arpeggios over soft `pizzicato` (Ivanovitch 338–41; scoring inf.). A small tribe: a staccato dotted march with raised fourths on `piccolo` (SW 19).
- **With / against** - With youth and joy. With power, `bassoons` and `tuba` in quick staccato. With grace, the courtly game; canons and musical puzzles as elegant play (Chapin 311–26 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a quick staccato answer. Whole: a play-song.
- **Not** - Mischief's edge: play is innocent.

#### Mischief
- **Cues** - **Mischief is play, not malice**: the sneaking tiptoe (staccato `bassoons` or low `clarinets`, `pizzicato`, sudden stops as if caught), one wrong note as a wink, then the prank's pop. Keep diminished and augmented chords for a malicious sprite only - a mischief sketch with them under the line lost to a plain comic one (host pairwise test, 2026-10). Over a tonal background, displace notes by a semitone, reach and leave them by leap, repeat them (§6 r23 [W]); humour by banality (§6 r24). Buffa: fast patter, swerving gestures (HAT23); sudden dynamic or texture changes for no structural reason (Hunter 79–82 top.). Displaced accents, broken-off phrases (Rumph 493–500; Beghin 552–70 top.); the accompaniment stopping dead under the tune (Guymer 589–90 conv.). Comic dances: lively, mischievous, agile (McKee 169 top.).
- **Melody** - Alla zoppa limping syncopation (T054). The b6 – 5 – #4 double chromatic neighbour round the fifth, the trickster's wriggle, cousin to dark-magic themes (Lehman 258 n24 conv.). A poor, repetitive idea with a loud fanfare in the wrong place; antiphony repeated too often (Hunter 73–78 top.). A noble-hearted trickster: a 6/8 fanfare with one cheeky chromatic note (Monelle 97 conv.). Kobolds: fairy figures (stepwise, within a fifth, sequences, thirds) moved down to `bassoons`, low `clarinets`, `cellos` (Dick. 123 top.). (inf.) Fast 2/4 or 6/8, staccato, mostly major.
- **Harmony** - Corrected: malice in a sprite comes from seventh, diminished and augmented chords under the high fairy figure, not from minor mode (Dick. 122 top.). A chromatic tune with odd harmonies (Webster 201, 208 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Sneaking*, *Mickey Mousing*, *Deflation*, comic *Stings*. Goblin patter on `bassoons` and `piccolo` (T044). Tiptoe march: `piccolo` + `flutes`, `pizzicato`, wind–string antiphony (inf.). A `piccolo` shriek over slow low winds (Brill 27 conv.). Medieval mischief: `oboe` + `bassoons` + `flutes`, `triangle` (Webster). The comic thief: low `clarinets` sliding to b5 over a walking `pizzicato` bass and soft snare (TLTT 583–88 conv.). Mocking glissandi for malicious laughter (Dick. 119 top.).
- **With / against** - With cunning, the trickster; a trickster king answered by minions (`choir` or `brass`, Giuffre 101–02 conv.). With the fey, a pixie (`celesta` and wrong notes). With evil, share the neighbour cell so a later turn reads as kinship (inf.). Magic out of hand: a scherzo adding a layer as the spell multiplies; the master returns with one gesture, a dead stop, a soft chord (Halfyard 219–20 conv.). A sly Lombard dance straight after a tempesta (Sisman 98 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one wrong note repeated. Whole: a sneaking scherzo.
- **Not** - Tragic or tense scenes, which it turns into cartoons.
- **Tell apart** - From *The comic and the absurd*: mischief is a character's sly intent (sneak, wait, pop); the comic is incongruity aimed at the listener (deflation, pomp punctured, mock-grave swerves). They overlap heavily; a trickster can take both.

#### The comic and the absurd
- **Cues** - Incongruity is the engine: serious against comic, the clown bursting into the dignified piece (Mirka 9; Hunter 65; Sisman 98 top.); parody exaggerates, irony mistimes (TLTT 413 inf.). Banal continuations and contrastive valence (§4 [W]); "amusing" is cross-cultural (COW [M]). Buffa: fast, patter, swerves, major, low style; it signals each switch with a cadence (T044; HAT23; Hunter 62–64 top.). Anachronism is comic: an 1893 march on an absurdist show scored humour 5.7x (TLTT 399–418 [T]). Speed without threat (busy, high, major, bright, no rough timbre) drew cartoon and "bustle, not dangerous" at 4.1x (TLTT 493–98 [T]).
- **Melody** - Patter: repeated notes or tiny-range figures, impossibly fast, on material already heard (Hunter 79–82 top.); staccato scraps between rests (inf.). The character's own theme as staccato semiquavers for humiliation (H&H 182 conv.). Satire by excess or banality: a march of nothing but triad notes, a trite piccolo tune set too high (Monelle 175–77 conv.). A three-note falling-semitone figure on soft `trumpets` as a comic warning (Summers 101–02 conv.). A quick two-pitch wobble as a double take; chromatic slithering as toil (TLTT 575–76 conv.).
- **Harmony** - Keep the thirds major (TLTT 494–95 inf.). Mock horror: every chord minor and keyless, played straight under farce (Lehman 82 top.). Deflation: an apocalyptic chromatic chain cut off by a perky diatonic tune (Lehman 73 conv.). Semitone-climbing pump-ups with accelerando tip into self-parody (Lehman 249 conv.); the worn whole-tone wash for a comic dream (Lehman 52–53 top.). A pat V – I landing on a pratfall (Lehman 200–01; inf.). Parody needs a live cliché, e.g. bVII → V (TLTT 358 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Mock melodrama*, *Deflation*, *The dreadful bard*, *Mickey Mousing*, `tuba` lead; kazoo as staccato `bassoons`, xylophone as `pizzicato` + staccato `piccolo` (Kojak; TLTT). A hocket of one-note scraps between `bassoons`, `oboe`, `pizzicato` (Hunter 79–82; scoring inf.); a repetitive orchestral "blanket" under recitative-like fragments (Hunter top.); a fugato in `basses` (Sisman 111 conv.). Toy soldiers: thin single-voice march with imitation (Hatten 523–29 top.); `trumpets` and drums arriving on an ordinary key change (Galand 463–65 conv.).
- **With / against** - With menace, a blustering ogre (§7.6 r3); with pride, pomposity punctured; seria language in a coward's mouth (Hunter 84 top.); an ombra introduction blown away by a bright allegro (McCl. 295 top.). With the army, the circus march. A pause in a brilliant texture lands as a joke (Margulis 636–38 [ev]).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one sting. Whole: a mock melodrama or buffa patter.
- **Not** - A really sad scene. Koch's warning: no cuckoos or music boxes in serious music (Head 275 top.). Renaissance-wind colour on a scene meant to be magical (Webster 201 conv.). National comic stereotypes (TLTT 355 conv.). An exposed wonder cliché in a serious scene draws a chuckle (Lehman 260 conv.).
- **Tell apart** - From *Mischief*: see there. Listeners heard a comic sketch with a tiptoe hocket as more mischievous than the mischief one (host pairwise test, 2026-10): the tiptoe belongs to both.

#### Grief
- **Cues** - The sadness profile [S]: slow, soft, few dynamic changes, low, narrow, falling minor seconds, legato, slow attacks, dark timbre (§6 r4); in game music fewer notes a second (−.78) (HM [W]); a peak then a sudden collapse (§7.6 r8 [W]). Corrected (TLTT 315–17 conv.): minor is optional; the seven traits of silent-film sad pieces in either mode are mostly andante, rich but never harsh harmony, quiet legato arpeggios or sedate block chords, a clear legato melody, downward semitone pulls, no sudden dynamics, no quickly repeated notes; Hevner's minor weight (HEV cl.2 [M]) is the weaker guide. Weeping: pathetic accents, sobbing rubato (Head 273–74 top.).
- **Melody** - The *pianto*: a slurred falling semitone stressed on the upper note, often chained; added to any topic it grieves (T029; Monelle 4, 80, 108 top.). Sighs by step or leap, falling-tear figures in imitation, a dotted farewell figure with a sobbing repeated note (Head 268–69 top.); a falling diminished-seventh outline (Sutcliffe 131–34 top.). Sighing falls of a sixth or seventh, then a step up (TLTT 217–20 top.). Approach 1 from b7, not a leading tone (Lehman 98 conv.). Slow 3/4 or 12/8 (pastoral lament, siciliana; Monelle 217–37).
- **Harmony** - The lament bass 8–b7–b6–5, or chromatic 8–7–b7–6–b6–5; it is heard only once b7 sounds, so reach b7 at once or start on bVI6 (Caplin 415–17, 438–39 top.); settings i – v6 – iv6 – V, with 7–6 suspensions, or i – bVII – bVI – V (Caplin 417–19 top.). Precadential iiø7 → V → i = woe (Dowland, Dido; TLTT 181–86 top.). i ↔ bVI pendulum (Lehman 100, 255 conv./top.); siciliana close bII6 – V – i (Monelle 217 top.); minor plagal iv → I or iiø7 → I in major (Lehman 199, 238 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - Solo `cellos`, `solo_violin`, `english_horn`, `oboe` over held chords (Sisman 106–07 conv.). A people's lament: one high voice alone, high `choir` or solo `flutes` over soft strings after a fall (LOTR [T]; Lehman 172 conv.). Lament over a long-held chord, `solo_violin` free above (Sutcliffe 134; Mayes top.; invent the mode, R12). The sarabande for dignified grief: slow 3/4 or 3/2 beginning on the downbeat; a beat-2 stress is one option, not a rule (Mirka 23 n. 39 top.). *Lament*, *Leitmotif upon Death*, *The missing answer*.
- **With / against** - With love, whose grief it is; with rage, vengeance. Grief becoming resolve: the elegy grows into the appassionato (Dick. 110 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one long appoggiatura at a cadence. Strong: a lament bass. Whole: a lament; as a loop, avoid full cadences until a story beat; the last cycle can turn to bIII for the lift at the pyre (Caplin 426–36 top.; loop inf.). After a full build, cut to a lone voice at the loss (Stephen 68 conv.).
- **Not** - Generic grief for a known person (use their tune). A fall of more than an octave reads as bathos (TLTT 217 top.). The hexatonic pole, which reads as catastrophe (Lehman 100–01 inf.).

#### Bittersweet and melancholy
- **Cues** - Happy and sad felt at once (§7.2 [M]). "Bittersweet" is a listener category (COW); melancholy loads on GEMS nostalgia (.52). Melancholy and sentimental codes went to the slow legato tunes (VVA 1235, 1031 [T]); paired-opposite answers (smiles and tears) came only from the sighing tunes (TLTT 158–66, 224–25 [T]). The pastoral's founding mood is twilight sadness inside tranquillity (Monelle 186 top.).
- **Melody** - The siciliana: slow 6/8 or 12/8, lilting dotted figure, languid, often minor (T014; Mirka 40; Haringer 204–06 top.). Restate the opening in minor with its major sixth shrunk to a minor sixth: the sun clouds briefly (TLTT 216, 231 top./[T]). Put the 1 → 7 step of I → iii in the tune as the hook (Lehman 90–100 inf.). Rising semitones in an inner voice: longing creeping in (TLTT 178–79 top.). The *Winterlied*: a plain song tune with an ache of painful longing (Dick. n. 18 top.). Serenity with regret: a 9/8 major `clarinets` tune over a tonic pedal with chromatic passing chords (Monelle 248 conv.). Cooke's falling major second (§7.1 [W]).
- **Harmony** - The loss third I ↔ iii (Lehman 100 conv.). The mixed plagal iv → I or iiø7 → I, transcendence and bittersweet (T157; Lehman 199, 238 top.); an aged ending I – bVII – iiø7 – I (Lehman 238–39 conv.). iadd9 held as its own sonority: sad, wistful, bitter-sweet; a passing minor sus9 inside a major romance adds a touch (TLTT 206–08, 458, 577–79 top./conv.). Minor iv6 in sad or nostalgic ballads (TLTT 195–97 top.). Mourning lifted: a lament bass that turns to rise in a major variation (Caplin 444–46 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Melancholy Major Key*, *Softer and slower*. A melancholy waltz with wordless `choir` and `celesta` (Elfman; Halfyard 12; Binns 140 conv.). A low `oboe` pushes an ambiguous tune toward poignant pastoral (TLTT 380–82 inf.). The distant `horns` in the woods carries faint regret (Monelle 104–07 top.). Brooding: low `cellos` in madd9.
- **With / against** - Layered: the major carries, minor colour enters at the turns. Corrected: fast, bright *modal minor* can be wistful, a joy that is remote or lost ("elsewhere and long ago"; TLTT 327–30 [T]); Grieg's slow elegiac minor = somewhere else + long ago (TLTT 321 conv.). A gavotte with sighs and agitated accompaniment: nostalgia for a lost paradise (Mirka-b 366–73 top.). With memory, nostalgia; with love, the *fête galante* (Monelle 262–64 top.). Corrected: autumn as such belongs to the euphoric hunt; use the falling melancholy only for autumn as fading (Monelle 58, 69–70, 95 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one borrowed iv, or one I → iii under a held note. Whole: a siciliana, or a I ↔ iii undulation closing iiø7 – I (inf.).
- **Not** - Bright and fast in major, which is simply happy. The musette, which is merry and naive (Haringer 205–06 top.). The siciliana as always sad (Monelle 219, 232 top.). A general pause inside a minor siciliano, which tips it ominous (Margulis 636–38 [ev]).

#### Loneliness
- **Cues** - Solitary settings went with calm, nostalgia and sadness (§8.1 [M]). One voice, wide spacing, long rests (inf.). Abandoned-and-alone over-scored for R&J (66 vs 17; VVA 1236 [T]); its close solo restatement drew lonely 4x, single individuals 3.3x (TLTT 471–73 [T]); *Sayonara*'s slow, still, strings-only texture drew lonely/left behind 3.0x (TLTT 521–37 [T]). Figure over ground is the individual against the world; a lead that does not blend with its accompaniment is a person apart (MM 514; TLTT 366–78 [T]/inf.). The solo takes freer timing and swells against a steadier tutti (Guymer 592–93 conv.).
- **Melody** - Solitude is the single line (Sutcliffe 134 top.). The calling snap: two quaver pickups, an on-beat quaver, then a top note held about seven quavers, a voice calling over hills: distance and loneliness (TLTT 347–54 conv.). A solo recitative over held chords for ennui or bewilderment (Sisman 106–07 top.). (inf.) Slow, ♩≈56–66, free rubato, minor or modal, wide intervals with long rests.
- **Harmony** - Corrected: the lonely chord is the minor add-nine, iadd9 (2 grinding against b3), held as a chord in its own right; a major add9 is bright and reads otherwise (TLTT 188, 206–08, 577–79 top.). A slow quiet iadd9 crunch is an adult alone in adversity (TLTT 460–64 [T]). Schubert's *Einsamkeit*: a stumbling drone, v going straight to i with no dominant (TLTT 188–89 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - `english_horn`, solo `oboe`, a lone `flutes` over open fifths; *The cell*, *Solo Duet*. Roland's horn: a far `horns` call that no one answers (Monelle 106–07 top.). One `solo_violin` over a chord held a very long time (Sutcliffe 134 top.). The high lonesome sound: a `tremolo` line held high with long reverb (TLTT 473; part inf.). A sudden switch from the full band to a laconic rubato solo with heavy vibrato, soft `trumpets` or `clarinets` with `wobble` (TLTT 555–98 [T]). No contact at all: drop the bowed strings, keep `harp` and winds (Brill 28–29 conv.). A hermit: a glum sarabande on `horns` (SW 55).
- **With / against** - With longing, an exile. With vastness, the lone rider and the calling snap. With stature, a lonely king: the orchestra swells and drops away (inf.). With love, the close solo restatement. Isolation against the mob (Binns 144 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: the accompaniment stops under one phrase. Strong: tutti falls to one exposed voice (Lehman 172 conv.). Whole: a solo over a drone.
- **Not** - Full strings.

#### Longing
- **Cues** - Slow (§1); withholding the expected cadence builds emotion (§6 r20). Hevner's yearning/plaintive cluster: slow 16, minor 12, flowing, slightly high (HEV cl.3 [M]); "tender/longing" is a listener category (COW). Longing sits with dreaming and remembering (VVA 5934); pleading is a voice anaphone (VVA 596P). "Drang", restless yearning, is extreme sensibility: sighs, broken phrases, a plaintive tune, introverted and private (McCl. 281; Head 264–68 top.). The pastoral is itself a longing for a spring already lost (Monelle 186–95 top.). *Sayonara*'s still bundle (slow, quiet, wide spacing, static bass) drew longing 6.6x and pleading (TLTT 521–37 [T]).
- **Melody** - The rising minor-sixth pickup 5 → b3 in minor, then 2, back to 5; its comparison set is a catalogue of longing and homesickness; doubling the leap intensifies it, and the 2 after b3 carries the ache (TLTT 444–53 conv./inf.). A portamento leap (T035; HAT23). *Messa di voce* on every long note; the *dragg*, a slow falling glide, used sparingly: a `slide` down on a held `solo_violin` or `oboe` note (Day-O'Connell 245–48 top.; inf.). The *Winterlied*: painful longing in a simple song tune (Dick. n. 18 top.). End phrases on 2 or b7 (inf.); Cooke's falling major second (§7.1 [W]). Rising chromatic steps in an inner voice, repeated sighs (TLTT 178–79, 217–25 top.).
- **Harmony** - The home never reached: bVI – bVII – i looped and stopped on bVII (Lehman 263 n42 conv.). The half-diminished as a pivot that never lands: the uncertainty inside longing (TLTT 187–90 top.); yearning at sunset, a loop over iiø7 → V7b9 with legato arpeggios (TLTT 197 top.). A flat-side purple patch: a sudden drop to a mixed bVI or bIII over a drone, artless and static, a vision of an absent place (Galand 466–67 top.). The 5 – b3 – 2 incipit over iadd9 (TLTT 453).
- **Colours and devices** - *Yearning*, `english_horn`, `strings`. Two herdsmen calling across a valley: `english_horn` answered by a distant `oboe` (Monelle 101–02 conv.). A distant free-tempo `horns` call, echoing, for homesickness (the alphorn; T002, HAR10 conv.: not Monelle's claim, who treats the alphorn only as an influence on the pastoral horn). Archaizing topics evoke a past old enough to feel lost, near enough to be missed (Dick. n. 29 top.). Pining: falling chromatic sighs (SW 36).
- **With / against** - With love, the unanswered duet. With home, an exile's tune. With an unfulfilled goal, stay in modal minor and give no full-ensemble major arrival, so longing stays in the future tense (TLTT 390–91 conv.). It resolves only when the wish is granted.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one phrase ending on 2, or one portamento leap. Whole: *Yearning*, or the bVI – bVII loop that never lands.
- **Not** - A triumphant ending; clear V → I arrivals (TLTT 189 top.); ending the loop on i (Lehman 263 conv.). Plain 5 – b3 then falling, which loses the ache (TLTT 453 inf.).

#### Hope
- **Cues** - A stepwise rise over a stepwise bass reads as willed hope (§7.6 r8 [W]); rising contour (§1 [W]); dark to light (§7.6 r4). Confidence in the future (VVA 1102) and opening-up movement (VVA 534); "hopeful" and "uplifting" are common tags (JAM; AMM). Innocent optimism: major, few dissonances (Brill 36 conv.). *Emmerdale*, with predictable circle-of-fifths harmony in a calm 6/8, drew hopeful 5.3x (TLTT 513–15 [T]). Keep the thirds major; flattened they turn fretful (TLTT 494–95 inf.).
- **Melody** - (inf.) Rising stepwise phrases in 6/8 or 4/4, moderate ♩≈80–100, major, each peak a step higher, ending on 5 or 3 rather than 1. A rapid upward run into a downbeat ("a world of shining hope"; TLTT 447 conv.). A heart between fear and hope: a restless, wavering figure (Koch via Mirka 33–37 top.). Mattheson's courante = sweet hope (Mirka 7 top.).
- **Harmony** - bVI → V6/4 with the tune climbing to a consonant fifth under a swell: opening from the dark side to the bright (TLTT 212–13 top.), driven in by a falling inner semitone and a chain of ninths (TLTT 211–12 top.). Hope after darkness: i → bIII in place of the darker i → bVI (Lehman 100, 131 conv.), the tune holding b3 or 5 over the change (inf.). The lament's last cycle lifted to bIII; iii → I as the road to resurrection (Caplin 426–36; Byros 404 top.). A lydian II with a hemiola (SW 54, conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - A `horns` line rising; the turn to major; a lydian #4; high bell-like `bells`, `glockenspiel`, `celesta` for optimism (Stephen 71 conv.). The first half of *Rallying*, *Withholding*, *Sentimental Music Cue*.
- **With / against** - With despair, false hope undercut by a minor chord. With dawn, the rise. Musical arrival is happiness, so hold back the full major arrival until hope is fulfilled (TLTT 389–91 inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one stepwise climb. Whole: dark to light.
- **Not** - Arrival: hope is motion toward the cadence. The bVI → V6/4 with the tune falling onto a b9, which is anguish (TLTT 212–13 top.).

#### Fear
- **Cues** - Quiet plus staccato is the strongest predictor [M]; large sudden dynamic swings, fast, wide range, high register, rising lines (§6 r7); hissy spectra, unpredictability (§8.5 [W]). Threat in game music tracks loudness (.62), large intervals (.58), wide range (HM [W]). Scary music need not be dissonant (§8.6 r12 [M]); Cowen's raters linked it with dissonance (COW, qualitative): treat dissonance as optional, though a discord registers faster than a tune (Herrmann via Lehman 247 conv.). Players feel tension, not fear (§7.2). Ombra's body signs: syncopation as a throbbing heart, tremolando as shaking, an uneven dotted rhythm as an irregular heartbeat, repeated notes as footsteps (McCl. 283–85 top.); paint the fear, not the threat (Mirka 33–37 top.). Freeze (awe, ombra) against fight (frisson, tempesta) (McCl. 287 top.). Horror alone may let the music lie (Summers 127–34 conv.).
- **Melody** - Panic and flight: fast repeated notes, rapid rising and falling scales (McCl. 284 top.); fast minor with sforzandi, "hounds of hell" (TLTT 317–19 conv.). The unseen predator: 1 ↔ b2 (Lehman 250 conv.). A static, insistent triplet ostinato over falling low winds for a swarm (Brill 30 conv.). In ombra the sigh is fear, not longing (McCl. 283–85 top.).
- **Harmony** - vii°7 for stress and fear (Lehman 161 conv.). Panic: an isolated iiø7 hammered ff, allegro furioso; a semitone clash inside the chord makes horror bodily, without it the unease is psychological (TLTT 188–92 top.). Shock: a dim7 stinger, a crawling octatonic bass, a pedal, an unresolved cluster built note by note (Lehman 33–34, 53–54 conv.). The pump-down: the whole key dropping a semitone at the attack (Lehman 64 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Tense Tremolo*, the *Psycho* stab (`violins` ff short strokes with `slide` grace notes; TLTT 100–01 top./conv.), a `pizzicato` heartbeat, *Shock*. The unaccompanied rising chromatic `pizzicato` bass; a single repeated `timpani` note as heartbeat (T158; LAV conv.). Horror palette: `unhinge`, `slide`, clustered lines, low `bells`, `timpani` rolls (Summers 128–31; parts inf.). A proximity signal: a held high `tremolo` or one repeated note whose dynamics follow distance (Summers 127–30 conv.; parts inf.). Bewildered panic: `solo_violin` recitative over held strings (Sisman 106–07 top.).
- **With / against** - Against courage, *Rallying*. With the uncanny, horror. A pastoral foil just before (R4). Up to four fear layers added as the threat nears; the layer count is the fear level (Summers 131 conv.). A diegetic dance band as control and reality (Webster 202–03 conv.). With wonder, a jolt reappraised as harmless becomes frisson (Lehman 170–73 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one quiet staccato figure. Whole: a fear cue built on uncertainty. Relief: a sudden major turn with crashing cymbals (Brill 30 conv.).
- **Not** - Dissonance alone. Dropping the build when players stop: hold static creepiness instead (Summers 131–32 conv.). False scares outside horror. Scoring the supernatural's first appearance: its silence is helplessness (Brill 35 conv.).

#### Dread
- **Cues** - Low energy with high tension (§7.6 r10 [M]); low register; pauses and repetition (§1); an ominous bed amplifies a later startle (§8.6 r13 [M]). Steady repeated notes and pedals make everything above unstable; pauses on an unresolved chord (McCl. 283–85 top.). Michaelis: extreme uniformity, one chord, very slow motion, long pauses, as if over a chasm (McCl. 290–94 top.). Let the threat music arrive before the deed (MM ch. 14). Silence that usually means safety, but not always, unsettles (Summers 129–30 conv.).
