- **Melody** - A plodding `pizzicato` bass on the tonic at ♩≈58 plus one long minor-six chord on soft mid-low brass: "something unavoidable is coming", ominous 6.1x (TLTT 539–79 [T]). 5 ↔ b6 in eighths over a tonic pedal, one key for minutes (Lehman 41–42 conv.); drooping semitones in every voice (Lehman 81 conv.). A slow minor siciliano with a general pause after the cadential six-four raised ominousness, in no other topic (Margulis 636–38 [ev]). Two `flutes` in thirds sliding chromatically down over a static `harp` octave (Brill 31 conv.). A single choral rhythmic line, no dissonance (LOTR [R]).
- **Harmony** - Corrected (Lehman 100–02, 171 top.): the aeolian shuttle i → bVI is dread only slow, low and darkly scored; brightly scored it reads as myth and awe; the dependable form is i → bvi. Deepening darkness: all-minor chains by semitone or third, i → vi → #iv → biii (Lehman 81, 161 conv.). A threat entering an idyll: the bass b6 – 5 – #4 – 5 with vii°7 over #4, lurching into minor (Byros 393–99 top.). A one-note bass and `timpani` pedal under minor and diminished chords (TLTT 565 conv.); a falling-third figure for an evil land (LOTR).
- **Colours and devices** - *Drone of Dread*, *Heartbeat Soundtrack*, *Bell ostinato*, *Musical Spoiler*. Repeated-note bass in `pizzicato` `cellos`/`basses` with soft `timpani`, mid `tremolo` (inf.). Funeral terror: soft `timpani` rolls, silences, veiled `kit` gong; a long silence, then one gong stroke ff (Monelle 128–29 top.). Before a sacred, dangerous object: `celesta` with gentle `wobble`, high ppp `tremolo`, `harp` glissandi (Brill 31 conv.). Big ominous gestures tied to no event (Summers 129 conv.).
- **With / against** - The undercurrent under any surface: low chords creeping under an idyll (Barron 87 conv.); a sudden minor passage after calm threatens more than any slow introduction (McCl. 295 top.). Pick wonder or dread per magical place (Brill 31 conv.). Party still: static creepiness; retreating: calm (Summers 131–32 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a low pedal entering under calm music. Whole: drone, heartbeat, minor shuttle; the end of everything dwindles to one pulse, then silence (Hayward 54 conv.). Withhold a section the players know is there (Sisman 109–10 top.; inf.).
- **Not** - Loudness: dread waits. The pulse alone; under lush major strings the plodding bass is harmless (TLTT 566 inf.).

#### Menace (the threat of force)
- **Cues** - The threat profile was an inference (§6 r16), now backed by convention and reception: low brass stabs on a chromatic line in irregular durations = male violence, steady or erratic (Sth99 [T]); a `timpani` roll = distant thunder, danger; a final drum roll on an added-ninth chord = danger still to come (MM ch. 9; Kojak §6.1.3.5 [T]). Threat in game music: louder (.62), larger intervals (.58), wider range (HM [W]). The manuals' recipe: a sharp, low, prolonged sfp "menace note", a sudden drop in register, a falling inflection at every phrase end, mid and high dissonance over a low pedal, occasional heavy percussion (TLTT 651–61 conv.). A loud low reverberant boom swallowing quiet high ticking drew menacing 3.1x (TLTT 633–51 [T]). Reverse the "not dangerous" five: no shimmering consonant harp, `timpani` beyond I and V, brass present, low register present, dissonant strings (TLTT 299–305 [T]/inf.).
- **Melody** - A staggering, chromatically bent minor tune low in `bassoons` doubled by `basses`, with `slide` into the bent notes (Summers 74 conv.). A rapid repeated note dropping a semitone in minor-third parallels on `bassoons` (SW 14); slow low first-inversion minor triads moving chromatically (SW 27). Moderate ♩≈60–80, narrow, phrases ending falling (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The stock tension generator: rising minor thirds of minor triads, i → biii → #iv → vi, each promising a major haven and landing minor (Lehman 161 top.). Unstoppable: i – bVI – biii – VII – bii – VI – i (Lehman 70 conv.). Whole passages lifted by minor thirds or tritones (Lehman 64, 249 conv.). Minor tritone progressions for danger from places and things (a trap, a blizzard) more than persons (Murphy 172 n. 14 conv.). Familiar harmony that never closes: menacing yet enveloping (TLTT 661–64 inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Low `brass`, `tuba`, `basses`; *Power* on low brass; *Creepy jazz* for amused menace. The menace note: `timpani` + `tuba` + kit bd sfz, then a held low note diminuendo in `tuba` or `basses` (TLTT 651 inf.). Three f `trombones` notes after they have been silent all piece (McCl. 288 top.). Slow whole-tone neighbours in `trombones` and `basses` (SW 16); a syncopated low minor ostinato (SW 52). A hostile landscape: `toms` with eerie low `flutes` and wordless male `choir` (TLTT 377 conv.).
- **With / against** - Against a gentle surface, menace in the bass only; it stays underneath until one moment. An army approaching unseen: a long minor crescendo from far-off pp (McCl. 295; Hunter 76 top.). Let the theme arrive when the threat is first named, and play it in their empty room (Summers 74–75 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: three notes of the motif in the basses, one `timpani` roll, or one i → biii (inf.). Strong: the full rising cycle. Whole: *Power*, the motif restated a minor third higher each time (inf.).
- **Not** - Continuous fortissimo, which spends the threat. High, bright, major and dry: drew "not dangerous" even at 126 bpm (TLTT 493–98 [T]).

#### Rage
- **Cues** - Loudness plus a sharp timbre are the strongest predictors [S]; fast, dense, sharp attacks, staccato, rough irregular rhythm, chromatic or atonal harmony, small pitch variation (§6 r6). Anger lives in intensity and timbre (§8.3 [M]); more roughness, less key clarity (EV [M]); loudness (.72) and large intervals in game music (HM [W]). The aggression field (VVA 121); rough timbre carries male power (MM ch. 13). The tempesta covers rage arias: fast, driving, syncopated irregular rhythm broken by pauses, mostly loud with strong accents and crescendos (T028; McCl. 282–87 top.).
- **Melody** - Tempesta lines: disjunct, fragmented, very wide and augmented leaps, rapid scales, repeated notes, fast 4/4 (McCl. 282 top.). The enraged Statue: a sequence pushed a step higher each time, a glissando, an angular line (McCl. 288–89 top.).
- **Harmony** - Minor, D or C minor above all; vii°7, augmented sixths and bII, often over a V pedal (McCl. 282 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - `strings` to the fore in `tremolo` and fast runs, full brass and `timpani` (McCl. 282 top.); timpani-backed tutti outbursts (Sisman 90–91 top.); brass stabs, `taiko`, *Rock drive*; rasping low brass for roughness. A berserker: tempesta (T028).
- **With / against** - With comedy, Osmin (§7.6 r3). With grief, vengeance. Suppressed fury breaking out: a composed minuet collapsing into a contredanse, a fall down the rank ladder (Hunter 87 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a stab on one accent. Whole: the anger profile in tempesta.
- **Not** - Sustained shrill dissonance throughout (§7.6 r12); felt anger comes mostly from music people dislike (§7.2). Rage as noble: Mattheson ranks anger low (Mirka 3–7 top.).

#### Peace
- **Cues** - Slow, tonal, stepwise motion opening into a leap, flute timbre, low complexity, flowing rhythm (§6 r38); GEMS peacefulness; small dynamic range (§1). Hevner's serene cluster: slow 20, simple harmony 10, higher pitch 8 (HEV cl.4 [M]); "calm" is the commonest of 28 rater categories (COW); calm is the second commonest story emotion at the D&D table (BAR). Corrected: the top tranquillity tune was the romance concerto, not the pastoral (49‰ vs 18): no drone, a walking tune at ♩≈92 over harmony and waves at half speed or slower, soft pad, no percussion, calm highest and speed zero (TLTT 158–77 [T]); the oboe-pastoral finding (VVA 112 [T]) stands as one route. Pastoral time is frozen, without goals, good for safe-haven loops (Monelle 189–97 top.; inf.).
- **Melody** - Celestial: stepwise thirds, lilting, regular phrases, gentle tempo (McCl. 287 top.). The circling tonic-arpeggio figure of the shepherd's pipe (Rumph 507–11 top.). 6/8 over a tonic pedal (Monelle 248 conv.); the march trio's cantabile as peace after war (Monelle 124 top.). Zefiro breezes: murmuring sixteenths round neighbour notes, `violins` in thirds, a solo `flutes` above (Sisman 101 top.). A held common tone in the tune across chord changes (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The Ave Maria chord I – ii7 (IV6 over held 1) – V – I (TLTT 173–79 top./[T]). Few chords, sparingly (Scheibe via Haringer 207–08 top.); I – V – I over a pedal (Monelle 243–48 top.); a tonic pedal for still water (TLTT 238–39 top.); a drone after the cadence for Arcadia (Galand 456 conv.). Modal stasis, I ↔ vi without dominant drive; smooth common-tone links (Lehman 141–43, 206 conv.). A step down in key relaxes (Lehman 54, 62–64 top.); every voice sliding down a semitone = calm amid danger (Lehman 105–07 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - `flutes`, `harp`, soft `strings`; *Lullaby*, *Avian Flute*; pastoral `oboe` over a drone (T011). Paired `flutes` restore calm after panic (Sisman 106–07 conv.). Peacetime: piping winds and a `pizzicato` popular tune (Sisman 109–10 top.).
- **With / against** - Against war, the pastoral before battle and regained after: pastoral, threat, pastoral (Rumph 511; Hatten 518 top.). After a fight, music that lowers arousal (§7.6 r16). After terror, the Blessed Spirits (McCl. 287 top.). For comfort in pain see **Consolation and comfort**.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a flowing `harp` figure. Whole: a pastoral or a romance-style walking tune over waves.
- **Not** - Dullness: slow stepwise music with no leap reads dull (§3; YM). Tritone or semitone harshness in the chords (TLTT 179 top.).

#### Suspense and tension
- **Cues** - Louder, higher, more notes per beat, more dissonance and complexity, repetition and sequence (§6 r11 [M]); a rest after an unresolved chord (§6 r12); expectancy (§4). "Suspenseful" is the commonest story emotion in labelled D&D talk, low valence and arousal (BAR); tension is its own GEMS factor and film axis (EV). A quiet, high, held violin dissonance = mental tension (Sth99 [T]). Run-ins, inner semitone slides, accelerando and crescendo drew "about to" answers (TLTT 101–02 [T]); *Streetcar* took tense, sweat and about-to-happen (5.4x) (TLTT 539–50 [T]). Game intensity dials: denser rhythm, faster tempo and harmonic rhythm, louder, clearer lines, more instruments, busier strings (Summers 122 conv.). Corrected: a general pause raised tension only in tempesta (one of two excerpts); elsewhere it made ominousness, playfulness or sublimity, so choose the topic first (Margulis 636–38 [ev]).
- **Melody** - A heavy chromatic pickup into every downbeat: tension, struggle, bad consequences (TLTT 590–95 [T]). Overture build: the tune climbing 1 → 3 or a whole octave in crescendo over a drum bass (Sutcliffe 136–37 top.). *Suspensio*, delaying the arrival; *dubitatio*, a feigned doubt before the cadence (Beghin 552–70 conv.). (inf.) Short repeated cells, narrow range, rising by step at each repeat.
- **Harmony** - Rising minor thirds of minor triads, i – biii – #iv – vi (Lehman 161 top.); octatonic spikes (Lehman 78 top.); vii°7 as coiled energy (Lehman 62 conv.); the half cadence as a question; V → vi falling back (Lehman 54, 199 conv.). A tonic pedal under the climb (Sutcliffe 136–37 top.). The crisis chord, iv6, iiø7 or bVI on the beat 60–80% through a period (TLTT 191–92, 211 top.). A countdown: a pulsing ostinato over a minor-third cycle that backs up when the outcome wavers and breaks out when it is saved (Lehman 161–63 conv.). A silence straight after vii°7 (McCl. 289–90 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Tense Tremolo*, *Tick Tock Tune*, *Silence and the stop*; a high held `violins` dissonance; a syncopated low minor ostinato (SW 52). Tick-tock: high short `pizzicato` or `toms` over a pulsing `basses` pedal, overriding the location music once the clock runs (Summers 160–64 conv.; parts inf.). After an accelerando, a `timpani` roll over a suddenly static `cellos`/`basses` pedal (TLTT 598–99 [T]). Endings: a `tremolo` cluster crescendo cut to silence; accelerating strokes into a tutti hit (Lehman 264 n2 conv.; parts inf.).
- **With / against** - A moment device inside any piece before a reveal; release by a resolving cadence. One minor iv6 before a happy moment makes it count (TLTT 194 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a held dominant. Whole: a build; scale it with 3–4 stems (pad, pulse, melody, full) using the same logic in every bed (Summers 131, 190–92 conv.).
- **Not** - A fade-out. Unreliable escalation outside horror (Summers 123, 134 conv.). Complex harmony under key dialogue (Lehman 32 conv.). Too many half-diminished crisis chords, which turn to syrup (TLTT 203–04 top.).

#### Excitement and action
- **Cues** - Arousal is the easiest dimension to read (§6 r1; EV R² to .85); note rate (.71) leads in game music (HM [W]); Hevner's exciting cluster: fast 21, complex harmony 14 (HEV cl.7 [M]). Dynamism over-scored for the synth action tune (VVA 101 [T]); asymmetric rhythm over regular metre = hurried excitement (Kojak §6.5.1 [T]). Corrected (TLTT 165–68, 246–47, 483–98 [T]): perceived speed and action come from tempo + surface rate + power and aggression cues (brass stabs, low hits, rough timbre) + low reiteration; the fast layer must be high or mid, detached or accented and reiterated, not low legato waves; bright, high, major, threat-free busyness reads as bustle ("not too much action"). Positive bustle: a high, narrow, consonant tremolo or arpeggio at ≥350 notes a minute (TLTT 246–47 top.). Brilliant style sits at high arousal, positive valence (Margulis 632–35 [ev]). The hunt: adventure, risk, exhilaration (Monelle 95 top.).
- **Melody** - "Ready-steady-go": short-short-short-LONG figures on a steadily rising tessitura (TLTT 341 conv.). Brilliant figuration: rapid octaves, fast parallel thirds, chromatic scales, continuous sixteenths, with several voices kept audible (Dick. 111–12 top.; Horton 649–53 conv.). The allegro 6/8 gallop (Dick. 125 top.). Battle-speed calls around 160 with `trumpets` triplets over a running pulse (Monelle 121, 166–81 top.). One accent per half bar sounds quick, per two bars broad (Mirka-b 361–66 top.).
- **Harmony** - Step modulations up a semitone or tone; several in a row feel inexorable (Lehman 57, 64 top.). Mixed pantriadic chains hold a montage together (Lehman 83 conv.). A blunt step modulation cuts through noise (Lehman 56 conv.): at the table, through dice and crosstalk (inf.). Brilliant runs over static I or V (T038).
- **Colours and devices** - The Mannheim rocket (T050); the trommelbass (T052); murky bass (T053). The noisy allegro: tutti chords plus `tremolo` (Sisman 107 top.). "Three hands": tune in `horns` or `cellos`, `violins`/`flutes` runs above, `harp` or string arpeggios below (Dick. 112; conv.). Imbroglio: quick syncopated p motive passed among winds, then `timpani` tutti bursts (Sisman 90–91 top.). Syncopated string trills and `harp` sweeps (TLTT 300 conv.). Build then hit: `basses` and `toms` alone, add layers, then `trombones`, `horns` marcato, `taiko` and spiccato low strings at once (Summers 136 conv.; parts inf.).
- **With / against** - With joy, a romp; with menace, combat; with speed, a chase. Strip to bass and drums when plans go wrong, back to the verse when on track, the chorus at the goal (Summers 190–92 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a rocket into a phrase. Whole: a fast ostinato with brass; a rising-register crescendo adding instruments (Sisman 106–08 top.).
- **Not** - Even semiquavers all the way: "fast, going nowhere" (TLTT 297 [T]). Endless accelerating pump-ups (Lehman 249 conv.). Brash nonstop action under a gritty, consequential scene (Summers 89–92 conv.).

#### Ecstasy and abandon
- **Cues** - Unbridled abandon (ecstatic, passion, wild, oblivion) is apart from joy and drama and needs its own music (VVA 105). Euphoric and ecstatic are listed moods (COW; HEV cl.7; AMM). The fast form: joy's urge to dance pushed further, fast, loud, dense, accelerating (§7.2; §1 inf.). The waltz's "voluptuous panic": dancers fainted, and a spinning race across the hall was banned for killing them (McKee 176–77 top.). Corrected: a slow form exists; the slow (♩≈84–90) legato madd9 love tune drew ecstatic/passion 5.1x, the ecstasy of yearning love (TLTT 432–34 [T]).
- **Melody** - Vertigo: one one-bar gesture repeated with written accelerando and crescendo; irregular 1+3 or 2+3 groups against two-bar phrasing set dancers leaping like madmen (McKee 180–87 top.). The lyrical outburst: a leap from far below onto the ninth of V9 (TLTT 207 top.); a roving, yearning Tristan-like line (Lehman 232–33 conv.). The galop, fast 2/4 (T071); the tarantella, relentless fast 6/8 (T069); the friss, accelerating throughout (T076, a structure not an ethnic marker).
- **Harmony** - The spin: one chord a bar, I and V, pedals, artless, so all the energy is in the motion (McKee 180–87 top.). Rapture: roving chromatic harmony circling a few reference chords by I – III, I – bVI, tritone and step moves; an fff arrival a tritone away (Lehman 231–33 conv.). Giddy major: all-major chains (Lehman 82–83 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - Tutti with cymbal and `triangle` on the irregular group (McKee 180–87 top.); accelerando to the end. A cursed dance that kills: the spinning race at breakneck speed (inf.). A spree: fast strummed `pizzicato` or `harp` in place of the fandango guitar (TLTT 338 [T]; parts inf.).
- **With / against** - With festival, the revel at its peak; with the fey, a revel you cannot leave; with madness, the tarantella frenzy; with love, the slow rapture.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one accelerating phrase. Whole: a galop speeding to the last bar.
- **Not** - Control in the fast form: it has no brakes; keep it short (R6). Settings meant for children, which drew no abandon at all (TLTT 251 [T]).

#### Contentment and ease
- **Cues** - Thayer's low-energy, low-stress quadrant: calm, warm, laid-back, gentle (MRX); Hevner's "satisfying, leisurely": slow, simple harmony (HEV cl.4 [M]). Tagg's stasis field (everyday, "as it always has been", no conflict) over-scored for the oboe pastoral (VVA 102, 112 [T]); recreation and outings (VVA 276) go with the romance-concerto texture (TLTT 158–66, 249 [T]). Strolling: a walking ♩≈92 tune over a slower accompaniment drew walking/strolling most of all tunes (TLTT 168, 215 [T]). Mattheson's contented dances: allemande, bourrée (Mirka 7 top.); Scheibe's pastoral, even-tempered, without fire (Haringer 207–08 top.). Topics presented straight with texture signposts give listeners comfort (Lowe 603–26 top.).
- **Melody** - *Emmerdale*: a long stepwise descent, nothing larger than a second but one minor third, 6/8 at ♩.≈72, poco andante, molto legato: secure 9.1x, harmonious 6.2x (TLTT 503–15 [T]). The new siciliana: 6/8, intimate song tune over a rocking accompaniment, parallel thirds, soft (Monelle 237–48 top.); Koch's pastorale, fairly slow 6/8 (Monelle 229 top.). The Ländler: relaxed, arpeggiating constant eighths (McKee 176–80 top.). Four-bar symmetrical lyrical phrases close to dance (Dick. 114 top.). Utter contentment by merging compatible topics in one theme: pastoral, singing style, yodel leaps, Ländler sway (Hatten 516 top.).
- **Harmony** - Mostly diatonic, slow harmonic rhythm, strong cadences (I – IV – V – I, a full V – I every four bars) (Dick. 114 top.; numerals inf.); circle-of-fifths progressions in four-bar periods (TLTT 503–16 [T]); only I and V over a soft pedal (McKee 180; Monelle 245 top.). Plain functional harmony reads as normal life; modal I ↔ vi for placid ease (Lehman 44, 203–07 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - The galant norm and the Alberti bass (T051, T046): light slurred broken chords in `violins2` or `bassoons` (Dick. 114 top.; parts conv.). A moderate 6/8 `oboe` or `clarinets` tune over `harp` arpeggios (Emmerdale, Sth99). A sunny port: a relaxed syncopated groove in `harp` and `pizzicato` with `harp` rolls and `triangle` (Summers 163 conv.; parts inf.). Corrected: Biedermeier is not a salon idiom but old-fashioned Mozartian elegance with middle-class respectability and nostalgia; it suits comfort, but its core is class and propriety (Dick. 114–15 top.).
- **With / against** - With home, the hearth; with the rural, an untroubled village. The before of a story: contentment is what danger interrupts (R4). A jovial loop that plays on through small mishaps (Summers 105 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: an easy galant accompaniment. Whole: a leisurely 6/8 tune.
- **Not** - Joy's dance or peace's stillness: contentment strolls. Chromatic mediants, which break ease (Lehman 66 top.). Pure predictability tips into banal domesticity (TLTT 513–15 [T], weak).

#### Beauty
- **Cues** - "Beautiful" is a cross-cultural feeling dimension (COW [M]); rated beauty tracks tenderness (.77) and sadness (.59) (EV [M]); feelings of beauty and harmony as an aesthetic class (GMC). Beauty over-scored for the romance-concerto tune (VVA 114: 72 vs 17 [T]); concert strings, a concertante soloist and hall reverb read beautiful, serious and refined at once (TLTT 215–16 [T]). Singing style is "linked sweetness": legato, connected, comprehensible, the opposite of the bumpy and bizarre (Day-O'Connell 240–54 top.); sweetness, noble simplicity, restrained pathos (Head 271–72 top.). Singing style was rated sublime, and a pause raised that (Margulis 632–38 [ev]).
- **Melody** - A long arching legato line; *messa di voce* on long notes, Hogarth's line of beauty (Day-O'Connell 245–48 top.). The skip above the octave to 6, "the warm tone" (TLTT 225–27 top.). Nocturne and aria manner: a lush cantabile with fioriture, wider range than song (Dick. 106–08 top.; Horton 644 conv.). An elf-home: two arpeggiated major triads a major third apart, each decorated inside by 5 ↔ b6 (Lehman 177, 258 conv.). Slow, ♩≈60–72, 3/4 or 4/4 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Rich but consonant; the Ave Maria chord I – ii7 – V – I (top on beauty and tranquillity; TLTT 177 [T]). I ↔ bVI with surface major sevenths and augmented fifths, or the single chord I(add b6) (Lehman 177, 255 conv.). A chill-giving lift: the tonic jumping a semitone from phrygian minor to lydian major (Lehman 174 conv.). Common-tone links join remote chords "magically" (Lehman 142 conv.). Parallel colour chords built from 1–2–5 and 1–5–b7 spread over four octaves turn harmony into colour (TLTT 533–35 [T] conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - Legato `strings` with `harp` arpeggios; the string pad (T185); `harp` and `celesta` arpeggios (inf.). Nocturne scoring: tune on `cellos`, `horns` or `clarinets`, a second voice a sixth below on `bassoons`, `harp` beneath, `pizzicato` bass notes (Dick. 108; parts conv.). Flowing hair and garments: continuous cascades, octave-wide legato rises and falls, much `harp`, a rich string carpet (TLTT 158–66, 260–65 top./[T]). Up-and-back `harp` sweeps for elegance and waterfalls (TLTT 386 [T]). A golden wood: a single high voice (LOTR [W][T]; R12 on borrowed scales).
- **With / against** - With sadness, its natural partner. With wonder, the gentler half of the sublime. With the fey, a beauty too perfect: add one wrong `wobble`.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one arching phrase over a pad. Whole: a slow romance-concerto texture without percussion.
- **Not** - Loud or fast. Overused attention-begging harp sweeps feel manipulative (TLTT 387 [T]).

#### Grace and elegance
- **Cues** - Hevner's graceful cluster: major 21, high 16, simple harmony 12, flowing, slightly falling (HEV cl.5 [M]); flowing broken-chord rhythm (§1); elegance sits with beauty (VVA 1144). Legato reads round and flowing, staccato angular (TLTT 169 top.); prepared suspensions and ninths in a concert acoustic give classical elegance (TLTT 205 top.). Gentle characters played lighter and softer (Guymer 579–81 conv.). Galanterie: distinction won by pleasing others (Zbik. 147–48 top.); the minuet's natural grace and noble simplicity, danced to be watched (McKee 168 top.).
- **Melody** - Corrected: the courtly gavotte is alla breve 2/2, unhurried, with a half-bar pickup of two repeated equal notes and steps on beat 3; in a lively 2/4 it reads as a servant-class tune or a contredanse (Mirka-b 366–73; Zbik. 144; Hunter 84–85 n. 16; McKee n. 6 top.). Grazioso: scalar sixteenth pairs in the gavotte (Hunter 84–85 top.). The minuet, moderate 3/4 (T057). Balanced four-bar periods; poised, untroubled Classical grace (Dick. 103, 114 top.). The bourrée's upbeat: two eighths on the last quarter (Zbik. 144–60 top.) against quarter-note upbeats (Irving 540–47 conv.); Zbikowski is the stronger.
- **Harmony** - Simple diatonic homophony, I – IV – V – I in regular periods, slow harmonic rhythm (T046; Mirka-b 366–73 top.); prepared suspensions and added ninths (TLTT 205 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - `flutes` and `violins` with `harp`; the galant light accompaniment. The courtly musette: sweet, intimate, dignified, for rooms: `oboe` or `clarinets` in thirds over a soft held open fifth (Monelle 210–14 top.; parts inf.). The *fête galante* in parkland (Monelle 261–64 top.). Ballet drew the legato wave stack (21‰ vs 4; TLTT 158–66 [T]). Brilliance as order, "thousands of orderly lamps" (Ivanovitch 342–48 top.).
- **With / against** - With nobility, courtly bearing (bourrée and gavotte share the minuet's old elegance; Dick. Table 1 top.). With playfulness, a light dance. With menace, a graceful assassin: the gavotte over a drone (R4), or a waltz over i ↔ bvi for charm with dark mystery (Lehman 256 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a gavotte upbeat. Whole: a galant dance.
- **Not** - Heavy bass or brass. Unmarked dances that listeners confuse (minuet vs passepied, bourrée vs march); lean on marked rhythms (Rumph 493–500 top.).

#### Anguish
- **Cues** - Tagg's three traits separate anguish from dirge or depression, which share slow minor: the minor-add-nine, whose 2 and b3 grind a semitone apart; the half-diminished chord (also as a minor six); and a twisting, leaping melody leaning on its dissonances; taut timbre, strained register and irregular accents add to it (Tagg Anguish [T]). "Painful" (COW) and "anguished" (AMM) name it. The ombra sigh is pain (McCl. 283–85 top.); the chromatic genus and skilful discords (Head 266–67 top.). Naked anguish: strip to one voice after a build (Stephen 68 conv.).
- **Melody** - 5 ↗ b3 → 2, the second straight after the minor third, legato, in an exposed opening (TLTT 453–61 conv.); the anguish ambitus, a slow line bounded by 5 and b3, sloping down (TLTT 452 conv.). Cooke's 1 – b3 – 5 – b6 – 5 (TLTT 189–90 top.). Over V9, a fall of a minor seventh from the ninth to the third (TLTT 206 top.). A very slow, disjointed, winding line with a falling chromatic figure for an ancient anguish (Brill 32 conv.).
- **Harmony** - The iadd9 crunch, b3 against 2 with no gap, grinding until resolved; struck again and again at ♩≈62 over a bass falling 1 – b7 – b6 (TLTT 453–57 conv.); a lone ringing iadd9 to open and close, V7b9 on the word "die" (TLTT 465–66 conv.). Corrected (TLTT 194, 204, 212–25 top./[T]): the half-diminished reads as woe when it is the second chord after a minor tonic (i → iiø7) or precedes a minor cadence, as worry when it pivots; in regular slots inside a major romance it even lowered sadness. bVI → V6/4 with the tune falling onto a b9 (TLTT 212–13 top.). Minor-six chords under a cantabile marked *straziante* (TLTT 595–97 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - A tortuous solo (`oboe`, `solo_violin`, `english_horn`) over madd9 and half-diminished chords in `strings`, strained high. Corrected: sighing sixths and sevenths turn anguished only when the fall lands on a b9; in a major romance they mean love and pain-with-comfort (TLTT 217–25 top.). Weight from contrabass-register fills and duplets against 6/8 (TLTT 465–66 conv.).
- **With / against** - The style socialises the chord: legato strings, flute and a trembling close solo (corrected: a single `tremolo` line, not `harp`, in place of the mandolin) with all-diatonic pitches give tragic love; plunger brass, rock timbre and chromaticism give urban crime (TLTT 462–73 [T]). Register decides: low growling `trombones` = nausea, middle = sadness (TLTT 576–77 inf.). With grief, grief at its sharpest; with guilt, torment; psychological anguish keeps semitone clashes out of the chord, bodily horror adds them (TLTT 190 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one iadd9, or the crunch as a quiet mid-register oscillation that nags (TLTT 464 inf.). Whole: a tortuous melody over the anguish chords; the madd9 can be belted out too (TLTT 463–64 inf.).
- **Not** - A plain minor dirge, which is sadness, not pain. The chord as the whole message without the style around it (TLTT 462 [T]).

#### Despair
- **Cues** - Despondency and pessimism in the sadness field (VVA 1233, 1238); bleak, gloomy, somber (MRX); Hevner's depressing cluster: minor, low, slow, complex harmony (HEV cl.2 [M]); high complexity with low dynamism (§1 [W]). A high-style affect (Scheibe; Mirka 3–7 top.). Calm withheld by repeated storms (Michaelis via McCl. 290–94 top.). Dejection: slow, a feebly stumbling drone, minor v → i with no dominant (TLTT 188–89 top.).
- **Melody** - A wide, falling, blues-like descent from an accented high note, sinking into the accompaniment's rhythm: dejection, giving up, fateful closure (TLTT 595–97 [T] conv.). Drooping semitones in every voice (Lehman 81 conv.). Before damnation: an angular line in bare octaves with no harmony, `tremolo` beneath, p growing louder (McCl. 289–90 top.). A slow minor siciliana with Neapolitan cadences (Monelle 217–18 conv.). The lugubrious: slow minor chromatic descents, low (T086 [partly gen.]).
- **Harmony** - A falling chromatic bass that never turns. All-minor chains getting ever "more minor", i → vi → #iv → biii (Lehman 161 conv.); falling minor triads by chromatic mediant after a deceptive cadence (Stephen 68–69 conv.). Minutes of unmoving minor over a 5 ↔ b6 ostinato and tonic pedal (Lehman 41–42 conv.). A loop ended on its last, least stable chord (Lehman 263 conv.). Free-standing iv6 chords without direction = urban angst (TLTT 192–93 top.). Nihilism: tonality dissolving (T089).
- **Colours and devices** - Low `strings` and `bassoons`; a theme stripped to fragments (*The hero's breakdown*); LOTR's doom themes (names only).
- **With / against** - With hope, false hope then the fall. With rescue, the moment before (*Saved by the Church Bell*). With courage, the rally out of it.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a cadence that fails to arrive. Whole: a slow chromatic descent ending unresolved. The end: everything stops but one low, sustained, reverberant note (TLTT 660–61 conv.), or the music dwindles to one soft `timpani` or `pizzicato` pulse, then silence (Hayward 54 conv.).
- **Not** - Grief: despair has lost even the object, so keep the line from singing.

#### Tragedy
- **Cues** - Hatten's tragic mode: minor and dissonance in the high style, slow forms (sensibility, pathétique, ombra; funeral march, chromatic lament) and fast forms (tempesta rockets and diminished sevenths, appassionato) (R9; HAT23; Hatten 523 top.). The tragic style puts fate high with learned or French-overture textures (T032). One consistent high eloquence; uninterrupted, little topic switching; minor narrows to tight, near-monothematic, backward-looking writing with suspensions and stretto (Hunter 68; Sutcliffe 129–34 top.). R&J drew tragic 5.4x, destiny 5.0x and "against outside obstacles" 7.9x: adversity framed as outside forces between people (TLTT 434, 461 [T]).
- **Melody** - Rocket answered by sigh in one theme: action answered by grief (Hatten 523–24 top.). Change a note or two of a call and it becomes the tragedy of men who must die (Monelle 166 top.). A plainchant-like four-note cell; the Dies irae cell in natural minor (SW 38, SW 37). A theme built from fragments and heard whole only at the catastrophe (Murphy 169 conv.). (inf.) Slow 4/4 or 3/4, ♩≈52–66, wide range, falling at phrase ends.
- **Harmony** - The hexatonic pole, I → bvi or i → III, for catastrophe (Lehman 101 top.). A deceptive close onto a hollow minor, then falling chromatic-mediant minor triads (Stephen 67–69 conv.). A pre-emptive elegy: I ↔ iii (Lehman 100 conv.). i – bVI under a wordless chorus (SW 38). A crisis chord at the climax near the end (TLTT 191–92 top.); on-beat half-diminished chords for love and hope suffused by tragedy (TLTT 198–203 top.). Minor-six chords under a heart-rending cantabile (TLTT 595–97 conv.). Requiem keys C minor, D minor (McCl. 294 top.).
- **Colours and devices** - *Opera Means Drama*; *The climax* in minor; a wordless lament with b6 and b9 dissonances (SW 37). A tragic hero from a monster: reorder its motif's transformations to bring the pathos (i → bVI) and heroic (I → VI) moves forward (Lehman 149–53 conv.).
- **With / against** - Heroism's material in the tragic mode; with fate, the inevitable. Tragic to transcendent: a sudden breakthrough to a major goal (Lehman 262 conv.), or a bifocal ending setting iii and the major fanfare I side by side by rupture (Byros 404–08 top.). The pastoral is its opposite pole (Monelle 22 top.); horn calls with the *pianto* under a happy scene foreshadow it (Monelle 80 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one high-style minor cadence. Whole: a tragic arc to a catastrophe.
- **Not** - Stock chord placement alone, which reads as lip service (TLTT 204 [T]). Corrected: high style is one route (Hatten), not a requirement; a sentimental, all-diatonic setting with the anguish ambitus and madd9 still drew tragic 5.4x (TLTT 432–34 [T]); the tragic frame is what matters.

#### Agitation and alarm
- **Cues** - "Agitated" heads the GEMS tension factor (1.00) with nervous and impatient; "anxious/tense" and "annoying" are cross-cultural dimensions (COW). "Agitated" ("roll initiative") is the second commonest story emotion at the D&D table (BAR). Stress over-scored for the fast sports tune (VVA 1226 [T]); short motifs sound breathless (MM ch. 8). Urgency needs unpredictable gaps: even, quantised repeats signal continuity and strength instead; measured reiteration is external and mechanical, unmeasured tremolando inner (TLTT 472, 654–56 inf.). Agitato: syncopation, dissonance, churning minor (Sisman 108–10 top.); fast minor with sforzandi and repeated notes (TLTT 317–19 conv.). An encounter switches instantly, no fade: the switch is the alert, then a segue into the gameplay bed (Summers 21, 166 conv.).
- **Melody** - The telegraph: a syncopated repeated note on one pitch, quavers and semiquavers in irregular order; news and stress/hectic 5.9x (TLTT 477–93 [T]; MM ch. 13). A tune in fits and starts with `trumpets` in parallel thirds filling the gaps and an accelerando: urgency, chase, struggle (TLTT 598–99 [T]). Disruptive octaves announcing conflict; agitated scales that break off a songlike stanza (Dick. 133, n. 41 top.). Infantry-type calls, high (C4 to G5), fast, on one or two notes, sound nervous (Monelle 140–41 top.). Patter as rising panic; frantic loud off-beat ornaments for a terrified dupe (Hunter 66, 79–82 top.).
- **Harmony** - The alarm chord: an isolated iiø7, ff, allegro furioso (TLTT 192 top.). A vii°7 stinger; spiky octatonic figures (Lehman 34, 78). The anti-pivot: a seam no chord can bridge (bVI straight to V of ii), gears grinding (Lehman 219 conv.). Tempesta bursting in through a deceptive cadence, the second time turning major into its own minor (Agawu 484 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - Staccato repeated notes in irregular groups (`pizzicato`, woodwinds), high `tremolo`, quick dynamic swings, jerky woodwind stabs (Kojak §6.1.1). High-string tempest figures (Dick. 116 top.). "Under attack": a short fanfare (Summers 120 conv.). Agitation in the accompaniment alone: a tremolo broken-chord bass under a calm tune (Mirka-b 366–73 top.). An ambush: a jittery octatonic figure with a mock-Baroque minor subject (SW 21). `solo_violin` recitative over held chords for panic (Sisman 106–07 top.).
- **With / against** - The instant before combat; hands over to **Combat and the duel** or **Chase and pursuit**. With fear, panic. Peace broken: a leisurely distant call over a still high chord, cut off by a fast military call (Monelle 164–65 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one telegraph burst. Whole: a nervous ostinato that keeps breaking its pattern.
- **Not** - Regular, square drive, which is excitement. A high, consonant, even figure, which is pleasant bustle (TLTT 246–47 top.). A slow fade into an ambush (Summers 166 inf.).

#### The uncanny
- **Cues** - "Eerie/mysterious" is a reliable category (COW); "creepy" co-occurs with "tense" (YM); creepy and weird sit with ugliness (VVA 1242). The uncanny hovers between two readings, the familiar brought too close to the strange (Murphy 149–50 conv.). Ombra's tonal uncertainty and sudden pauses (T027). Blur score and world: a drip, wind or bell becomes the cue's pulse; an eerie tone or rumble carries across a scene change (Murphy 150; Hayward 49–51; Summers 128–29 conv.). Distant keys between neighbouring cues (Lehman 33 conv.). Freezing one figure while keys move beneath: enchantment, disembodiment (Agawu 484 conv.).
- **Melody** - High slides and whines read weird (the head); a short chromatic figure repeated says "something weird is going on" (TLTT 575–77 conv./inf.). Hold the one common tone (the third) in the tune across a slide (Lehman 104 conv.; inf.). A plain diatonic tune over looping pantriadic chords (Lehman 71 conv.). An innocent repeated-note figure (a bird call) turned ominous by context (Hatten 527–33 top.). (inf.) Slow or rubato, ppp, narrow, chromatic neighbours.
- **Harmony** - The two "dependably strange" moves: the slide (I ↔ #i, i ↔ VII) and the hexatonic pole (I ↔ bvi), the pole being Freud's eerie familiarity (Lehman 70–71, 101 top.). The hexatonic collection, triads a major third apart joined by semitones, no tritone; clustered it is magical and unsettling at once (Murphy 160–61 conv.). Slides stitched by familiar iv – I and v – I joins; a chain that "cadences" a semitone off home (Lehman 80, 220–21 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - `celesta` with `wobble`, the ominous music box, a children's song in the wrong place (T106, T108); whole-tone `harp`, high soft `tremolo`. A theremin-like wail: `solo_violin` or soprano `choir` "oo" with `slide` and uneven `wobble` (Barron 84–85 conv.); high, layered, chromatically slipping voices (Stephen 64–65 conv.). A presence: high `tremolo` with `unhinge` and sharp slides (Summers 128–29; parts inf.). Corrected (TLTT 588–90 conv.): two recipes on a shared high-dissonance base, told apart by lead and pedal: eerie ice = high dissonant chromatic `strings` over static harmony (`celesta` sparkle benign, harsh dissonance deadly); fog = a legato chromatic `flutes` with raised fourths over a low tonic pedal and a tight minor cluster. A canon on a dance (the "Witches'" minuet; Chapin 313 top.); a historical dance as the past calling from afar (McKee 165; Mirka 44 top.); horn calls on violas (`violins2`), strange yet known (Agawu 490 conv.).
- **With / against** - With innocence, the haunted nursery; with undeath, the ghost; with mystery, the eerie clue. Learned style as foreign otherness (Chapin 311 top.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one detuned `celesta` phrase. Whole: a familiar tune slightly wrong throughout.
- **Not** - Loud horror: the uncanny is quiet and almost normal; loud slides read as threat (Lehman 221 inf.).

#### The grotesque
- **Cues** - "Nauseating/revolting" is a listener category (COW); the ugliness field (disgusting, crude, bombastic, gaudy) and the putrid (VVA 124, 1258). The scherzo as jest, grotesque and caprice, demonic or ironic in Mahler (T092); circus music for clowns (T107). Grotesque is one of Sulzer's four stage-dance kinds (McKee 169 top.). Pan's world: beautiful but grotesque, irresponsibility turned animal (Monelle 268 top.).
- **Melody** - Low `trombones` growling the 9 – b3 semitone in slowly swinging triplets with upward grace-note slides (`slide`, `wobble`): nauseated/disgusting, drunk (TLTT 573–77 [T]). A poor, repetitive figure with a loud fanfare in the wrong place (Hunter 73–78 top.); offbeat fz, awkward repetition, crude or absent harmony, keeping the gestures without any ethnic label (Hunter 62–64 top.; R12 inf.). Minor-mode fanfares for grotesque heroism (Monelle 99 conv.); war as black grotesque: an unmarchable 5/4, serpentine chromatic lines, mocking bugle calls (Monelle 180 conv.). A lurching 3/4 with the downbeat in the wrong place, ♩≈120–140 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Chords sketched in dissonance (SW 18); a discordant drone in fifths, I5, under a waltz (Hayward 47 conv.); crude or missing harmony (Hunter).
- **Colours and devices** - A grotesque crime lord: solo `tuba`, tumbling arpeggios, a strange lurching dance (SW 18). Low `bassoons` and `tuba` in a lurching scherzo; `trombones` slides. A feral society: a discordant hurdy-gurdy waltz, `oboe` + `bassoons` on a fifth drone, `solo_violin` with `unhinge` (Hayward 47 conv.). "Elephants dancing": a fugato in the `basses` (Sisman 111 conv.). Odd instruments for odd situations (Brill 25, 27 conv.).
- **With / against** - With the comic, the grotesque-comic; with the monster, a slime or ghoul; with decay, rot; with pride, gaudy bombast. Abrupt high-low style clashes were heard as ugly incoherence: use them on purpose here, sparingly elsewhere (Horton 657 conv.; inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one `trombones` slide. Whole: a lurching grotesque scherzo.
- **Not** - Horror: the grotesque disgusts or amuses; it does not stalk.

#### Adversity and struggle
- **Cues** - Adversities, confusion, frustration and "against external obstacles" (VVA 1221–27), the last over-scored for R&J (31 vs 4 [T]); loneliness and adversity are the ground shared by the madd9 tunes, and style decides the story (TLTT 460–61 [T]). "Frustrated" (HEV cl.2; YM). Calm withheld by repeated storms (McCl. 290–94 top.). Fast minor 6/8 for hard labour and hardship on land or sea (TLTT 305–07 conv.).
- **Melody** - A line climbing by sequence against a falling or static bass; a phrase that restarts after each failure. A slow low vamp with a chromatic pickup into each downbeat: difficulty, sweat, bad consequences (TLTT 590–95 [T]). The progress ladder: each success sounds a `brass` note a step higher, a setback resets to the lowest (Summers 194 conv.). (inf.) 4/4 or 6/8, ♩≈84–104, minor, short rising cells.
- **Harmony** - Minor as ground to cross: aeolian bVI – bVII – i over a pedal, lifting to major at each victory (Stephen 74 conv.). Upward step modulations for strain, one per stage of the ordeal (Lehman 54–64 top./conv.); rough root-position blocks in one mode for effort (Lehman 143 conv.); a slide shifting the ground under a held tune (Lehman 151 conv.). Strange ground: open on vi, a chain of parallel 6/3 chords with accents on beat 2, then home by fifths vi – ii – V – I (Zbik. 151–57 top.; numerals inf.). Overcoming: the threat's octatonic area restated as a mixolydian tune on the same root (Lehman 58 conv.). iadd9 at the setbacks.
- **Colours and devices** - A symbolic tempest for struggle that is not weather; against storm and pursuers at once, the tempest kit in major (Dick. 116 top.). Low, minor, ceaseless momentum indifferent to the struggler (Dukas; TLTT 593 conv.). *Rallying*'s first half.
- **With / against** - With determination, carried through; with despair, given up; with the journey, the hard climb (**Mountains and heights**). A looping unease cue for a long grind (Summers 169 conv.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one sequence step that falls back. Whole: a climb with three setbacks and a summit.
- **Not** - Combat: adversity is the obstacle, not the enemy.

#### Trance and meditation
- **Cues** - "Meditative" loads on GEMS peacefulness (.58); "entrancing" was the one category of 28 no sample evoked reliably (COW): trance is hard to score on purpose. "Hypnotic" (AMM). Soft, slow, grooveless, irregular periodicity reads as meditative rhapsody (MM ch. 8); ritual songs keep one rhythm (§8.3 [W-M]). Michaelis's extreme uniformity: one note or chord, long notes, very slow motion, long pauses (McCl. 290–94 top.). Parlando rubato over a drone with no felt pulse reads as timeless expanse rather than mourning (TLTT 317 inf.). A static sound field, layered strata over a pedal (Horton 660 conv.).
- **Melody** - A modal chant on one reciting tone; a short phrase repeated over turning chords, never cadencing (Lehman 79, 133 inf.); one figure frozen while harmony travels slowly beneath (Agawu 484 conv.). (inf.) Free or slow ♩≈50–60, narrow range, stepwise.
- **Harmony** - No cadences. Root–2–5 voicings, neither major nor minor, moving in parallel: Zen-like stillness (TLTT 533–35 conv.). Nonfunctional, unprepared chromaticism for out-of-body states; Wagnerian pantriadic trances (Lehman 50, 71–72 conv.). Hypnosis: falling minor-third cycles under a repeated phrase (Lehman 79 conv.). Short modules with nonfunctional chords feel timeless (Lehman 133 conv.).
- **Colours and devices** - Overlapping, slowly evolving ostinati of different lengths with no percussion: `harp`, `pizzicato`, `celesta`, phasing (Summers 134–35 conv.; parts inf.); the library "hypnotic" ostinato on `flutes`, `celesta`, `harp` (MM ch. 7). Breathy unison `flutes`, quiet (Hayward 47 conv.). Drift: divisi `strings` pp with long swells, soft `organ` pedal, low humming `choir`, `tremolo` ppp (inf.). A crystalline contrapuntal stillness (Chapin 315–18 conv.).
- **With / against** - With ritual, the rite's inner state; with magic, scrying; with dreams, drifting.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a circling `harp` figure. Whole: one ostinato with a slowly turning drone.
- **Not** - Development or climaxes.

#### Fragility
- **Cues** - "Brittle" (MRX), "faint" (YM); the weakness field (VVA 1260). (inf.) Thin, high, exposed textures, soft attacks, long gaps, one voice without support. A close, exposed solo where every hesitation is heard gives vulnerability: mix it close and dry (TLTT 472 inf.).
- **Melody** - Sensibility's "trembling fragility": a plaintive line broken by sighs (Head 264 top.). (inf.) High, pp, slow, short phrases separated by rests, stepwise with small falling sighs; free rubato.
- **Harmony** - (inf.) Thin open voicings with no bass: I or vi as bare thirds and sixths high up, unresolved suspensions; a phrase left hanging on IV or V.
- **Colours and devices** - `solo_violin` or `flutes` high and soft, `celesta`, single high `harp` notes, high soft `tremolo`. The glass harmonica's weightless fragility: `celesta` + `flutes` in thirds pp with slight `wobble`, or very high `violins` pp (Murphy 155–56 conv.). The trembling note: a `wobble` on the resolution of a sigh, after the clavichord *Bebung* (Head 272–73 top.; scoring inf.).
- **With / against** - With sickness, the frail patient; with innocence, the fragile child; with beauty, the precious thing about to break; against weight, one fragile line over a heavy world.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a phrase left with no bass. Whole: a single exposed line.
- **Not** - Lightness, which is carefree: fragility could break.

### F. Time and age

#### Youth
- **Cues**: Quick, energetic dance music made a product sound young (§8.4 [M]; §8.6 r18). High register, bright timbre, wide range, rising lines (*Voice and register*); Tagg files childhood and freshness with lightness, and high pitch reads as light (VVA 1155-56; MM ch. 8). Tagg's tests split youth in two. Youth **as culture** comes from an ensemble the young could play themselves; youth **as market** comes from glossy production (a rock-band tune drew teenagers at 4.5×) [TLTT pp. 607-621] [T]. Child images came only from a festive brass march (parade, ice-cream) and a lone pentatonic tune [TLTT p. 251] [T].
- **Melody**: Arching rising fourths and fifths; the Lydian #4 for a young hero's optimism (SW 24, conv.; Lehman pp. 16, 62 conv.). For a small child, a cheerful 2/4 folk-like song (Monelle p. 251 conv.). Short, quick, high phrases at a brisk tempo (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Lydian I ↔ II rocking for youthful energy [Lehman pp. 3, 16] top.; plain major triads around it (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: Flute, violins, high clarinets; *Yearning*, *The sidekick's answer*; glockenspiel and celesta for the nursery (MM ch. 9); the children's-song topic, uncanny in horror (T108, GRAB). A young adventuring band as "their own" music: kit, a low unison riff in cellos, basses and bassoons, rough trombone fifths, one lead with `slide` on trumpets or solo_violin [TLTT pp. 618-621] (inf.).
- **With / against**: The hunt stands for youth, vigour and danger overcome (Siegfried; Méhul's young king) [Monelle pp. 76, 95-96; Lowe pp. 625-26] top. Pastoral time is the time of children and young lovers [Monelle pp. 189, 271] top. Growth: the theme drops an octave over the arc (*Voice and register*). Hidden destiny: the villain's cells tucked inside the child's tune (SW 24).
- **Intensity**: A hint is the tune moved high. The whole piece is a quick high dance, or the band riff.
- **Not**: Tuba or basses leading, unless that is the joke. Adult love idioms: the slow madd9 love tune scored zero for young people [TLTT pp. 441, 463] [T]. Hyperactive rock rhythm when you want "for children": avoiding it is what made a tune family-safe [TLTT p. 375] [T].

#### Innocence
- **Cues**: Simplicity, consonance and regular metre, processed easily from birth (§8.2 [S]); lullaby or play-song register (§8.6 r8-9); low energy, low stress (MRX; YM); the pure and unspoiled sit with lightness (VVA 1158). Children's carefree music works by leaving out conflict, passion and thought [TLTT p. 430] [T]/(inf.). Purity, major and few dissonances carry mystical innocence [Brill pp. 25, 36] conv. The singing style and the folk tone mean innocence, nature and home, and feel familiar on first hearing [Day-O'Connell pp. 249-254; Monelle p. 223] top.
- **Melody**: Singable at once by anyone: close to the tonic, stepwise, a range of a fifth to an octave, no hard leaps or flourishes, strophic, complete without its bass [Day-O'Connell pp. 249-254; Monelle pp. 220-221] top. Triple time with dotted lilts, or 6/8; it can start with no introduction, like a soliloquy [Monelle pp. 220-221; Head pp. 271-272] top.
- **Harmony**: I, IV and V only, nothing distant [Monelle pp. 220-221] top.; plain major triads give the toy quality [TLTT pp. 494-495] (inf.). Childlike awe: Lydian I ↔ II, then a lift up a major third; a child's near-holy wonder: V7 landing on bIII and staying [Lehman pp. 3, 16, 200-201] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Celesta and glockenspiel; *Nostalgic music box*, *Lullaby*, *Cherubic Choir*; the music-box topic (high tinkle, plain tune, mechanical regularity, T106). Eden before sin: a tender oboe solo over rippling violins, voices paired in thirds (Haydn's *Creation*) [Monelle pp. 240-241] conv. The musette for naive simplicity [Haringer p. 205] top. The innocent outsider: wordless choir, a melancholy waltz, celesta, warm strings [Binns p. 140; Halfyard Intro p. 12] conv.
- **With / against**: **Against threat**, *Innocence corrupted*: the same music a little too slow, with `wobble`, in minor or over a drone (T106). Lost innocence on minor iv6 chords (Morricone) [TLTT p. 196] top. With memory, children in a summer meadow [TLTT p. 251] [T]. Music heard among ordinary folk only during wondrous acts marks wonder and strangeness at once (see **The outsider**) [Binns pp. 142-145] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one music-box phrase. The whole piece is a lullaby.
- **Not**: `wobble` or drones unless corruption is meant. Coarseness: rustic innocence is never crude (Sulzer) [Haringer pp. 207-208] top. Pentatonic as automatically "childlike", a label Tagg shows to be patronising [TLTT pp. 531-532] conv. Treating the folk tone as always safe, since it also feeds nationalism [Monelle pp. 257, 269-270] top.

#### Age and antiquity
- **Cues**: Slow, low, deliberate (§1 Register; Tagg Death); the perfect fourth suggested the old and desolate (§1 Intervals [W]). Once major became the everyday norm, minor and the modes sounded old, then nostalgic (Tagg Death; TLTT pp. 312-313 conv.). Old styles run on without interruption [Sutcliffe pp. 129-131] top. A dated idiom carries its date: play a bygone era's idiom straight [TLTT pp. 539-550] [T]; a 6/8 band march drew "olden times" at 3.2× [TLTT pp. 399-409] [T]. Players prefer film's version of the past [Summers pp. 110-112] conv.
- **Melody**: Stile antico (long white notes, suspensions, imitation) or Romantic Baroque (fugal entries, driving semiquavers) [Dick p. 124] top. A chant-like whole-note line over a modern accompaniment [Rumph p. 505] top. Old folk: la-pentatonic, dorian, aeolian; one b7 can call up "days of yore" [TLTT pp. 322-326 conv.; Lehman p. 16 conv.]. A slightly flat b7, one-note horn calls and calls in fourths sound medieval or immemorial [Monelle pp. 35-37, 104, 170] top.
- **Harmony**: No dominant: minor v → i [TLTT pp. 188-189] top.; i - v - bIII - bVII on long chords for esoteric antiquity; I - bVII - iiø7 - I [Lehman pp. 114, 238-239] conv. Planing (organum) and closed parallel fifths at a cadence for epic antiquity [Lehman pp. 81-82, 238] conv. 7-6 suspension chains, a passacaglia bass in i, a half-diminished chord in a circle-of-fifths chain for "Baroque" [Sutcliffe pp. 129-131; TLTT p. 183] top.
- **Colours and devices**: Organ, low choir, modal chant, learned counterpoint (T019); drone minor (Mussorgsky's *Old Castle*) [TLTT p. 321] conv. Archaic bourrée, gavotte and chorale; by 1900 sarabande and minuet mean "the ancient world" [Dick p. 124; Monelle pp. 264-267] top.; the pavane (T072 [gen.]). Classical antiquity: no bowed strings, harp as lyre, oboe + english_horn as aulos, two flutes with two harps and celesta [Brill pp. 29, 32, 34; Monelle p. 267] conv. Ancient empire: forced brass, hammering cellos and basses, one wailing high voice (choir or solo_violin), open-fifth horns [Summers p. 111] conv. Medieval: open-interval trombones, hunt calls mixed in [Dick pp. 119, 126] top.
- **With / against**: With wisdom and stone; with legend, the told past; lament + archaism = an elegy for a fallen empire [Caplin pp. 446-47] top. Elder races keep their own harmony system (R12).
- **Intensity**: Hint: a modal or chorale cadence, or a drone under a tune. Strong: a stile antico passage. Whole: a fugue, an old dance, or a slow modal piece [Dick pp. 106, 124] top.; ladder (inf.).
- **Not**: A busy modern pulse. Doh-pentatonic with major sixths, which reads as frontier, not age (correction, TLTT pp. 319-326 conv.). Organum, parallel fifths and the Picardy third as the 19th-century archaizing topic: Dickensheets does not list them (T095 [gen.] → (inf.)); Lehman's film planing is the stronger support. Foreign colour as age: ethnic markers say "elsewhere" [TLTT pp. 329-330] (inf.). One European art-music history for every people [Summers pp. 96-97] conv.

#### Memory and nostalgia
- **Cues**: One of the most often felt musical emotions (§7.2 [S]; GEMS sentimental, dreamy, nostalgic). Its main route is episodic memory: bring back a known theme, slower and thinner (§8.1 [M]; §7.6 r11; §8.6 r5); a returning theme is a chill trigger (§4). The oboe pastoral in 6/8 over-scored on nostalgia; *Emmerdale* drew remembering 4.5× and nostalgia 4.1× [TLTT pp. 513-515] [T]; minor and modes carry the past (Tagg Death). The pastoral lives in the long ago, cut off by time [TLTT pp. 517-519 conv.; Monelle pp. 186-187] top. Looking back on what just happened needs irregular periods and blurred harmony, not slowness [TLTT p. 604] [T].
- **Melody**: The old theme, slower and thinner; for a trauma flashback, slight augmentation; after a long gap, an exact reprise beats a variation [Summers p. 170] conv. Four-bar diatonic Classical phrases for comfortable nostalgia for a recent past (Biedermeier) [Dick pp. 114-15] top. Nostalgic sighs (*An die Musik*) [TLTT pp. 218-220] top. A "Paramount" ending: drawn-out major cadence, tune rising 2 → 3 [Lehman p. 264] conv.
- **Harmony**: iv - I, the mixed plagal "harmonic nostalgia" (T157, LEH13); iv - iiø7 - I or I - bVII - iiø7 - I for old Hollywood [Lehman pp. 40, 201, 238-239] conv. (Lehman: the iv → I cadence works through what listeners expect of old films more than through what they did, p. 264 conv.). Minor iv6 for country-house nostalgia (Elgar) [TLTT p. 195] top. A flat-side bVI or bIII over a drone for a vision of an absent place [Galand pp. 466-67] top.
- **Colours and devices**: *Nostalgic music box*, *The half-remembered tune*, *Musical Nod*, *Melancholy Major Key*; oboe or english_horn, a modal tune, reverb, slow surface (Tagg). Timbre-quotation rule: a call on its own instrument is present; on violins or clarinets it is remembered [Agawu p. 490] conv. A horn call set high over a minuet turns an adventure into a childhood memory [Hatten pp. 516-19] top. Horn fifths mean distance, memory, farewell [Sisman p. 102] top.; old dances (minuet, waltz) mean a vanished society [Monelle p. 6; McKee p. 168] top.
- **With / against**: An idyll broken by solemn trombones [Monelle pp. 253-254] conv.; a reverie broken and restored [Sisman p. 110] top. Players bind game themes to their own lives; one fixed signal each session builds nostalgia [Summers pp. 15-16, 158-169] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one bar of an old theme. The whole piece is that theme softer and slower.
- **Not**: Fresh material: there is nothing to recall unless the theme was heard before. A strong modern lead kept over the evocative timbre [TLTT pp. 382-383, 472] (inf.). The alphorn for homesickness as Monelle's: only conv. (T002) (correction).

#### Fate
- **Cues**: Inevitability through repetition (§1 Form); a motif acting as an agent (§7.1 [W]); exact repetition of the same cue for a prophecy that keeps coming [Winters pp. 124-126] conv. Tagg's destiny code over-scored for the slow minor-add-nine tune, where adversity is framed as outside forces (5.0×; "impossible" 7.9×) [TLTT pp. 434, 461] [T]. A funeral-tempo low onbeat pulse drew implacable and fateful [TLTT pp. 563-566] [T].
- **Melody**: Repeated melodies over a repeating bass [Dick p. 104] top.; a repeated-note "fate" figure that breaks in on other music [Hatten pp. 527-33] top. A wide falling phrase from an accented high note gives premature closure [TLTT pp. 595-597] [T]; a tutti la-pentatonic run falling an octave to the tonic forces closure [TLTT pp. 630-631] conv. A theme heard before its meaning is known seems to foresee [Murphy p. 152] conv. Slow, low, minor (inf.).
- **Harmony**: The chaconne/passacaglia ground, often a falling tetrachord (T056, WIK top.). The aeolian shuttle: Lehman shows a major bVI reads as myth or awe, so for doom use i ↔ bvi, or keep i - bVI slow, low and dark (MM ch. 9 vs Lehman pp. 100-102 top.; Lehman stronger). Languid bitter fate: slow i - iv over a tonic drone, tune sinking b7 - b6 - 5 [TLTT p. 189] top. One key held relentlessly; doom announced by a jump up a semitone into minor; the far fifth I ↔ v as portent [Lehman pp. 41, 56, 100] conv. (Prophecy harmonies: see **Prophecy and the oracle**.)
- **Colours and devices**: Timpani (conv.), bells, *Doom*, *Bell ostinato*, *Musical Spoiler*; a chaconne ground in basses; the Dies irae for the Last Judgement (T090). A cosmic contest: palindromic stepwise ostinato with fierce chant (SW 25). Destiny fulfilled: choir and horns, triumphant at last (LOTR [S]).
- **With / against**: With death, a doom. **Against the hero**: hero's theme against a fate ostinato; whichever ends the piece wins (*Themes in conflict*). With tragedy, inevitability.
- **Intensity**: Hint: a timpani figure under other music, or the fate figure breaking in once [Hatten] (inf.). Whole: a chaconne or doom ostinato. Full doom: deep rumbles (basses, tuba, organ pedal, taiko rolls) under churning `tremolo` with `slide`, thinning to one pulse, then silence [Hayward pp. 53-54] conv.
- **Not**: Chance (see **Luck and fortune**). A bright, loud major bVI shuttle, which turns doom into awe.

#### Night and darkness
- **Cues**: Low register, dark timbre, soft and slow (§1 Timbre (inf.)); night can be peaceful or threatening (§7.4 [W-M]). High is light, low is dark and heavy (MM ch. 8). In Tagg's tests night went with the urban minor jazz and rock tunes, darkness with slow minor jazz (VVA 1250, 3934 [T]); *Streetcar* scored dark 6.6×, black 5.7×, night 3.3× from a minor-six chord on soft mid-low brass over a low pulse [TLTT pp. 539-572] [T]. Night and dungeon scenes take ombra even with nothing supernatural there [McClelland pp. 285-287] top.
- **Melody**: The nocturne: a singing, ornamented cantabile over arpeggios in the tenor register, apart from the bass, often with a second voice a sixth below [Horton pp. 644-45; Dick pp. 106-8] top. Darkness settling in: a leap up then a long stepwise fall; a rising line for peaceful settling; a minor-sixth pick-up can already sound nocturnal [TLTT pp. 443-444] conv. The horn of nocturnal mystery: a slow legato solo horn with faint regret [Monelle pp. 104-107] top.
- **Harmony**: All-minor chromaticism with no clear key for shadowy states [Lehman p. 107] conv.; the darkest horn form: strings throbbing on I5 while the horn falls by semitones [Monelle p. 108] conv. A nocturne episode a semitone below home (bI) inside a busy cue [Horton pp. 653-56] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Orchestral nocturne: harp arpeggios or legato violins2 in broken chords, pizzicato cellos on the bass notes, soft held strings; tune on horns, cellos or clarinets, second voice on bassoons [Dick; Horton] conv./(inf.). Low clarinet, celesta for stars; *Lullaby* for peaceful night, *Dread* for threatening night, *The hour strikes* (T090). A soft horn call at night answered by flute, clarinet and oboe echoes [Monelle pp. 109-110] conv.; a phantom army: muffled winds and pizzicato bass [Monelle p. 174] conv. Low fillers for weight [TLTT p. 465] conv.
- **With / against**: With dawn, a trajectory. With love, a nocturne under the stars [Dick p. 106] top. With the city, the night streets (see **Crime and the underworld**). With sleep, the celestial: flutes and harp, gentle lilting major [McClelland p. 287] top. Slow, quiet, still strings give evening and twilight, not night [TLTT pp. 521-537] [T].
- **Intensity**: A hint is a low clarinet line or one soft horn call. The whole piece is a nocturne.
- **Not**: Treating dark as automatically evil.

#### Dawn, daylight and sun
- **Cues**: Moving a phrase up an octave makes it happier (§1 Register [M]); brightening timbre, arrival in major, a crescendo and widening range (§7.6 r4; §4). Sunrise, morning, summer and sun went to slow legato tunes, a temperate northern light (romance: summer 105‰, sun 46‰) [TLTT pp. 158-166, 671] [T]. Chromatic major chords sound brighter than diatonic ones [Lehman p. 261] [ev]. Dawn and dew: a very slow pulse (about 50) under a very fast high surface [TLTT pp. 168, 246-247] top. Still, wide-spaced parallel fifths in strings drew both sunrise and sunset, not midday [TLTT pp. 521-537] [T].
- **Melody**: Sisman's sunrise topic: a slow climb in register with a crescendo, instruments joining as it rises [Sisman pp. 106-108] top. Morning as the hunt's hour: a solo horn on a triadic call; first light as a tiny two-horn fanfare, then a plain folk-like tune [Monelle pp. 68-69, 240-241] top./conv. Misty dawn: very slow 6/4, ornate solo oboe over low pedals; sunset: gentle falling triads [Monelle pp. 110, 249-250] conv.; midday: short woodwind fanfares [Monelle p. 172] conv.
- **Harmony**: bVI → V6/4 (V with 2 in the bass) under a rising tune feeds sun, morning, spring [TLTT p. 213] top. Warm, static major tonic after long instability [Lehman pp. 237-238] conv.; sunburst: vi - V, then #IV in place of IV, opening a bigger statement a semitone lower [Lehman p. 172] conv. Midday glare: fast full-range arpeggios on I with 3 in the bass [TLTT p. 247] top. Parallel majors drifting over a held tonic (SW 40).
- **Colours and devices**: The sunrise build: cellos and basses pp, then violins2, violins, oboe, flutes, horns in waves over a tonic pedal [Sisman p. 106] (inf.). Sunlight through branches: high soft violins or `tremolo` on one note, distant horns, a clarinet cuckoo [Monelle p. 258] conv. Dew: harp, celesta and high violins over slow chords (inf.). Birdcalls on flute and oboe (T018); *Saved by the Church Bell*; Lydian.
- **With / against**: Against night, a trajectory. With hope, the rise. With the divine, radiance. With the hunt: morning, a distant horn, birdsong [Monelle p. 69] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one chord turning major. The whole piece is a sunrise swell.
- **Not**: Opening bright: begin in darkness so the light can arrive. Low ripples for light: the romance's light score was only average [TLTT pp. 246-249] [T].

#### Decline and fading
- **Cues**: A waning people: a noble theme whose phrases sink at the end, a sanctuary theme turned sad as its folk leave (LOTR Gondor, Rivendell [A]). Low pitch and falling seconds and thirds suggest loss of energy (Tagg Death). The staged diminuendo over a repeated motive means farewell [Sisman pp. 104, 108] top. Twilight: sadness mixed with calm, good days slipping from memory [Monelle p. 186] top. Grieg's slow legato minor for "it's over" and evening [TLTT p. 321] conv.
- **Melody**: Cadences that fall where they should rise; a realm's anthem breaking into fragments as it falls [Summers p. 96] (inf.). "Downshifting": every voice slides a semitone down into the next chord [Lehman pp. 105-107] conv. A slow falling line in cellos and low violins2 for autumn-as-fading (inf.). Moderate to slow, stately (inf.).
- **Harmony**: One bright chord swapped for its minor (bVI → bvi) as the tide turns [Lehman p. 181] conv.; modulation down a step for sighing release [Lehman pp. 54, 62-64] top. A lament bass + archaism = an elegy for a fallen empire [Caplin pp. 436-47] top./(inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: Harp arpeggios thinning; a horn call answered more faintly each time: offstage horns receding (falling `dynamics`, fewer horns per call) [Monelle pp. 80-81] conv./(inf.). *Requiem for a Landmark*; the pavane's dignity (T072); the minuet as the old regime's weight [McKee p. 168] top.
- **With / against**: Decline is loss with dignity; decay is putrefaction. With age, an elder race; with renewal, the long arc back (LOTR Fourth Age). Magic leaving a world over a campaign: state the main theme less and less, cut it to its first phrase, finally drop celesta, harp and choir [Webster pp. 207-210] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one cadence falling where it should rise, or one phrase repeated softer. The whole piece is a staged diminuendo, instruments leaving one by one [Sisman p. 108] (inf.).
- **Not**: Ruin or horror; keep the loss dignified. Autumn as automatically sad: in the topic tradition autumn is the euphoric hunting season (correction, Monelle pp. 58, 69-70, 95 top.); use the falling melancholy only for autumn as fading.

#### Legend and lore
- **Cues**: The bardic dialect, now checked against Dickensheets: sweet melodies and a plaintive solo theme, declaimed only now and then, minor turning to a tender major, sudden mood changes that keep up the sense of an epic being recited [Dick pp. 126-27] top. (Correction: the modal/pentatonic turns and open fifths of T094 [gen.] are not her features; open voicings are the nearest.) Recitative for narration: speech rhythm, sparse chords or tremolo under a free line (T041, GRAB). The late-Romantic idiom makes mythic space [Winters pp. 127-128] conv.
- **Melody**: Narrator's theme in bare octaves [Dick p. 133] top.; stanza-like 4+4+4+5-bar groups with altered endings [Dick n. 41] top. Ballad gap-fill: arpeggio up the minor tonic to 5, a quick falling fill, a zigzag down through the dominant to 1 [TLTT pp. 379-380] conv. Mythic idiom: a falling-fifth motif in dotted compound duple, and the 2+3 "Bruckner rhythm" [Winters pp. 119-122] conv.
- **Harmony**: Parallel chords, open voicings, keys a third apart between sections (i → bVI → #iii), parallel suspensions after the narrator's octaves [Dick pp. 126, 133] top. i → bVI for mythic weight; I → v for venerability; a modal four-chord loop on long chords for the told past [Lehman pp. 100, 114] top./conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Harp is central, real or evoked (pizzicato rolled upward, quick broken chords in clarinets and bassoons) [Dick p. 126] top./conv.; the bard's voice on english_horn or cellos [Dick pp. 113-14] conv.; heraldic trumpets grown from the bard's theme [Dick p. 127] top. Ossianic colour: choir with two distant horns and low-middle harp [digest] conv. A mythic hymn for choir and orchestra opening and closing the story [Summers p. 17] conv.
- **With / against**: Ballad frame: narrator's octaves and harp open and close; rough "harp" chords break into the tale [Dick pp. 133-36] top. One motif, a topic per character (bardic, Lied, low style, heroic) [Dick pp. 133-35] top.; one bard theme re-dressed per tale [Agawu pp. 479-80] conv. With fate, prophecy (see **Prophecy and the oracle**). In-world songs can later enter the underscore [Summers pp. 178-179, 213] conv.
- **Intensity**: Hint: a bare-octave phrase over a single rolled harp chord. Strong: the bard's tune for one solo instrument with harp, plus one spoken-style passage. Whole: open the frame, state the theme, turn it to tender major, tell a battle chapter (fanfare into tempest into heroic), close the frame [Dick pp. 125-27] top.; ladder (inf.).
- **Not**: Free speech-rhythm throughout [Dick pp. 126-27] top. Banning battle: large bardic works absorb tempest, military and heroic for their battles; keep the narrator's frame around them (correction, Dick p. 127 top.).

#### Renewal and rebirth
- **Cues**: The budding and new sit with lightness and openness; bursting forth with eruptive motion (VVA 1155, 562). Birdsong as harbinger of spring (T018, HAT23). Restoration themes: a reborn kingdom, nature striking back with rising chords on a high voice or violins (LOTR [A][S]). Hatten's pastoral, threat, pastoral regained (HAT23).
- **Melody**: The rising-register crescendo of a sunrise [Sisman pp. 106-108] top.; the old theme restored to its first colour (*Corruption* reversed); a lament bass turned to rise in the major [Caplin pp. 444-46] top. A rising sequence for raising the dead [Summers p. 77] conv. Moderate, lifting, major (inf.).
- **Harmony**: The epiphany form: start tonally vague, clarify step by step, blaze out on one big major chord [Lehman p. 227] top. iii → I, the Mass's path from Crucifixus to Resurrexit [Byros p. 404] top.; the lament's last loop turning to the relative major [Caplin pp. 426-36] top. bVI → V6/4 (2 in the bass) with the tune climbing for spring and opening out [TLTT p. 213] top. Life going on: a clear V7 - I ending on a major triad [Murphy p. 169] conv. A creation lift: the love theme up a tone over a held pedal [Lehman pp. 56, 58] conv.; a threat mode (octatonic) recoloured as mixolydian on the same root [Lehman p. 58] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Birdcalls on flute and oboe; rising string chords; high pure choir. Resurrection: soaring choir with cascading violins [Barron p. 84] conv. Spirits healed: celesta + soft high strings on one held chord [Murphy p. 160] conv.
- **With / against**: Needs the loss first (decline, decay, winter). After a storm, a 6/8 dance of rejoicing; after a funeral march, a hymn then a paradise of winds [Sisman pp. 110-111] conv. Story arc: an unfamiliar static mode (phrygian) at the start, a warm static major at the end [Lehman pp. 237-238] conv. With healing, the land healed; with redemption, a person reborn.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one birdcall over a held chord. The whole piece is the old theme back in its first colour. The eucatastrophe: after silence, the theme in a new key at full scoring [Halfyard App. p. 224] (inf.).
- **Not**: Triumph: rebirth unfolds rather than wins.

#### Immortality (the undying)
*New. Not Undeath (rot, the macabre) or Age and antiquity (the old): the timeless one who outlives everyone.*
- **Cues**: Music beyond ordinary tonality for royalty and the deathless [Stephen pp. 71-74] conv. Slow, sustained, close-layered (inf.). Exact reprise of the theme across arcs makes a figure timeless [Winters pp. 124-127] conv.
- **Melody**: Phrases that leave the question open, ending on a suspended dominant; the tonic touched only in passing [Stephen pp. 67-68] conv. Long notes, narrow steps, no strong downbeat (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Close-voiced add9 chords that belong to no single key (Iadd9, IIadd9, IVadd9, bVIIadd9) [Stephen pp. 71-74] conv. Outliving love: resolution evaded, then a deceptive close onto a hollow minor and a falling chain of minor triads by chromatic thirds [Stephen pp. 67-69] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Close-layered choir + brass on the add9 chords [Stephen pp. 71-74] conv.; strings holding underneath (inf.).
- **With / against**: With royalty, an undying king; with tragic love, the immortal's grief: at the beloved's death strip to one voice [Stephen pp. 67-74] conv. With legend, the unchanged reprise [Winters] conv. Against Undeath: the same deathlessness made rotten takes Undeath's palette instead.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one add9 chord with no key behind it. The whole piece is a layered choral chord stream that ends unresolved [Stephen] (inf.).
- **Not**: Undeath's macabre colours (bones, Dies irae) (inf.). A firm perfect cadence, which brings it back into time (inf.).

#### Time bent (stopped, stretched, looped)
*New. Spells and places where time halts, slows, repeats or folds; not ordinary slowness or stillness.*
- **Cues**: Non-functional modules and ostinati make music that seems outside time [Lehman p. 133] conv. Remote modulations make listeners underestimate durations; awe chains stretch felt time [Lehman pp. 189-191] [ev]/conv.
- **Melody**: A rising sequence that returns to its start, climbing for ever without arriving, like a barber's pole [Lehman pp. 77, 161] top. A short repeating cell (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Time halted: a deceptive cadence, V → vi [Lehman p. 188] conv. Time stretched: a long chord chain with no footholds; time cut short: a chromatic swerve resolved at once [Lehman pp. 189-192] conv. Time and space bent: functional at the edges, a Neapolitan treated as home in the middle, a snap back to a half cadence (i - v - bII ↔ iv - bV - V7) [Lehman pp. 208-210] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: A celesta or harp module repeating while strings hold (inf.); *Bell ostinato* or a ticking pizzicato for clockwork time (format) (inf.).
- **With / against**: With stillness, stasis on one key [Lehman p. 65] top. With fate, the loop that cannot be escaped (inf.). With magic, the spell's moment.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one deceptive cadence as the spell lands. The whole piece is a cycle that rises and returns, then a cadence that breaks out (inf.).
- **Not**: Ordinary slowness: tempo alone reads as calm or grief, not distorted time (inf.).

#### The seasons and the turning year
*New. The year as the story's clock: spring, summer, harvest, autumn, midwinter. Winter as cold is* Ice and cold*, spring as rebirth is* Renewal and rebirth*.*
- **Cues**: In the idyll only seasons and hours mark time [Monelle pp. 189, 195-197] top. Seasonal pieces carry fixed topics: autumn ends in a hunt (Vivaldi), Haydn's *Seasons* has a hunting chorus and a harvest revel, the hunting year closes on St Hubert's day [Monelle pp. 58, 69-79, 242] top. Summer: slow legato strings, warm and wide (TLTT 671 [T]).
- **Melody**: Autumn: triadic horn calls in 6/8, euphoric [Monelle pp. 69-70, 95] top. Harvest: a 6/8 dance with drones and fiddling [Monelle pp. 239, 242] conv. Midwinter: 12/8 pipers' music, two reeds in thirds over a drone, rocking [Monelle pp. 198-200, 229-230] top. Spring: birdsong figures and a 6/8 new-siciliana [Monelle pp. 186, 235-248] top.
- **Harmony**: Tonic and dominant drones for the rural year; at the harvest dance the drone moves with each new key [Monelle pp. 210, 239] top.
- **Colours and devices**: Autumn: horns. Harvest: oboe over a drone with solo_violin fiddling. Midwinter: oboes over a bassoon-and-cello fifth, bells. Spring: flutes and piccolo birds [Monelle] (inf.). Winter cold: see **Ice and cold** (triangle as sleigh bells, celesta).
- **With / against**: With festival, the season's feast (harvest revel, St Hubert's banquet, midwinter) [Monelle pp. 58, 198-200, 242] top. With loss, Arcadia's eternal spring is always already gone [Monelle pp. 186-187] top. With decline, autumn as fading.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one seasonal colour (a horn call, a drone, a birdcall). The whole piece is a seasonal set piece: hunt, harvest dance or pipers' carol (inf.).
- **Not**: Autumn as automatically melancholy [Monelle pp. 69-70, 95] top.

#### The new and the fashionable
*New. The latest thing: an artificer's unveiling, a decadent court's fashions, an upstart guild's showy modernity. The opposite pole of Age and antiquity; about being current, not money (Wealth) or labour (Industry).*
- **Cues**: Tagg's most period-specific responses: modern/today 6.3×, fashion show 10× (exclusive), trendy and flashy 5.9×, "tomorrow's world" [TLTT pp. 607-617, 633-650] [T]. The same up-to-date idiom drew the most scorn (bad, trash, trite) [TLTT pp. 633-643] [T]. Dry, quick, glossy, metronomic (inf.).
- **Melody**: Short hooky cells repeated over a dry insistent pulse; quick bright ornaments with sharp attacks; a note ending in a fast falling `slide` (the substitute for the syndrum "peew") [TLTT pp. 647-650] [T]/(inf.).
- **Harmony**: The latest harmony of its day: Tagg dates quartal minor-eleventh chords to 1959 on [TLTT pp. 577-579] conv. In a fantasy world, take the newest harmony of the setting's own idiom (R12) (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: Dry toms in quick figures, harp glissandi, glockenspiel and celesta sparkle, a metronomic kit pulse (inf.). Avoid any real-world trendy sound [TLTT pp. 647-650] (inf.).
- **With / against**: With **Wealth and luxury**, display; with **Industry, work and machines**, invention; with **Pride**, the fop. **Against Age and antiquity**: the old order's dance answered by the newcomers' dry quick pulse, alternated by moment (inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one sparkling glissando. The whole piece is a dry, quick, glossy loop.
- **Not**: Expecting it to age well: trendy idioms used for everything dated into a bare period marker, and only devices with classic precedents survive [TLTT pp. 553-555, 605-606] conv. The campaign's main voice; use it as satire or local colour.

### G. Nature and the elements

#### Nature and wildness
- **Cues**: **Overlap: to listeners gentle nature *is* the pastoral** (Tagg's tags; the host chose the pastoral sketch for 'nature' (host pairwise test, 2026-10)). For green, kindly nature use *The rural and pastoral*; this entry's own idiom (pedals, open fifths, distant calls, waves at two scales) reads as vast, awakening, untamed - use it for wilderness and scale. Anaphones: figures shaped like natural sound and motion (§7.6 r2 [W-M]); less regular metre for the untamed (inf.). Tagg's library study: "nature" music is tagged pastoral, calm, romantic, nostalgic, archaic, spacious, fresh, and its opposite pole is technology and industry (Tagg Nature conv.; TLTT pp. 517-519 conv.). Nature images come from **gesture**: corn and sea wave, trees sway, rivers flow, one gesture at many scales (hill range, bay, tree, hair) [TLTT pp. 252-271] top.; listeners named meadows, sea and rivers consistently [TLTT p. 254] [T]. Two scales at once: a slow swell plus fast inner notes for leaves, spray, haze [TLTT pp. 171-172, 246] top.
