- **Melody**: A falling fourth as nature's own interval (Mahler's clarinet cuckoo; a game world's life-force motif) [Monelle p. 258; Summers p. 173] conv. Arpeggio figures born of pipes and horns [Rumph pp. 507-11] top. Birdsong: stylised calls, trills, repeated notes, the falling cuckoo third (T018, HAT23).
- **Harmony**: The far fifth I → v (or i → IV) for nature, depth and vastness [Lehman pp. 100-101] conv. Klangfläche: one pedal across octaves, tonic and dominant merged, layered rhythms, sonority over function [Horton p. 660] conv. Feral: whole-tone; primitive: an octatonic bass in minor thirds under tritone-related sevenths [Lehman pp. 43, 249] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Nature theme: long open-fifth pedals, distant fanfares, birdcalls (T017 [gen.]). The Mahler 1 model: one high soft note held across the violins (or `tremolo`), falling fourths in woodwinds, a cuckoo, distant soft trumpets [Monelle p. 258; digest] conv. Paired flutes piping or trilling in thirds [Sisman pp. 106-107 top.; Monelle p. 235 conv.]. *Avian Flute*; horns for woodland. Wild beasts: low horns and polyrhythmic toms; an indifferent wild land: low flutes, menacing toms, wordless male choir [TLTT pp. 377-378] conv. Insects: fine flutter (MM 498).
- **With / against**: **Against industry**: keep the natural realm acoustic, choral and string-warm, give the corrupting power percussive, metallic sound, and let that sound invade [Barron pp. 80-92] conv. (Tagg Nature; LOTR). Figure and ground: a slow broad tune over smaller, faster waves on the same harmony = someone moving through a living landscape [TLTT p. 269] [T]. With peace, the pastoral; with age, an ancient wood.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one birdlike flute figure. The whole piece is a nature theme emerging from silence, or a wild polyrhythm.
- **Not**: Courtly regularity. Harsh, jagged gestures (anvils, crags): the wave stack drew nothing rough or cold [TLTT p. 266] [T]. Expecting birdsong or storm figures to be recognised unaided: name them [Monelle pp. 243-244] top.
- **Tell apart** - From *The rural and pastoral*: pastoral is tamed and human-sized (drone + 6/8 + oboe, dance lilt, cadences); wild is vast and unmetred (no dance metre, irregular phrases, low horns, wide spacing, animal energy). Name the wildness in the brief or it will be heard as pastoral.

#### The sea and waters
- **Cues**: Swelling and ebbing dynamics, a rocking 6/8 (§1 Change). The romance tune drew open sea at six times average: continuous, regular, wavy figures in the **low-middle register**, a smooth rolling tune, no sharp edges or shocks (TLTT 249-252; MM ch. 13 [T]). Correction: higher, quicker regular figures read as glitter (or a garden, if short and rising); rustle and flutter need sporadic, irregular high flecks [TLTT pp. 233-249] top. Water by type [TLTT pp. 233-240] top.: **brook**, one-beat waves of 4+ quick notes spanning a sixth to an octave; **river**, longer, wider waves in mid register over moving harmony; **lake**, gentle 3+3 ripples over a pedal; **calm sea**, a long low-middle swell with no eruptive surges (the romance scored 0 on eruption [T]).
- **Melody**: Barcarolle: moderate 6/8 rocking like an oar, a tune in thirds or sixths (T099, WIK top.; gondolier song, Dick Table 3 top.). Zefiro: murmuring neighbour-note sixteenths in violins in thirds, solo flute above [Sisman p. 101] top. Lake tune: arched, legato, closing on a falling sixth [TLTT pp. 238-239] top. Sailors: fast modal-minor 6/8 for hard travel and danger [TLTT pp. 305-307] conv.; gigue and hornpipe (T059 [gen.]).
- **Harmony**: River = directional (moving bass, sequences, modulation), a passing companion; lake = static tonic pedal, a backdrop [TLTT pp. 236-239] top. The far fifth I ↔ v for nautical adventure; the deep: an exposed I ↔ v that later becomes a choral hymn [Lehman pp. 61, 76, 258] conv. A sea god: i → bvi moves and polychords (Holst's Neptune) [Lehman p. 255] conv. Oceanic calm: all voices sliding down [Lehman pp. 105-107] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Brook: pizzicato and high harp. River: harp or divided cellos mid-register. Lake: harp ripples over held basses, distant high celesta [TLTT pp. 233-239] top./conv. A big swell: divided cellos in legato broken chords, never pizzicato (correction, TLTT pp. 171-172, 233-236 conv.). *Under the sea*, *Accordion to Most Sailors* (clarinets and a drone), *Merry in Minor Key*. A ship sets out: slow timpani or taiko plus a trumpet fanfare [Brill pp. 31-32] conv.; arrives: a snare roll and a harp chord [Summers p. 120] conv. Styx: the barcarolle in minor (T099).
- **With / against**: Against calm sea, storm: shipwrecks are tempesta and need surges [McClelland pp. 280-294; TLTT pp. 239-240] top. Deep menace: a bass `slide` under the ripple; inescapable doom from the waters: deep rumbles under churning `tremolo` with `slide` [Hayward p. 53] conv. With the army: a fanfare answered softly by the sea motive, for a seafaring people [Monelle pp. 174-175] conv./(inf.). Archipelago: one theme per island [Summers p. 75] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one rising and falling harp figure. Scale up from brook to river to sea by making the waves longer, wider and lower [TLTT pp. 235, 243] top. The whole piece is a seascape or barcarolle.
- **Not**: A strong beat or staccato under water. A tonic pedal under a river; wide, low, long waves for a brook; surges and brass hits on a calm sea [TLTT pp. 236-240] top.

#### Sky, air and flight
- **Cues**: High register, lightness (inf.); floating and lifted feelings (§7.4). The soaring topic: leaps up of a fifth or more, stepwise climbing, harp sweeps, ever-rising arpeggios, the Lydian #4, high strings and winds over a steady motor rhythm (T164, ATK19; LAV top.). Gliding and floating need hyper-legato, no more than moderato, no sudden changes or sharp attacks; the legato wave texture scored floating, flying, clouds and sky [TLTT pp. 131, 158-166] top./[T]. Gusts: sporadic, sudden small high events over a steady wave [TLTT pp. 248-249] top. Whole-tone harmony maps onto weightlessness [Lehman pp. 52-53] top. Brilliance once meant twinkling stars [Ivanovitch pp. 332-341] top.
- **Melody**: Rising fifths and a steady climb; a rising harp figure for ascent [Brill p. 34] conv.; zefiro murmurs for breezes [Sisman pp. 101, 111] top.; the rising-register crescendo for a climb into the sky [Sisman pp. 106-108] top. A first flight: the world theme in rhythmic diminution [Summers p. 170] conv.
- **Harmony**: Lydian I → II (T164). bVI → V6/4 (2 in the bass) with a rising tune feeds "air, on high, open views" [TLTT p. 213] top. Lift-off: a step modulation timed to leaving the ground, or a whole-tone lift with applied dominants [Lehman pp. 55, 57] conv. Rescue into flight: a dire aeolian lifted a minor third (i → biii), then minor triads moving by thirds [Lehman pp. 166-167] conv. A flying beast's awe: i ↔ bVI [Lehman p. 260] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Flutes, piccolo, high violins, harp runs and glissandi, celesta, *Ethereal Choir*; horns on a broad rising sixth for a sky-city. Weightless floating: celesta + flutes in thirds pp with `wobble` [Murphy pp. 155-156] conv. A flying creature first seen: harp shimmer with glockenspiel and triangle [Brill pp. 35-36] conv. A breeze: occasional high harp notes or a flute flick over the waves (inf.).
- **With / against**: With freedom, flight. With the storm, see **The storm**. With the monster, the dragon ride: soaring over a low brass motif.
- **Intensity**: A hint is a high flute line or one harp glissando. The whole piece is the soaring topic: rising fifths over a motor rhythm.
- **Not**: Low brass leading; a mechanical ostinato (machine flight). A constant high tremolo for wind: it reads as glitter or bustle, so keep the tremolo swell for a storm wind (correction, TLTT pp. 246-249 top.; (inf.)).

#### The storm
- **Cues**: Big fast dynamic swings, dense fast notes, a sudden forte, chromatic runs (§1 DynVar; §4). The **tempesta** topic (the name "Sturm und Drang" is dropped as a myth, McClelland pp. 280-282 top.): fast, chromatic, diminished sevenths, wide and augmented leaps, rapid scales, tremolo, repeated notes, driving syncopated rhythm broken by pauses, full octave-doubled texture, loud and accented, brass and timpani (T028; McClelland p. 282 top.). Usually minor, D or C minor [Galand Table 16.1] top.; but a **major** tempest serves battle or struggle (correction, Dick p. 116 top.). Thunder is a timpani roll [Dick p. 115; MM ch. 9] top.; low string tremolo, agitated high strings for wind and rain [Dick p. 115] top.
- **Melody**: Wind: running quavers that undulate and turn without warning [Dick p. 115] top.; a leaf-like motif (four falling semiquavers, a leap, a falling resolution) passed between flute and oboe, re-transposed each time [Dick p. 116] top. Rocket arpeggios for lightning flashes [Hatten p. 518] top. (Both hold; Dickensheets warns against rockets alone for wind.) A huge chromatic plunge from the top of the orchestra to the bottom [McClelland pp. 294-296] top.
- **Harmony**: vii°7, augmented sixths and bII over a dominant pedal [McClelland p. 282] top. Lightning: a sudden tutti V7, or darker, vii°7 over the dominant root in winds and low brass [Dick p. 116] top. Thunder: tritone-related sevenths I7 ↔ bV7, biggest on the widest shots [Lehman pp. 138-139, 228] conv. Enter by a deceptive cadence or a plunge into the relative minor [Galand pp. 463-65; Agawu p. 484] conv.; subsiding to a Picardy third [McClelland pp. 289-290] top.
- **Colours and devices**: Strings to the fore, full brass, timpani; trombones bursting in and timpani doubled for a deluge [McClelland pp. 282, 294-296] top. Kit: low `tremolo`, violins running, flutes and oboe tossing the motif, timpani rolls, a tutti `hits` chord for lightning [Dick] conv. An earthquake: tempesta in unison [Sisman p. 102] top.
- **With / against**: With rage or the divine, a god's wrath; tempest also covers elemental spirits and the demonic [Dick p. 115] top. With the pastoral: calm, storm, thanksgiving, the kind god after the angry one [Sisman pp. 100-101; McClelland pp. 280-282] top. With the sea, see **The sea and waters**. Inside a bard's tale, the storm chapter [Dick p. 127] top.
- **Intensity**: Hint: one timpani roll. Strong: wind quavers over low tremolo with one lightning chord. Whole: a three-part storm, approach, raging middle, subsiding [McClelland pp. 289-290; Dick pp. 115-16] top.
- **Not**: An even dynamic level: storms move in gusts and lulls. Relying on storm figures alone to be read: narrate it [Monelle pp. 243-244] top.

#### Fire
- **Cues**: No research on fire itself. "Fiery" loads on the GEMS power factor and sits with the aggressive, volatile, high-energy, high-stress moods (GEMS; MRX C5): fire as mood is power plus agitation. Tempesta covers conflagrations and flames [McClelland pp. 280-282] top. Flicker, glimmer and quiver are fine-motor anaphones (VVA 5421; MM 498); tremolo is the convention for trembling (MM ch. 9). Fast arpeggios fool the ear the way a whirled coal looks like a ring [Ivanovitch pp. 342-348] top. Warmth went to slow legato tunes (TLTT 671 [T]).
- **Melody**: Wildfire: flickering fast figures, rising runs, trills, irregular staccato crackle, fast tempo (inf.). Hearth: a slow, warm legato tune in the middle register (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Wildfire takes tempesta harmony: diminished sevenths, chromatic movement (inf. from McClelland). A city burning: the hexatonic pole I → bvi for catastrophe [Lehman p. 101] conv. Hearth: plain I, IV, V (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: Piccolo and trumpets for brightness, tremolo violins, glockenspiel sparks, timpani rolls for the roar; a heat shimmer as `wobble` on long notes (inf.). A ring of fire: rapid harp and flute arpeggios circling one chord [Ivanovitch] (inf.). Hellfire: the Furies' tempesta with trombones [McClelland pp. 289-290] top.; *The infernal*. A hearth: *The tavern*'s warmth (VVA 2634). A volcano: low toms and taiko under a slow chromatic rise in trombones (inf.); game lava music uses low percussion (T181, LAV; its South Asian colours are a stereotype, R12).
- **With / against**: Destructive fire takes rage and power; a hearth fire takes tenderness and home. With the monster, dragonfire. With the storm, the burning city's tempesta.
- **Intensity**: A hint is a flickering string figure. The whole piece is a blaze.
- **Not**: A single fire sound: the hearth and the wildfire call for opposite music.

#### Ice and cold
- **Cues**: A corpus of 160+ winter game tracks: heavy reverb (82%), twinkling broken chords, metal percussion of every kind, plucked strings, no skin drums, mostly major 4/4, sometimes a skating waltz; one signifier (sleigh bells) can stand for all (T174, LW23 conv., corpus count). Snow is silent, so score it as touch and movement (Semio99 p. 27). "Wintry" is low energy, high stress (MRX). Emotional cold ("ice-cold nerves") came from high, dissonant, chromatic violin lines over static harmony [TLTT pp. 539-550, 588-590] [T]. Haydn's winter introduction is ombra: slow, sombre, sustained [McClelland p. 294] top.
- **Melody**: Sparse, slow, high lines with small intervals; twinkling broken-chord figures (inf. from T174). For the frozen wild, a soft solo horn cantilena over still strings (Sibelius 5) [Monelle p. 107] conv.
- **Harmony**: Mostly major (T174). The polar unknown: a hexatonic pole (i → #III, E♭ minor to G major) then a slide [Lehman pp. 120-124] conv. The polar sublime: a wall of all-major chords, the orchestra as organ [Lehman p. 252] conv. An arctic reveal: a deep bass plunge to a far minor chord, then a tritone [Lehman pp. 139-141] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Celesta, glockenspiel, harp, bells, triangle for sleigh bells, pizzicato, high soft `tremolo` (glass harmonica as celesta + high strings); heavy reverb, no drums. Benign, magical ice: very high strings with celesta sparkle; deadly ice: harsh high string dissonance, `tremolo` with `wobble` or `unhinge` [TLTT p. 589] conv. *The north*, *Bell ostinato* for a winter hunt, a skating waltz (T066).
- **With / against**: With cruelty, a cold heart; with stillness, a frozen world; with festival, midwinter; with loneliness, the winter journey. With the forest, the cold northern wood under a soft horn [Monelle p. 107] conv. With the uncanny: ice is high dissonant string lines over static harmony, fog is a chromatic flute over a low pedal (correction, TLTT pp. 588-590 conv.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one celesta glint. The whole piece is sparse, reverberant and drumless, with twinkling arpeggios.
- **Not**: Drums. Warm cellos and horns leading the glittering winter palette, though a soft solo horn may lead a cold wood (correction). The Winterlied as the sound of cold: it is defined by longing with pain (Dick n. 18 top.), so for cold it is name-only (inf.); see **Longing**.

#### Earth and stone
- **Cues**: Low register for dignity and weight (§1 Register); unisons and octaves; firm, regular rhythm; slow. Hevner's heavy and dignified both win on low pitch and firm rhythm (HEV cl.2, cl.8). Stone, mountain and cliff fields (VVA 267, 322, 3412). Dwarven halls: lost grandeur and rising threat in low brass (LOTR [A]). An earthquake is tempesta in unison [Sisman p. 102] top.
- **Melody**: Walking statues: majestic, ponderous dotted rhythm on triadic lines [McClelland p. 284] top.; the stone guest stamps and the ground opens: three loud repeated trombone notes, fierce dotted figures, a chromatic descent [McClelland p. 288] top. A dwarven work-dance on the bourrée: the pickup is **two eighths on the last quarter** lifting onto beat 1, with dotted-quarter + eighth and weight on the downbeat (correction, Zbikowski pp. 144, 151-160 top.; its documented sense is courtly swagger, not rustic).
- **Harmony**: A dwarven hall: almost purely aeolian root-position triads with wordless choir; a root-position bVI on the reveal; a perfect cadence into bIII when the view returns to the heroes [Lehman pp. 177-178] conv. Earthy folk get diatonic harmony, magical races chromatic [Lehman p. 178] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: *Under the mountain* (low male choir, trombones and tuba, cellos and basses, an anvil rhythm as bells and taiko, an echo). "Granite pillars": block chords in low brass and choir [Lehman p. 166] conv./(inf.). Earth-deep power: a low pedal on basses, bassoons and tuba with slow swells, soft taiko, low choir [Hayward pp. 43, 51-52] (inf.). Basses, timpani.
- **With / against**: Reinforced by age and endurance. With industry, the forge. With the underground, the hall inside the mountain. With the monster, a stone giant or walking statue.
- **Intensity**: A hint is a low octave doubling. The whole piece is a stone hall.
- **Not**: High bright woodwinds leading. Imitating a didjeridu or its slowed drones as "primal earth": the source film's use was appropriation [Hayward pp. 43-46, 51-52] conv.

#### The underground
- **Cues**: Dark, low, slow; reverberant envelopment (§7.4); low energy with tension (§7.6 r10). The game cave topic: low activity, heavy reverb, minor, thin texture, low instruments, chromaticism, a mallet colour (T179, LAV). Reverb builds the room and cramped figures mean enclosure (MM ch. 8; VVA 1253, 304). Underworlds and dungeons are ombra [McClelland pp. 279, 285-287] top. Dungeon practice: short loops chosen by location, changed by events (a kill, a new area), fading after about 30 s without movement [Summers pp. 35-36] conv.
- **Melody**: Narrow-range figures for tunnels; phrases echoed softer; slow, minor, few notes (inf.). Something rising from the depths: the demonic's low rising chromatic scale [Dick pp. 118-19] top.
- **Harmony**: Minor, chromatic, over static pedals (T179). A vast hall: the aeolian root-position triads of a dwarven city (see **Earth and stone**) [Lehman pp. 177-178] conv. A crypt: ombra's vii°7 and surprise chords (McClelland) (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: *Dungeon ambience*, *Drone of Dread*, *Under the mountain*'s echo; the mallet colour as harp and pizzicato. Gnomes and earth-spirits: fairy music moved down into bassoons, low clarinets and cellos [Dick p. 123] top. A deep world cut off from humankind: no bowed strings, harp alone with winds and brass [Brill pp. 28-29] conv.
- **With / against**: With wonder, a huge cave: wide spacing and long reverb in place of the tunnels' tight figures. With dread, the crypt. With the alien, the underdark. A lich's crypt: ombra while the threat speaks, tempesta when it strikes [McClelland pp. 287-290] (inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one phrase echoed softer. The whole piece is a dungeon loop.
- **Not**: Events in the music during a long exploration.

#### The rural and pastoral
- **Cues**: The peace profile (§6 r38); the oboe reads pastoral (§7.5). Tagg's oboe pastoral (6/8 about 72, legato oboe, arpeggios, held strings, circle-of-fifths periods) drew countryside at 4.2× and "always has been" [TLTT pp. 503-514] [T]. **The drone decides**: one and the same singing tune is rustic over drones on I and V and merely songful without them [Sisman p. 93] top.; drone + compound metre are the pair that cannot be removed [Rumph pp. 497-98] top. The topic: major, narrow stepwise tunes, simple harmony, parallel thirds and sixths, soft climaxes, Arcadia as paradise lost (T011, HAR10; HAT23 top.). Haringer's dial: the richer the texture, the more courtly (a fey picnic, not goatherds) [Haringer p. 208] top. Pastures come from a stillness bundle (slow, quiet, bare parallel fifths, blurred metre, static bass), not from pentatonicism (correction, TLTT pp. 531-536 [T]).
- **Melody**: An uncontested legato oboe in 6/8 at about 72, falling by step [TLTT pp. 503, 516-517] [T]. Pastorale: fairly slow 6/8; musette: 6/8 with slurred eighths, slower than a gigue [Haringer pp. 204-205; Monelle p. 229] top. Rustic markers: sharp 4, flat 7, gapped modal scales, Scotch snap, pipes in thirds and sixths [Monelle pp. 207-270; Lowe p. 612] top. Ländler: arpeggiating constant eighths [McKee p. 180; Dick pp. 102-3] top.
- **Harmony**: I - V, nothing distant; drones on I and V with changing chords above (I, IV/I, V/I, ii/I) [Monelle pp. 210, 220-221; Haringer pp. 204-205] top./(inf.); at a rustic dance the drone follows each new key [Monelle p. 239] top. Parallel bare fifths with tied triplets [TLTT pp. 532-533] [T]. Mixolydian for folksy colour [Lehman p. 16] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Oboes and flutes in pairs [Monelle pp. 207-208; Sisman p. 108] top.; the low oboe [TLTT pp. 380-382] conv. Bagpipe: bassoons + cellos pp on an open fifth, oboe or piccolo in thirds above [digest] (inf.). Pastoral horn: soft, echoing, over a drone [Monelle pp. 100-104] top. Ländler: solo_violin, clarinets, pizzicato bass, stamps on toms [Dick; McKee p. 176] conv./(inf.). The musette is a sweet, intimate courtly pipe, not a peasant skirl (correction, Monelle pp. 210-214 top.).
- **With / against**: Defined by its opposite: pastoral, threat, pastoral regained (HAT23; Horton p. 643 top.); industrial sound only as the invader, low chords creeping in under it [Barron pp. 86-92] conv. Heroic + pastoral = inner victory (R11).
- **Intensity**: Hint: two winds in thirds or an oboe over a held fifth. Strong: a 6/8 section. Whole: a pastoral, siciliana or pipers' piece [Monelle] (inf.).
- **Not**: Grand orchestration. Siciliana and musette as one thing: the siciliana is tender, often minor and has no drone; the musette is naive, merry, major (correction; Haringer pp. 204-206; Monelle pp. 215-220 top.). Mixing high and low pastoral unawares [Monelle pp. 227-230] top.
- **Tell apart** - From *Nature and wildness*: see there. For a listener this is the default sound of nature.

#### The forest
- **Cues**: The game forest topic: flutes, strings and wood sounds, a high and a low layer with little between, long pedals, reverb, Dorian (T178, LAV). Woods over-scored for the romantic and pastoral tunes (VVA 314 [T]), and the wind-in-the-woods responses came from a thick mid-register string pad under long low waves [TLTT pp. 158-170, 243-246] [T]. The horn is the woodland's instrument: the forest is the place outside the walls, meaning freedom and innocence but also danger, sorcery and sadness [Monelle pp. 101, 107] top.; at its call the whole forest stops to listen [Haringer pp. 196-197] top.
- **Melody**: By day: triadic horn figures with repeated notes in 6/8 [Monelle pp. 89-90, 98] top./(inf.). By night: the horn of nocturnal mystery, a slow legato solo with faint regret [Monelle pp. 104-107] top. A Dorian tune; birdcalls.
- **Harmony**: Long pedals (T178). Calm woodland paths: the Ave Maria progression (Lully's "Bois épais") [TLTT pp. 173-178] top. An elven wood: phrygian minor jumping to a Lydian major a semitone up [Lehman p. 174] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Horn in its low-middle register, doubled or answered by cellos [Monelle pp. 83-84] top.; one soft distant horn call [digest] (inf.); woodwind birdsong even when unseen [Monelle pp. 15-16] top.; toms and pizzicato for wood. Leaves: sporadic harp notes, high pizzicato or a flute flick (inf.). Forest as cathedral: horns in fifths over `tremolo`, a brass chorale [Winters pp. 122-123] top. Tree-folk: slow low horns and trombones, dry toms (LOTR [T]).
- **With / against**: With the fey, the enchanted wood (night horn, celesta; airy male choir and flutes where lovers meet [Barron p. 83] conv.). With menace, the dark wood: a drone in the empty middle. With the cold, the northern forest under a soft horn [Monelle p. 107] conv. With the hunt, the ranger's day; a ranger's signal: one- or two-note call on solo horn or soft trumpets [Monelle pp. 82, 138-139, 281] top./(inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one distant horn call. The whole piece is the forest topic in Dorian with birdcalls, or a horn nocturne.
- **Not**: A constant high tremolo for leaves (glitter) [TLTT pp. 246-248] top. Double-tongued trumpets, which make a woodsman a parade soldier [Monelle pp. 80, 166] top. The empty middle as a rule for every wood: it fits the deep or dark game forest; a horn-led or windblown wood fills the middle (corrections, Monelle; TLTT).

#### Mountains and heights
- **Cues**: Wide sweeping gestures, a large range and large reverb suggest big space (MM ch. 8); mountains over-scored for the galloping Western, 5.9× (VVA 322; TLTT pp. 281-288 [T]). Up-and-back harp sweeps drew high mountains and waterfalls; a solo-to-ensemble switch drew "switches to mountains" [TLTT pp. 386, 388] [T]. Vistas need slow harmonic rhythm (src-tagg §4 (inf.)). The alphorn call (ranz des vaches) for mountains and distance (T002, HAR10); Monelle finds it too rare to be a topic of its own, its legacy being the pastoral horn's sharp 4 and flat 7 [Monelle pp. 101-104] top. (Monelle stronger).
- **Melody**: Ranz des vaches: triadic, yodel-like, free tempo, with the horn's sharp 4th [Monelle pp. 101-102] top.; the yodel's wide leaps [Hatten n. 10] top. A posthorn-like call in octaves and fifths, slow and far off, over a high still violin chord [Monelle pp. 84, 164-165] conv. A long held top note carrying over the hills [TLTT p. 354] (inf.).
- **Harmony**: bVI → V6/4 (2 in the bass) under a climbing tune feeds mountains and panoramas [TLTT pp. 158-166, 213] top./[T]; harmonic waves (IV → V → I) for hills and valleys [TLTT p. 214] [T]. Beacons lit peak to peak: seven triads climbing by step with one shared note, held in the tune, into a modal cadence and a triumphant theme [Lehman pp. 174-176] conv./(inf.). Vertigo: a slide pair sounded together (i + VII) [Lehman p. 154] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: A solo horn echoed by a second far off; english_horn answered by a distant oboe, or english_horn then horns [Monelle pp. 101-102] conv. Open fifths, wide spacing from basses to high strings, slow chords, cymbal swells. An upland tavern: a 3/4 Ländler on I and V, solo_violin and violins2 arpeggios, stamps on toms, open-fifth drone in cellos and basses [McKee pp. 176-180] (inf.).
- **With / against**: With the sublime, the summit. With adversity, the climb: a low ostinato under the wide texture for a dangerous pass. With dwarves, *Under the mountain* inside the peak.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one echoed horn call. The whole piece is a slow, wide texture rising to a summit chord.
- **Not**: Cramped figures or a narrow range. The alphorn's homesickness as Monelle's: it is conv. only (correction).

#### Vastness and open country
- **Cues**: Take away the pulse: the stillness of vast spaces (Semio99 p. 27); drones give wide stillness, and other cues decide serene, desolate or threatening (MM ch. 9 conv.); mixolydian leans to open space (EvTon). The wide-open-spaces topic: open fifths, pedals, plain hymn-like folk tunes, I and V sounding together, reedy winds, heroic brass (T150, CWE14 top.). An open texture of consonant, high, energetic strings with almost no low register drew prairie 8.3× and over/across 6.8× [TLTT pp. 281-302] [T]. Big, low, long, wide waves mean a large expanse [TLTT p. 243] top.
- **Melody**: A rising "calling" snap onto a long held top note: distance crossed and discovered [TLTT pp. 352-354] conv. Doh-pentatonic with major sixths; a frontier tune falling 2 → 1 → 6 → 1 [TLTT pp. 325-326; Lehman p. 264] conv. A slowly unfolding reed tune over no pulse (T187).
- **Harmony**: The big-country family [TLTT pp. 357-362] conv.: the cowboy half cadence bVII → V (obligatory for the frontier, Lehman pp. 147, 200 top.), the I ↔ bVII pendulum, bVII taken as ii of a new key; in modal minor, bII → i; weaker forms: majors a tone or minor third apart, i ↔ v, plagal cadences. It means vastness in general, not just the West [TLTT p. 358] conv. A low pedal with high chords wandering above for sheer size [Stephen p. 65] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Open fifths in horns and strings over a pedal; oboe, english_horn; horn fifths for landscape [Sisman p. 102] top.; little or distant percussion. A high wordless voice with a long tail (choir high or solo_violin) for one free spirit in a vast land [TLTT pp. 651-652] (inf.). The reveal: drop the soloists, full strings mf to f [TLTT pp. 387-388] [T].
- **With / against**: With loneliness, the lone wanderer; with the journey, the long road; with freedom, open country; with dread, the same drone in minor at dusk. With the hunt, breaking cover: brighter, fuller, trumpets joining [Monelle p. 77] top./(inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one open-fifth drone. The whole piece has no pulse, a pedal and a slow melody; for long travel montages play whole long pieces from a wider playlist [Summers p. 96] conv.
- **Not**: Busy figuration that is low or dissonant, or a march; light, consonant, high busy motion does read as crossing open country (correction, TLTT pp. 281-302 [T]). V7s and pivot dominants in a folk-modal vista [TLTT pp. 345-346] [T]. A narrow ensemble range [TLTT pp. 515-516] [T].

#### The desert and the wastes
- **Cues**: Tagg's barren field (desert, tundra, polar) over-scored for the galloping Western (VVA 323 [T]): desert 9.7×, though prairie still outnumbered it 2:1, and desert answers came from the twangy lead [TLTT pp. 281-288, 362-366] [T]. Emptiness (VVA 1481); the perfect fourth as desolate (§1 Intervals [W]); stillness and drones for vast empty distance (T187; MM ch. 9). Hijaz is the Western shorthand for "distant desert" (MM ch. 9; EvTon), a borrowed marker to use sparingly if at all (R12; LAV). An evil wasteland: a falling-third harmonic figure and a skipping rhythm (LOTR). Glare: high dissonant held pitches (MM ch. 8).
- **Melody**: A slow solo reed line with ornaments over a drone, free in time; flat-seventh minor; fourths (inf.). A thin, ringing, metallic lead in flat-seventh minor [TLTT p. 382] (inf.). Scavenger folk: small chromatic figures with an oom-pah (SW 7 conv.).
- **Harmony**: A drone; flat-seventh minor (i, bVII, v) (inf.). An evil land turns the drone into a shuttle, safest as i ↔ bvi (Lehman correction, see **Fate**). An invented mode rather than a real people's (R12).
- **Colours and devices**: A heat shimmer as `wobble` on long string notes, a drone, a solo reed with ornaments (oboe or english_horn), sparse toms. Dust clouds: a soft rising `tremolo` (VVA 3415). The twang: harp in the low-middle register left ringing, or pizzicato [TLTT p. 382] (inf.).
- **With / against**: With vastness, the crossing. With the evil land, the drone turned to a shuttle. With the sun, midday glare. With ice, the polar waste (same emptiness, colder palette).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one shimmering held note. The whole piece is a drone with a slow solo reed and no pulse.
- **Not**: One "oriental riff" for every desert people (R12). A reedy pastoral lead over arpeggios, which says meadow [TLTT p. 382] (inf.).

#### The hunt
- **Cues**: The caccia: quick, major, 6/8, carried by the horn (T001, HAR10; HAT23; Sisman p. 101 top.). Hunting figures lie **low**: the horn sounds an octave below the trumpet, hunting themes often go to cellos, only the halali climbs high (correction, Monelle pp. 52-57, 83-84 top.). No checklist of five marks is needed: horn fifths alone, or even galloping eighths alone, signal a hunt; more markers make it clearer (correction, Rumph p. 497; Lowe pp. 610-26 top.). Meanings: nobility, adventure, risk, exhilaration, youth, morning, autumn, the outdoors; euphoric [Monelle p. 95] top. In concert music the horn was heard as sweet, not shrill [Haringer pp. 196-197] top.; the real French field style was hard and brassy, with rolled vibrato and howling upward slides [Monelle p. 53] top. (Use the sweet horn by default; the field style, with `wobble` and `slide`, for a rough real hunt.)
- **Melody**: The older call style that concert music copies: triadic, many repeated notes, 6/8 [Monelle pp. 43-57, 89-90] top. Two-part horn fanfares, galloping eighths, regular periods, echoes [Lowe pp. 610-26; Haringer] top.; a unison arpeggio [Sisman p. 101] conv. The halali: high, last notes echoed; the curée: one note [Monelle pp. 36, 52-78] top.
- **Harmony**: Horn motion: two horns note against note in thirds, fourths and fifths, the commonest hunt sign, which also works on violins2 and cellos [Monelle pp. 51, 83, 98] top.; horn fifths over V - I at cadences (inf.) [gen.]. Tonic and dominant, a timpani drone. Keys D and E♭; a hunt can pull the music up a semitone [Galand pp. 457-62] top.
- **Colours and devices**: Two or three horns; a 6/8 gigue in strings; timpani answering the horns [Monelle p. 81] conv.; pizzicato hooves under a light horn [Monelle p. 108] conv. Real horns = a hunt now; the same calls on low violins or clarinets = its memory [Agawu p. 490] conv. The call sequence as a cue sheet: assembly, hounds loosed, gallop, sighting, the quarry doubles back, takes water (danger), breaks cover, at bay, kill, homeward ride [Monelle pp. 52-56, 76-78] top.
- **With / against**: With the noble horse and the military: shared gallop and heroism [Monelle pp. 70-100] top. **Wild Hunt**: hunt + demonic: minor triads, calls in fourths, the dysphoric gallop, one-note blasts with a falling semitone, trombones and choir [Dick Table 2; Monelle pp. 7-99] top. Party as quarry: **Chase and pursuit**.
- **Intensity**: Hint: a lone horn sounding repeated notes then a triad, or a horn pair moving together into a cadence. Strong: a section of 6/8 calls. Whole: the full call sequence, with markers kept dense and steady, because constant mixing with other topics loses the listener [Lowe] top.
- **Not**: A march, snare, or double-tongued trumpet figures, which make it martial [Monelle p. 80] conv. Shrill horns [Haringer] top. Showing the kill by default [Monelle pp. 95, 272] top. Calls set high (corrected above).

#### The garden and the pleasant grove
*New (merged from two proposals). The cultivated or charmed small green place: garden, arbour, grove, glade, palace park, fairy dell. Not the wild Forest, the working Rural and pastoral, or the open meadow.*
- **Cues**: The literary minimum is a tree, grass and water; birds, flowers and a breeze are optional [Monelle pp. 13-15] top. Size rule: small, high, short-wave accompaniment means a small space [TLTT p. 243] top.; garden songs run on short, narrow, higher waves, often only rising, with a quick flick [TLTT pp. 241-243] top. The romance tune drew gardens, parks and flowers at four times average [TLTT pp. 158-166] [T]. Soft and slowish [Monelle p. 245] top.; composers add birdsong even when the text omits it [Monelle pp. 15-16] top.
- **Melody**: A light song tune over a busy accompaniment (*On Wings of Song*) [TLTT pp. 241-243] top., or a 6/8 new-siciliana or serene 12/8 with long unbroken lines [Monelle pp. 237-248] top. Birds: two flutes and piccolo trilling in thirds; flutes echoing the voice [Monelle p. 235] conv. Water: rippling sixteenths, or rocking thirds [Monelle pp. 244-245] top.
- **Harmony**: Simple I - V over a soft pedal [Monelle pp. 243-245; TLTT pp. 241-243] top. A Victorian garden of love: rippling sextuplets with the Ave Maria chord [TLTT pp. 173-178] top.
- **Colours and devices**: Harp in the upper middle in rising broken chords with a grace flick; flute or solo_violin tune; pizzicato for drops; one glockenspiel note for a dewdrop (inf.). Flutes and piccolo for birds, soft violins2 for water. A lovers' night garden: horn with cellos [Monelle p. 104] conv. A courtly park: the fête galante, minuet or sweet musette in thirds over a soft fifth [Monelle pp. 210-214, 261-264] top.
- **With / against**: With love and youth, the lovers' garden [Monelle pp. 260-261] conv. With magic, an enchanted or illusory garden: pastoral horns and woodwind birdsong [Monelle pp. 15-16] conv. With the fey, smaller and higher, toward glitter [TLTT pp. 243-248] top. With danger, Pan's chromatic flute against the calm (inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one rising harp arpeggio with a flick, or one flute trill in thirds. The whole piece is a light song over high rising arpeggios with water and birds (inf.).
- **Not**: Drones and rustic sharp 4ths, which make it a farm [Monelle pp. 227-230] top. Long low waves (meadow, sea) or a still drone (a lake) [TLTT pp. 238-246] top. Birdsong alone without narration [Monelle pp. 243-244] top.

### H. Magic and the uncanny

#### Wonder
- **Cues**: **Overlap with Magic**: both use celesta, harp, choir and I - bVI; listeners hear shimmer as wonder (the host chose a magic sketch for 'wonder' (host pairwise test, 2026-10)). Wonder needs time: a swell into the arrival, not a sudden f out of silence, and at least about eight bars; glitter on top is welcome. Music's strongest hook and most rewarding felt emotion (§7.2 [S list]); GEMS wonder: happy, amazed, dazzled, moved (GEMS [S]). It needs an **arresting** event: a new timbre, an unexpected modulation, a widened register (§7.6 r11; §4). Two forms (after Huron): **frisson**, a local surprise inside a clear key, then release; **awe**, a long chain with no footholds that keeps tension up [Lehman pp. 171, 185, 191] top. Frisson shortens felt time, awe stretches it [Lehman pp. 189-191] [ev]. A general pause and a sudden thinning raise sublimity [Margulis pp. 636-38] [ev].
- **Melody**: A slow, meandering tune in horns or low clarinets over a falling music-box ostinato (bells, celesta, triangle) on static harmony [Brill p. 27] conv. Sustained awe: a steadily rising line over a slow chord chain [Lehman pp. 192-197] conv. The Lydian #4 (LAV).
- **Harmony**: Pantriadic chromaticism, consonant triads linked by chromatic mediants (T156, LEH13 top.; Murphy p. 156 conv.). The chromatically modulating cadence: V7 → bIII taken as the new tonic for a bigger restatement [Lehman pp. 14, 201] top. Exposed awe moves: I → #IV, I → bVI, I → v, i → bVI; instant awe i → bVI with the tonic in the bass, tutti and choir [Lehman pp. 171, 260] top. Amazement: I → III [Lehman p. 101] top. Quiet marvel: one slide, i → VII [Lehman p. 103] conv. Set-piece awe chain: 10-14 non-repeating chords by chromatic thirds and slides in two-bar units, framed by i → biii, resolving into the place's theme [Lehman pp. 192-197] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: *Wonder and discovery*, *Ethereal Choir*; wordless choir, massive tutti, harp and celesta, openings that buzz out of nothing, no clutter so the chord change is heard [Lehman] conv. Glorious wonder: lush strings with brass flourishes, against Herrmann's austere static awe [Brill pp. 34-35] conv. Celestial shower: high harp + glockenspiel arpeggios [Ivanovitch pp. 338-341] conv./(inf.).
- **With / against**: Reinforced by the divine, magic and the sea; with fear, **The sublime**; with elves, chromatic mediants (T169). Wonder and eerie dread are the two magical entrances; pick one per place [Brill p. 31] conv. Frame a frisson with a clear key before and a known theme after; stack frissons biggest first, the last absorbed into a full cadence with a solo-to-tutti switch [Lehman pp. 178-185] top./conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one Lydian chord or one mediant shift. One frisson per revelation; an awe chain only for true set pieces [Lehman pp. 66, 235] top. Whole: a swell to a far-key landing, then stillness; spectacle adds timpani and full brass at the reveal [Brill p. 16] conv.
- **Not**: A march for awe, which freezes; but frisson works inside a ride or a pulsing countdown (correction, Lehman pp. 162-185 conv.). Cadences during the awe chain; unresolved real peril, which turns awe to dread [Lehman pp. 169-170] conv. Irony [Brill p. 36] conv. Repeating the up-and-back harp gasp [TLTT pp. 386-387] [T].
- **Tell apart** - From *Magic*: wonder is a reaction - an arrival that opens space (a swell to a far chord, then stillness); magic is an agent's power - shimmer layers, spell motifs, chromatic instability. When both are meant, they combine freely.

#### Magic
- **Cues**: Tonal ambiguity breeds uncertainty; unexpected harmony draws attention (§4). Harmonic sorcery: humans diatonic, the supernatural chromatic, whole-tone, octatonic (Glinka on), augmented triads for doubt (T104, FW23 top.). Chromatic, surprising harmony = magic set apart; diatonic I - IV - V - I = magic tamed [Webster pp. 200-209] top. **Sound marks the magical, silence the ordinary**: underscore comes in at the first sign of magic and returns louder and less broken each time [Webster pp. 198-200] conv. Kind magic is celestial; witches and incantations are ombra [McClelland pp. 279, 287] top. Glitter: about two octaves above "nature" waves, very short narrow waves at ≥350 notes a minute over slow harmony [TLTT pp. 246-248] top.
- **Melody**: Spells as short pitch motifs: the order is the identity, rhythm free; retrograde undoes a spell, a palindrome reflects; new pitches unlock with progress [Summers pp. 80-82] conv. The Hedwig model: a minor waltz with chromatic passing notes, a raised 4th, a tritone at section ends; triple metre feels less grounded [Webster p. 208, n. 17] conv./top. A hostile spell: both registral extremes, glissandi, jagged lines [Summers p. 77] conv.; in our parts, piccolo and basses at once, trombones and violins with `slide`, high `tremolo` with `wobble` [digest] (inf.). Incantation: a bass monotone over simple dark chords, dactyls [McClelland pp. 284-287] top.
- **Harmony**: Shimmer chain I - IV - bII - bIII - I [Lehman pp. 68-69] conv.; hexatonic spell loop I - bVI - I - III - I (Imaj7 - bVImaj7) [Lehman pp. 78-79] top.; casting ramp: minor triads falling a minor third (i - vi - #iv - biii), rising for tension [Lehman pp. 79, 161] conv.; octatonic for primordial or hostile sorcery (T104; Lehman p. 43 top.). Whole-tone is worn by parody: use it briefly, or the hybrid scale 1-2-3-#4-5-b6, or I → bVI (correction, Lehman pp. 52-53 top.).
- **Colours and devices**: *Spellcraft* (celesta, glockenspiel and harp arpeggios, high soft `tremolo`, a flute run up, low strings sliding up); a harp glissando or celesta flourish as a spell fires (T104). Harp + celesta + choir, the fantasy magic triad [Webster p. 200] conv. Benevolent: celesta, harp, treble choir, chimes (celesta + high glockenspiel + triangle); malevolent: bass choir, hard pizzicato with dry toms, low harp struck with pizzicato basses and low bells, tolling bells [Webster pp. 201, 205] conv.
- **With / against**: With the arcane, scholarly; with the fey, glamour; with evil, octatonic. A spell motif can pass into the underscore, and an underscore theme played by an enchanted instrument lands as a spell [Summers p. 213; Halfyard App. pp. 229-230] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one harp glissando or celesta arpeggio; strong is a shimmer layer under the tune. Set the campaign's dial once: compound, chromatic, surprising = theatrical magic; duple, diatonic = a realistic world where only evil is fantastic [Webster pp. 194-212] conv.
- **Not**: A whole piece of glitter with nothing beneath. Purely mechanical timbre: magic needs acoustic warmth or a voice [Barron p. 92] conv. Period winds, which say medieval [Webster p. 201] conv.
- **Tell apart** - From *Wonder*: see there (magic acts, wonder arrives).

#### The arcane (hidden, learned, dangerous lore)
- **Cues**: Mystery's uncertainty joined to wisdom's solemnity and the learned topic (§6 r21; §7.2; §7.1); forbidden lore can take a tritone (§1 Intervals [W]). The learned style signifies God, cosmos, number, law and history, and also curiosity and the cabinet of curiosities [Chapin pp. 323-326] top.; dense counterpoint sounds like difficulty itself [Lowe pp. 603-10] top. The **fantasia** for scrying and opening a spellbook: free rhythm, arpeggiated chords, sudden far modulations through diminished sevenths, declamation, pauses (T037, MCK07). Grave melody over harmony the ear cannot predict [McClelland pp. 290-294] top. Phantasmagoria: keep listeners torn between a tonal and an irrational explanation [Lehman pp. 70, 251] conv.
- **Melody**: Learned artifices: fugue, counter-fugue, crab canon, scales snaking round a subject, limping rhythms [Chapin pp. 311-314] top. A tune functional at its edges and strange in its middle [Lehman pp. 207-208] conv. A slow modal english_horn line; moderate to slow (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Tethered chromaticism: chromatic chords forced around a firm tonic read as sorcerous [Lehman p. 212] top. A sorcerer who bends space and time: i - v - bII ↔ iv - bV - V7, the Neapolitan briefly home, then a snap back [Lehman pp. 208-210] conv. Fantasia swerves through vii°7 (T037).
- **Colours and devices**: English_horn modal tune, *Mystery and investigation*'s pulse, low choir on held chords, organ, celesta; fugato for scholarship, a shimmer for the spell (src-topics §7). A wizard: a learned noun (fugato, stretto) with a fantasia or Klangfläche adjective [digest §3.4] (inf.). An academy: puzzle canons, a crab canon, a canonic minuet [digest] (inf.). Sorcery as science: a signature buzz (brass + `tremolo` with `unhinge`) planted early that later snaps into tune as the hero's theme [Halfyard App. pp. 223-230] conv.
- **With / against**: Reinforced by mystery, secrecy and wisdom. With corruption, a necromancer. With **Artifacts and relics**, the forbidden book. Power is neutral; the mad scholar is a stock villain [Halfyard App. pp. 221-224] conv., and power stolen from the gods makes a sorcerer dangerous [Brill p. 21] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one fugato entry. The whole piece is a slow modal study with a shimmer, broken by one fantasia passage.
- **Not**: Fairy glitter.

#### The fey
- **Cues**: An unfamiliar idiom and mercurial changes suit fey places (§8.6 r16 [M]; §7.6 r1). Fairy music, now defined by Dickensheets (the Handbook only names the topic, correction): almost always **high and shimmering**, little or no bass, fleet running quavers or semiquavers [Dick pp. 122-23] top.; Mendelssohn's prototype adds pp staccato scurrying, motifs tossed between groups, sudden swells, a closing flute run, held high wind chords that open the fairy world (T091, WIK; staccato is his trait, not her definition). It grew from the **celestial** style (gentle, G or F major, lilting thirds, flutes and harp), sped up and made mischievous [McClelland n. 11 top.; digest (inf.)].
- **Melody**: Stepwise or small leaps, figures within a fifth, frequent sequences, glittering parallel thirds; a slower form for sensual beings (nymphs, dryads, an elf court) [Dick p. 122] top. Pan's syrinx slide: a fast flute scale over two octaves, returning unchanged [Monelle pp. 265-267] conv. A melancholy fairy-tale waltz [Binns p. 140; Webster n. 17] conv.
- **Harmony**: Mostly diatonic (I, IV, V, ii) [Dick p. 122] top./(inf.). **Evil fey**: unresolved V7 and vii°7 and augmented I+, register still high [Dick p. 122] top. Elf-home: I ↔ bVI with maj7 and #5 colour; entering faerie: the tonic jumps a semitone from phrygian minor to Lydian major; fey speech lands on I → III; fey menace: i ↔ bvi under a graceful waltz [Lehman pp. 143, 174, 177, 256] conv. (mapping (inf.)).
- **Colours and devices**: Soft violins in thirds, flutes and piccolo at the octave, celesta or glockenspiel sparkle [Dick pp. 122-23] top./conv.; harp for the slow form (inf.). Fairy-tale frame: wordless soprano choir, harp, celesta over warm strings [Binns p. 140] conv. Queen Mab: high soft `tremolo`, single high harp notes, triangle; the horns of Elfland: a soft far-off horn call [digest; Monelle pp. 109-110] conv. Elves playing at shepherds: a 6/8 musette, drone in bassoons, oboe and flutes in thirds [Haringer pp. 204-205] top./(inf.).
- **With / against**: Fey + demonic = Unseelie: hold the high register, so it sounds like faerie gone wrong [Dick p. 122] top. Moved low = gnomes, ogres, trolls (bassoons, low pizzicato, tuba) [Dick p. 123 top.; TLTT p. 321 (inf.)]. Fey music leaking into the mortal world = wonder and danger [Binns pp. 142-145] conv.; a seduction waltz with breathing tempo [Barron p. 83] conv.; a fey king's song with call-and-response minions [Giuffre pp. 97-105] conv. With menace, a glamour slightly out of tune (`wobble`).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one high held wind chord or one sequential thirds figure in flutes. The whole piece is an elfin scherzo [Dick p. 122] top.
- **Not**: Minor mode as the mark of malice: Dickensheets uses dissonant chords (correction, stronger than the T091 RPG note; Grieg's quick minor elves are folk colour, TLTT p. 321 conv.). Wide, leaping tunes [Dick] top. Industrial timbre, or an infernal villain swamping the fey tale [Barron pp. 81, 90-92] conv. Plain rustic woodland (that is nature).

#### Dreams
- **Cues**: Drifting harmony, flexible tempo (conv.); the border of waking and sleep (§7.4). Hevner's dreamy: slow, minor, flowing, slightly high (HEV cl.3 [M]); wavy contours (MM ch. 9); faint over-scoring for soft pentatonic strings (VVA 1036 [T]). Screen correlates: slow motion, soft focus, suspension, fluid flight, the unreal; the romance drew dream and mystical at twice average and alone drew "slow motion" [TLTT pp. 158-166, 268-269] top./[T]. The fantasia's loose links and disembodied fragments [Head pp. 259-263] top.; sleep is celestial [McClelland p. 287] top.
- **Melody**: The dream melody (Stefani and Marconi): slow, smooth, arched or wavy over a wide range, phrases longer than a breath, the motif changing all the time, unexpected intervals, no hard accents [TLTT pp. 220, 268] top. Drifting off: a triple-time tune over one static chord, slowing and fading [Webster p. 210] conv. A sincere dream scale 1-2-3-#4-5-b6 [Lehman pp. 52-53] conv.
- **Harmony**: I → bVI, the Romantic dream progression [Lehman p. 101] top. The slide (I ↔ #i) as a boundary state [Lehman p. 104] top. A sudden flat-side drop to bVI or bIII over a drone, held still: a vision outside time [Galand pp. 466-67] top. Wide parallel non-triadic chords (1-2-5, 1-5-b7) drew dreams and mystical [TLTT pp. 533-535] [T]. Circle-of-fifths sequences give dreaming a goal; drop them for aimless drift [TLTT pp. 268-269] top. Augmented chords for hallucination (T104).
- **Colours and devices**: *Dream and hallucination* (chords a third apart, gentle `unhinge` on all parts, slides, flexed tempo, true pitch on waking). Floating: celesta + flutes pp with `wobble` [Murphy pp. 155-156] conv. A mystical harp opening [Dick p. 127] top. Hallucination: solo_violin with `slide` and `wobble` over V7 with a flat fifth, high chromatic strings [TLTT pp. 575-576, 588-589] conv. One figure frozen over a slow harmonic journey [Agawu p. 484] conv.
- **With / against**: In a dream every part goes soft together; in madness a single voice cracks. With memory, *The half-remembered tune*. A prophetic dream that comes true reuses the identical cue [Winters pp. 124-125] conv., and may end on a bare tritone held into waking [Murphy p. 161] (inf.). Delirium breaks song into recitative [Head pp. 271-272] top.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one drifting chord. The whole piece is a dream sequence.
- **Not**: The madness device. A plain whole-tone wash in a serious dream: worn by parody (correction, Lehman pp. 52-53 top.).

#### The alien
- **Cues**: An unfamiliar idiom brings uncertainty (§8.3 [M]); unpredictable sounds (§8.5 [W]); no functional harmony. The major tritone progression (two major triads a tritone apart, alternating, loud, sustained, roots held) means space, the unknown, the beyond, including fantasy realms (T153, MUR06 top.). An idiom foreign to the home style sounds strange simply because it is not the norm, with no people attached [Chapin p. 311; Sutcliffe pp. 124-129] top. Meaning builds: an empty oscillation becomes alien awe once the story pairs it with the being [Lehman p. 61] conv. Electronic timbre reads as machine in fantasy; only a high human voice made it mystic [Barron pp. 84-92] conv.
- **Melody**: A gliding voice: solo_violin with slides over high choir (the theremin's stand-in) [Lehman pp. 102-103] conv. A hypnotic repeated phrase over octatonic falling minor thirds for a mind presence [Lehman p. 79] conv. Slow, with wide and unpredictable intervals (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Cosmic arrival: I ↔ #IV twice, the pair moved to a new level, then a drift into minor triads [Lehman pp. 102-103] conv. Almost familiar: i with major 7th ↔ bvi down the hexatonic cycle, then a "cadence" a semitone off home [Lehman p. 80] conv. Alien awe: an exposed I ↔ v later sung as a hymn [Lehman pp. 61, 76] conv. An unreadable encounter: slide oscillations stitched with iv - I and v - I joins [Lehman pp. 220-221] conv. Aberrant geometry: a slide pair sounded together (i + VII), symmetric moves flung across octaves [Lehman pp. 144, 154] conv. Major/minor blur (I ↔ i), unclear key, clusters [Sutcliffe] top./(inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: *The other-worldly glide*; an MTTP for the first sight of the far realm. Remove the human: no bowed strings, harp alone with winds [Brill pp. 28-29] conv. A construct from beyond: a march in low bassoons and clarinets [Brill p. 30] conv. Modernist dread: `unhinge` on `tremolo` clusters, slides, `hits` of dissonant chords [Summers p. 131] conv./(inf.).
- **With / against**: With menace, a far-realm horror. Made warm and major over a steady pulse, friendly strangeness. With the planes, the far realm (see **The heavens and the planes**).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one gliding phrase. The whole piece is the glide over tritone pairs.
- **Not**: Landing on the true home; it may borrow familiar joins or cadence a semitone off (correction, Lehman pp. 80, 220-221 conv.). Synth or machine timbre.

#### The sublime
- **Cues**: Overwhelming power felt as awe mixed with terror; Burke made terror its source, Beattie spoke of "pleasing horror" (T026, FW23; McClelland pp. 290-294 top.). Correction: it is **not a topic** but a category carried by the French overture, fanfare, empfindsam, learned style, ombra and tempesta; choose one of those and enlarge it [McClelland p. 294] top. Alison: loud, low, long, growing; Michaelis: extreme uniformity (one chord, long weighty notes, long pauses) or extreme profusion (a thundering torrent) [McClelland] top. Awe is a freeze: held breath, stretched time [Lehman pp. 171, 189-192] conv. A general pause raises sublimity [Margulis pp. 636-38] [ev]. The numinous: low reverberant ambience that swells forward now and then [Hayward p. 49] conv. (see **The numinous**).
- **Melody**: Massive unisons; long notes mixed with quick ones [Guymer p. 584] conv.; sudden transitions, headlong repetition, growth to a climax [McClelland] top. Slow, wide range (inf.).
- **Harmony**: The *Creation* model: dissonant chaos that delays every cadence, then a sudden tutti I (T026). The diminished seventh for terror; keys that veer, unorthodox and unpredictable resolutions [McClelland] top. Pantriadic chains for vast, ambiguous spaces [Lehman p. 72] conv.; a wall of all-major chords, the orchestra as organ [Lehman p. 252] conv. Slow harmonic rhythm.
- **Colours and devices**: Unison low brass and choir, a long crescendo out of silence, a vii°7 `tremolo`, then a sudden tutti; gong and timpani; *The climax*. A static Klangfläche after the decisive cadence [Horton p. 660] conv.
- **With / against**: With the monster, the dragon's reveal; with the divine, a terrible god; with mountains, the summit. Wonder is the tamed sublime; for the existential kind keep the peril real [Lehman pp. 169-170] conv. Freeze (awe, ombra) against fight (tempesta) [McClelland p. 287] top. Sources of terror read like a bestiary: gods, demons, storms, seas, volcanoes, monsters, plague [McClelland] top. The festive sublime: a grand allegro as a victory ode [Sisman pp. 92-96] top.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one sudden tutti after a hush. The whole piece is chaos to light (or to darkness).
- **Not**: Mere volume: without the preceding hush and the sheer size it is just loud. Treating it as one topic (correction).

#### The heavens and the planes
- **Cues**: The major tritone progression means space, distance and the beyond, fantasy realms included; versions one note off give glimpses (T153, MUR06 top.); its cosmic sense comes from Holst's Mars and Saturn [Lehman p. 102] top. In a story it can take a local meaning (rescue), its minor mirror going to the foe [Murphy pp. 155-158] conv. Tagg's cosmic field drew only faint responses (VVA 335 [T, weak]); a still, wide parallel-fifth texture with no bass motion drew planets [TLTT pp. 521-537] [T] weak. The **celestial** serves kind gods, heaven, Elysium, sleep, benevolent ghosts [McClelland p. 287] top. Space as size: a low pedal with high chords wandering above [Stephen p. 65] conv.
- **Melody**: Celestial: stepwise in thirds, lilting, regular, gentle [McClelland p. 287] top. Angels: a languid dotted 6/8, flutes in garlands of thirds and sixths, no drone [Monelle pp. 232-233] conv. Paradisal calm: a long string tune in 3/4 at about 72 over a horn pedal, with a flat 7th, then a wandering high flute answered by other winds [Monelle p. 249] conv. A planar marvel: the 1-2-3-#4-5-b6 scale [Lehman pp. 52-53] conv.
- **Harmony**: Two majors a tritone apart (I ↔ #IV) in loud held brass over held roots (T153). Smooth major, G or F [McClelland] top. A mixolydian bVII line over a held I [Monelle p. 249] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Celesta and harp for stars; high wide-spaced choir (*Ethereal Choir*). Elysium: flutes and harp, murmuring inner strings for its breezes, a paradise of winds in major [McClelland; Sisman p. 111] top./conv. A gliding solo_violin with wordless choir over the tritone pair [Lehman pp. 102-103] (inf.). An astral sea: soaring with no ground beneath (T164 without the motor). Each plane its own harmony system (R12): elemental planes from the element entries, the hells from **The infernal and demonic**, faerie from **The fey**, the far realm from **The alien**.
- **With / against**: With wonder, a planar city first seen; with the divine, the upper planes. A planar journey in Gluck's order: ombra at the gate, tempesta for the Furies, celestial for Elysium [McClelland pp. 287-289] top. With **The liminal and the in-between**, the portal.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one tritone chord pair. The whole piece is a tritone cycle under a slow melody, or a celestial 12/8 piece.
- **Not**: A synth palette or laser-like effects, which date into "tomorrow's world" [TLTT pp. 633-650] [T].

#### Artifacts and relics
- **Cues**: Music cannot name an object (R7); a leitmotif gives the relic its theme and the story attaches it (§8.1). The cursed ring's four faces: its history as a string melody, its power as a low brass fanfare over dark harmony, its allure as a pure humming choir over minor fragments and bass-drum strokes, its fate as choir and horns (LOTR [R][A][S]). Plant the relic's signature sound before it is explained [Halfyard App. p. 225] conv.; a motif heard often enough becomes automatic [Lehman pp. 135-136] conv.
- **Melody**: A power theme for the object (a minor third rising and falling) with a derived motif for whoever wields it [Halfyard App. pp. 226-229] conv. A legendary sword: a call stretched to a tenth [Monelle p. 171] conv. Revealed a fragment at a time (*Musical Spoiler*). A relic that is an instrument: each power its own short motif [Summers pp. 80, 178-179] conv.
- **Harmony**: i → bvi (the Tarnhelm, named after a magic helmet) for evil artifacts [Lehman p. 101] top. Dark relics: minor triads rotating in clashing registers, triads a semitone apart (SW 60); minor tritone pairs for danger from objects [Murphy n. 14] conv. A holy-terrible relic: tritones tied to a fixed tonic [Lehman p. 255] conv. An enigma: I - iii - bIII over a pedal on their shared note [Lehman p. 148] conv. A map to the lost: two minor triads a tritone apart (SW 48).
- **Colours and devices**: A short motif in an unusual colour (oboe with slides, a celesta cell). Divine weapons, a helmet on and off: harp glissandi with a percussion shimmer [Brill pp. 35-36] conv. A sacred, dangerous relic: celesta with `wobble`, high `tremolo`, harp glissandi [Brill p. 31] conv. A holy relic: a temple chorale with flutes in thirds and harp [McClelland n. 11] (inf.). A magic horn's own soft cantilena; Roland's unanswered horn [Monelle pp. 105-107] conv./top.
- **With / against**: With corruption, the object that turns its bearer. With legend, its history. With wonder, the item revealed (T156). The motif can wear many topics [Agawu pp. 479-80] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is three notes of the motif. The whole piece is the theme in its four faces (history, power, allure, fate).
- **Not**: A real culture's instrument as the sound of a cursed thing (R12; T171).

#### The monster
- **Cues**: Threat cues: loud, large intervals, wide range (HM [W]), low register (§1 Register). The horror topic: ombra plus tempesta, whole-tone, clusters, rasping low brass, a rising chromatic pizzicato bass (T158, McC14). Tempesta covers dragons and flight from them; monsters top the sources of sublime terror [McClelland pp. 280-294] top. Herrmann: the heavier the creature's evil, the lower the instruments [Brill p. 25] conv. Leave the first appearance unscored, bring music in as the hero gains ground, score the final fight throughout [Brill p. 35] conv. "Fear emitters": layers rise as the party nears and calm as it backs off [Summers pp. 131-132] conv.
- **Melody**: A slow heavy motif with a shape and a gait (R8). A terse threat motif whose chords all turn around one held note [Lehman p. 150] conv. Ogres: fairy figures moved low, a light-footed giant [Dick p. 123] top. A whole-tone fanfare for the unnatural (McC14).
- **Harmony**: i - vi - bVI - IV with no dominant [Lehman pp. 149-153] conv. Primal: octatonic, a bass in minor thirds, semitone rocking; chords flung into different octaves for mass [Lehman pp. 43, 144, 248] conv. A dragon attack in D or C minor with vii°7 over a dominant pedal [digest §14b] (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: Low brass, tuba, taiko, low choir; trombone `slide` roars. A giant: low brass, timpani, cymbals, then bells and gong; a colossus walking: a slow brass-and-timpani march; a dragon: thumping timpani over crawling basses; a hatchling: flutes and glockenspiel growing into low brass and harp; a swarm: harp flurries, pecking staccato oboe, clarinets and piccolo over falling low winds; a skeletal foe: dry pizzicato + glockenspiel [Brill pp. 25-34] conv. Dragon attack: falling violin scales, `tremolo`, repeated-note basses, emphatic trombones, octave unisons [digest] (inf.).
- **With / against**: With weight, the giant; with the comic, a blustering ogre (§7.6 r3). With compassion, reorder the monster motif's moves into a heroic theme [Lehman pp. 149-153] conv. With the hunt, the party as hunters. Rank its battle cue by importance (see **Combat and the duel**) [Summers p. 167] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is a low brass roar. The whole piece is a heavy motif building to a sublime reveal; the beast beaten: a sudden turn to major with crashing cymbals [Brill p. 30] conv.
- **Not**: Stock stabs: give each creature its own contour and way of moving (R8).

#### Transformation and shape-shifting
*New. The moment a form changes (lycanthropy, polymorph, transfiguration); not the creature itself (The monster) or spell colour in general (Magic).*
- **Cues**: A monstrous change seen in shadow: high, dissonant, pitch-blurred strings (high soft `tremolo` with `unhinge`), scattered toms and snare, a soft snare roll as a rattle [Brill pp. 35-36] conv.
- **Melody**: Shrinking: two lines moving apart by semitones, one falling, one rising [Brill p. 25] conv.; the same pair converging approaches a dreadful place [Brill n. 6] conv. The character's leitmotif returning transfigured [Stephen p. 64] conv.
- **Harmony**: Hexatonic chains (triads a major third apart joined by semitone moves), each chord melting into the next [Murphy p. 160] (inf.).
- **Colours and devices**: A transfiguring or resurrecting change: high layered choir sliding chromatically with celesta [Stephen pp. 64-65] conv. A string `slide` to catch the moment (inf.).
- **With / against**: With the monster, let the change resolve into the creature's theme [Brill pp. 35-36] conv. With healing, a change undone, the original form restored over harp [Brill] (inf.). With the fey, a glamour dropped.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one diverging semitone pair. The whole piece is the parting lines, a dissonant peak, and the character's theme transfigured.
- **Not**: The unchanged theme after the change: a new form needs a new version [Stephen p. 64] conv.

#### The liminal and the in-between
*New. Portals, mirrors, the veil, the afterlife, any state between two conditions. Not Thresholds (a story's beginnings and endings) or Dreams (sleep), though it borders both.*
- **Cues**: Quiet and unemphasised, the slide gives hushed amazement [Lehman p. 103] conv. Crossing by a hidden pivot feels magical; crossing by an irreconcilable chord feels like gears grinding [Lehman pp. 218-219] conv.
- **Melody**: Over a slide oscillation keep the shared third high and exposed in the tune: the one note on both sides [Lehman pp. 104-105] conv. Slow, legato, hovering (inf.).
- **Harmony**: The **slide**, I ↔ #i or i ↔ VII: near by voice leading, far by key, the boundary-state move (dream, afterlife, other worlds); not yet a cliché [Lehman pp. 104, 255] top./conv. A hidden reality behind ours: a long pantriadic chain [Lehman p. 71] conv. A parallel world entered and left: the Neapolitan briefly treated as home, then a snap back [Lehman pp. 208-210] conv. A hall of mirrors: I - IV - bII - bIII - I [Lehman pp. 68-69] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Whole-tone harp glissandi for portals and mirrors [Lehman pp. 52-53] conv.; soft held strings with a celesta glint (inf.).
- **With / against**: With undeath, the ghost who does not know it is dead [Lehman pp. 104-105] conv. With **Thresholds**, the passage itself; with **The heavens and the planes**, the portal (inf.).
- **Intensity**: A hint is one slide at a doorway. Strong is a slide oscillation under the scene. The whole piece is a slide chain stitched with diatonic joins (inf.).
- **Not**: Settling on one chord of the pair: the meaning is the ambivalence (inf.).

#### Prophecy and the oracle
*New. The pronouncement: an oracle, a curse spoken, a talking statue, a lich's verdict, a god's voice. Legend and lore tells the past; Fate is the force; this is the voice that announces it.*
- **Cues**: A documented subtype of ombra: slow tempo that changes within the scene, dark colours, sudden silences [McClelland pp. 282-287] top. Awe is a freeze, so hold the pulse still [McClelland p. 287] top. A fanfare as the oracle's call to attention [Sisman p. 115] top. A prophecy that recurs keeps the identical cue [Winters pp. 124-126] conv.
- **Melody**: A bass voice intoning on a monotone or a triad [McClelland pp. 285-287] top.; majestic dotted rhythm on triadic lines; fragmented, exclaiming lines around it, dactyls for incantations [McClelland pp. 282-284] top. Recitative: speech rhythm over sparse chords or tremolo (T041). Over a hexatonic cycle the line can toll on b3 and 5, the held notes [Lehman pp. 90-94] (inf.).
- **Harmony**: Simple homophonic chords in dark flat keys, sudden vii°7, surprise progressions, pauses on unresolved chords [McClelland pp. 282-287] top. Prophecy as a hexatonic cycle, i ↔ bvi balanced by i → iii [Lehman p. 255] conv.; i ↔ bvi with tritone shifts ending on a minor chord with major 7th [Lehman pp. 132-133] conv.
- **Colours and devices**: Trombones with low string chords (the *Idomeneo* oracle); the line in trombones, tuba, low horns or low choir in unison [McClelland p. 285; digest] top./(inf.). Brass as the stentorian supernatural [Sisman p. 108] conv.
- **With / against**: With fate, the monotone over a timpani heartbeat (inf.). With the reveal: keep trombones silent until the oracle speaks [McClelland p. 288] top. Against the party's song: the oracle's fanfare breaks in and silences it [Sisman p. 111] conv.
- **Intensity**: A hint is one monotone phrase in low brass. The whole piece is an ombra oracle scene with pauses on vii°7 (inf.).
- **Not**: A tune: its words are chanted on one pitch, never sung as a melody [McClelland pp. 285-287] top. A fixed march pulse for the invocation itself.

### I. Society and place

#### The city
- **Cues** - Busy and dense: overlapping short figures, a quickish pulse; tempo and loudness set the people's pace (§8.4 [S pattern]). Choppy, angular gestures suggest skylines and drilled crowds, the reverse of rural waves (MM ch. 13 [T]); dense clutter is the faceless crowd (MM 514). "Big city" topped three of Tagg's tunes (a bright busy sports tune with traffic 7.9×, a low riff, a slow minor jazz tune) [TLTT 477–482, 539–550, 607–617] [T]; urban readings come from style, not the chord [TLTT 441, 458–462] [T]. Game town topic: major, lilting, moderate to slow (T176, LAV). Keep one district's music continuous; change it only at a new district [Summers 162–163] conv.
- **Melody** - Hub and variations: the square loops a town tune, each shop or inn plays a variation in its own timbre, and returns to the square make a rondo [Summers 75] conv. A repeated-note "telegraph" figure in high semiquavers for trade and traffic [TLTT 487–498] [T]. Quick song and dance scraps passed round the sections [Sutcliffe 122] top., scoring (inf.). 2/4 or 4/4, moderate-brisk (inf.).
- **Harmony** - One city, three moods: major thirds cheery or comic; minor thirds darker and threatening; a mixolydian bVII worldly without gloom [TLTT 494–495] (inf.). Free-standing iv6 chords going nowhere = urban angst [TLTT 192–193] top. Seen from above: drift among a few keys with no firm arrival [Lehman 72] conv.; first sight: an awe chain landing on its theme [Lehman 66, 192–197] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *The place's own rhythm*, *The hour strikes*, *Musette*, *Creepy jazz*. Square: flutes, oboe and strings over toms and triangle. The old bustle code as pizzicato, staccato piccolo and violin semiquavers: dated for modern streets, quaint for a fantasy town [TLTT 494–496] conv. (inf.). Tower trumpets at the gate or at dusk [Monelle 126, 167; Haringer 198] top.; civic fanfares and marches for councils and entries [Monelle 125–126] top. A magical city: mimic what moves (string ostinato wheels, glockenspiel gold, low brass hubbub) [Webster Potter 206] conv.
- **With / against** - The city gives the pastoral its meaning; score both [Horton 643] top. With wealth, the upper town; with crime, the slums; with night, the streets. An ordinary town can keep to source music and score only the marvel [Binns 144–146] conv. (see The mundane). Change to a district's variation at a phrase junction [Summers 75] conv. Shops: Commerce and the market.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a clock chime or tower call. Strong: a district's variation. Whole: the hub rondo of the city's bustle.
- **Not** - A busy loop under long table talk (§8.6 r2; §7.6 r16). Wavy legato pads and harp waves, the gestural opposite of streets [TLTT 266] top.

#### Home and hearth
- **Cues** - Lullaby features (§8.6 r8); the familiar is liked (§4); nostalgia through memory (§8.1). Tagg's stasis field ("everyday, familiar, home", "as it always has been") and "protected, secure" went to the oboe pastoral (VVA 1020, 1029, 1123 [T]); predictable four-bar periods drew everyday, home and suburbs [TLTT 513–515] [T]. Plain, untroped topics with clear textures = parlour comfort [Lowe 603–626] top. The domestic singing style marks the amateur, the sociable, the household [Day-O'Connell 249–254] top. An ordinary home may stay unscored until magic intrudes [Webster Potter 198–200] conv.
- **Melody** - A tune that sounds known on first hearing: strophic, narrow, stepwise, catchy [Monelle 221–223] top.; small-compass ballad or canzonet [Day-O'Connell 250–253] top. Home village: pastoral 6/8 with a drone and yodel leaps, cadences over a tonic pedal, F or G major [Galand 456–462; Hatten n. 10] top., recipe (inf.). Homesickness: a rising minor sixth 5 → b3 on the homeland's name [TLTT 444] conv. Weave a companion's motif into their birthplace's tune [Summers 170] conv.
- **Harmony** - Diatonic for home life [Lehman 44] conv.; warm I–IV. The Ave Maria chord (I – ii7/IV6 over a held tonic – V – I) for domestic peace and the home estate [TLTT 173–178] top. Home regained: chromatic intruders cleared away, the diatonic home key back [Lehman 259 n33] conv. Leaving and returning as a harmonic-rhythm arch (a chord a bar, then a minim, then reversed) [TLTT 214] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *The tavern*, *Lullaby*, *Nostalgic music box*. LOTR's homeland: one diatonic tune in one key dressed three ways (pensive on flute, a country dance, a hymn) on folk colours: flute (whistle), harp, solo_violin (fiddle), soft toms (bodhrán) (T167, WIK-LOTR conv.; Bratman finds Celtic a poor fit for an English Shire). Pastoral home: strings with a little brass, folk tune on flutes or oboe [Barron 83] conv. Folk lullaby with a plucked high harp note [Monelle 254] conv. Biedermeier: old-fashioned elegance and middle-class propriety in a Mozartian idiom (four-bar phrases, slow diatonic harmony, strong cadences, Alberti-like bass); it suits home but its core is class and respectability [Dickensheets 114–115] top. (corrected from "cosy salon idiom", T096).
- **With / against** - With longing, an exile's home; with separation, a lone whistled call far from the door (*Lassie*) [TLTT 444–446] conv. With loss, *Requiem for a Landmark*. With renewal, the home restored. A contredanse at home lifts a low mood [McKee 170] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a warm I–IV. Strong: the hearth tune on one folk colour. Whole: the hearth tune in three settings (pensive, dance, hymn).
- **Not** - Grandeur. Chromatic or unsettled harmony at the hearth itself.

#### The journey and the road
- **Cues** - A walking pulse entrains (§7.6 r6; §8.1). About 90 bpm strolls, 120 marches, 160 runs (MM ch. 8). A crotchet tune near ♩=92 over half-speed harmony scored highest of all tunes for walking [TLTT 168, 270] [T]. Coming, going and passing across need different music [TLTT 134 fn. 68] top. Location themes shifting with the terrain create travel; bigger musical change = greater distance [Summers 75, 162–163] conv. Fast minor 6/8 = travel through labour and danger [TLTT 305–307] conv. Game world-map topic: woodwind and string runs, snare, motor or galloping rhythm, major, fanfares (T177, LAV). The march eases fatigue (Sulzer) [Haringer 200] top.
- **Melody** - Short notes driving into a longer downbeat move harder than an even ostinato; pitched pickups beat monotone ones (by 23%); going somewhere 4.0×, travelling 3.5× [TLTT 281–288, 297] [T]. Even note values "move fast, go nowhere" [TLTT 297] [T]. A rising "calling" snap to a long top note = distance from home [TLTT 352–354] conv. Posthorn calls leap octaves and fifths; the courier's 6/8 "riding post" [Monelle 84, 123] top./conv. Develop the world theme on the way: diminution for speed, augmentation for flashback, fragments for intimacy, combined with the villain near the end [Summers 170 n. 41] conv.
- **Harmony** - ii–V–I spirals into new keys = "from A to B", the best forwards score (walks by rivers, across meadows) [TLTT 215, 269] [T]. Directional harmony under steady ripples for a river beside the road [TLTT 236–238] top. Arc: begin in a strange static mode, end in warm static major [Lehman 237–238] conv. Autumnal road: the far fifth [Lehman 98] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *The road*: a lone flute over open fifths. Wheels: a string ostinato with repeating harmony and the theme on top [Webster Potter 206, 210] conv.; carts: trotting pizzicato (VVA 2653). Setting out: slow timpani or taiko and a trumpet fanfare [Brill 31–32] conv. Distant posthorn: soft trumpets far back [Monelle 164–165] conv. Pilgrims: slow processional march [Monelle 127] conv. Trail ride: heavy-pickup ostinato, oboe on top, major, horse's pace [TLTT 590–592] conv.
- **With / against** - With freedom, the open road; with vastness, the long crossing; with the far-away, a regional colour at the destination; ends in **Arrival and homecoming**. Riders who never reach a town: keep the riding texture and make the tutti a vista [TLTT 394] (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a walking pizzicato bass. Strong: the travel ostinato under the party's tune. Whole: *The road* or a world-map theme; on long travel play long pieces to delay repeats [Summers 96] conv.
- **Not** - A march with one pickup per beat and a long downbeat, which goes nowhere and signals an army [TLTT 297 fn 14] conv. Even note values throughout.

#### Freedom
- **Cues** - Wide range, open intervals, rising leaps, phrases that do not close, release from an ostinato (inf.). "Free" is a crowd-sourced factor (ES); carefree sits high-energy, low-stress (MRX). Expansive gestures and a range past an octave (Tagg MM ch. 8–9). Freedom scored 4.6× for a strict 6/8 ostinato with an open texture: consonant, high, energetic, no low weight, one lead [TLTT 281–288, 302] [T]; the galloping Western with lone lead over-scored freedom 13 vs 3 (VVA 1154 [T]). Breaking free is exitive (VVA 5323). Forest and hunt as the free place outside the walls [Monelle 95, 101, 107] top. Music that does not react to every outcome lets players feel free [Summers 105] conv.
- **Melody** - The soaring topic: rising fifths into held tones (T164, ATK19). A lone lead over steady motion, range an octave plus, major, 6/8 or 4/4, moderate to brisk (inf.). A soloist improvising over a fixed ground: free will within rules [Summers 106] conv.
- **Harmony** - Consonant and open: I, IV, V, a held chord opening out, a key change up (inf.); no low clusters [TLTT 302] [T].
- **Colours and devices** - *The road*; a soaring flute or horn; soft trumpets, flutes or horns over a ground [Summers 106] conv. A high wordless voice with a long tail for one free spirit in a vast space (Morricone): high choir or held solo_violin [TLTT 651–652] (inf.).
- **With / against** - **Against captivity**, as a trajectory: the cell's lone voice, then the orchestra opens beneath it. A major tune whose middle turns minor, narrows and thins makes the freedom around it feel romantic [TLTT 327] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one wide upward leap. Strong: the lone lead over an open ostinato. Whole: the breakout.
- **Not** - Low weight and a narrow range, which block freedom [TLTT 292–302] [T]. Strict metre is fine with an open texture (corrects concepts-v2 "Not: strict metre").

#### Captivity
- **Cues** - Narrow range, a confined repeating figure, an ostinato as walls (§1 Form), sadness cues (§6 r4) (inf. in v1). Tagg files confinement and imprisonment with darkness and weight (VVA 1253, 1254 [T, by grouping]); cramped figures mean enclosed space and a range of a third or less sounds hemmed in (MM ch. 8–9). A dungeon is ombra: the prisoner's dread without anything supernatural [McClelland 285–287] top.
- **Melody** - A line that cannot get past a third, with pauses and echoes (inf.). In *A Life on the Ocean Wave* the "caged" words turn minor, shrink in range and thin out: captivity can be one shrunken phrase inside a free tune [TLTT 327] conv. Slow, free time, minor (inf.).
- **Harmony** - One key held with no change: no way out [Lehman 41] conv. A low held note or pedal under the voice (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - *The cell*: a lone reedy voice (clarinets for harmonica) with bends, long pauses, an echo. A beloved imprisoned since long ago: an archaic plucked harp line in the middle register [Halfyard Apprentice 225] conv.
- **With / against** - With longing, a prisoner's dream. With freedom, the trajectory from cell to open orchestra. With dread, the dungeon's ombra.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one echoed phrase. Strong: the narrow line over a held pedal. Whole: *The cell*.
- **Not** - Full strings: captivity is one voice. Modulation, which opens a door.

#### The exotic and the far-away
