- **Cues** - Psychophysical cues carry feeling across cultures; scales and instruments are local (§8.6 r14 [S]). One or two traits of a foreign style call up the style and through it a culture; used often enough, a synecdoche becomes a norm (§7.6 r2; TLTT 101–102 top.). These codes were Western, made for home audiences, not ethnography [Dickensheets 128; Brill 22–24] top.; insiders do not hear them (0 of 42 Latin-American listeners heard the "Hispanic" tune as theirs) [TLTT 334] [T]. Instruments, not scale or texture, pin down an ethnic reading: pentatonic over parallel fifths drew Japan from only 2 of 122 listeners, mostly countryside, calm, distance [TLTT 521–532, 553–555] [T] (corrects "pentatonic strings drew Japan and China"). Far away and long ago share signs (bare fifths, drones, pentatonics) [TLTT 518, 533] conv.; happy scenes in modal minor read as there or then [TLTT 327–330] [T].
- **Melody** - Historical dialects, to recognise and mostly avoid [Dickensheets Table 3] top.: "Oriental" (one mode, circling one pitch, melismas); Spanish (five-note turn on 5, bolero rhythm); chinoiserie (pentatonic, small precious gestures, trills; mainly late-Romantic); Italian (aria line, tarantella, the gondolier's 6/8); style hongrois (spondee and alla zoppa rhythms, abandoned violin). Alla turca: duple, repeated notes, short figures, unisons, quick ornaments, long notes opening phrases [Mayes 215–216] top. Those hongrois traits are the post-1820s (Liszt) form; the earlier domestic Hungarian style was duple, four-bar, nearly all I and V, with rebounding thirds marked *dolce* [Mayes 216, 224–228] top. Lehman's 5 → b6 over a major tonic [Lehman 98, 100] conv.
- **Harmony** - Near fifth (I → iv, i → V): alluring elsewhere [Lehman 98–100] conv.; mixolydian b6 (bVII, iiø7 in major) semi-exotic [Lehman 238–239] conv. Phrygian bII–i; i–bVII–bVI–V (Andalusian) [Dickensheets Table 3; Caplin 417–419] top. Hongrois: triads side by side, sudden key shifts [Dickensheets 134] top. Chinoiserie: planed add9 and maj7 chords [Dickensheets Table 3] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Regional colour*, *Desert*, *The north*, *Islands*, *Highland pipes*; english_horn and distant horns for distance. Substitutes: harp or strummed pizzicato for guitar; tremolo or fast repeated pizzicato for a mandolin or any "ethnic lute" (all heard alike) [TLTT 467] (inf.); harp repeats with tremolo and slight `wobble` for cimbalom; triangle and toms for tambourine [Dickensheets Table 3] conv. Janissary band: kit bd on downbeats, cymbal, triangle, piccolo, glockenspiel for the jingling pole [Monelle 117–119] top., parts (inf.); quiet = an army far off [Hunter 75–76] top. Verbunk trio: solo_violin, violins2, cellos, harp repeats [Mayes 218–225] (inf.). For invented peoples: one shared frame, each people adding one mode, one instrument pairing and one dance rhythm, kept consistent (R12) [Summers 110–120] conv.; [Dickensheets Table 3; TLTT 553–555] (inf.).
- **With / against** - The carrying concept sets the dials; a foreign idiom over rage turns comic (§7.6 r3). Public vs private: Turkish for loud public scenes, Hungarian for small, intimate ones [Mayes 225–228] top. — e.g. a band at a fortress gate, a trio in its inn (inf.). Even-handed: tinge home and foreign themes with the same mode [Halfyard & Hancock n. 10] conv. Real instruments of another culture jar in an orchestral fantasy score [Brill 33, 37] conv.
- **Intensity** - One gesture is only a colour; a dialect needs several together [Dickensheets 125] top. Whole: a people's own dance.
- **Not** - Any real culture's code for a fantasy people, least of all a villainous one (T079, T171; [Mayes 216–218] (inf.)). The Indianist package in any form [Dickensheets Table 3] top. Stock figures (bandit, harem, ridiculed "native") [Dickensheets 100] top.; the East as sensual and despotic [Brill 19–28] conv. (Turkish style was military at its core [Mayes 221–223] top.). Borrowed sacred music [Hayward 41–46] conv. Pentatonic "orientalism", long since camp [TLTT 531–532, 645–646; Summers 62] conv. Chinoiserie for a human culture; keep it for tiny artisan beings (inf.).

#### The crowd and community
- **Cues** - Shared strong experiences build community (§7.4); a marked, stamping pulse (§8.1). Social anaphones: unison = one voice, heterophony = many saying it their own way, clutter = faceless crowd (MM 514). The march gave about three-quarters of all social-activity answers; town square and small town up to 10× [TLTT 399–409] [T]. With no lead tune, listeners pictured only collectives [TLTT 657–658] [T]. Solo against tutti: the individual free in timing, the crowd steady and louder [Guymer 592–593] conv. Herder's *Volk*: unlettered wisdom, also fuel of nationalism [Monelle 220–228] top. Oratorio choruses stand for a people [Chapin 323–326] top.
- **Melody** - Short, strong, rhythmic, in unison; the chorus taking up the march tune [Monelle 161] conv. Sprightly semiquavers after a plainer A section call up dance and community [TLTT 391–394] (inf.). A solo phrase that others join one by one, each varying it (Forkel's fugue image) [Chapin 323–326] top. 2/4 or 4/4 march tempo (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Plain I–IV–V under the unison; a fugato for an assembly finding its voice that resolves into homophony [Chapin 320–326] (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - *The crowd* (choir in unison or octaves on a short figure, low brass, stamping drums), *National Anthem*, *Jubilant congregation*. Call and response between leader and crowd shows both his authority and their bond [Giuffre 101–102] conv. Many close-layered voices = collective dominance [Stephen 74] conv. Buffa ensembles: each group in its own music at once [Hunter 65–66] top. The concerto style for one voice answered by many (T048). Solo → full ensemble on the tune = arrival among people [TLTT 387–394] (inf.).
- **With / against** - With rage, a mob; with triumph, a finale; with festival, the fair. A layer that enters as a room's people wake and leaves as they doze [Summers 75] conv. A people's anthem louder as you near their settlements [Summers 96] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one choir unison. Strong: call and response. Whole: a crowd song.
- **Not** - Soft harmonised choir chords, which are prayer. A private, single-lead texture.

#### Industry, work and machines
- **Cues** - Machine music: ostinati, motor rhythms, metallic percussion, dissonant repetition; repetitive motion separates machine flight from magic flight (T109, ATK19 top.). Fast, even semiquavers are external and mechanical [TLTT 472] (inf.); "mechanical, tiring, noise" went to a low riff, "age of the machine" 6.3× [TLTT 607–623] [T]. Timbre decides: one short-short-short-LONG rhythm is a ticker or a gallop [TLTT 341] conv. Library music sets technology against nature as tense (Tagg Nature). Learned style signified routine and the mechanical [Chapin 323–326] top.; display was grouped with the mechanical [Ivanovitch 331, 341] top. Square periodicity is labour (MM ch. 8). Game factory topic: high percussion, syncopation, active bass (T182).
- **Melody** - Short interlocking cells in 5/4, 5/8 or 7/8; a rising melodic-minor scale for an industrial power [Summers 160–161] conv. Train effect: unchanging, mildly dissonant semiquaver clusters with horn and woodwind tunes over them [TLTT 654–656] conv. Chromatic slithering for toil [TLTT 575–576] conv. Constructs: falling three- and four-note cells on winds and tuba (SW 13). Guild work songs in any lively metre [Haringer 200] top.
- **Harmony** - Quartal chords for technical modernity (Kojak §6.3 [T, conv.]); non-functional moves for constructs (SW 13). A marvel: i(maj7) under cascading harp, celesta and string arpeggios [Murphy 160] conv. Repeating harmony under a wheels ostinato [Webster Potter 206, 210] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *The place's own rhythm*, *Tick Tock Tune*, *Mickey Mousing*. Isengard's five-beat ostinato on plates and drums (T170) → low bells, taiko, toms, low brass sforzandi, kit gong [Barron 86–90] conv.; chains as snare and triangle. Low bells and taiko over a building bass line [Summers 136] conv. Dry high strokes (typewriter) → pizzicato, staccato piccolo, glockenspiel [TLTT 496] conv. Constructs march in low woodwinds [Brill 30] conv.; a golem: quartal pizzicato ostinato with choppy brass.
- **With / against** - With tyranny, a war machine. With playfulness, a gnome's workshop. **Against nature**: in a pastoral tale machine sound should be an invasion [Barron 86–92] conv. With work, square labour song.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a ticking pizzicato. Whole: a 5/4 machine ostinato under a tune.
- **Not** - Realistic sound effects; suggest the sound in rhythm and pitch.

#### Crime and the underworld
- **Cues** - Tagg's best-documented code: minor, jazz tinge, chromatic lines and prominent tritones (a melodic b5, half-diminished and minor-six chords) (Tagg Tritone; TLTT 579–588). The slow minor jazz tune drew the most answers and agreement of all ten: gangsters 6.4×, murder 8.9×, slums 5.8×, smoke 5.3× [TLTT 539–550] [T]; with the same chord as a romance it won detective 8–28× and crime 50:0 [TLTT 458–460] [T]. Music fixes milieu and types (the mob, a private eye), never particulars [TLTT 602–603] (inf.).
- **Melody** - The noir lone-wolf line: high violins opening on b5 (b5–4–b3) with triplet syncopation; b5 falling to 4 before the cadence = street-wise [TLTT 580–583] [T] conv. The ninth rubbing the minor third in the foreground (sleuth themes) [TLTT 464] conv. Slow, low, plodding pulse, minor, 4/4 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - i6 and iiø7 held as chords in their own right on soft mid-low brass; the harsher they are, the nearer horror [TLTT 566–572] conv. iadd9 voiced chromatically with the b3–2 rub [TLTT 461–462] conv.; i(maj9) = spy [TLTT 206 fn. 58] top. bVI and a slide over bii, ending open [TLTT 541] [T]. Minor triads moved as blocks = a seedy world where no one is trusted [Lehman 212–213] conv. Free-standing iv6/iiø7 = jazz angst [TLTT 192–193] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Creepy jazz*. Low clarinets or english_horn sliding (saxophone), soft trumpets, soft snare, walking pizzicato basses. Trombones groaning a semitone with `slide` and `wobble` [TLTT 461–462] conv./(inf.). Medieval setting: keep the plod, the chords, the groans and the b5 line; drop jazz markers [TLTT 563–583] (inf.). Spy: soft snare tattoos, a twangy lead as harp left ringing [TLTT 377–378] conv. Crime lord: tuba grotesque dance (SW 18).
- **With / against** - With cunning, the guild; night, the alleys; poverty, the slums; menace, the enforcer. Genteel manor crime gets light filigree (harp, pizzicato), noir the docks [TLTT 580–583] conv. A seedy venue can be comic: minor-second dyads on an off-kilter ostinato [Summers 164] conv. A swindle: merry gigue → panic → mock pomp → a laugh [Hunter 66] top.; a heist gone wrong, see Intrigue and farce.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one b5 in the melody. Whole: slow minor theme on low reeds over a walking bass.
- **Not** - Glamour by default: these tunes carry danger and anguish. The cliché unchanged; refresh it by breaking other parameters [TLTT 585–588] conv.

#### Festival and celebration
- **Cues** - Joy and festivity (fairs, markets, carnivals) over-scored for the brass-band 6/8 march (VVA 113, 1138, 272 [T]); festivities 7.9× and flags 10×, with party, market and circus outnumbering war 14:1 [TLTT 399–410] [T]; children pictured parades and ice-cream [TLTT 251] [T]. Festive trumpets of triumphs and games [Haringer 198] top. Brisk modal minor with a festive rhythm = a festival long ago or far away [TLTT 327–330] [T]; some peoples hear minor as festive [TLTT 320] conv. Game Christmas topic: brass, voices, sleigh bells (T175).
- **Melody** - Brisk 6/8 march with a chromatic tune skipping to a sixth or ninth over I–V: fun, display, parade [TLTT 97, 123] top./[T]. Intrada: two short 6/8 or 3/8 reprises with a catchy opening [Haringer 203] top. Village fête: fast 2/4 or 6/8, triplet lilt, 4+4 phrases, terraced dynamics (inf.). Harvest revel: 6/8 fiddling over drones [Monelle 239, 242] conv. Jovial baroque-dance counterpoint [Summers 163] conv.
- **Harmony** - Every chord major with its own II-type neighbour = giddiness [Lehman 82–83] top. Drones that move with each new I [Monelle 239] top. I–V march harmony.
- **Colours and devices** - *Festival trumpets*, *Jubilant congregation*, *The tavern*; 6/8 brass march, gigue, contredanse (T059, T064); triangle and glockenspiel (sleigh bells); pealing bells. Court feast: 6/8 dance on flutes, triangle, lyre-like harp [Brill 36] conv. South: tarantella, saltarello [Dickensheets 107] top. Peasant scherzo over a tonic pedal [Monelle 243] conv. Midwinter: brass chorale, celesta, pifferari drone [Monelle 198–199] top. Hunt feast: horns and voices (St Hubert) [Monelle 58, 69–70] top.
- **With / against** - With the crowd, the fair; with ecstasy, the peak; with dread, the feast before the attack (R4 by moment). Contredanse as a whole-cast finale [McKee 169] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a pealing bell figure. Whole: festival march then a dance.
- **Not** - Solemn ceremony: a festival is noisy and loose.

#### The dance
- **Cues** - Joy's urge to dance (§7.2; GEMS .72). Metre and tempo cannot be removed from a dance [Rumph 498] top.; a dance type is rhythm and movement, so any tune can be set into one [Dickensheets 101, 103] top. Historical dances (allemande, bourrée, gavotte, gigue, sarabande, siciliano) evoke idealized pasts; current ones (contredanse, minuet, Ländler) feel bodily and present [McKee 165–166] top.; an old dance means a passing society [Monelle 6] top. Dance answers were always public places [TLTT 391–394] [T]; a busy public dance is spectacle, not courtship [TLTT 498–501] [T].
- **Melody** - Minuet 3/4, sentences 2+2+4 [McKee 172–173] top. Bourrée: duple, two-eighth pickup, lift onto 1 [Zbikowski 144–157] top. Gavotte: 2/2 with half-bar pickup; in 2/4 it reads servant-class or contredanse [Hunter 84–85; McKee n. 6] top. Contredanse: fast 2/4 or 6/8, major, triplet lilt, 4+4 [McKee 169–173] top. Early waltz: 8+8 with trio, one chord a bar, flowing eighths [McKee 180–187] top. Ländler: slower, arpeggiated, ending in a stamp-stop [McKee 176; Byros 398] top. Sarabande: slow 3/2 or 3/4 from the downbeat; weight on 2 is one reading (Allanbrook, Rumph) not agreed [Mirka Intro 23; Rumph 493–500] top. One long-middle three-note cell becomes six dances by tempo and bar position [Rumph 499–500] top.
- **Harmony** - Ländler/Deutscher: I and V, a chord a bar [McKee 180–187] top. Bourrée: V/I alternation, then vi and 6/3 chains [Zbikowski 151–157] top. Court waltz: minor and modulation as sophistication [Dickensheets 103] top. A dark ball: waltz over i ↔ bvi [Lehman 256 n1] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Minuet and polonaise for court; bourrée (pleasant, swaggering, historical; can serve dwarves through its swagger, not as "rustic") and gavotte for old courtly grace [Mirka Intro 7; Dickensheets Table 1] top.; contredanse for every rank together [McKee 166–169] top.; Ländler, galop, tarantella, waltz, pavane, tango (T016, T066–T074). Real rustic signs: drone, untidy pastorela, stamping Ländler; siciliano is tender and "high" pastoral, middle or upper rank [Sutcliffe 135–136; Haringer 204–206; Monelle 219–220] top. Lead in with swirls or a fanfare [TLTT 101–102; McKee n. 41] top. *The court dance*, *The tavern*, *Façade*.
- **With / against** - The dance tells rank and place, the tune tells who; a court ball against a village dance: modulating balanced phrases vs diatonic arpeggio tunes [Dickensheets 102–103] (inf.). A dance drifting between source and score stitches mundane to magic [Webster Potter n. 19] conv. A people's own dance keeps its hemiola, strum on alternate beats [TLTT 336–343] (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one dance rhythm in the accompaniment. Whole: the dance.
- **Not** - The wrong rank unless it is the joke. "The waltz" as one class: peasant and high forms differ [Dickensheets 102–103] top. Oom-pah under an 18th-century waltz [McKee 180–187] top. A waltz alone for a coarse, feral folk [Hayward 47] conv.

#### Revelry and drink
- **Cues** - Rowdy, boisterous, hedonistic (AMT; MRX; AMM). Drink and smoke went to a low riff (alcohol 5.6×), living it up to a glossy club tune (9.2×), bars and smoke to the slow minor jazz tune [TLTT 539–550, 607–643] [T]; orchestrally, a loud stamping folk dance. Hobnail stamping and clapping with stops [McKee 176] top.; an irregular phrase sets beer-hall dancers leaping [McKee 180–187] top. The modal-minor happy tune drew Mediterranean tavern dancing, the strum figure tavern and laughter [TLTT 329, 338] [T]. Source music makes the room real; underscore is inner life [Binns 144; Webster Potter 198] conv.
- **Melody** - Quick 6/8 or 3/4 dance tune, stepwise with leaps, major or modal minor (inf.). Ländler stamp-stop (two eighths, a quarter, silence) and Scotch snaps [Hatten 517; Lowe 612] top. A drunk: slow semitone slides between two pitches [TLTT 575–577] conv.
- **Harmony** - I–V drones and I–IV–V; a pause before the cadence lands as a joke [Margulis 636–638] [ev], tavern use (inf.). A haunted tavern: the tune's chords loop pantriadically under a normal melody [Lehman 71] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *The tavern*: solo_violin (fiddle), flutes (whistle), harp (lute), toms (hand drum), triangle; gigue, contredanse, buffa patter (T059, T064, T044). Wine-house songs: tremolo or fast repeated pizzicato and high harp for the mandolin [Dickensheets Table 3 n. e] top./conv. A drunk: trombones with `slide` and slow `wobble` [TLTT 575–576] conv. A musette pair (oboe and bassoons) as the accordion [Summers 75] conv. (parts inf.). A rowdy turn: 1+3 phrase with cymbal and tutti winds (inf.).
- **With / against** - With festival, the feast; with crime, the smoky den; with the bard, a lay the room joins; with menace, the brawl. An extra layer joins when the drinkers wake and stops when they doze [Summers 75] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a stamping rhythm. Whole: a tavern dance that speeds up to the last dance of the night [McKee 165–166] top.
- **Not** - Polished orchestral sweep: a tavern band is small. Underscoring a gesture unless source music is already playing [Webster Potter 203] conv.

#### Contest and games
- **Cues** - Competition sits in the dynamism field (VVA 1011). The fast bright sports tune drew big city, chase, television and dance competitions more than sport (corrects "over-scored for sport" VVA 274): evidence for the bustle and show of a public contest [TLTT 477–501] [T]. Sport ritualises combat: sport and military marches swap, the brisk march drew equestrian sport 8.8× [TLTT 399–406, 426] [T]. Serious contests take little music, only between phases; arcade fun takes brash continuous music [Summers 89–92, 110] conv. Trumpets at sacred games [Haringer 198] top.
- **Melody** - The sports fanfare: rising fifths with no third [TLTT 624–625] conv. A progress ladder: one step up per success, back down on a setback [Summers 194] conv. Battle of wits: two motifs tossed between oboe and violins in shorter and shorter answers [Hunter 73–78] (inf.). Taunting = too-repeated simple antiphony [Hunter 73–78] top. A bards' contest opens with two answering pipes [Monelle 271] (inf.). An old-style event is a 6/8 march [TLTT 409–412] conv.
- **Harmony** - Open fifths and I–V for the fanfare; plain major for the bouts (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Brass fanfares between bouts, a quick staccato pizzicato and piccolo ostinato, snare. Tournament: chivalric fanfares and gallop (**Chivalry and knighthood**). Games venue or fair: jovial baroque-dance counterpoint [Summers 163] conv. Puzzle canons for a game of wits [Chapin 311–314] top.
- **With / against** - With spectacle, the arena; with combat, the duel; with comedy, a contest gone wrong.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a fanfare between rounds. Whole: a quick staccato ostinato with brass.
- **Not** - Real danger: a contest has rules. Music under a serious race.

#### Spectacle and performance
- **Cues** - Presentational field (parade, circus, pageant) over-scored eightfold for the brass-band march (VVA 273 [T]); circus 9.4×, pomp and bombast [TLTT 399–406] [T]. Brilliance is high and prominent, legible from afar [Ivanovitch 338–348] top./conv.; the concerto sets who is in control and where eyes go: public over private, surface over substance [Ivanovitch 332–333] top. Brilliance is double-edged: a virtuoso, or someone acting one [Ivanovitch 348] top. Finales and credits showcase music [Summers 17] conv.
- **Melody** - The virtuosic style: rapid octaves, parallel thirds, chromatic scales [Dickensheets 111–112] top.; aria style: florid, wide leaps, ornaments [Dickensheets 108] top. Bravura, cantabile and brillant as three faces [Horton 649–653] conv. A showpiece as a parenthesis: solo flight, an exit trill, then the group restores order [Ivanovitch 342–348] conv.
- **Harmony** - Static harmony under display (T038). Logo fanfare: lydian I–II rocking, then bVI–bVII–I; plain V–I is "not wondrous enough" [Lehman 3] conv.; martial close bVII → I [Lehman 242 n9] conv.; fanfare on I(add b6) [Lehman 255 n35] conv. Learned to brilliant: 7–6 chain on a falling bass, then the solo [Ivanovitch 342–348] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *The circus*, *Showman's bookends*, a 6/8 brass march, solo_violin cadenza (Paganini manner) [Dickensheets 111] top. Barrel organ: celesta and clarinets over oom-pah with slight `wobble`. String `tremolo` displays the band's discipline [Ivanovitch 336–338] top. Up-and-back harp sweeps [TLTT 386] [T]. "I rise to speak": grand opening chords [Beghin 552–570] conv. Tuning as a signal: oboe A, strings join [Summers 16] conv. Stadium pomp: full brass and choir over kit [TLTT 422–426] (inf.). A bard playing an underscore theme lands like a spell [Halfyard Apprentice 229–230] conv.
- **With / against** - With the bard, a performance; menace, the sinister troupe; pride, the showman. Circus and parade share the brisk 6/8 march [TLTT 97, 123] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a pickup flourish. Whole: circus march with a virtuoso solo.
- **Not** - Sincerity. Display read as meaning on its own [Ivanovitch 341] top. Virtuosity as only fast notes [Guymer 587–588] conv. A famous modern voice [Halfyard Intro 11] conv.

#### Sanctuary and safety
- **Cues** - "Protected, secure" over-scored for the oboe pastoral (VVA 1123 [T]); secure 9.1×, harmonious 6.2× for oboe in 6/8 at 72, circle-of-fifths harmony, no percussion [TLTT 503–515] [T]. Calm = high valence, low arousal (BAR); lullaby features (§8.6 r8); lower arousal after a fight (§7.6 r16). Silence usually marks a safe room; the danger bed stopping says the area is clear; the calm rubato returning says the threat has passed [Summers 116–130] conv. Pastoral frozen time as a haven [Monelle 189, 195–197] top. Stirred, then allowed to recover (celestial after the Furies) [McClelland 287] top.
- **Melody** - Slow stepwise flute or oboe tune, lilting thirds, regular phrases, G or F major [McClelland 287] top. A falling minor fifth on solo cellos for a remote holy refuge (SW 53).
- **Harmony** - The Ave Maria chord (I – ii7/IV6 over a held tonic – V – I), moderato or slower: consolation, well-being [TLTT 173–179] top. Elven refuge: I ↔ bVI with maj7 and #5 colour [Lehman 177] conv. Smooth common-tone voice leading [Lehman 141–143] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Harp arpeggios, held strings, flutes in thirds, soft choir; no percussion. Pastoral drone for a safe village (T011); chorale for a temple refuge (T022); curving arpeggios for an elven house (LOTR Rivendell [A]). A priest's chant in a temple after the storm [Sisman 110] conv.
- **With / against** - With healing, rest after battle; with home, the hearth; with dread, a sanctuary under siege (a drone creeping underneath).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one harp arpeggio. Whole: slow arpeggiated texture with no pulse.
- **Not** - Anything sudden. Tritone or semitone harshness, diminished chords [TLTT 179] top. Breaking the safe-room silence outside horror [Summers 129–134] conv.

#### The mundane
*New (from Halfyard): the non-magical side of a fantasy frame, marked by the absence of score.*
- **Cues** - Leave the ordinary world unscored so underscore can mean magic [Webster Potter 198–200] conv. Source music furnishes a room; underscore projects inner life [Binns 144; Webster Potter 198] conv. Letting sound effects through with the score stopped favours realism [Webster Potter n. 22] conv.
- **Melody** - Only tunes that seem to belong to the room: a tavern song, a dance band [Binns 144–146; Webster Potter 202–203] conv. Plain duple tunes in steady four-bar phrases (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Diatonic, standard I–IV–V–I in duple: reason and the familiar [Webster Potter 203, 209] top.
- **Colours and devices** - An in-world band: clarinets, pizzicato basses, snare taps, trumpets [Webster Potter 202–203] conv.
- **With / against** - Against magic, by moment: silent until the first magical intrusion brings music in [Webster Potter 199] conv. A self-aware fantasy hands the mundane popular music so it "sounds real" [Binns 146] conv. A dance that slips from source to score stitches the two [Webster Potter n. 19] conv. With the city, an unscored street with the marvel alone scored.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one source cue in an unscored scene. Whole: a silent scene, or only the in-world band.
- **Not** - Underscoring errands and paperwork, which spends what should signal magic (inf.).

#### The outsider
*New (from Halfyard): a wondrous stranger in a community that reads difference as threat. Not Loneliness (a feeling) or The exotic (a far people).*
- **Cues** - The outsider's music sounds in the community only during their wondrous acts; elsewhere the town keeps its own source music [Binns 142–146] conv. When it leaks into the town it makes the magic and marks it as foreign [Binns 142, 145] conv.
- **Melody** - A melancholy waltz [Binns 140] conv.; slow 3/4, minor, a lyrical line with a lift and fall (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Rich and tonal, not unstable, so the stranger reads as wondrous, not mad [Binns 140] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Wordless choir, celesta, harp, full strings [Binns 134, 140] conv.
- **With / against** - Threatening and threatened at once [Binns 139–145] conv. Against the mob: celesta and a solo line against the crowd [Binns 144] conv. The community hears fantasy and madness alike as Other [Binns 139] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one celesta phrase cutting through the town's band. Whole: the outsider's waltz with choir.
- **Not** - Exotic codes [Brill 19–24] conv. Madness devices: strange is not deranged [Binns 139] conv.

#### Heralds, signals and messengers
*New (merged from Monelle and Tagg proposals): the call that carries news or an order — herald, watchtower alarm, courier, crier, sending spell. Not Arrival (the arrival itself) or Agitation (the felt state).*
- **Cues** - Named functions to build from: saddle up, mount, rally, retreat, set the watch, attention, church call, attack, charge [Monelle 136–137, 164–166, 179] top. Register sets tone: low and slow dignified, high and fast nervous [Monelle 139–141, 179] top. News code: a one-pitch figure in syncopated long-short values with irregular gaps (Morse) drew news 5.9×, importance and credibility [TLTT 487–493] [T]; even gaps lose the urgency and read as an engine [TLTT 654–656] (inf.). Very quiet high dry pulses turn heads [TLTT 644–645] [T].
- **Melody** - Herald: two trumpets, a short triadic fanfare [Monelle 163–164] top.; signals on harmonics 3–8, often one repeated note [Monelle 166, 281] top. Courier: posthorn octave and fifth leaps, 6/8 "riding post" [Monelle 84, 123] top. Intrada flourish for a great entry [Monelle 135] top. The telegraph figure can sit under a slow lyrical line [TLTT 487–493] conv.
- **Harmony** - Unison for a field signal; harmonised parts for a civic or court call [Monelle 167–169] top. Major for confident tidings, minor thirds for dire, mixolydian bVII for worldly news [TLTT 494–495] (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Offstage trumpets far back for a tower or distant herald [Monelle 126, 167] top., placement (inf.). A call cutting into a peaceful texture [Monelle 164–165] conv. Telegraph rhythm on kit snare or one high pizzicato note; soft trumpets for an official signal; staccato piccolo and glockenspiel for cheery news; timpani bursts for a quarrelling council [TLTT 487–493] conv. (inf.).
- **With / against** - With rescue, the tower call announcing the rescuer [Monelle 167] conv.; with death, the slow church call [Monelle 179] top.; with satire, the call soured on each repeat [Monelle 175–178] conv. With war, the dispatch; with the city, the crier; with alarm, the beacon (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one soft distant call or one telegraph burst. Strong: call and echo, or the telegraph ostinato under the tune. Whole: a proclamation or news fanfare framing the scene (inf.).
- **Not** - A quoted real bugle call, which reads modern [Monelle 165–166] top. Double-tonguing on horns [Monelle 80] top. Evenly quantised repeats.

#### Commerce and the market
*New (from Summers). Shops, stalls, traders.*
- **Cues** - Light and bouncy, pizzicato everywhere; a non-reactive loop that does not comment on each bargain [Summers 102–106] conv.
- **Melody** - Perky little tunes in unison flutes or clarinets, short calls answered by solo_violin, trills as decoration [Summers 102–103] conv. Each shop a variation of the town tune in its trade's timbre (oboe hornpipe for a craftsman, harp arpeggios for a mapmaker) [Summers 75] conv. 2/4, allegretto, major (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Bright diatonic major, frequent cadences, nothing dark; light-orchestral optimism (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Light-orchestral pastiche (Leroy Anderson): pizzicato strings, doubled flutes or clarinets, solo_violin answers, harp and glockenspiel, woodwind and string trills [Summers 102–103] conv.
- **With / against** - With **The city**, a shop is one variation in the hub rondo [Summers 75] conv. With wealth, a grand emporium with a string pad; with cunning, a swindler's chromatic slip (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a pizzicato figure and a glockenspiel glint as the door opens. Whole: the full light-orchestral cue (inf.).
- **Not** - Stings on every purchase [Summers 105] conv. A busy cue at full level under haggling [Summers 153–155] conv.

#### The frontier and the borderland
*New (from Tagg): where two peoples' idioms meet — border marches, a seam town, a mixed caravan route. Not Vastness (no people) or The exotic (one people).*
- **Cues** - Fast, bright, consonant with a light bass: the border at peace reads lively and festive [TLTT 336–341] [T]. Placing one side's strum under the other side's tune locates the action on the border itself [TLTT 341] (inf.).
- **Melody** - Blend one marker from each side: the home side's rising "calling" snap to a long top note; the other side's compound 6/8 and a short-long falling cell at phrase ends [TLTT 357] (inf.). Flat-seventh minor (dorian, aeolian) for the settlers, a phrygian descent for the other side [TLTT 322–336] conv.
- **Harmony** - Home side: i ↔ bVII or i ↔ v, bVII → V, no dominant pull; other side: i–bVII–bVI–V and pivotless relative major/minor swings [TLTT 334–362] conv. Pivot dominants and unprovoked Picardy thirds sound like an outsider's polish [TLTT 343–346] [T].
- **Colours and devices** - The strum figure (a quaver and four semiquavers) on pizzicato or harp; soft snare clops on quavers 2 and 5; a twangy lead as harp left ringing [TLTT 336–343] conv./(inf.).
- **With / against** - With raids or smugglers, darken with a low drive and dissonance (see Chase and pursuit); love across the line, see Tragic love [TLTT 305, 469] (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: one phrygian bII–I cadence in a home-side tune. Strong: the strum under the home tune. Whole: the two idioms alternating by section with a hybrid tune (inf.).
- **Not** - Either side's markers as a real people's music: insiders did not recognise them (0 of 42) [TLTT 334] [T]. Comic national stereotypes [TLTT 355] conv.

### J. Motion, energy and the body

#### Speed
- **Cues** - Tempo is the strongest single cue [S]; notes per second add to it (§1 Tempo); accelerando is a vitality affect (§1 Change). But tempo is not speed and surface rate is not enough: the busiest, fastest tune (126 bpm, 504 notes a minute) drew fewer fast and action answers than a slower one with power, aggression and low, unchanging reiteration [TLTT 483–486] [T] (corrects concepts-v2 "surface rate often matters more", VVA 522). Fast notes count only in the foreground or detached; a low legato pedalled arpeggio at 276 a minute under a walking tune gave zero speed [TLTT 165–168] [T]. Register sorts the kind: fast and high = chases, abstract action, traffic; fast, low, one pitch = engines, vehicles, menace [TLTT 496–498, 622–623, 655–656] [T]. Speed without threat turns to bustle or comedy [TLTT 493–494] [T]. Gaits: about 52 a funeral, 66 largo, 92 walking, 120 brisk march, 140 cavalry trot, 160 running and the charge (MM ch. 8; [TLTT 168 fn. 3] top.; [Monelle 119–123, 180–181] top.). "Speedy" goes with "tense" in game music (YM).
- **Melody** - The gallop: three or four quick notes into one as long as all of them, simple or compound metre [TLTT 295] conv. Engine: staccato semiquaver octaves on an unchanging pitch, accents by loudness, two bars on and two off, in cellos, basses and bassoons [TLTT 654–656] conv. [T]. Patter: very fast in a tiny range [Hunter 79–82] top. Diminution of a known theme [Summers 170 n. 41] conv.; switch to shorter beat units to fake a faster tempo [Mirka Topics and Meter 361–366; Rumph 505–506] top. Double-tonguing lifts a call's surface rate [Monelle 166–167] top.
- **Harmony** - Pump-ups (step modulations) paired with tempo or metre changes [Lehman 57] conv. Fast harmonic rhythm over a pedal; minor thirds for threat (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - String runs, a snare or pizzicato ostinato, a gallop, *The great battle*'s two speeds. Fast harp or string arpeggios blur into a ring [Ivanovitch 342–348] top. Tie the tempo to the speed of what the hero rides [Summers 193–195] conv.
- **With / against** - With weight, broad horns over a fast high layer. With fear, flight. With pursuit, **Chase and pursuit**. With the cavalry, quick marches; infantry slow [Haringer 200] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one fast run. Strong: a low reiterated engine figure under the tune. Whole: a chase.
- **Not** - Speed alone: fast reads as joy or anger until other cues decide. Bright, high, threat-free busyness when you want velocity [TLTT 483–498] [T]. Low legato waves counted as speed.

#### Stillness
- **Cues** - Very slow, sparse notes, the lowest activation (§1 Tempo); a small dynamic range (§1 DynVar); time standing still (§7.4). No explicit pulse = kinetic stillness (Semio99 p. 27); held chords or slow harmonic change = stasis; drones suspend time (MM ch. 8–9). No pulse and no goal = time stopped [Summers 102] conv. The slowest-surface tune (168 notes a minute) drew calm 3.8×, motionless (excl.) and rest 7× [TLTT 521–537] [T]: slow, quiet, wide spacing, a static bass, asynchronous rhythms, parallel non-triadic chords on muted strings [TLTT 535–536] (inf.) [T]. "As it always has been" stasis comes from predictability instead (square periods, circle of fifths) [TLTT 514] [T]. Michaelis: one chord repeated, very slow motion, long pauses [McClelland 290–294] top. Pastoral time stands still [Monelle 189, 195–197] top.; still dawn largo about 50 [TLTT 168 fn. 3] top.
- **Melody** - A line hanging an octave above a slow wavy accompaniment, triplets tied over the bar to blur the metre [TLTT 532–533] [T]. Augmentation: whole notes over a running pulse [Rumph 505] top. Waiting: a slowed, quiet version of a known theme [Summers 19] conv. High trills held at peaks step outside time [Monelle 243–245] conv. Solo recitative suspends time [Head 259–263] conv.
- **Harmony** - Unchanging key: the true opposite of the pump-up, and expressive in itself [Lehman 65] top. Static held triads with no harmonic rhythm (awe; a dream's drift) [Brill 24, 34; Webster Potter 210] conv. Tonic drone: the lake going nowhere, the timeless place [TLTT 238–239] top. Phrygian drone for the remote [Lehman 237–238] conv. Deceptive V → vi can halt time [Lehman 188] conv. Klangfläche: I and V merged over a pedal [Horton 660] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Held drone, held choir chords, single harp notes, *Silence and the stop*. Soft horn over an unmoving string chord; one high note held by all strings [Monelle 100–101, 258] conv. A refrain that freezes a parting [Head 272–273] conv.
- **With / against** - With wonder, the moment after the peak; with dread, waiting. A still scene turning into a walk when a bird interval becomes a song [Monelle 258] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a bar of held fifth. Whole: no pulse at all.
- **Not** - Boredom: keep one moving voice (§3; YM; GMC). Walking pace with directional harmony, which strolls forward [TLTT 168, 215] [T].

#### Weight
- **Cues** - Low register, slow, a chord on every beat, octaves and unisons (§1 Register, Rhythm, Intervals). Hevner's heavy clusters win on low pitch (HEV cl.2, cl.8). Low is heavy, dark, deep (MM ch. 8; VVA 1252): low notes take more energy, decay slowly and blur when fast; the same figure two octaves up lightens [TLTT 568] (inf.). Weight comes from long beat units and held notes more than from loudness [Mirka Topics and Meter 358–361; Guymer 579–581] top. Herrmann's scale: low heavy instruments for evil, light balanced textures for heroism [Brill 25] conv.
- **Melody** - The heavy anacrusis: pedal crotchets with a chromatic run on beat 4 into each downbeat, in low octaves; slow and low it drew dark, heavy, difficult, sweat [TLTT 590–595] [T]. Powerful bass melodies and unisons [Sisman 92–96] top. Heavy duplets against 6/8 and contrabass fillers [TLTT 465–466] conv. Four heavy steps a bar, or four across two bars (Elgar's tread) [Monelle 130–133] top. Half-note beats, slow, low octaves (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Blocky root-position chords in one mode for effort; with register leaps, massiveness [Lehman 143–144] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Low brass, basses, tuba, timpani, low choir; a heavy upbeat into each downbeat. A colossus: slow brass and timpani march [Brill 30] conv. Molto pesante massiveness as a stock idiom [TLTT 470] conv.
- **With / against** - With power, the march. **Against lightness**: a giant who dances, a light tune over a heavy bass. A heavier-footed peasant dance after a light one [Hunter 62–64] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a low octave doubling. Whole: slow and low.
- **Not** - Loudness for its own sake.

#### Lightness
- **Cues** - High, soft, few harmonics, flowing broken chords (§1 Rhythm; Hevner); graceful/sparkling: major, high, simple (HEV cl.5). High is light in both senses (MM ch. 8; Kojak §6.1.2); lightness, openness and freshness share a field (VVA 115). Consonant, high, energetic music with almost no low register sounds open, across a horizon [TLTT 302] (inf.). Two kinds: detached and quick, or gliding (hyper-legato, no faster than moderato, no transients) [TLTT 131] top. Fast, high, staccato, major and dry tips into comedy [TLTT 493–498] [T].
- **Melody** - Buffa scurrying: light-footed, *p*, a small upbeat figure [Hunter 62–64] top. Slurred eighths gentler (musette), detached livelier (gigue) [Haringer 205] top. Short beat units and short notes; for light but slow, short values under a slow tempo [Mirka Topics and Meter 358–361; Guymer 579–583] conv. Grieg's quick leggiero minor for sylphs and elves [TLTT 321] conv. High register, small steps and thirds (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Whole-tone harmony for weightlessness [Lehman 52–53] top. Major triads in high open voicing, no bass (inf.).
- **Colours and devices** - Flutes, pizzicato, harp, celesta, high violins, no bass. Butterfly flutter: fine-motor figures (VVA 2992; MM 498).
- **With / against** - With playfulness and youth. **Against weight**. Light balanced textures carry heroism and beauty against heavy evil [Brill 25] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a staccato flute answer. Whole: nothing below the treble.
- **Not** - Brass. Staccato as the only lightness: staccato is sharp and angular, legato is round and flowing [TLTT 169] top.

#### Sickness
- **Cues** - No research on sickness as such. Tagg files illness with darkness, weight, confinement and decay (VVA 1257 [T, by grouping]); groans and moans are paralinguistic anaphones (VVA 5965); semitone glides connote moaning (MM, after TLTT 573–577). Register sorts sliding small intervals: low = nausea (the stomach), middle = sadness (the chest), high = the weird (the head) [TLTT 576–577] (inf.) conv. (corrects the viola slide). (inf.): a dragging tempo, a weak or irregular pulse, falling lines, thin texture; fever as fast tremolo with `wobble`.
- **Melody** - Low slides and semitone groans for nausea; a slow two-pitch wobble for seasickness; a low growl drew "nauseated" (excl.) [TLTT 573–577] [T] conv. Quarter-tones for delirium and drugs [TLTT 575–576] conv. A short falling line in slow 4/4 or free time, minor, narrow range (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Weak, unresolved chords: a held minor chord drifting off pitch with `unhinge` and back (inf.). A tarantella for poison or a curse that compels (T069).
- **Colours and devices** - Bassoons and trombones with `slide` and slow `wobble` for nausea; wide `wobble` and `unhinge` for delirium [TLTT 573–576] conv.; high strings only for delirium. A frail solo with `wobble` (*The ghost's voice*); a bodily strand of heartbeats under the story music: soft timpani or a pizzicato pulse [Summers 67–69] conv. (parts inf.). Plague: warning bells (T101), the Dies irae (T090).
- **With / against** - **Against healing**, a trajectory: the pitch comes back true. With fragility, the frail patient.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one wobbling held note. Whole: a sickroom vigil.
- **Not** - Horror: sickness is weak, not menacing. A mid-range viola slide, which reads as sad.

#### Healing
- **Cues** - Healing songs had the fewest rhythm changes (§8.3 [W-M]); lullaby features (§8.6 r8); consonance (§8.2 [S]); dark to light (§7.1, §7.6 r4). Tranquillity includes security and harmony (VVA 1123–1124). Healing or sleep magic as the celestial: gentle tempo, regular phrases, G or F major [McClelland 287] top. (inf.).
- **Melody** - Lilting stepwise thirds in flutes, slow, regular four-bar phrases, major, 3/4 or 6/8 (McClelland 287 top.; tune (inf.)). A sighing seventh falling onto the ii7 (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The Ave Maria progression (I – ii7/IV6 over a held tonic – V – I): consolation, "sweet sleep" [TLTT 173–178] top. Plagal close. Laying a curse or spirit to rest: a tritone-free hexatonic swell that resolves and thins [Murphy 160–165] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Cherubic Choir*, *Lullaby*; harp for healing and revival [Brill 29, 34–36] conv.; celesta and soft high strings on one held chord as light rises [Murphy 160] conv.; flutes in thirds over harp arpeggios. A pitch drift resolving true (`unhinge` ending). A healer's rest: low strings or horns on I – ii7 – V – I (inf.).
- **With / against** - With holiness, a miracle. **Against sickness**, a trajectory. With renewal, the land healed.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one consonant chord after a dissonance. Whole: a calm, unchanging hymn.
- **Not** - Triumph: healing is quiet. Brass or percussion entries.

#### War
- **Cues** - War songs: low, fast, wide-ranging, the most rhythm changes (§8.3 [W-M]); trumpets signal war (§7.5); anger and power cues (§6 r6, r10). The brass-band march is evidence for parade, display and victory, not war: listeners heard it with zero enmity, festivities outnumbering war 14:1 [TLTT 410] [T] (corrects VVA 275 reading). Battle needs a driving low line, dissonance, syncopated trombones and tom fills [TLTT 300] [T]; since 1944, newsreels gave war sombre underscore and kept marches for victory [TLTT 416–417] conv. The epic topic: large forces, fast heavy drum ostinati, major chords with dissonance, brass tunes (T161). Battle = tempest + military + heroic together [Dickensheets 127] top.; a battle tempest can be major [Dickensheets 116] top. The dysphoric march is 19th-century (Berlioz's march to the scaffold) [Horton 643–644] top., and the doomed soldier is a lied tradition [Monelle 176–180] top. (corrects "euphoric until the 20th century", T005).
- **Melody** - Thrusting linear arpeggios = battle, circling = peace [Rumph 507–511] top. Battle cry: minor, obsessive sequences starting in cellos and bassoons and sweeping the orchestra [Monelle 171] conv. Calls at 160 [Monelle 121, 180–181] top. War as horror: unmarchable 5/4, serpentine chromatics, mocking calls [Monelle 180] conv. Fast 4/4 or 5/4, low, minor or major with dissonance (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Armies meeting: phrygian → minor → octatonic [Lehman 65] conv. Battle speech: IV → III6 swerve [Lehman 70] conv. Charge: bII – V – I as a solo jumps to tutti [Lehman 181] conv. Imposed order: rigid root-position planing [Lehman 144–145] conv. Mass horror: minor tritone pairs falling by whole tones [Lehman 156–158] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Power*, *The great battle*, *Encounter battle music*; snare, taiko; ominous chant for a dark host (T159). Allies charging: trumpet calls, bare brass fifths, driving bass, taiko and timpani [Stephen 65] conv. Drum calls (assembly, charge, retreat) on snare or toms [Haringer 197–198] top. (inf.). National strand: snare, piccolo trills, the enemy's anthem in minor [Summers 67–69] conv. Peace then war: piping winds and pizzicato, then battle percussion [Sisman 109–110] top.
- **With / against** - Against peace. With grief, *Sad Battle Music*, the dysphoric march or the war-film adagio [Summers 148] top. With the army, the muster. A swing between one soldier's body and the nation [Summers 63–69] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a snare roll. Strong: battle states from "mobilize" up [Summers 37–38] conv. Whole: a told battle: fanfares from the theme, the storm, the theme as heroic tutti [Dickensheets 127] top. (inf.); build, then hit hard [Summers 136] conv.
- **Not** - One dynamic throughout. Glossy action music, which confuses war with fun; keep consequence: falls, minor, weight [TLTT 633–647, 665–666] [T] (inf.). A perky march for the fighting itself.

#### Chaos
- **Cues** - High complexity plus high dynamism = anxiety and aggression (§1 Rhythm [W]); irregular rhythm (§6 r8); atonality; competing expectations (§6 r21). Disorder field (VVA 128); clutter is chaos's social anaphone (MM 514); fast asymmetric atonal rhythm = barbarism (Kojak §6.5.1 [T]). Mixing characters was the condemned "disorderly style" [Mirka Intro 5–8] top. Messy and hostile went to dense, loud, low, dry percussion with nothing to follow [TLTT 633–643] [T], weak. Haydn's *Representation of Chaos* is ombra (slow, dark, uncertain), not tempesta [McClelland 294] top.: primordial chaos can be slow.
- **Melody** - Motifs overlapping in different metres; runs in opposite directions; a singer changing manner every few bars [Hunter 65–66] top. Fragments, no stable metre, wide leaps (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Symmetric cycles read as chaos with irregular phrase lengths, dynamics and scoring, and as order with regular ones [Lehman 156] conv. Restless keys (twenty-six minor keys in four minutes) [Lehman 46–47, 80–82] conv. Out of it, a sudden huge triad (i → bVI6) for awe [Lehman 171] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Drumbeat of Chaos*, `unhinge`, clashing entries; buffa ensembles with several groups at once [Hunter 65–66] top. Creation's chaos: dissonance, uneasy winds, delayed cadences before the light (T026).
- **With / against** - **Against order**: order in one layer, chaos breaking in. With creation, chaos resolving to light.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one clashing entry. Whole: chaos, kept short.
- **Not** - Long stretches of noise (§7.6 r12; §3).

#### Chase and pursuit
- **Cues** - Chase code over-scored for the fast sports tune and an electronic action tune (VVA 594 [T]). Regular, low, metronomic reiteration on one pitch is a chase engine: the strongest chase tune (5.2×) was dry staccato octaves two bars on, two off [TLTT 494–498, 654–656] [T]; what relaxes a chase is height, brightness and major, not square phrasing (corrects concepts-v2 Not). Riding becomes chase only with a heavy active bass, dissonant mid-high strings and woodwinds, and brass and percussion stabs [TLTT 299–305] [T]; chase is unscenic and needs two sides with clashing aims [TLTT 298–302] [T]/(inf.). Flight and pursuit are tempesta [McClelland 285–287] top. Chases need many agogic off-beat accents [TLTT 600–601] conv.
- **Melody** - Fast octave bass riff 1–3–4–#4–5 with a syncopated accent on #4 (cellos, basses, bassoons, timpani on accents): fight, chase, panic [TLTT 599–601] [T]. Fits-and-starts tune in irregular teleprinter rhythm, trumpets in thirds in the gaps, accelerando, ending on a timpani roll over a static low pedal [TLTT 598–599] [T] conv. Fast minor with sforzandi and repeated notes [TLTT 317–319] conv. Galloping eighths as the minimal marker [Lowe 610–626] top. Irregular groupings (2+2+2, 2+3) (SW 50). The pursuer's motif a semitone higher at each stage [Lehman 57–58] conv.
- **Harmony** - Chained pump-ups timed to the ordeal; when the hero wins, octatonic turns mixolydian on the same root [Lehman 57–58] conv. Minor-third modulations for danger [Lehman 249 n15] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Fast string ostinato in uneven groups, snare, staccato brass, the trommelbass (T052). Panic: fast repeated notes and scales up and down [McClelland 284] top. Wild Hunt: 6/8 horns over a rising chromatic bass and trombones [Dickensheets Table 2] top. Exhilarated pursuit: hunt fanfare + stretto + brilliant runs + gigue [Hatten 523–529] top. Escape: 6/8 timpani and snare, low strings on I and V, then oboe and choir building to tutti [Summers 51] conv.
- **With / against** - With fear, flight; with the hunt, the hunter's side (the stag's beats as a cue sheet [Monelle 53–56] top.); with comedy, a slapstick chase. Off course strip to bass and drums, on course restore the tune [Summers 190–192] conv.; fade battle music when the party flees [Summers 166] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one accelerating run. Whole: a low reiterated ostinato that speeds up.
- **Not** - Bright high major busyness for a chase with stakes [TLTT 494–498] [T]. Harp sweeps, shimmer, tonic-dominant timpani, no brass [TLTT 300] (inf.). Legato waves at walking pace [TLTT 165–166] [T]. Pump-ups and accelerando past belief [Lehman 249 n16] conv.

#### Riders and the horse
- **Cues** - The noble horse topic (from the 1790s): fast, noble, masculine, warlike; it always gallops because the gallop carries the nobility [Monelle 23–31, 130] top.; dysphoric as the witches' or death's ride (T010, MCK07). Tagg's Western over-scored horses 8× and riding 6× (VVA 283, 564 [T]); riding 9.4×, heard after two bars [TLTT 292, 297] [T]. A gallop needs its stack: rhythm, clop timbre and genre together [TLTT 296–297] (inf.). Calm, scenic riding: no brass, a light bass, consonance, timpani only on I and V, harp or celesta shimmer [TLTT 299–300] [T]. Compound time itself meant cavalry; walk 72, trot about 140 [Monelle 123] top.
- **Melody** - The gallop: three or four quick notes into a note as long as them all, in 6/8, 9/8, 12/8 or 2/4 and 4/4 (*William Tell*) [TLTT 295; Monelle 4–5] conv./top. (corrects "two quick notes" and 6/8-only). Chivalric gallop as allegro 6/8 with horn fifths on top [Dickensheets 125] top. Strum figure: a quaver plus four semiquavers per dotted crotchet near 76 [TLTT 292–296] conv. Gaits: clip-clop walk near 108, minor trot near 104, gallop [TLTT 303–305] conv. Posthorn octaves and fifths for a courier [Monelle 84] top. Trail ride: heavy-pickup ostinato, oboe on top, major [TLTT 590–592] conv.
- **Harmony** - Horse-lords: a dorian theme, tritone swerves before each new theme, the last swallowed into bII – V – I tutti [Lehman 179–185] conv. Frontier rider: bVII → V [Lehman 200] top.
- **Colours and devices** - Calm ride: gallop in mid-register spiccato violas and cellos or harp repeats, soft clops as kit snare or high pizzicato on quavers 2 and 5 [TLTT 292–296] conv.; horns on the tune. Basses, low toms and timpani only on I and V; the low gallop is for a chase, charge or dread rider [TLTT 295–305] conv. [T] (corrects "6/8 in strings and toms"). Cavalry: trumpets (low, dignified calls), timpani, brass-only marches [Monelle 117–123, 139–141] top.; *boute-selle* to mount [Haringer 197–198] top. Horse-lords: solo_violin (Hardanger) over an open-fifth drone, dorian, horns and trumpets (LOTR Rohan [W][S]). Dread rider: minor gallop with trombones and choir (T010).
- **With / against** - With vastness, the plain; chivalry, the knight; the hunt, the shared 6/8 [Monelle 70, 86] top.; the dysphoric, the headless horseman [Monelle 87] conv.; loneliness, the lone rider. With chase, add low drive, dissonance, brass stabs [TLTT 305] [T]. The horse can be implied without a gallop [TLTT 303–305] [T].
- **Intensity** - Hint: a gallop figure in mid strings. Whole: a 6/8 ride.
- **Not** - A 4/4 march, which is infantry (a 4/4 gallop is not a march). A rigidly literal gait, which loses "going somewhere" [TLTT 297] (inf.).

#### Weariness
- **Cues** - "Weary" (AMM); the tired pole of energy arousal (EV). Slow means low energy and age; low pitch and falling seconds and thirds mean energy draining (Tagg, Death); listlessness (VVA 1237, 1260). Low riffy unchanging repetition drew tiring and boring [TLTT 622–623] [T]. The march eases fatigue: for tired marchers keep the pulse steady and let the tune droop [Haringer 200] top., droop (inf.). Jaded dejection: slow, a stumbling drone, minor v → i [TLTT 188–189] top. Ennui: solo violin recitative over held chords [Sisman 106–107] top.
- **Melody** - "Going down slow": a wide falling phrase that tails off [TLTT 595–597] conv. Phrases that sag at each cadence; *alla zoppa* for a limp (T054). Slow 4/4, low-middle, minor or plain major (inf.).
- **Harmony** - A step modulation down: relaxation and sighing [Lehman 54, 62–64] top. Minor v → i, no leading note [TLTT 188–189] top.
- **Colours and devices** - A slow walking bass dragging late (pizzicato basses), *Softer and slower*, thinning texture. The ride home after the hunt (*retraite*) [Monelle 55, 78] top. The end-of-parade call quoted softly under a triple-time serenade: rest, the war is over [Monelle 169–170] conv.
- **With / against** - With the journey, the long march home; with adversity, the struggle near its end; with rest, relief. With sanctuary: calm woodland on the Ave Maria progression [TLTT 177] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a phrase that sags. Whole: a slowing march.
- **Not** - Grief: weariness is the body, not the heart.

#### Rest and sleep
- **Cues** - Lullaby features: falling contours, low register, even timing, warm timbre (§8.6 r8 [S-M]). Lullaby topic: rocking 6/8 or triple, I–V over a pedal, repetition with pauses, soft, flat keys (T098). What makes it a lullaby is the slow surface, short conjunct phrases, droned shuttles and constant low dynamics; the rocking itself equals a romance arpeggio's [TLTT 250] top. Still strings drew rest 7× [TLTT 521–537] [T]. Sleep is celestial [McClelland 287] top. Break music: a quiet slowed variant that invites people back [Summers 19] conv.
- **Melody** - Unornamented, folk-like tune over a simple accompaniment (Lied-style subtype) [Dickensheets 106] top. Rocking 6/8 with a wind duet in thirds, or folk simplicity [Monelle 233, 254] conv. Narrow range, stepwise, falling phrase ends (§8.6 r8).
- **Harmony** - I–V over a tonic pedal (T098). "Sweet sleep": the Ave Maria progression [TLTT 177–178] top. Whole-tone for the half-asleep state [Lehman 52–53] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Lullaby*: harp and soft strings over a pedal, flutes in thirds, celesta at the end. A soldier's rest: triple-time serenade with a fanfare outline below [Monelle 169–170] conv. Lullaby as magic: a flute tune that lulls a guardian, for a bard [Webster Potter 195–197] conv.
- **With / against** - With home, the hearth at night; innocence, the cradle; threat, an inverted lullaby (T106); night, the nocturne.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a rocking bass. Whole: a lullaby.
- **Not** - A cadence that wakes: no sudden endings.

### K. Story moments

#### Triumph
- **Cues** - Loud, major, bright; a returning theme (§4); suffering turned to joy (§7.1); a narrow range can read triumphant (§1 Range [W]). Triumphant/heroic is cross-cultural (COW) and a GEMS power item (.76). Success over-scored for the brisk brass march (VVA 1164 [T]); successful 6.9×, its "war" answers were victory and homecoming [TLTT 399–410] [T]. Timpani mean grand and triumphal [Sisman 108; Haringer 198] top. Win cues congratulate in proportion [Summers 23–24] conv.
- **Melody** - Crescendo on a rising line, marked rhythms, powerful bass and unisons (victory-ode allegro) [Sisman 92–96] top. A rising line into the last tonic [Lehman 192–197] (inf.); a leap of an eleventh to a held high tonic at the end of an AABA arc = the goal reached [TLTT 658–660] (inf.). The victor's entry: slow alla breve march, dotted, the chorus taking up the tune [Monelle 160–161] top.
- **Harmony** - Subtonic victory bVI – bVII – I (in minor, Picardy to the same) [Lehman 3, 200] top.; hard-won bVI – IV – I or bIII – IV – I [Lehman 199–200] top.; i → biii, then a big major tonic [Lehman 152] conv.; withhold V – I until victory is sure [Lehman 23] conv. Picardy i → I as elevation [TLTT 383, 389] (inf.); aeolian bVI–bVII–i lifted to major [Stephen 74] conv. Two kinds: grandioso (public; theme augmented, ff, brass chorale over a pedal) and bifocal (a fanfare bursting from minor without resolving it: inner victory at a cost) [Byros 405–407] top. Closure by force: falling la-pentatonic tutti turnaround [TLTT 630–631] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Triumphant Reprise*, *Victorious Chorus*, *Fanfare*, *The climax*. Apotheosis: heroic fanfare, then the theme broadened as chorale (T085; [Dickensheets 118, 124] (inf.)); heroic style dropped in at any topic's turning point gives the victorious turn [Dickensheets 118] top. Mixed choir with cascading violins [Barron 84] conv.; earlier themes combined in one fugato [Hatten 526] top.; the power theme over the agency rhythm as drum ostinato [Halfyard Apprentice 229] conv. Film: Lydian brass blaze; major chorale with wordless choir (SW 22, SW 58).
- **With / against** - With grief, a costly victory (bifocal); with the community, the finale; themes heard apart returning together [Horton 658–659] conv. The campaign theme must be known early: withhold only its full tutti apotheosis (Summers correction).
- **Intensity** - Grade it: skirmish < boss < arc, with the main theme reprised at the arc's end [Summers 17, 23] conv. Hint: one bVI – bVII – I. Strong: the last statement lifted a tone [Winters 120; Lehman 56–58] conv./top. Whole: apotheosis, perhaps a still sound-field after the last cadence [Horton 660] conv.
- **Not** - Triumph not earned, or tutti when nothing was won [Summers 153] conv. A victory with no hero theme, which reads as relief [Brill 28] conv.

#### Defeat
- **Cues** - Two kinds. Story defeat (a cutscene): falling lines, collapse after a peak (§7.6 r8), slowing, minor; Tagg files failure with weakness and fear (VVA 1266). Defeat in play: the fail cue cuts straight in, brief and commiserating, then hands back to play so the table wants to go again [Summers 23, 166] conv. (correction). Music rarely marks failure; less music or silence is the usual tool [TLTT 389] conv.; silence while the hero loses = helplessness [Brill 35] conv.
- **Melody** - A fall of more than an octave = disappointment or bathos [TLTT 217] top. A phrase that stops short; slow 4/4, minor, falling seconds (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Deceptive V → vi: falling back [Lehman 54] conv. A loop that stops before the tonic (bVI – bVII …): a failed return [Lehman 263 n42] conv. bVI turned bvi as the tide turns [Lehman 181] conv. The villain's motif completing its cadence.
- **Colours and devices** - *The hero's breakdown*, *Deflation*. Funeral march for a defeat cutscene (T007). Surrender: a snare *chamade* alone, then silence [Haringer 197–198] (inf.). The march that leads to the protagonist's death [Horton 643–644] top.
- **With / against** - With hope, the rally to come; with desperation, the last stand failing.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a phrase that stops short. Strong: everything stops but one low held note (futility) [TLTT 660–661] conv. Whole: the theme broken, or a modal minor that never reaches its major tutti [TLTT 390–391] (inf.).
- **Not** - Corruption: defeat breaks the theme, it does not change it. Wallowing after an in-play loss [Summers 23] conv. An arrival gesture for a failed arrival [TLTT 389] conv.

#### The reveal
- **Cues** - A sudden loud rough onset raises arousal (§8.6 r2); dread first makes it land (§8.6 r13); music can replace ambience at a key revelation (§6 r36); an anomalous chord as frozen insight (§7.6 r8 [W]). Surprise is unreliable (EV), so prepare it. Biggest moves on reveals and wide shots [Lehman 139–141] conv.; congruence boosts emotion and memory [Lehman 63] [ev]. Unprepared switches of scoring, key or tempo send listeners looking for the story reason [TLTT 555–562] [T]. Land the event on the music: take a beat before revealing [Summers 73, 76] conv.; or put the musical arrival slightly off the reveal to layer expectation [Lehman 261 n29] conv.
- **Melody** - One unidirectional sweep (clarinet scoop, unison string run, harp glissando): up = transition, down = flashback [TLTT 559–562, 598] [T] conv.; a one-second run-up (MM 516–522). Revealed truth: falling minor arpeggio triplets on harp and celesta (SW 23). Let a figure's theme arrive before the figure is revealed [Summers 74, 160–161] conv.
- **Harmony** - Chromatically modulating cadence: V7 → bIII taken as home [Lehman 14, 201] top.; a tritone swerve into a clear theme [Lehman 179–185] conv.; a quiet slide for quiet realisation [Lehman 103] conv. Pause on an unresolved chord, then the outburst [McClelland 283–285] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Shock*, *Stings*, *Silence and the stop*, run-up (T186). Hold one instrument back all score and bring it in at the reveal (trombones) [McClelland 288] top.; start quiet so the band bursts in [Sisman 91] top. Hidden reference: a theme on flute or cellos, revealed on horn [Monelle 3, 100] top. (lineage (inf.)). Plant a sound or chord early, explain it later [Halfyard Apprentice 225; Lehman 104–105; Agawu 479] conv. Music before a sealed letter opens [Webster Potter 199] conv.; low rumble as the coming end is understood [Hayward 53] conv.
- **With / against** - After suspense or dread. A general pause then a thin texture reads as awe [Margulis 636–638] [ev]. A floating theme revealed to link three scenes [Murphy 151–153] conv. Repeated, a sweep becomes form (episode, then closure) [TLTT 555–562] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a sting. Strong: a stop, then the hit. Whole: a prepared build to the revealed theme.
- **Not** - More than one shock or sting a session. Harmonic frissons can be stacked in one cue if each comes in a new context and the first is biggest [Lehman 179–181] conv. (correction).

#### Redemption
- **Cues** - Suffering to healing, dark to light, ending in tonic major (§7.1; §7.6 r4); the outsider absorbed (§7.6 r5); Hatten's tragic-to-transcendent (HAT23). Peace by stripping away: the resolved character's theme returns quietly, Lied-style, without the agitated material [Dickensheets 136] top.
- **Melody** - A villain's motto first hesitant, minor and sighing, later turned (SW 41/42). Restore a motive pushed off the beat back to its plain on-beat form [Rumph 501–511] top. Slow, stepwise, major at last (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Keep the villain's moves but change their order and weight [Lehman 149–153] conv. Undo the dark move: i → bVI becomes i → bIII [Lehman 131] conv.; threat mode recoloured mixolydian [Lehman 58] conv.; chromaticism liquidated into a plain V – I [Lehman 233] conv.; breakthrough to major [Lehman 262 n30] conv. iii → I = resurrection [Byros 404] top. bVI → V6/4 with the tune climbing to a consonant fifth: pain opening upward [TLTT 212] top. Mixed plagal cadence for a sacrifice remembered (T157).
- **Colours and devices** - *Corruption* reversed, *Sentimental Music Cue*, *Triumphant Reprise*. Funeral march → hymn → winds in major [Sisman 111] conv.
- **With / against** - Needs the fall heard first. A Picardy third as demons vanish is release without redemption [McClelland 289–290] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one chord restored to its bright form, or the last chord major. Whole: the theme's full journey back (inf.).
- **Not** - Skipping the darkness. Adding brass glory where stripping away is the point.

#### The fall
- **Cues** - Minor, relative major, then minor = tragedy or relapse (§7.6 r4); false hope undercut (§7.1); descent (VVA 535). A downfall theme on the Dies irae cell with wordless lament (SW 37); a boy's tune hiding the villain's march (SW 24).
- **Melody** - Downshifting: every voice sinks a semitone [Lehman 105–107] conv. A theme chromaticised step by step until a tritone cracks it [Lehman 114–117] conv. The le–sol–fi–sol lurch into minor from a major home [Byros 384–385] top., as fall (inf.). Slow, minor, descending (inf.).
- **Harmony** - A literal plunge: i ↔ bVI with tutti and choir, then a cut to a lone high voice [Lehman 260 n17, 261 n21] conv. Keys going flatter at each refusal [McClelland 288] top.
- **Colours and devices** - *Descent*, *Corruption*, *Last Note Nightmare*. A ready damnation storyboard (Gluck's *Don Juan*) [McClelland 288–290] top.: dread with tremolo and angular bass; the summoner's rising chromatics over a pedal, vii°7 and trombone octaves; heaven refused in an irregular phrase; a glissando (`slide`) as he is seized; three loud trombone repeats as the ground opens and vii°7s falling; demons in ff falling scales; a silent pause after vii°7; a minor coda of rushing semiquavers dying away.
- **With / against** - With pride, the proud theme decaying. Reverse it for redemption.
- **Intensity** - Hint: a turn in the last bar. Whole: a *Descent*.
- **Not** - One sudden switch, unless the abruptness is the point.

#### Farewell
- **Cues** - Swan-song simplicity (§3; §6 r28 [W-M]); peak and end dominate memory (§7.6 r19). Parting over-scored for the R&J tune (VVA 532 [T]): goodbye 6.1×, leaving 8.1×, absent from crime tunes on the same chord [TLTT 434, 459] [T]; still strings drew goodbye with longing [TLTT 521–537] [T]. Sighs carry paired opposites: parting and meeting together [TLTT 158–166, 224–225] [T]. Exitive music differs from arriving [TLTT 134] top. Antique melancholy as farewell [Head 268–269] top.
- **Melody** - Lebewohl: three slow plain chords into an interrupted cadence, top voice in horn fifths (T003). Horn fifths for farewell and memory [Sisman 102] top. A dotted figure with a sobbing repeated note [Head 268–269] top. Legato diatonic line with a b3–2 rocking [TLTT 453–464] conv. The end-of-parade call, slow and soft [Monelle 169–170] conv.
- **Harmony** - iadd9 under the line [TLTT 453–464] conv. A slightly widened lament bass under sighing suspensions [Head 272–273] top. I – bVII – iiø7 – I with closed parallel fifths for an old farewell; a warm static final major [Lehman 237–239] conv. End on I6 or Iadd9.
- **Colours and devices** - *Melancholy Major Key*, *Withholding and the last reprise*. Two horns on the Lebewohl. Solo_violin suspensions over a cellos lament bass, `wobble` on each resolution [Head 272–273] (inf.). One voice haloed by harp and flutes in sixths at the sob [Ivanovitch 333–335] conv. Offstage horns receding [Monelle 80–81] conv. Sea passage: flute and high voice (LOTR Grey Havens [S]). Shepherds' farewell: paired 3/8 after oboe and clarinet pipe calls [Monelle 246–247] conv.
- **With / against** - With nostalgia and love. With the fellowship, the company scattering voice by voice: Haydn's staged diminuendo [Sisman 108] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one pair of horn fifths or an open final chord. Strong: the farewell figure as refrain. Whole: a simplified theme thinning to one voice.
- **Not** - Ornate writing.

#### Sacrifice
- **Cues** - A mixed emotion, negative turning to positive (§7.4; §7.6 r20). Heroic death is the funeral march's own meaning (T007). Ritual sacrifice is ombra ceremonial: stile antico, alla breve, unison chant, funeral march [McClelland 285–287] top. A call bent a note or two = men who must die [Monelle 166] top.; Roland's horn calling too late [Monelle 106–107] top.
- **Melody** - Le–sol–fi–sol, the schema of submission and acceptance [Byros 396–404] top. In extremity: a firm minor tune modulating twice on horns and trumpets (SW 56). A hero's death: minor siciliana or lament bass, then the celestial for the soul at rest (inf.).
- **Harmony** - Two kinds. Sacrifice by yielding: hybrid major/minor tritone pairs (I–#iv, i–bV), few of them, no melodic tritone leap; then tritone-free death rocking (i ↔ v); silence; a quiet clear key ending on a major triad [Murphy 159, 167–169] conv. (correction). Sacrifice by fighting or reversed (eucatastrophe): the sacrifice in iii, then a sudden tonic-major fanfare that does not reconcile them [Byros 404–408] top. Pre-emptive elegy: I ↔ iii as the hero readies to die [Lehman 100] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Yielding: soft strings and horns, a lone trumpet line [Murphy] (inf.). Fighting or reversed: *Leitmotif upon Death*, then *Triumphant Reprise*; *Cherubic Choir* on the first major chord after. Heroic elegy: bells, pause, funeral march, pause, an elegy swelling into passion for the cause [Dickensheets 110] top. National sacrifice: after long silence, an adagio on strings and horns (Nimrod manner) [Summers 148] top. Last-post trumpet (T184).
- **With / against** - Grief and triumph clash: give each its moment (loss, a stop, the theme at its noblest). Heroic + lament, then continued in major [Caplin 446–447] top.
- **Intensity** - Hint: the theme's last statement in major. Whole: the full arc.
- **Not** - Averaging grief and triumph into a mild middle. The fighting hero's full tritone blaze for a sacrifice by yielding [Murphy 167–169] conv.

#### Combat and the duel
- **Cues** - "Roll initiative": agitation is the commonest high-arousal story feeling at the table (BAR). Conflict over-scored for slow minor jazz and action tunes (VVA 592 [T]); the slow jazz tune drew fighting 8.9× [TLTT 539–550] [T]. Asymmetric rhythm = hurried excitement over regular harmony, terror over atonality (Kojak §6.5.1). Antiphony is the duel (MM 514); a duel needs two clashing parties [TLTT 302] (inf.). Standard battle cue: allegro vivace, heroic-military, brass marcato and fanfare figures, snare and cymbal, driving strings, able to sustain long fights [Summers 166] conv. Combat as tempesta: syncopated inner strings, repeated-note basses (inf.).
- **Melody** - Fight riff: low octaves 1–3–4–#4–5, accent on #4 [TLTT 599–601] [T]. Boss progress ladder: each big hit answered a step higher in brass, back to the bottom if the boss heals [Summers 194] conv. Gun-duel trumpet: florid and lament-inflected [TLTT 371] conv. Party action theme opening on a rising brass fifth [Halfyard & Hancock 187–188] conv. A duel as two characters' topics built from one motif [Dickensheets 135] top.
- **Harmony** - Blunt step modulations cut through a fight's noise [Lehman 56] conv.; each new stage a minor third up keeps danger rising [Lehman 249 n15] conv. Tragic duel: chant-like cell over i–bVI with chorus (SW 38). Ambush: tempesta entering on a deceptive cadence [Agawu 484] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Encounter battle music*, *Happy Battle Music*, *Boss Remix*. Virtuosic figuration as forward drive [Dickensheets 135–136] top.; timpani-backed tutti between quick detached exchanges [Sisman 90–91] top. Climactic battle: open on low repeated pitches with timpani, toms, snare; then angular string bursts and organ antiphony; end on a long low pedal and cymbal, or fade [Summers 51] conv. Skeletons: scherzo macabre with pizzicato, glockenspiel, toms, brass `slide` [Brill 30–34] conv. Standoff: a solo with slides over a held fifth, a bell, long silence (T152) — the twang as low-middle harp left ringing or pizzicato, not english_horn (correction) [TLTT 376–382] conv.; snare crack, struck bells, low flute [TLTT 370] conv.
- **With / against** - Ordinary fights share one campaign cue; foe colour only from lieutenant rank up, a final boss heard once [Summers 167] top. (correction). Bookends: instant switch in, fanfare on a win, fade on flight, short sting on a loss [Summers 166] conv. An ally's death overrides the battle cue with their theme [Summers 160] conv. With war, scale up; with comedy, slapstick.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one staccato brass figure or one pump-up at the turn. Whole: a battle loop restated higher each round (inf.); withhold the hero theme until the turn [Brill 35] conv.
- **Not** - One unchanging texture (critic check). Cutting music mid-fight [Summers 153] conv. Hitting every blow with choir and orchestra [Binns 140] conv.

#### Desperation and the last stand
- **Cues** - The desperate sits with danger and tension (VVA 1224). The stag at bay, standing to the end [Monelle 55, 78] top.; Roland's unanswered horn [Monelle 106–107] top. The hero theme stays silent while the stand fails and enters at the turn [Brill 35] conv. Two treatments: pounding (below) or, for a noble stand, long silence then a broad adagio for the final minutes [Summers 148] top.
- **Melody** - The hero's theme in minor or fragments (HAT23), sharply drawn, changing key twice (SW 56). An angular unharmonised line in octaves over tremolo, growing from *p* [McClelland 289–290] top. A high 6/8 halali with its last notes echoed softly [Monelle 52] top. (inf.).
- **Harmony** - The dire stand: aeolian over drums and a held bass pedal, grim [Lehman 167] conv. The "problem": a bright bVI darkened to bvi, then a long chord a tritone away [Lehman 179–185] conv. If saved: major tritone I ↔ #IV, loud and sustained [Murphy 155–157] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - Pounding low strings, timpani and taiko over a basses pedal (inf.); horns and trumpets on the theme; appassionato surging minor (T033). Noble stand: strings and horns in an elegiac English-pastoral adagio [Summers 148] top. *The turning tide* if it holds, *Sad Battle Music* if not.
- **With / against** - With sacrifice, the stand that costs a life; with rescue, lift the whole texture a minor third (i → biii) as help arrives [Lehman 166–167] conv.; with hope, the turn. The silence before the adagio is part of it [Summers 148] conv.
- **Intensity** - Hint: the theme's head in minor. Whole: the theme driven through two modulations to a climax.
- **Not** - Panic: a last stand is resolved and steady.

#### Arrival and homecoming
- **Cues** - Adventive field (approaching, arriving, coming home) and "goal reached, finally" (VVA 531, 033); arrival needs its own music [TLTT 134] top. Solo → full strings, mf → f, minor → relative major or Picardy = arrival, which is musically happy ("happy ending", "good wins") [TLTT 387–391] [T]. A change of music on entering makes a place register as new [Summers 163] conv. Entrées introduce people and must be shaded by who enters [Haringer 202–203] top.; fanfares call attention for court, town or field [Sisman 115] top.
- **Melody** - At arrival the accompaniment's semiquavers move into the tune and final quavers split: dance and community [TLTT 387–394] (inf.). Herald's two-trumpet call; intrada for a great person [Monelle 135, 163–164] top. The *cavalquet*, an army nearing a city [Haringer 197–198] top. The hunt's ride home [Monelle 55, 78] top.
- **Harmony** - Static warm major after instability [Lehman 237–238] conv.; a modal cadence into a triumphal theme [Lehman 174–176] conv. Close over a tonic pedal, a drone after the cadence for Arcadia regained [Galand 456] conv. "Goal reached": a plagal cadence arriving on the piece's first root-position tonic as the bass returns, motif echoed an octave up [TLTT 536–537] [T]. Parallel major chords over a held tonic for a safe landing (SW 40). The leave-and-return harmonic-rhythm arch [TLTT 214] top.
- **Colours and devices** - The road's walking pulse landing on a held tonic; the home theme in full; a lord's fanfare (offstage soft trumpets). Hero's return: trumpets, timpani, rising dotted scales; priestly: horns and trombones, softer (inf.). Reinforcements: rising cellos `slide` to one bells stroke; a ship: snare roll and a harp chord [Summers 120] conv. Hunt call over a pastoral pedal with string trills [Horton 659] conv.
- **With / against** - With the journey, its end; with home, the hearth regained; with nostalgia, the place changed (home theme plus one new chord). Approaching, the region bed gains instruments until it becomes the destination's theme [Summers 75–77] conv. Minor solo on the trail → major community tutti = the outsider settling [TLTT 394] (inf.).
- **Intensity** - Hint: a tonic pedal under the last phrase. Whole: the home theme over the road's pulse.
- **Not** - A victory fanfare for a simple homecoming. An arrival gesture for a failed or baffling arrival: cut the music [TLTT 389] conv.

#### Rescue
- **Cues** - Unlooked-for aid: a pure high voice entering as help arrives (LOTR eagles [T][S]); Hatten's tragic-to-triumphant (HAT23); a brass chorale for divine intervention (T022); an unexpected event feels intended (§4). Shore's arc: the harmony poses a problem, then one bold gesture launches a leitmotif [Lehman 178–179] conv.; resolve fast into a known theme so the jolt becomes relief [Lehman 173, 185] conv. Kind gods calming the storm: celestial over tempesta [McClelland 280–287] top.
- **Melody** - The rescuers' theme, bold and clear; a high choir or flute line over the despairing texture; a wind fanfare breaking into a hymn [Sisman 111] conv.
- **Harmony** - Tonal deus ex machina: a dire aeolian lifted a minor third, i → biii, still minor so it is rescue, not victory [Lehman 166–167] conv. The riders arrive: i (dorian) → bVI → bvi → long chord a tritone away → the hero theme in the parallel major [Lehman 179–185] conv. The hand caught at the last second: major tritone I ↔ #IV [Murphy 155–157] conv.; terror lifted: sudden i → I with cymbals [Brill 30] conv.
- **Colours and devices** - *Saved by the Church Bell*, *The turning tide*. "Help is available": a rising cellos `slide` ending on one bells stroke, always with this meaning [Summers 120, 153] conv. Watchtower trumpet announcing the rescuer, far then nearer [Monelle 167] conv. (inf.).
- **With / against** - Needs desperation first; with the divine, a god's help; with friendship, companions returning.
- **Intensity** - Hint: one high voice entering. Whole: despair turning on one chord; or silence, then the theme in a new key at full scoring [Halfyard Apprentice 224] (inf.).
- **Not** - Arriving early. Lingering on the strange chord: the frisson needs a clear key on both sides [Lehman 185, 191] top.

#### Exploration and discovery
- **Cues** - Exitive field: exploration, finding the way (VVA 5322); "discovering novelty" (GMC); wonder needs an arresting event (§7.6 r11). Searching, questions and "a long time" came from harmonic blur: parallel motion holding a mild dissonance, IV ↔ V never arriving [TLTT 560–561] [T]. Sandbox exploration takes a non-reactive loop; fade it if the table stalls about 30 seconds [Summers 36, 104–106] conv. The hunt's unforeseen place outside the walls [Monelle 65–68, 95] top.
